The Dashwoods had been rooted in Sussex so long that the very hedgerows seemed to remember their name. Norland Park sat in the middle of its acres like a well-kept hearthstone—broad lawns, old trees, and, in the seasons of fruit, an air that carried sweetness from orchard to pantry. For generations the house had been lived in with such steady decency that the neighboring families spoke of it as they spoke of a familiar tune: not startling, not new, but comforting in its repetitions.

The late master of Norland had been a bachelor, and for years his household had moved to the quiet rhythm kept by his sister—his companion, his housekeeper, the one who knew which linen wanted airing and which preserves needed turning. When she died, ten years before him, the rooms altered at once; not in their furniture, but in their sound. A single footstep echoes differently when it is no longer answered by another. To fill that long hush, he invited into Norland the family of his nephew, Mr. Henry Dashwood—the legal inheritor of the estate, and the man the old gentleman meant to favor.

So Mr. Henry Dashwood came with his wife and daughters, and with him came the domestic music of a younger household: children’s voices in the passages, quick laughter from the shrubbery, the scrape of chairs at supper, the clink of spoons in bowls of warm milk. In time even the old man’s breathing seemed easier within such company. Mr. and Mrs. Henry Dashwood attended him with a constancy that was not merely dutiful but kind; they learned the small preferences of age—when the fire should be made up, how the tea should be drawn, what hour best suited a walk beneath the cedars. Their goodness gave him a solid comfort, and the children—by their very being alive and hungry and in motion—gave relish to days that might otherwise have tasted thin.

Mr. Henry Dashwood had a son by a former marriage, and three daughters by his present lady. The son, John, was a steady, respectable young man, made secure by his mother’s fortune, half of which became his on coming of age. His own marriage soon after enlarged his means again. The Norland succession, then, was never so necessary to him as it was to his sisters; for their portion, without their father’s inheritance, must be small indeed. Their mother had brought nothing; their father had only seven thousand pounds at his own disposal. The remainder of his first wife’s fortune was tied to her child, and he himself held only a life-interest in it—like a man permitted the scent of a dish but denied the right to carve.

Then the old gentleman died. The will was read, and, as wills so often do, it managed to distribute disappointment as carefully as property. He was not so unjust, nor so ungrateful, as to cut off his nephew; but he left Norland in such a manner as to diminish, by a single legal turn, half the value of the inheritance. Mr. Dashwood had wished for the estate far more on account of his wife and daughters than for himself or his son; yet it was secured not to him freely, but to his son—and after him to his son’s son, a child of four. The whole was tied up for the benefit of that small heir in a way that left Mr. Henry Dashwood without power to provide for those dearest to him, who most needed it: no charge could be laid on the estate, no sale made of those valuable woods whose timber, standing straight and ancient, seemed to promise rescue.

The old gentleman had meant no unkindness. It was only that, in those occasional visits when John brought his wife and little boy to Norland, the child had gained upon his great-uncle’s affections with the common arts of infancy—half-formed words, a determined insistence on his own way, cunning tricks performed for praise, and a great deal of noise. Against that lively clamor, the years of considerate attention from Mr. Dashwood’s wife and daughters counted for less than they ought. Still, as a mark of affection, he left each of the three girls a thousand pounds.

Mr. Henry Dashwood’s first shock was severe. Yet his temper was cheerful and hopeful; he could imagine years ahead, and the possibility—by living economically—of laying by a considerable sum from an estate already large, and capable of almost immediate improvement. He began, in his mind, to measure out economies as a cook measures flour: not starving, only careful; not mean, only exact. But the fortune that had come so tardily was his for only one twelvemonth. He survived his uncle no longer. When he died, ten thousand pounds—his own seven thousand, with the three legacies—was all that remained for his widow and daughters.

His son was sent for as soon as his danger was known. The sickroom at Norland had its own low soundscape: the soft fall of a log settling in the grate, a whispered consultation at the door, the careful pouring of broth so it would not splash and startle. With what strength illness could command, Mr. Dashwood pressed John to protect the interest of his stepmother and sisters. John did not have the warm, overflowing feelings of the rest of them; but a recommendation made at such a time, in such a voice, could move even a cooler heart. He promised to do everything in his power to make them comfortable.

His father was eased by that assurance. And once the funeral was over, once the heavy black draperies were taken down and the visitors’ carriages ceased to crunch on the drive, Mr. John Dashwood had leisure to consider what, prudently, might be in his power to do.

He was not ill-disposed—unless coldness and selfishness deserve that name. In general he was well respected, for he behaved with propriety in the common discharge of his duties. Had he married a more amiable woman, he might have been made still more respectable; he might, perhaps, even have been made amiable himself, for he was very young when he married and very fond of his wife. But Mrs. John Dashwood was a strong caricature of him: narrower, sharper, more resolutely turned toward her own advantage.

When John gave his promise, he had it in his mind to increase the fortunes of his sisters by a gift of a thousand pounds apiece. At that moment he truly believed himself equal to it. The prospect of four thousand a year, in addition to his present income, besides the remaining half of his own mother’s fortune, warmed him into something like generosity. In the days that followed, he seasoned the thought as one seasons a dish—adding, tasting, admiring his own liberality.

“Yes,” he told himself, “I will give them three thousand pounds. It will be liberal and handsome. It will make them completely easy. Three thousand pounds! I can spare so considerable a sum with little inconvenience.”

He dwelt on it not only for a day, but for many days successively, and did not repent of his intention.

Yet no sooner was his father’s funeral concluded than Mrs. John Dashwood arrived—without sending the smallest notice to her mother-in-law—bringing her child and a train of attendants. No one could dispute her right; from the moment of Mr. Dashwood’s death the house was her husband’s. But the indelicacy of coming so, of taking possession as if grief had no claim to privacy, was the greater for being lawful. To a woman in Mrs. Dashwood’s situation—newly widowed, uncertain in her future, still raw with sorrow—it must, with only common feelings, have been highly unpleasing. In her mind, where honor was keen and generosity romantic, any offense of this kind—given or received—became a source of fixed disgust.

Mrs. John Dashwood had never been a favorite with any of her husband’s family; but until now she had not had occasion to show, at full length, how little regard she could pay to the comfort of other people when it suited her to act. Mrs. Dashwood felt the ungracious behavior acutely, and despised her daughter-in-law with such earnestness that she would have quitted Norland forever on the very day of their arrival, had not her eldest girl restrained her—first by entreaty to consider what propriety required, and afterward by appealing to that tender love which made Mrs. Dashwood endure much for the sake of her children and avoid, for them, an open breach with their brother.

Elinor, the eldest, whose counsel proved so effectual, possessed strength of understanding and coolness of judgment which qualified her, though only nineteen, to advise her mother. Again and again she had counteracted—quietly, firmly, to the advantage of them all—that eagerness of mind in Mrs. Dashwood which would otherwise have led to imprudence. Elinor’s heart was excellent. Her disposition was affectionate; her feelings were strong; but she knew how to govern them—knowledge her mother had yet to learn, and which one of her sisters had resolved never to be taught.

Marianne’s abilities were, in many respects, quite equal to Elinor’s. She was sensible and clever, but eager in everything. Her sorrows, her joys, could have no moderation. Generous, amiable, interesting—she was everything but prudent. The resemblance between her and her mother was striking. Elinor saw, with concern, the excess of her sister’s sensibility; but Mrs. Dashwood valued it and cherished it, as if intensity were proof of truth. Together now, mother and daughter encouraged each other in the violence of their affliction. The first agony of grief, overwhelming as it was, they did not allow to soften with time; they voluntarily renewed it, sought it, created it again and again. They gave themselves up wholly to sorrow, searching every recollection for whatever could deepen wretchedness, and resolving against ever admitting consolation.

Elinor, too, was deeply afflicted; but she could struggle and exert herself. She could consult with her brother, could receive her sister-in-law on her arrival and treat her with proper attention, and could strive to rouse her mother to similar exertion, to similar forbearance. She bore, in her very composure, the quiet strain of holding a household together when the world’s law and language favored another branch of the family—when the estate spoke, in its cold legal terms, of heirs and entailments, and could not be persuaded to pronounce the warmer, more human words: wife, daughters, home.

Margaret, the youngest, was good-humored and well disposed; but she had already imbibed a good deal of Marianne’s romance without having much of her sense. At thirteen she did not, as yet, bid fair to equal her sisters at a more advanced age.

Mrs. John Dashwood took Norland the way a new cook takes a kitchen: not with loud command, but with a quiet, complete possession—keys that clicked in locks, inventories taken in a low voice, cupboards opened and shut as if the house itself had been a larder newly assigned to her care. The older mistress of that ample place—Mrs. Dashwood—found herself, with her daughters, reduced to guests in rooms that still smelled of their former life: beeswax on the stair rail, lavender in the linen press, yesterday’s woodsmoke caught in the curtains. They were treated with an even politeness by Fanny, and with as much kindness by Mr. John Dashwood as he could spare for any creature outside the small circle of himself, his wife, and their little boy.

He did press them, with what he believed to be real earnestness, to consider Norland their home. And because no plan seemed so tolerable to Mrs. Dashwood as remaining where every path and hedge-row spoke a remembered happiness—until she could find a smaller house close by—she accepted his invitation. To stay was to keep her grief from breaking apart into the open air; to stay was to fold it into familiar routine. When she was cheerful, no one could be more bright, more quick to taste pleasure and expect more of it, as if happiness were a broth that, once set to simmer, must surely deepen. But in sorrow she was equally carried away—her imagination, like a pot left untended, boiling over beyond consolation.

Fanny did not at all approve of what her husband had once, in the first flush of duty, intended to do for his sisters. Three thousand pounds, taken from the fortune of their dear little boy, seemed to her not generosity but theft done with a tender name. She spoke of it as if he meant to ladle away Harry’s portion—spoon by spoon—until the child’s future sat thin and pale in the bowl.

She begged him to think again. How could he answer to himself, she asked, for robbing his only child of so large a sum? And what possible claim had the Miss Dashwoods—connected only by half blood, which she treated as scarcely blood at all—on his liberality to such an extent? It was, she said, very well known that affection was never supposed to exist between children of different marriages; why should he ruin himself, and their poor little Harry, by giving away his money to half sisters?

“It was my father’s last request,” her husband replied, “that I should assist his widow and daughters.”

“He did not know what he was talking of, I dare say,” returned Fanny, with the brisk certainty of a person who hears only what suits her. “Ten to one he was light-headed at the time. Had he been in his right senses, he could not have thought of begging you to give away half your fortune from your own child.”

“He did not stipulate for any particular sum,” said Mr. Dashwood. “He only requested me, in general terms, to assist them, to make their situation more comfortable than it was in his power to do. Perhaps it would have been as well if he had left it wholly to myself. He could hardly suppose I should neglect them. But as he required the promise, I could not do less than give it; at least I thought so at the time. The promise, therefore, was given, and must be performed. Something must be done for them whenever they leave Norland and settle in a new home.”

“Well, then, let something be done,” said Fanny. “But that something need not be three thousand pounds. Consider—when the money is once parted with, it never can return. Your sisters will marry, and it will be gone for ever. If, indeed, it could be restored to our poor little boy—”

“Why, to be sure,” he answered, very gravely, as if she had brought him a sensible account-book truth. “That would make great difference. The time may come when Harry will regret that so large a sum was parted with. If he should have a numerous family, for instance, it would be a very convenient addition.”

“To be sure it would.”

“Perhaps, then, it would be better for all parties if the sum were diminished one half. Five hundred pounds would be a prodigious increase to their fortunes.”

“Oh! beyond anything great!” cried Fanny. “What brother on earth would do half so much for his sisters, even if really his sisters! And as it is—only half blood!—But you have such a generous spirit!”

“I would not wish to do any thing mean,” he replied, pleased to be thought liberal even while shrinking from liberality. “One had rather, on such occasions, do too much than too little. No one, at least, can think I have not done enough for them: even themselves, they can hardly expect more.”

“There is no knowing what they may expect,” said Fanny, “but we are not to think of their expectations. The question is, what you can afford to do.”

“Certainly—and I think I may afford to give them five hundred pounds a-piece. As it is, without any addition of mine, they will each have about three thousand pounds on their mother’s death—a very comfortable fortune for any young woman.”

“To be sure it is; and indeed it strikes me they can want no addition at all. They will have ten thousand pounds divided amongst them. If they marry, they will be sure of doing well, and if they do not, they may all live very comfortably together on the interest of ten thousand pounds.”

“That is very true,” said he, feeling his conscience soften under her arithmetic, “and therefore I do not know whether, upon the whole, it would not be more advisable to do something for their mother while she lives, rather than for them—something of the annuity kind I mean. A hundred a year would make them all perfectly comfortable.”

Fanny hesitated—only a little, but enough to show that even a hundred pounds, paid steadily, struck her as a regular draught upon the household, like a leaky tap you cannot stop.

“To be sure,” she said at last, “it is better than parting with fifteen hundred pounds at once. But then, if Mrs. Dashwood should live fifteen years, we shall be completely taken in.”

“Fifteen years! my dear Fanny—her life cannot be worth half that purchase.”

“Certainly not; but if you observe, people always live for ever when there is an annuity to be paid them; and she is very stout and healthy, and hardly forty. An annuity is a very serious business; it comes over and over every year, and there is no getting rid of it. You are not aware of what you are doing. I have known a great deal of the trouble of annuities; for my mother was clogged with the payment of three to old superannuated servants by my father’s will, and it is amazing how disagreeable she found it. Twice every year these annuities were to be paid; and then there was the trouble of getting it to them; and then one of them was said to have died, and afterwards it turned out to be no such thing. My mother was quite sick of it. Her income was not her own, she said, with such perpetual claims on it; and it was the more unkind in my father, because otherwise the money would have been entirely at my mother’s disposal.”

Mr. Dashwood listened, and the image of money leaving his hands with the steady, unavoidable rhythm of rent days—like a kitchen dripping, drip after drip, into a bowl you must empty and fill again—grew more disagreeable the longer she spoke.

“It has given me such an abhorrence of annuities,” Fanny concluded, “that I am sure I would not pin myself down to the payment of one for all the world.”

“It is certainly an unpleasant thing,” he admitted, “to have those kind of yearly drains on one’s income. One’s fortune is not one’s own. To be tied down to the regular payment of such a sum, on every rent day, is by no means desirable: it takes away one’s independence.”

“Undoubtedly; and after all you have no thanks for it. They think themselves secure, you do no more than what is expected, and it raises no gratitude at all. If I were you, whatever I did should be done at my own discretion entirely. I would not bind myself to allow them any thing yearly. It may be very inconvenient some years to spare a hundred, or even fifty pounds from our own expenses.”

He yielded as he always did when she framed reluctance as prudence.

“I believe you are right, my love,” said he. “It will be better that there should be no annuity in the case. Whatever I may give them occasionally will be of far greater assistance than a yearly allowance; because they would only enlarge their style of living if they felt sure of a larger income, and would not be sixpence the richer at the end of the year. It will certainly be much the best way. A present of fifty pounds, now and then, will prevent their ever being distressed for money, and will, I think, be amply discharging my promise to my father.”

“To be sure it will,” said Fanny, and her tone was that of a person smoothing dough, pressing out the air until it lies obedient and flat. “Indeed, to say the truth, I am convinced within myself that your father had no idea of your giving them any money at all. The assistance he thought of was only such as might reasonably be expected of you: looking out for a comfortable small house for them, helping them to move their things, and sending them presents of fish and game, and so forth, whenever they are in season. I’ll lay my life he meant nothing farther.”

And then she spoke of incomes and interests, of sums that sounded large when spoken quickly—seven thousand pounds here, a thousand there—until they became, in her telling, a plentiful pantry for four women who, she insisted, would live “so cheap.” Their housekeeping would be “nothing at all.” No carriage, no horses, hardly any servants; they would keep no company and have no expenses of any kind. Five hundred a year among them! She could not imagine how they would spend half of it; and as to his giving them more, it was absurd—indeed, they would be much more able to give him something.

“Upon my word,” said Mr. Dashwood, his mind settling comfortably into the chair she had placed for it, “I believe you are perfectly right. My father certainly could mean nothing more by his request than what you say. I clearly understand it now, and I will strictly fulfil my engagement by such acts of assistance and kindness as you have described. When my mother removes into another house, my services shall be readily given to accommodate her as far as I can. Some little present of furniture too may be acceptable then.”

“Certainly,” returned Fanny—until she remembered another store-room to keep. “But one thing must be considered. When your father and mother moved to Norland, though the furniture of Stanhill was sold, all the china, plate, and linen was saved, and is now left to your mother. Her house will therefore be almost completely fitted up as soon as she takes it.”

“That is a material consideration,” he allowed. “A valuable legacy indeed! And yet some of the plate would have been a very pleasant addition to our own stock here.”

“Yes; and the set of breakfast china is twice as handsome as what belongs to this house,” said Fanny, with a small bitterness, as if the very glaze affronted her. “A great deal too handsome, in my opinion, for any place they can ever afford to live in. But, however, so it is. Your father thought only of them. And I must say this: that you owe no particular gratitude to him, nor attention to his wishes; for we very well know that if he could, he would have left almost everything in the world to them.”

This, delivered like a final seasoning—sharp, decisive—ended the matter. The argument was irresistible because it gave Mr. Dashwood what he most desired: the feeling of acting rightly while doing little. Whatever tenderness had been in his first intention was pared away, slice by slice, until only a thin offering remained. He finally resolved that it would be absolutely unnecessary, if not highly indecorous, to do more for the widow and daughters of his father than such neighbourly acts of assistance and kindness as his own wife had pointed out.

Mrs. Dashwood stayed at Norland through a slow-turning season, as if grief itself required a certain number of mornings before it would loosen its grip. At first, every familiar corner—the turn in the passage where her husband’s step used to sound, the window-seat that kept the late sun, the cedar walk that held last year’s needles like old letters—set her heart to a sharp, sudden pain. But time, that domestic servant who works without praise, at length restored her to the capacity of ordinary exertion; and the moment she could think of anything but sorrow, she became impatient to be gone.

Not gone far—never far. To remove at a distance from Norland felt to her like tearing up the root while the plant still lived. She wanted a dwelling in the neighbourhood, some smaller house where the days might be stitched again into usefulness. She was indefatigable: sending notes, asking questions, listening to every report of a vacancy as attentively as a cook listens for the first whisper of a simmer. Yet every place that promised comfort proved, when measured by Elinor’s steadier judgment, too dear, too large, too hungry a mouth for their reduced income. Mrs. Dashwood could be charmed by breadth of prospect and a pretty parlour; Elinor, with accounts in her mind as exact as weights on a scale, rejected what her mother would have approved.

There remained, as a comfort in their uncertainty, the remembrance of a promise. Her husband had told her—had died with the thought of it easing his last reflections—that his son had solemnly engaged to provide for them. Mrs. Dashwood doubted this assurance no more than her husband had doubted it. For her daughters’ sake, the expectation of that kindness lay in her mind like bread set aside: not to be consumed in greed, but to keep anxiety from gnawing too sharply. For herself, she was persuaded that far less than seven thousand pounds would support her in what she called affluence; she had always known how to make warmth from small means, how to find a kind of richness in daily order. And for her son’s sake too—because she wished his heart to be at ease with his own conduct—she rejoiced, and reproached herself for having once believed him incapable of generosity.

Indeed, his attentive behaviour to her and to the girls encouraged her belief for a long while. His visits were dutiful; his inquiries after their comfort sufficiently frequent; his manner, when he spoke to them, appeared to acknowledge that their welfare mattered. She relied on the liberality of his intentions with a confidence that did honour to her feelings.

It was not so with her feelings towards her daughter-in-law. The contempt Mrs. Dashwood had conceived early for Mrs. John Dashwood did not soften; it hardened, as certain airs and certain turns of speech—smooth words that closed like a fist—became more familiar through half a year’s residence under the same roof. The two ladies, differing in temper as widely as a hearth differs from a drawing-room fire made only for show, might very likely have found it impossible to live together so long, had not one particular circumstance arisen to render, in Mrs. Dashwood’s opinion, their continuing at Norland not merely tolerable but desirable.

This circumstance was a growing attachment between Elinor and the brother of Mrs. John Dashwood.

Edward Ferrars had been introduced to their acquaintance soon after his sister’s establishment at Norland; and since then he had spent the greatest part of his time there. In another mother’s mind, the connection might have been encouraged from interest—for he was the eldest son of a man who had died very rich—or repressed from prudence, since, except a trifling sum, the whole of his fortune depended on the will of his mother. But Mrs. Dashwood was alike uninfluenced by either consideration. It was enough that he appeared amiable, that he loved her daughter, and that Elinor returned the partiality. It was against every doctrine of hers that difference of fortune should keep asunder a couple drawn together by likeness of disposition; and that Elinor’s merit should not be acknowledged by everyone who knew her, was to her comprehension impossible.

Edward was not recommended by any peculiar graces of person or address. He was not handsome; and his manners required intimacy to make them pleasing. There was in him a diffidence that kept his best qualities, at first, like a well-covered dish whose fragrance must wait for the lid to lift. He was too shy to do justice to himself; but when that shyness was overcome, his behaviour gave every indication of an open, affectionate heart. His understanding was good; his education had solidly improved it; and yet he was neither fitted by abilities nor disposition to answer the wishes of his mother and sister, who longed to see him “distinguished”—as they hardly knew in what. They wanted him to make a fine figure in the world in some manner or other. His mother wished to interest him in political concerns, to get him into parliament, or to see him connected with some great men of the day. Mrs. John Dashwood wished it likewise; but in the meanwhile—until one of these superior blessings could be attained—it would have quieted her ambition to see him driving a barouche.

Edward had no turn for great men or barouches. All his wishes centred in domestic comfort and the quiet of private life: a settled table, a familiar room, voices that did not force themselves above one another. There was something in him that preferred the honest sound of a household—chairs set down carefully, a book laid open, a kettle beginning its low song—to the roar of public consequence. If he had any ambition, it was for peace.

He had been several weeks in the house before he engaged much of Mrs. Dashwood’s attention; for she was then so absorbed by affliction that she was careless of surrounding objects. She saw only that he was quiet and unobtrusive, and she liked him for it. He did not trouble the wretchedness of her mind with ill-timed conversation, nor force gaiety upon sorrow. In a world already full of hard noises—of talk that struck like a hammer, of opinions spoken to conquer—his restraint was a kind of gentleness.

She was first called to observe and approve him further by a reflection Elinor happened one day to make upon the difference between him and his sister. The contrast recommended him, in an instant, to her mother.

“It is enough,” cried Mrs. Dashwood, with an eager warmth that made the room feel suddenly brighter, “to say that he is unlike Fanny—is enough. It implies everything amiable. I love him already.”

“I think you will like him,” said Elinor, “when you know more of him.”

“Like him!” her mother replied, smiling. “I feel no sentiment of approbation inferior to love.”

“You may esteem him.”

“I have never yet known what it was to separate esteem and love.”

From that moment she took pains to get acquainted with him. Her manners were attaching, and soon banished his reserve. She questioned him, listened to him, drew him out with the same natural art by which she could make a plain dinner feel like hospitality: not by costliness, but by warmth, by attention, by letting each person at table be seen and considered. Edward, met with such frankness, began to speak more freely; and as he did, the quiet in him ceased to look like coldness and showed itself as steadiness.

She speedily comprehended all his merits. The persuasion of his regard for Elinor perhaps assisted her penetration; but she truly felt assured of his worth. Even that quietness of manner, which at first had offended her established ideas of what a young man’s address ought to be, became no longer uninteresting when she believed his heart warm and his temper affectionate. And no sooner did she perceive any symptom of love in his behaviour to Elinor—an attention lingering a moment too long, a softness in his voice when he spoke her name, the involuntary turning of his ear to her slightest movement—than she considered their serious attachment as certain, and looked forward to their marriage as rapidly approaching.

“In a few months, my dear Marianne,” said she one day, as they sat together, “Elinor will, in all probability, be settled for life. We shall miss her; but she will be happy.”

“Oh, Mama—how shall we do without her?”

“My love, it will be scarcely a separation. We shall live within a few miles of each other, and shall meet every day of our lives. You will gain a brother—a real, affectionate brother. I have the highest opinion in the world of Edward’s heart. But you look grave, Marianne; do you disapprove your sister’s choice?”

“Perhaps,” said Marianne, “I may consider it with some surprise. Edward is very amiable, and I love him tenderly. But yet—he is not the kind of young man—there is something wanting. His figure is not striking; it has none of that grace which I should expect in the man who could seriously attach my sister. His eyes want all that spirit, that fire, which at once announce virtue and intelligence. And besides all this, I am afraid, Mama, he has no real taste. Music seems scarcely to attract him; and though he admires Elinor’s drawings very much, it is not the admiration of a person who can understand their worth. It is evident, in spite of his frequent attention to her while she draws, that in fact he knows nothing of the matter. He admires as a lover, not as a connoisseur. To satisfy me, those characters must be united. I could not be happy with a man whose taste did not in every point coincide with my own. He must enter into all my feelings; the same books, the same music must charm us both. Oh! Mama, how spiritless—how tame—was Edward’s manner in reading to us last night! I felt for my sister most severely; yet she bore it with so much composure, she seemed scarcely to notice it. I could hardly keep my seat. To hear those beautiful lines, which have frequently almost driven me wild, pronounced with such impenetrable calmness—such dreadful indifference!”

“He would certainly have done more justice to simple and elegant prose,” said Mrs. Dashwood. “I thought so at the time; but you would give him Cowper.”

“Nay, Mama—if he is not to be animated by Cowper!—But we must allow for difference of taste. Elinor has not my feelings, and therefore she may overlook it and be happy with him. But it would have broke my heart, had I loved him, to hear him read with so little sensibility. Mama, the more I know of the world, the more am I convinced that I shall never see a man whom I can really love. I require so much! He must have all Edward’s virtues, and his person and manners must ornament his goodness with every possible charm.”

“Remember, my love, that you are not seventeen,” replied her mother, with a tenderness that softened even her contradiction. “It is yet too early in life to despair of such a happiness. Why should you be less fortunate than your mother? In one circumstance only, my Marianne, may your destiny be different from hers!”

“It is such a pity, Elinor,” Marianne said, as they sat with the after-supper quiet settling over Norland like a clean cloth smoothed upon a table, “that Edward should have no taste for drawing.”

Elinor, who had been folding a square of linen with the patient exactness that turns household work into a sort of thinking, looked up at once. “No taste for drawing? Why should you suppose it? He does not draw himself, it is true; but he takes real pleasure in watching other people’s hands do what his own have never been taught. That is not indifference. It is only want of practice. Had he been in the way of learning, I believe he would have done it well. His only fault is a distrust of himself—he will hardly offer an opinion on a picture because he fears to say too much, or the wrong thing. But there is a natural propriety in him, a simplicity of liking and disliking, that generally guides him right.”

Marianne, who never handled a pencil without expecting a flame of feeling in return, held her peace for fear of hurting her sister; but the measured approval Elinor described seemed, to her, as thin as broth made only with water—nourishing perhaps, yet without that rich perfume which alone, in Marianne’s mind, deserved the name of taste. Still, she loved Elinor the more for that very blindness which warmed her judgment of Edward; it was an affection that, like an old family recipe, altered the flavour of everything it touched.

“I hope, Marianne,” Elinor continued, and her voice had that careful gentleness which always tried to keep peace in a room, “you do not think him deficient in general taste. Indeed I am sure you cannot; for you are perfectly cordial to him—and if you truly held him in contempt, you could never be civil.”

Marianne coloured, and for a moment listened not only to Elinor but to the house itself: the low stir of embers in the grate, the distant closing of a door, the soft, living hush that belonged to a country evening when no wheels or iron clatter invaded it. In that calm, she could not bear to make a harsh sound with words.

“Do not be offended, Elinor,” she said at last, choosing her phrases as if choosing herbs—knowing one leaf too much could spoil a whole dish. “My praise cannot in every thing equal your sense of his merits. I have not had so many opportunities of understanding the minuter propensities of his mind—his inclinations and tastes—as you have. But of his goodness and his sense I have the highest opinion in the world. I think him every thing that is worthy and amiable.”

“I am sure,” Elinor replied, smiling with honest relief, “that his dearest friends could not be dissatisfied with such commendation. I do not see how you could express yourself more warmly.”

Marianne was glad to have pleased her. Elinor, once set upon Edward, went on as if she must finish the account, as a careful cook finishes her directions so that nothing may be misdone.

“Of his sense and goodness no one can doubt,” she said, “who has seen him often enough to draw him into unreserved conversation. His understanding and his principles are excellent; they are only concealed by that shyness which too often keeps him silent. You know enough of him to do justice to his solid worth. But those minuter propensities—as you call them—you have been kept from knowing so well as I have. He and I have, at times, been thrown a good deal together, while you have been wholly engrossed—on the most affectionate principle—by my mother. I have seen a great deal of him; I have studied his sentiments; I have heard his opinions on literature and taste. Upon the whole, I venture to say his mind is well informed; his enjoyment of books exceedingly great; his imagination lively; his observation just and correct; his taste delicate and pure. His abilities improve upon acquaintance as much as his manners and his person. At first sight his address is not striking, and his person can hardly be called handsome—till one perceives the expression of his eyes, which are uncommonly good, and the general sweetness of his countenance. At present I know him so well that I think him really handsome—or at least almost so. What say you, Marianne?”

Marianne’s eyes softened at the tenderness in her sister’s last question. “I shall very soon think him handsome, Elinor, if I do not now. When you tell me to love him as a brother, I shall no more see imperfection in his face than I now do in his heart.”

Elinor started, as if a sudden loud note had broken the room’s quiet harmony, and she regretted, instantly, the warmth into which she had allowed herself to fall. Edward stood very high in her opinion—too high, perhaps, to be safely talked of in a house where Marianne and their mother had that eager manner of believing, in which wishing becomes hoping, and hoping becomes certainty. Elinor required more than conjecture; she required knowledge, the sober kind that could bear being named aloud.

“I do not attempt to deny,” she said, recovering her composure, “that I think very highly of him—that I greatly esteem him—that I like him.”

Marianne’s whole spirit rose at the chill of the words. “Esteem him! Like him!” she cried. “Cold-hearted Elinor! Oh! worse than cold-hearted—ashamed of being otherwise. Use those words again, and I will leave the room this moment.”

Elinor could not help laughing; it came from her, not as mockery, but as steam rises when something too hot meets something too cold. “Excuse me,” she said, “and be assured I meant you no offence by speaking so quietly of my own feelings. Believe them stronger than I have declared; believe them, in short, such as his merit—and the suspicion, the hope, of his affection for me—may warrant without imprudence or folly. But farther than this you must not believe. I am by no means assured of his regard for me. There are moments when the extent of it seems doubtful; and until his sentiments are fully known, you cannot wonder at my wishing to avoid encouraging my own partiality by believing it—or calling it—more than it is. In my heart I feel little—scarcely any—doubt of his preference. But there are other points to be considered besides his inclination. He is very far from being independent. What his mother really is we cannot know; but from Fanny’s occasional mention of her conduct and opinions, we have never been disposed to think her amiable; and I should be much mistaken if Edward is not himself aware that there would be many difficulties in his way, if he wished to marry a woman who had not either a great fortune or high rank.”

It was as if Elinor had set before Marianne, not a romantic feast, but the plain bread of reality—good for sustaining, though it sat heavy at first. Marianne stared at her, astonished at how far she and her mother had let imagination run ahead, like a melody sung too fast.

“And you really are not engaged to him!” she exclaimed. “Yet it certainly soon will happen. But two advantages will proceed from this delay. I shall not lose you so soon; and Edward will have greater opportunity of improving that natural taste for your favourite pursuit which must be so indispensably necessary to your future felicity. Oh! if he should be so far stimulated by your genius as to learn to draw himself, how delightful it would be!”

Elinor had spoken her real mind, and now she felt the sting of it. She could not consider her partiality in so prosperous a state as Marianne imagined. There was, at times, a want of spirits about Edward which, if it did not denote indifference, spoke of something almost as unpromising. A doubt of her regard—supposing him to feel it—might give him uneasiness, but would hardly produce that dejection which so often attended him. A more reasonable cause lay in his dependent situation: a life lived under another’s will, as a household lives under a harsh and constant noise, never allowed the full comfort of its own quiet. His mother neither behaved to him in a way that made his home comfortable now, nor gave him any assurance that he might ever form a home for himself without submitting strictly to her views for his aggrandizement. With such knowledge Elinor could not be easy. She was far from depending, as her mother and Marianne did, on the certain result of his preference. Indeed, the longer they were together, the more doubtful seemed the nature of his regard; and sometimes—only for a few painful minutes—she believed it no more than friendship.

Whatever its limits, it was enough, once perceived by his sister, to make that sister uneasy—and, still more commonly, uncivil. Fanny watched affection as if it were a theft to be detected: the moment she suspected an attachment, she began to speak of it in the language of danger and capture, as though a young woman could “draw in” a man the way a market huckster draws in customers with bright talk and false sweetness.

She took the first opportunity of affronting Mrs. Dashwood. Her manner was all fine edge and polished hardness; and she talked, with expressive meaning, of her brother’s great expectations, of Mrs. Ferrars’s resolution that both her sons should marry well, and of the peril attending any young woman who attempted to draw him in. The words themselves were genteel; the tone was a blade.

Mrs. Dashwood could neither pretend not to understand, nor endeavour to be calm. Her answer marked her contempt; and she left the room immediately, her mind made up in an instant—that whatever inconvenience or expense might attend so sudden a removal, her beloved Elinor should not be exposed another week to such insinuations, not another day forced to live in an atmosphere where every sound had a sting in it.

In this fever of spirit, a letter was brought to her by the post—one of those small, ordinary packets that, in a household, can change the whole flavour of life as suddenly as a handful of salt dropped into a pot. It contained a proposal, and it arrived as if Providence itself had timed the delivery.

The offer was of a small house, on very easy terms, belonging to a relation of her own—Sir John Middleton, a gentleman of consequence and property in Devonshire. The letter came from Sir John himself, written with a frank friendliness that seemed to open the windows of the room and let in clean air. He understood she was in need of a dwelling; and though the place he offered was merely a cottage, he assured her that every thing should be done to it which she might think necessary, if only the situation pleased her. He pressed her, after giving particulars of the house and garden, to come with her daughters to Barton Park, his own residence, from which she might judge whether Barton Cottage—for the houses were in the same parish—could, by any alteration, be made comfortable to her.

The style of the letter, so sincerely accommodating, could not fail to give pleasure, especially at a moment when she suffered under the cold, unfeeling behaviour of nearer connections. She needed no time for deliberation. Her resolution formed as she read; and what, a few hours before, would have seemed the greatest objection—the distance, the very remoteness of Devonshire from Sussex—now became its first recommendation. To quit the neighbourhood of Norland was no longer an evil; it was an object of desire. It was a blessing compared with the misery of continuing her daughter-in-law’s guest; and to remove for ever from that beloved place would be less painful than to inhabit it, or even visit it, while such a woman was mistress there.

She wrote instantly to Sir John with thanks and acceptance, the pen moving quickly, as if the very scratch upon the paper could drown out the remembered sharpness of Fanny’s voice. Then she hastened to show both letters to her daughters, that she might be secure of their approbation before her answer was sent.

Elinor had always thought it more prudent for them to settle at some distance from Norland than immediately among their present acquaintance; on that head, therefore, she could not oppose her mother’s plan of removing into Devonshire. The house, too, as Sir John described it, was on so simple a scale, and the rent so uncommonly moderate, as to leave her no right of objection. And though the scheme brought no charm to her fancy—though it carried them farther from Norland than she wished—she made no attempt to dissuade her mother from sending the letter of acquiescence.

Mrs. Dashwood had scarcely folded her answer—had scarcely let the ink dry in her mind—before she yielded to the pleasure of telling her son-in-law and his wife that a house was secured, and that she would trouble them no longer than the short time needed to make it fit for their living in.

The announcement fell among them like a sudden change of weather. Mrs. John Dashwood, whose civility was ever a carefully measured portion, said nothing at all. Mr. John Dashwood, with a propriety that came easily when it cost him nothing, hoped his mother would not settle too far from Norland.

Mrs. Dashwood tasted the triumph of her reply as one tastes a well-kept preserve—sweet, bright, and a little sharp.

“Devonshire,” she said.

At the name, Edward, who had been quiet, turned on the instant. Surprise and concern were both in his voice, and there was no need for Mrs. Dashwood to ask what lay beneath them.

“Devonshire! Are you, indeed, going there? So far from hence! And to what part of it?”

She told him. It was within four miles northward of Exeter.

“It is but a cottage,” she added, with that airy courage which made a small thing seem spacious, “but I hope to see many of my friends in it. A room or two can easily be added; and if my friends find no difficulty in travelling so far to see me, I am sure I shall find none in accommodating them.”

The words were hospitality; the intention was also war. She ended with a kind invitation to Mr. and Mrs. John Dashwood to visit her at Barton, and to Edward she gave one with a warmth that, like the fragrance of bread in an oven, spreads itself whether one will or no.

Her late conversation with her daughter-in-law had fixed her resolution not to remain at Norland a day longer than necessity required; but it had not produced the effect which that conversation had chiefly aimed at. To separate Edward and Elinor was not her object; it was as far from it as ever. On the contrary, she chose this moment, by so pointed an invitation to her brother, to let Mrs. John Dashwood understand—without a single open quarrel—how little she regarded her disapprobation of the match, and how much she would insist on affection being spoken in her own household dialect, not translated into Fanny’s colder terms.

Mr. John Dashwood began at once to repeat, with great earnestness and with a conscientious vexation that almost deserved credit, how exceedingly sorry he was that she had taken a house at so great a distance as to prevent his being of any service to her in removing her furniture. For the one exertion to which he had bounded the performance of his promise to his father was, by this arrangement, rendered impossible; and he felt himself aggrieved by the impossibility, as men often feel most deeply injured by the loss of a virtue they never meant to practise.

The furniture, in truth, was all sent round by water—packed, corded, sealed: household linen folded into pale, clean strata; plate wrapped and muffled against the knocking of the waves; china nested like careful eggs; books stacked into heavy, mute bricks of memory; and Marianne’s handsome pianoforte, swathed and crated as if it were some great joint to be preserved for a feast at the other end of England. The yard and passage were full of the sounds of departure—the rasp of rope through a sailor’s hand, the dull thud of a box set down, the quick calls of men to one another that made the very air feel busier than it ought to be in a house that had lately known mourning.

Mrs. John Dashwood watched the packages go with a sigh. It was not grief; it was that sort of complaint which rises when one sees a good thing leave one’s reach. She could not help thinking it hard that, as Mrs. Dashwood’s income was to be so trifling compared with their own, she should yet carry away any handsome article of furniture. The clink of plate and the imagined sheen of polished wood seemed to offend her sense of proportion, as if elegance were a language reserved for the rich—and the poor ought to speak in plainer syllables.

Mrs. Dashwood took the house for a twelvemonth. It was ready furnished, and she might have immediate possession. No difficulty arose on either side in the agreement; and she waited only for the disposal of her effects at Norland, and to determine her future household, before she set off for the west. And because she was exceedingly rapid in the performance of everything that interested her, this, too, was soon done.

The horses left her by her husband had been sold soon after his death; and now an opportunity offered for disposing of her carriage. For her own pleasure—yes, and for the comfort of her children—she would willingly have kept it; there is a particular ease in being carried, sheltered from weather, the wheels making their steady music on the road. But Elinor, who heard always the longer consequence behind the short desire, advised its sale, and her discretion prevailed.

It was Elinor’s wisdom, likewise, that limited their servants to three—two maids and a man—chosen quickly from among those who had formed their establishment at Norland. The man and one of the maids were sent off immediately into Devonshire to prepare the cottage for their mistress’s arrival; for, as Lady Middleton was entirely unknown to Mrs. Dashwood, she preferred going directly to the house meant to be her home rather than arriving first as a visitor at Barton Park. She relied so undoubtingly on Sir John’s description of the cottage as to feel no curiosity to examine it herself until she entered it as her own—trusting, as some women do, to another’s cheerful report in place of her own cautious inspection, because her imagination was already seasoning the bare rooms with future warmth.

Her eagerness to be gone from Norland was not diminished by anything she saw around her. Indeed, it was preserved—fed, even—by the evident satisfaction of her daughter-in-law at the prospect of her removal; a satisfaction but feebly covered by a cold invitation to defer her departure, offered as one offers a dish one hopes will not be accepted.

Now, if ever, was the time when her son-in-law’s promise to his father might have been fulfilled with propriety. Since he had neglected to do it on first coming to the estate, their quitting his house might naturally have been considered the most suitable period for its accomplishment. But Mrs. Dashwood soon gave over every hope of the kind. From the general drift of his discourse, she became convinced that his assistance extended no farther than their maintenance for six months at Norland. He spoke so often of the increasing expenses of housekeeping, and of the perpetual demands upon his purse—demands, he said, to which a man of any consequence in the world was exposed beyond calculation—that he seemed rather to stand in need of more money himself than to have any intention of giving money away. His talk had its own tiresome rhythm, like a clock one cannot stop: cost, cost, cost—each word striking the ear, dulling the heart.

In a very few weeks from the day which brought Sir John Middleton’s first letter to Norland, everything was so far settled in their future abode as to enable Mrs. Dashwood and her daughters to begin their journey. Many were the tears shed in their last adieus to a place so much beloved. Even the house itself seemed to listen—to hold its breath between the familiar, gentle sounds of their former life and the new hush that would follow. The footfalls in corridors, the soft opening and shutting of drawers, the rustle of gowns as rooms were searched one final time: all of it made a kind of domestic music, and then, as each task ended, another note dropped away.

“Dear, dear Norland!” cried Marianne, wandering alone before the house on the last evening of their being there. Her voice trembled as if the air itself were cold against her throat. “When shall I cease to regret you!—When learn to feel a home elsewhere!—Oh! happy house, could you know what I suffer in now viewing you from this spot, from whence perhaps I may view you no more!—And you, ye well-known trees!—but you will continue the same.—No leaf will decay because we are removed, nor any branch become motionless although we can observe you no longer!—No; you will continue the same; unconscious of the pleasure or the regret you occasion, and insensible of any change in those who walk under your shade!—But who will remain to enjoy you?”

Their first miles away from Norland went by in a dull, aching silence that no one could quite break without seeming to betray the others. The wheels worried the road with a steady, wearisome rasp; the harness rang now and then with a metallic complaint; and whenever they passed a cart on the lane, its iron-bound rumble seemed an affront—too loud for grief, too indifferent to it. Yet even that sound, harsh as it was, marked the world continuing in its accustomed measure, and by degrees the cadence of travel—hoofbeats, creaking leather, the soft hiss of wind through hedgerow—began to draw their thoughts outward.

It was early September, when the air holds a last generosity: warm enough to carry scent, cool enough to sharpen it. As the carriage turned toward Barton Valley, the land opened into a pleasant fertility—pasture stitched with hedges, trees gathered in thoughtful clumps, and a fullness in the fields that promised winter stores. The valley had its own voice. Beyond the road’s small complaints, there were birds calling from the wooded rises, the susurration of grasses on the downs, and, nearer the cottages, the domestic music of rural life: a distant pail struck against stone, a gate lifted and dropped, a dog’s single bark answered by another farther off. The quiet was not emptiness, but a kind of care—an acoustic shelter after the louder, more anxious world they had left.

After winding along the green for more than a mile, they came to their own house.

Barton Cottage did not pretend to be what its name, in the mouths of poets, might promise. A small green court lay in front like a modest apron, and a neat wicket admitted them without ceremony. The building itself was regular; the roof was tiled; the walls were honest, not dressed with climbing honeysuckle; the shutters, disobedient to the fashionable idea of rural prettiness, had not even the mercy of green paint. It was comfortable, compact—serviceable in every line, but lacking the deliberate picturesque. A narrow passage ran straight through, so that from the first step inside one could already see the garden beyond, as if the house insisted on being passed through rather than admired.

On either side of the entrance were two sitting rooms, each about sixteen feet square; beyond them, the offices and stairs; above, four bedrooms and two garrets. It had not been built many years and stood in good repair. Compared with Norland, it was poor and small indeed; and recollection struck so quickly, so faithfully, that tears came as they crossed the threshold.

But sorrow cannot keep its face when it is met at once by other people’s joy. The servants, delighted at their arrival, moved about them with an eagerness that was almost festive. There was the quick thump of a trunk set down, the brisk opening and shutting of doors, voices calling to one another in the passage; and in that familiar bustle—so like the first day of any household settling—each Dashwood, for the sake of the others, found the resolve to appear contented. Tears were wiped away as one wipes a hand after kitchen work: not because the hand is clean, but because there is something else that must be done.

The fine weather did them an unspoken service. Because they first saw Barton under sun, the place offered itself kindly; and kindness received at the outset is remembered longer than it deserves. Behind the house high hills rose immediately, and not far on either side—some open downs, others cultivated and wooded. The village of Barton sat chiefly upon one of these hills, a scatter of roofs and pale walls that made a pleasant view from the windows. In front, the prospect widened: it commanded the whole valley and reached into the country beyond. The enclosing hills terminated the valley in that direction, but under another name and in another course it branched out again between two of the steepest of them, like a story that refuses to end where you thought it would.

Mrs. Dashwood, who had always loved a house the way some women love a recipe—less for what it is than for what it may become by additions, alterations, and a little cheerful daring—was, upon the whole, well satisfied with its size and furniture. Her former style of living made many improvements necessary, but to improve was a delight to her; and she had, at that moment, ready money enough to supply what was wanted of greater elegance.

“As for the house itself, to be sure,” said she, as they went from room to room, measuring with her eyes what might be done, “it is too small for our family; but we will make ourselves tolerably comfortable for the present, as it is too late in the year for improvements. Perhaps in the spring, if I have plenty of money, as I dare say I shall, we may think about building. These parlors are both too small for such parties of our friends as I hope to see often collected here; and I have some thoughts of throwing the passage into one of them, with perhaps a part of the other, and so leave the remainder of that other for an entrance; this, with a new drawing-room which may be easily added, and a bedchamber and garret above, will make it a very snug little cottage. I could wish the stairs were handsome. But one must not expect every thing; though I suppose it would be no difficult matter to widen them. I shall see how much I am before-hand with the world in the spring, and we will plan our improvements accordingly.”

There was, in such projects, the energy of a cook planning a feast before she has counted the flour. It was affectionate, it was hopeful, and it was—considering an income of five hundred a year and a woman who had never saved in her life—nearly heroic. Yet until those alterations could be made from the supposed savings of that income, they were wise enough to be content with the house as it stood; and all three were busy with the small, intimate labours by which a strange roof becomes one’s own.

If the cottage had lacked outward romance, the inside soon acquired the comforts of habit. Marianne’s pianoforte was unpacked and properly placed, so that the room had at once a promise of music; Elinor’s drawings were affixed to the walls of their sitting room, lending the bare spaces an air of remembered scenes and patient observation. There were books to lay within reach, work-baskets to establish near the light, linen to be counted and stowed. Even the kitchen—though not described in plans as Mrs. Dashwood described parlours—began to speak in its own familiar way: the clink of a pot-lid, the rush of water into a basin, the first scents of a simple breakfast clearing into the sweeter, green breath of the garden beyond. In every house, the true settling is not in the arrangement of furniture but in the return of ordinary appetites.

They were engaged in these occupations when, soon after breakfast the next day, their landlord entered to welcome them to Barton and offer them every accommodation from his own house and garden in which theirs might be deficient.

Sir John Middleton was a good-looking man of about forty. He had formerly visited at Stanhill, but too long ago for his young cousins to remember him. His countenance was thoroughly good-humoured; his manners were as friendly as the style of his letter. Their arrival seemed to afford him real satisfaction, and their comfort to be an object of real solicitude. He spoke much of his earnest desire that they should live with his family on the most sociable terms, and he pressed them so cordially to dine at Barton Park every day till they were better settled at home, that though his entreaties were carried to a point of perseverance beyond civility, they could not be offended. He was a man whose kindness made its own noise—cheerful, insistent, filling whatever silence might otherwise have lingered in the rooms.

Nor was his generosity content to remain in talk. Within an hour after he left them, a large basket arrived from the park, full of garden stuff and fruit—things still cool with earth and morning shade: crisp leaves that released their sharp, green bitterness when handled; beans snapped cleanly; carrots carrying the damp sweetness of their beds; apples and pears with skins that held the orchard’s sun. Before the end of the day, a present of game followed—feathers, rich scent, the unmistakable promise of suppers that might, for an hour, make them forget to be unhappy. Sir John insisted, moreover, on conveying all their letters to and from the post, and would not be denied the satisfaction of sending them his newspaper every day. If modern life, in other places, grows noisy with machines and hurried commerce, here was an older exchange: food carried by hand, news passed along by neighbourly insistence, and generosity expressed not in grand declarations but in what could be cooked, read, and shared.

Lady Middleton, he told them, had sent a very civil message, intending to wait on Mrs. Dashwood as soon as she could be assured that her visit would be no inconvenience. Mrs. Dashwood answered with an invitation equally polite; and the next day her ladyship was introduced to them.

They were, of course, anxious to see the person on whom so much of their comfort at Barton must depend; and the elegance of Lady Middleton’s appearance encouraged that wish. She was not more than six or seven and twenty; her face was handsome, her figure tall and striking, her address graceful. Her manners had all the elegance which her husband’s wanted. Yet they would have been improved by some share of his frankness and warmth. Her visit lasted long enough to lessen the first admiration by showing that, though perfectly well-bred, she was reserved, cold, and had nothing to say beyond the most common-place inquiry or remark.

Conversation, however, was not greatly wanted; Sir John was very chatty, and Lady Middleton had taken the wise precaution of bringing with her their eldest child, a fine little boy of about six years old. By that means there was always one subject to return to in moments of extremity. The ladies had only to enquire his name and age, admire his beauty, and ask him questions which his mother answered for him, while he hung about her and held down his head. Lady Middleton expressed surprise at his shyness before company, since he could make noise enough at home—an observation delivered with the same composed tone as if she were remarking upon weather, though it conjured at once the sounds of another household: running feet, shrill demands, laughter and cries echoing in corridors. It seemed, in that instant, that the child belonged less to the room than to the invisible soundscape of his own nursery, and that, like any small creature taken from its accustomed grove, he had become silent by mere change of air.

On every formal visit, a child ought to be of the party, by way of provision for discourse. In the present case, it took up ten minutes to determine whether the boy was most like his father or mother, and in what particular he resembled either; for, of course, every body differed, and every body was astonished at the opinion of the others. The debate, conducted with the mild tyranny of drawing-room politeness, was one of those social languages that everyone speaks and no one truly means—phrases that travel easily because they carry so little of the speaker’s private world. Whatever Marianne felt, whatever Elinor observed, whatever Mrs. Dashwood hoped, had no ready dialect in which to be safely offered to a new acquaintance; but a child’s resemblance could be discussed without risk, as if it were a dish everyone might taste and pronounce upon.

An opportunity was soon to be given them of debating the rest of the children; for Sir John would not leave the house without securing their promise of dining at the park the next day.

Barton Park lay scarcely half a mile from the cottage; the ladies had passed near it on their first walks along the valley, yet the house kept itself coyly out of their daily sight, shouldered away by a green projection of hill, as if the land itself chose when to reveal its grandeurs. In the morning, when the dew still held fast to the grass and the brook spoke in thin, bright syllables over stones, the Park seemed less a neighbour than an older chapter of the same place—heard in the distant clatter of hooves and the faint, organized barking of dogs, smelled in the wind when it turned and carried to Barton Cottage a ghost of smoke from well-seasoned logs and something richer, buttery and browned, as though the kitchens there began their work early and never quite ceased.

The house, when at last approached, proved large and handsome, its windows catching the last of the day like polished pewter. The Middletons lived in a style of equal hospitality and elegance: the first for Sir John’s gratification, the second for that of his lady. They were scarcely ever without friends staying in the house, and they kept more company of every kind than any other family in the neighbourhood. It was necessary to the happiness of both; for however dissimilar in temper and outward behaviour, they strongly resembled each other in that total want of talent and taste which confined their employments—when unconnected with the bustle of society—within a very narrow compass.

Sir John was a sportsman; Lady Middleton a mother. He hunted and shot, and she humoured her children; and these were their only resources. There are households where the calendar is measured by sowing and reaping, by the apple’s first blush and the hog’s killing; but at Barton Park the seasons were counted by engagements—by who dined, who stayed, who danced, who came to be amused and who was obliged to amuse. Lady Middleton had the advantage of being able to spoil her children all the year round, while Sir John’s independent employments were in existence only half the time. Continual engagements at home and abroad, however, supplied all deficiencies of nature and education; supported the good spirits of Sir John, and gave exercise to the good breeding of his wife.

Lady Middleton piqued herself upon the elegance of her table and of all her domestic arrangements; and from this kind of vanity drew her greatest enjoyment in any party. It was not, Marianne soon perceived, the pleasure of hunger honestly satisfied, nor the old comfort of food that carries a family’s story—no talk of what the dairy yielded, whether the cream had been too long in the pan, whether the herbs in the garden were sweet with rain. It was the smoothness of napery, the exact placing of plate, the polished obedience of servants moving as silently as shadows. Sir John’s satisfaction, on the other hand, was far more real. He delighted in collecting about him more young people than his house would hold, and the noisier they were the better he was pleased; as if the worth of an evening could be weighed in laughter and the clack of voices, regardless of whether those sounds harmonized with any peace the place might otherwise have possessed. To Elinor, whose mind attended as readily to temper as to tone, there was something almost violent in that preference; the Park had its own quiet languages—wind in leaf, fire in grate, a distant owl—and Sir John would gladly drown them beneath stamping feet and shouted witticisms.

Yet, for the juvenile part of the neighbourhood, he was a blessing. In summer he was for ever forming parties to eat cold ham and chicken out of doors—great joints carved in haste, the fat turned firm and translucent by cool air, slices laid upon bread that had been cut too thick in the excitement; and in winter his private balls were numerous enough for any young lady who was not suffering under the insatiable appetite of fifteen. The arrival of a new family in the country was always a matter of joy to him, and in every point of view he was charmed with the inhabitants he had now procured for his cottage at Barton. The Miss Dashwoods were young, pretty, and unaffected. It was enough to secure his good opinion; for, in Sir John’s creed, to be unaffected was all that a pretty girl could want to make her mind as captivating as her person.

The friendliness of his disposition made him happy in accommodating those whose situation might be considered, in comparison with the past, unfortunate. In showing kindness to his cousins, therefore, he had the real satisfaction of a good heart; and in settling a family of females only in his cottage, he had all the satisfaction of a sportsman—for a sportsman, though he esteems only those of his sex who are sportsmen likewise, is not often desirous of encouraging their taste by admitting them to a residence within his own manor.

Mrs. Dashwood and her daughters were met at the door by Sir John himself, who welcomed them to Barton Park with unaffected sincerity; and, as he attended them through passages that smelled faintly of beeswax and fire, repeated to the young ladies the concern which the same subject had drawn from him the day before—his mortification at being unable to get any smart young men to meet them.

“You will see,” he said, as if he had personally failed in the provisioning of the table, “only one gentleman here besides myself; a particular friend staying at the park, but neither very young nor very gay. You must excuse the smallness of the party; I assure you it shall never happen so again. I have been to several families this morning in hopes of procuring some addition; but it is moonlight and everybody is full of engagements. Luckily, however, my Lady Middleton’s mother arrived within the last hour; and she is a very cheerful, agreeable woman—so I hope you will not find it as dull as you may imagine.”

The young ladies, as well as their mother, were perfectly satisfied with having two entire strangers of the party, and wished for no more. Indeed, Mrs. Dashwood, who could take warmth from kindness as readily as from sunshine, was disposed to be pleased with everything that bespoke welcome.

Mrs. Jennings, Lady Middleton’s mother, answered Sir John’s description in all particulars, and exceeded it in volume. She was good-humoured, merry, fat, elderly; she talked a great deal, seemed very happy, and was rather vulgar. Her laughter rolled before her like a cart over loose stones, and her voice, rich and ungoverned, filled rooms that had been made for softer sounds. If the Park had possessed at certain hours a hush that might have let a person hear the settling of embers or the turning of a page, Mrs. Jennings shattered it without malice, as a cheerful hand smacks flour from a board. Before dinner was over she had said many witty things on the subject of lovers and husbands; hoped the Miss Dashwoods had not left their hearts behind them in Sussex; and pretended to see them blush whether they did or not.

Marianne, whose sensibility quickened at once to any perceived slight, was vexed for her sister’s sake. She turned her eyes towards Elinor to see how she bore these attacks, with an earnestness which gave Elinor far more pain than could arise from such common-place raillery as Mrs. Jennings’s. For Elinor could endure a jest; it was Marianne’s anxious watchfulness—so public, so tender, so incapable of disguise—that made every foolish word feel heavier. The untranslatable part of experience, Elinor thought, is not always grief; sometimes it is composure. She could not make Marianne understand, in any dialect of feeling, that an unguarded laugh need not draw blood.

Colonel Brandon, the particular friend of Sir John, seemed no more adapted by resemblance of manner to be his friend than Lady Middleton was to be his wife, or Mrs. Jennings to be Lady Middleton’s mother. He was silent and grave. His appearance, however, was not unpleasing, in spite of his being, in the opinion of Marianne and Margaret, an absolute old bachelor—for he was on the wrong side of five-and-thirty. Though his face was not handsome, his countenance was sensible, and his address particularly gentlemanlike. In the pauses of conversation—those small clearings where a quieter spirit might have found a place—he seemed to listen, not merely to words, but to the room itself: to the scrape of a chair against the floor, the snap of a coal as it split, the faint rush of wind at a window. It gave him, to Elinor’s eye, a steadiness that the others lacked, as if he had learned the value of sound not only for entertainment, but for peace.

There was nothing in any of the party which could recommend them as companions to the Dashwoods; but the cold insipidity of Lady Middleton was so particularly repulsive that, in comparison, the gravity of Colonel Brandon, and even the boisterous mirth of Sir John and his mother-in-law, was interesting. Lady Middleton seemed to be roused to enjoyment only by the entrance of her four noisy children after dinner, who pulled her about, tore her clothes, and put an end to every kind of discourse except what related to themselves. Their shrieks—sharp as the squeal of a kettle left too long upon the boil—rang through the rooms. One child demanded sweetmeats with a cry; another thumped at a chair leg as if testing its endurance; a third made a drum of a book. Lady Middleton, far from checking them, surrendered her attention to them with the same neat, automatic patience she bestowed on the arrangement of her table. To Marianne, who heard in their disorder the ruin of every finer sound, the clamour was like a crude modern intrusion upon a landscape that ought to have been pastoral: as though the Park, with its older trees and older manners, were being conquered by a little army of senseless noise.

In the evening, as Marianne was discovered to be musical, she was invited to play. The instrument was unlocked; everybody prepared to be charmed. Marianne, who sang very well, at their request went through the chief of the songs which Lady Middleton had brought into the family on her marriage, and which perhaps had lain ever since in the same position on the pianoforte; for her ladyship had celebrated that event by giving up music—although by her mother’s account she had played extremely well, and by her own was very fond of it.

The first touch of Marianne’s fingers summoned a different air, as if the room—overcrowded with talk, with children’s outcries, with Sir John’s hearty interruptions—had at last remembered how to breathe. Her voice rose clear and warm, like wine poured into a glass that has waited too long empty; it carried the sweetness of ripe fruit and the faint bitterness of peel. Even those who could not have distinguished a well-made melody from a street tune felt the charm by mere contagion. Sir John was loud in his admiration at the end of every song, and as loud in his conversation with the others while every song lasted. He praised, he laughed, he exclaimed; and his praises, like his dinners, were abundant, if not nicely considered. Lady Middleton frequently called him to order, wondered how any one’s attention could be diverted from music for a moment, and asked Marianne to sing a particular song which Marianne had just finished—her requests repeating themselves with the same unconsciousness as her children’s demands.

Colonel Brandon alone, of all the party, heard Marianne without being in raptures. He paid her only the compliment of attention—steady, unbroken, as if he would not waste a single note. Marianne felt a respect for him on the occasion which the others had reasonably forfeited by their shameless want of taste. His pleasure in music, though it amounted not to that ecstatic delight which alone could sympathize with her own, was estimable when contrasted against the horrible insensibility of the rest. She was reasonable enough, too, to allow that a man of five-and-thirty might well have outlived all acuteness of feeling and every exquisite power of enjoyment. And with that charitable severity—so easy to bestow when one is young, so hard to accept when one is no longer—she was perfectly disposed to make every allowance for the Colonel’s advanced state of life which humanity required.

Mrs. Jennings came to Barton like a gust that knew every chimney by its voice. Widowed, comfortably jointured, and long since done with the serious labor of bringing up her own two daughters into respectable marriages, she had turned the whole remainder of her days into a sort of bustling domestic ministry: to wed the unwedded, to pair off the unattached, to salt every quiet visit with insinuation, to shake a house by laughter until the very teacups seemed to clink in complicity.

She had a talent for hearing what other people missed. Where another woman heard only a song well sung, Mrs. Jennings heard an attachment forming, as plainly as one hears the first cautious crackle of kindling before the flame is visible. The very first evening that Colonel Brandon sat and listened to Marianne—listened, not merely with politeness, but with that deep, still attention that makes a room feel suddenly hushed—Mrs. Jennings’ mind was made up. And when, in due time, the Middletons returned the visit by dining at the cottage, and Marianne sang again, and the Colonel listened again, as if each note were a thing he could not afford to waste, then Mrs. Jennings pronounced it, decisively, joyfully, as if it were already entered in the parish register.

It must be so. Colonel Brandon was in love with Marianne Dashwood.

To her, the propriety of the match lay in plain ingredients: he was rich; she was handsome.
