Mrs. Jennings had always wanted Colonel Brandon well married—ever since Sir John’s connection had first brought him into her orbit—and she wanted a good husband for every pretty girl, as naturally as she wanted good butter with fresh bread. Besides, the immediate advantage to herself was delicious: it supplied her with inexhaustible jesting. At the park she would rally the Colonel; at the cottage she would tease Marianne; and the sport of it kept her cheerful, as if the day had been seasoned to her palate.

To the Colonel, her raillery—so far as it touched himself alone—appeared, at least, to glide off without leaving a mark. But to Marianne it was, in the beginning, like being addressed in an alien dialect: the words were plain, yet the meaning would not lodge. When, at last, she understood what Mrs. Jennings meant to suggest, she could not tell whether most to laugh at the absurdity or to condemn the impertinence. It struck her as unfeeling—an unkind triumph, at once, in the Colonel’s advanced years and in his forlorn situation as an old bachelor. Marianne’s mind, quick with ardor, had no patience for mirth that leaned on age as its joke.

Mrs. Dashwood, who could not think a man five years younger than herself so very ancient as he appeared to her youngest daughter’s fancy, ventured to clear Mrs. Jennings from the charge of intending cruelty.

“But at least, Mama,” Marianne cried, “you cannot deny the absurdity of the accusation, though you may not think it intentionally ill-natured. Colonel Brandon is certainly younger than Mrs. Jennings, but he is old enough to be my father; and if he were ever animated enough to be in love, he must have long outlived every sensation of the kind. It is too ridiculous! When is a man to be safe from such wit, if age and infirmity will not protect him?”

“Infirmity!” said Elinor. Her voice had a steadiness that often acted on Marianne like the clean ring of a spoon against china—cool, undeniable, and somehow restorative. “Do you call Colonel Brandon infirm? I can easily suppose that his age may appear much greater to you than to my mother; but you can hardly deceive yourself as to his having the use of his limbs!”

“Did not you hear him complain of the rheumatism?” Marianne returned. “And is not that the commonest infirmity of declining life?”

“My dearest child,” said Mrs. Dashwood, laughing, “at this rate you must be in continual terror of my decay; and it must seem to you a miracle that my life has been extended to the advanced age of forty.”

“Mama, you are not doing me justice. I know very well that Colonel Brandon is not old enough to make his friends yet apprehensive of losing him in the course of nature. He may live twenty years longer. But thirty-five has nothing to do with matrimony.”

“Perhaps,” said Elinor, “thirty-five and seventeen had better not have any thing to do with matrimony together. But if there should by any chance happen to be a woman who is single at seven and twenty, I should not think Colonel Brandon’s being thirty-five any objection to his marrying her.”

Marianne paused—only a moment, as if she had tasted a thought and found it disagreeable.

“A woman of seven and twenty,” she said at last, “can never hope to feel or inspire affection again; and if her home be uncomfortable, or her fortune small, I can suppose that she might bring herself to submit to the offices of a nurse, for the sake of the provision and security of a wife. In his marrying such a woman therefore there would be nothing unsuitable. It would be a compact of convenience, and the world would be satisfied. In my eyes it would be no marriage at all, but that would be nothing. To me it would seem only a commercial exchange, in which each wished to be benefited at the expense of the other.”

Elinor’s look, though calm, had that quiet persistence which never raised its voice, and yet was not to be shaken.

“It would be impossible, I know,” she replied, “to convince you that a woman of seven and twenty could feel for a man of thirty-five anything near enough to love, to make him a desirable companion to her. But I must object to your dooming Colonel Brandon and his wife to the constant confinement of a sick chamber, merely because he chanced to complain yesterday”—and she could not help adding, with a touch of reason that sounded like the soft settling of ashes in a grate—“(a very cold damp day) of a slight rheumatic feel in one of his shoulders.”

“But he talked of flannel waistcoats,” said Marianne; and the very phrase, to her, carried the odor of camphor and the muffled, airless warmth of rooms where the windows are never opened. “And with me a flannel waistcoat is invariably connected with aches, cramps, rheumatisms, and every species of ailment that can afflict the old and the feeble.”

“Had he been only in a violent fever,” Elinor answered, “you would not have despised him half so much. Confess, Marianne—is not there something interesting to you in the flushed cheek, hollow eye, and quick pulse of a fever?”

Marianne, who could make romance out of a storm and significance out of the very way the wind pressed against the panes, did not deny it; and yet she would not yield. Her imagination had its own vocabulary for feeling, and anything outside it—age spoken of without poetry, prudence offered like plain broth when she wanted spice—seemed to her not merely dull but almost a betrayal.

Soon after, Elinor left the room. The cottage, which had been full of voices, settled into a quieter sound: the faint tick of the clock, the soft scrape of a coal shifting, and beyond the walls the winter air moving in the hedges like a distant sea. In that gentler acoustic, Marianne’s thoughts turned—inevitably, passionately—to another subject, one nearer to her heart than Mrs. Jennings’ noisy prophecies.

“Mama,” she said, lowering her voice as if illness itself might be startled by loudness, “I have an alarm on the subject of illness which I cannot conceal from you. I am sure Edward Ferrars is not well. We have now been here almost a fortnight, and yet he does not come. Nothing but real indisposition could occasion this extraordinary delay. What else can detain him at Norland?”

“Had you any idea of his coming so soon?” Mrs. Dashwood asked.

“I had none. On the contrary, if I have felt any anxiety at all on the subject, it has been in recollecting that he sometimes showed a want of pleasure and readiness in accepting my invitation, when I talked of his coming to Barton. Does Elinor expect him already?”

“I have never mentioned it to her,” said Mrs. Dashwood, “but of course she must.”

“I rather think you are mistaken,” her mother returned, considering. “For when I was talking to her yesterday of getting a new grate for the spare bedchamber, she observed that there was no immediate hurry for it, as it was not likely that the room would be wanted for some time.”

“How strange this is! what can be the meaning of it!” Marianne’s words came faster now, as if they must outrun the chill apprehension that had settled over her. “But the whole of their behaviour to each other has been unaccountable! How cold, how composed were their last adieus! How languid their conversation the last evening of their being together! In Edward’s farewell there was no distinction between Elinor and me: it was the good wishes of an affectionate brother to both. Twice did I leave them purposely together in the course of the last morning, and each time did he most unaccountably follow me out of the room. And Elinor, in quitting Norland and Edward, cried not as I did. Even now her self-command is invariable. When is she dejected or melancholy? When does she try to avoid society, or appear restless and dissatisfied in it?”

Mrs. Dashwood listened, feeling the old, familiar ache of a mother who knows that her children live inside different languages of the heart. Marianne’s was a tongue of music and storms; Elinor’s, a careful speech where meaning was often carried not by sound but by silence. And in the stillness of the cottage—where every small domestic noise seemed magnified, where even the fire’s whisper sounded like counsel—Marianne could not help hearing absence as ominous, and composure as concealment.

The Dashwoods, by degrees, were rooted at Barton as a plant is rooted when it has been shifted—at first a little drooping from the change, then, with attentive tending, standing more upright than it ever did in the old border. The cottage—its low ceilings, its honest little rooms, its garden that seemed to keep the weather’s memory in every leaf—grew familiar to them; and the employments which had once made Norland tolerable were taken up again with an enjoyment more keen for having been interrupted by grief. There was comfort in the ordinary: the soft scratch of Elinor’s pencil; the whisper of pages under Mrs. Dashwood’s hand; the scissor’s brisk bite through thread; the kettle’s domestic song as it came up to the boil. Even the sounds outdoors had their order—wind moving in the hedge, rooks arguing in the elms, the thin far-off strike of a farm tool—so different from any harsher clamour that would have belonged to a town. Here, nothing mechanical drowned the living world; each noise had a source you could name, as each herb in the garden had a scent you could crush between finger and thumb.

Sir John Middleton, who in the first fortnight called upon them daily and was little used to finding women at home otherwise than in idleness, could never conceal his astonishment at encountering them always engaged. They were, to his thinking, perpetually doing something—reading, drawing, arranging, mending, planning—when, in his opinion, they might have been doing the far more sensible thing of driving about the neighbourhood and “seeing people.” But though he pressed them, and though he repeated with hearty generosity that his carriage was always theirs, Mrs. Dashwood’s independent spirit—made warmer, perhaps, by misfortune—overruled her desire of society for her children. She was determined to visit no family beyond walking distance.

This resolution, however, did not much enlarge their acquaintance; for there were few within reach, and fewer still to whom they could be readily introduced. On one of their earliest walks they had discovered, about a mile and a half from the cottage, along the narrow winding valley of Allenham (which issued from that of Barton), an ancient, respectable-looking mansion. Something in its aspect—older, graver, shaded by trees of a size that seemed to belong to another century—touched their imagination by reminding them, in a softened way, of Norland. They wished to know it better. Yet on enquiry they learned that its possessor, an elderly lady of excellent character, was unfortunately too infirm to mix with the world, and never stirred from home.

The country, nevertheless, made amends for the want of drawing-rooms. It abounded in walks which were not mere paths, but histories: lanes worn by generations of boots; banks where thyme grew, bruising its sharp, clean fragrance underfoot; little hollows where rain collected and held the sky in a trembling mirror. The high downs that rose so temptingly from almost every window of the cottage offered, when the valleys were clogged with mud, an alternative as wholesome as it was exhilarating. Their summits promised air that tasted of distance—salted by the unseen sea, spiced by gorse, and always carrying, in its sound, the wide liberty of open ground.

It was towards one of these hills that Marianne and Margaret, one memorable morning, directed their steps. Two days of settled rain had kept them within doors, and though the parlour was warm and the tea fragrant, the confinement had grown insupportable to Marianne. Now the sky was showery, but broken—patches of sunshine travelling, like blessings, across the fields; and Marianne, in that eager confidence of hers, declared the day would be lastingly fair, that every threatening cloud would be drawn away from their hills as if commanded.

The weather, however, was not tempting enough to entice the other two from their pencil and their book. Mrs. Dashwood had her reasons for preferring the hearth; Elinor, who observed the sky as carefully as she did a household account, shook her head at Marianne’s prophecy and stayed where she could hear the kettle and watch the light on paper.

So Marianne and Margaret set off together, and for a time were rewarded for their audacity. They gaily ascended the downs, rejoicing in their own penetration at every glimpse of blue; and when the high south-westerly wind met their faces—full of life and damp sweetness, carrying the smell of wet earth and crushed grass—they pitied, with laughing superiority, the fears that had kept their mother and Elinor indoors.

“Is there a felicity in the world,” cried Marianne, breathless with delight, “superior to this? Margaret, we will walk here at least two hours.”

Margaret, who loved motion and adventure chiefly because Marianne loved them, agreed at once. They pushed on against the wind, resisting it with laughing delight—skirts tugged and hair loosened, the sound of it in their ears like a great invisible instrument played over the hills.

For about twenty minutes they kept their triumph. Then, as if the sky had grown tired of being guessed at, the scattered clouds drew together above their heads, united into one heavy purpose, and a driving rain set full in their faces. The change was so sudden, so complete, that it felt like a rebuke. Chagrined and surprised, they were obliged—though unwillingly—to turn back; for no shelter was nearer than their own house.

One consolation remained, and in their present exigence it appeared not only proper but irresistible: they could run. The steep side of the hill led directly to their garden gate; and speed, if it could not stop the rain, might at least shorten the humiliation of enduring it.

They set off. The world became sound and sensation: rain striking their bonnets and lashes; the wind snatching at breath; the wet turf slick underfoot. Marianne, lighter and more impetuous, had at first the advantage. But a false step—no more than the smallest treachery in the grass—brought her suddenly to the ground. Margaret, unable to stop herself, was involuntarily hurried along and reached the bottom in safety, turning only when she had breath enough to cry out.

At that very moment a gentleman carrying a gun, with two pointers playing round him, was passing up the hill within a few yards of Marianne. He put down his gun at once and ran to her assistance. Marianne had raised herself from the ground, but her foot had been twisted in the fall, and she was scarcely able to stand. Rain streamed from her hair and cloak; the cold of it, and the shock, had driven the colour from her cheek—until it returned, all at once, in burning confusion, at finding herself observed.

The gentleman offered his services. Marianne’s modesty declined what her situation made necessary; she would have attempted, in spite of pain, to manage without him. But he, perceiving at once both her inability and her resistance, took her up in his arms without further delay and carried her down the hill.

It was done with a rapidity that left no room for ceremony—only for the quick, intimate awareness of another’s strength and nearness: the warmth of his coat against the chill of her wet clothes, the steady cadence of his steps, the sound of rain on his shoulders. Then, passing through the garden—the gate having been left open by Margaret in her haste—he bore her directly into the house.

Margaret had already reached the parlour, drenched and triumphant in her own rescue; and there, in the instant of astonishment before speech was possible, the gentleman entered behind her, still holding Marianne, and did not quit his burden until he had seated her carefully in a chair.

Elinor and her mother rose in amazement. The intrusion, the storm, Marianne’s disordered appearance, and the stranger’s presence—all struck at once. Yet as their eyes fixed on him, wonder mingled, in spite of themselves, with admiration. He apologized immediately, relating the cause of his entrance with a frankness and grace that made explanation itself a kind of charm. His person was uncommonly handsome, but it was the living addition of voice and expression—the ease of his manner, the warmth without presumption—that completed the impression.

Had he been old, ugly, and vulgar, Mrs. Dashwood’s gratitude would still have been secured by any act of attention to her child; she was not a woman to measure kindness by elegance. But youth and beauty and that air of breeding gave to the action an interest that came home more directly to her feelings. She thanked him again and again; and with that sweetness of address which never deserted her, invited him to be seated.

He declined, looking down at himself with a half-smile. He was, indeed, dirty and wet—the rain darkening the cloth, the mud staining the hem; the very scent of him brought the hillside into the room, a mingling of damp wool, cold air, and the sharp green of outdoors.

Mrs. Dashwood then begged to know to whom she was obliged.

His name, he replied, was Willoughby; and his present home was at Allenham, from whence he hoped she would allow him the honour of calling the next day to enquire after Miss Dashwood.

The honour was readily granted. He then departed—making himself, by leaving in the midst of a heavy rain, still more interesting. The door closed; the wind pressed at the window; the house seemed suddenly smaller for the absence of that unexpected energy.

His manly beauty and more than common gracefulness became instantly the theme of general admiration. Even the laugh which his gallantry raised against Marianne—who had, in truth, run too fast and trusted too much to luck—was warmed and enlivened by the memory of his exterior attractions. Marianne herself had seen less of his face than the rest; for the confusion which crimsoned over her cheeks when he lifted her had robbed her of the power of regarding him steadily after they entered the house. Yet she had seen enough to join in all their admiration, and with an energy which always adorned her praise.

His person and air were equal to what her fancy had ever drawn for the hero of a favourite story; and in his carrying her into the house with so little previous formality there was a rapidity of thought which particularly recommended the action to her. Every circumstance belonging to him became interesting. His name was good; his residence was in their favourite village; and she soon discovered—upon the evidence of her own heart—that of all manly dresses a shooting-jacket was the most becoming. Her imagination grew busy, her reflections pleasant; and the pain of a sprained ankle was treated as a trivial tax paid for a larger pleasure.

Sir John called on them as soon as the next interval of fair weather that morning allowed him to get out of doors. With the rain still ticking in the eaves and the air smelling of wet soil and cooling tea, Marianne’s accident was related to him; and he was eagerly asked whether he knew any gentleman of the name of Willoughby at Allenham.

“Willoughby!” cried Sir John. “What, is he in the country? That is good news however. I will ride over tomorrow, and ask him to dinner on Thursday.”

“You know him then?” said Mrs. Dashwood.

“Know him!—to be sure I do. Why, he is down here every year.”

“And what sort of a young man is he?” she asked, her tone soft but attentive; for she could never help being curious where her children’s feelings were engaged.

“As good a kind of fellow as ever lived, I assure you. A very decent shot, and there is not a bolder rider in England.”

“And is that all you can say for him?” cried Marianne, indignation flashing through her despite the bandaging of her ankle and the comfort of the chair. “But what are his manners on more intimate acquaintance? What his pursuits, his talents, and genius?”

Sir John, who liked a man for being agreeable and active and saw no need to inquire farther, looked rather puzzled.

“Upon my soul,” said he, “I do not know much about him as to all that. But he is a pleasant, good-humoured fellow, and has got the nicest little black bitch of a pointer I ever saw. Was she out with him today?”

But Marianne could no more satisfy him as to the colour of Mr. Willoughby’s pointer than he could describe to her the shades of Willoughby’s mind. What she carried away from the morning was not the dog’s coat but the cadence of a voice; not the facts of a family but the impression of a presence. And even in the room, the household’s ordinary sounds—the spoon against china, the low murmur of her mother and sister—seemed changed by the recollection of another sound layered upon them: the wind on the downs, and, against it, the clear human tone that had spoken her name with concern.

“But who is he?” said Elinor, whose curiosity moved by another road. “Where does he come from? Has he a house at Allenham?”

On this point Sir John could give more certain intelligence. Mr. Willoughby, he told them, had no property of his own in the country; he resided there only while visiting the old lady at Allenham Court, to whom he was related, and whose possessions he was to inherit; adding, with a broad significance meant to be both jest and counsel, “Yes, yes, he is very well worth catching, I can tell you, Miss Dashwood. He has a pretty little estate of his own in Somersetshire besides; and if I were you, I would not give him up to my younger sister, in spite of all this tumbling down hills. Miss Marianne must not expect to have all the men to herself. Brandon will be jealous, if she does not take care.”

“I do not believe,” said Mrs. Dashwood, with a good-humoured smile that yet held a quiet firmness, “that Mr. Willoughby will be incommoded by the attempts of either of my daughters towards what you call catching him. It is not an employment to which they have been brought up. Men are very safe with us, let them be ever so rich. I am glad to find, however, from what you say, that he is a respectable young man, and one whose acquaintance will not be ineligible.”

“He is as good a sort of fellow, I believe, as ever lived,” repeated Sir John, determined to be useful by repeating what he knew. “I remember last Christmas at a little hop at the park, he danced from eight o’clock till four, without once sitting down.”

“Did he indeed?” cried Marianne, her eyes sparkling as if the mere thought could dry the rain from her clothes and lift her from her chair. “And with elegance, with spirit?”

“Yes; and he was up again at eight to ride to covert.”

“That is what I like; that is what a young man ought to be. Whatever be his pursuits, his eagerness in them should know no moderation, and leave him no sense of fatigue.”

“Aye, aye, I see how it will be,” said Sir John, laughing with the satisfaction of a man who thinks he understands hearts because he enjoys teasing them. “I see how it will be. You will be setting your cap at him now, and never think of poor Brandon.”

“That is an expression, Sir John,” said Marianne warmly, the blood rising again—not with confusion now, but with principle—“which I particularly dislike. I abhor every common-place phrase by which wit is intended; and ‘setting one’s cap at a man,’ or ‘making a conquest,’ are the most odious of all. Their tendency is gross and illiberal; and if their construction could ever be deemed clever, time has long ago destroyed all its ingenuity.”

Sir John did not much understand the reproof—his ear was made for merriment, not for the fine distinctions of language—but he laughed as heartily as if he did, and replied, “Ay, you will make conquests enough, I dare say, one way or other. Poor Brandon!—he is quite smitten already, and he is very well worth setting your cap at, I can tell you, in spite of all this tumbling about and spraining of ankles.”

And while the parlour returned, as parlours must, to its ordinary order—the tea cooling, the rain lessening, the household voices settling into their accustomed tones—Marianne lay back and listened. The world outside was still ringing with wind and water; inside, a new name had been spoken into their domestic life. She could not have said, in any phrase Sir John would approve, what it meant to her; for what she felt had no neat translation into other people’s idioms. But it had entered her, as scent enters a room when a door is opened—uninvited, unmistakable, and impossible to forget.

Willoughby—Marianne’s preserver, as Margaret, with more elegance than accuracy, had named him—came early the next morning to the cottage, as if the dew were not yet off the grass and still he could not be kept from asking after her with his own lips. His horse’s hooves had scarcely softened on the lane before they were met by the cottage’s small, habitual music: the quick clap of a shutter eased open, the whisper of a broom along the threshold, the kettle beginning its steady, domestic murmur over the coals. It was the sort of sound a family makes without thinking, a tender order of life that no drawing-room applause can counterfeit.

Mrs. Dashwood received him with more than politeness—with that open warmth which gratitude will lend to good breeding, and which Sir John’s praises had already prepared. In a house where affection moved as naturally as the steam from a fresh pot, every word and look confirmed to him that accident had conducted him, not merely to beauty, but to sense, elegance, and that comfort which is as real as bread and as difficult to purchase as peace.

He needed no second visit to be persuaded of their personal charms. Miss Dashwood’s complexion was delicate; her features regular; her figure remarkably pretty. Marianne was still handsomer. If her form was less “correct” in the cold language of comparison, it had the greater advantage of height, and therefore struck more at once; and her face—so often, in careless praise, called beautiful—was one of those rare faces in which the word does less violence to truth than it commonly does. Her skin was very brown, yet so transparent that her colouring shone with uncommon brilliancy; her smile was sweet and drawing; and in her very dark eyes there lived such spirit, such eagerness—like a quick flame that answers to every change of air—that it was scarcely possible to meet them without delight.

At first, those eyes withheld themselves from him. The remembrance of his assistance embarrassed her; gratitude can, for a few moments, make the simplest civility feel like a debt. But embarrassment cannot long keep its ground where frankness is the fashion of the room; and when she found, in the perfect good-breeding of the gentleman, a union of openness and vivacity; and above all, when she heard him declare—without affectation, as if it were as necessary a confession as hunger—that he was passionately fond of music and dancing, she gave him such a look of approbation as secured to her the largest share of his discourse for the rest of his stay.

Only touch upon a favourite amusement, and Marianne’s silence was impossible. She had neither shyness nor reserve where feeling invited speech; and she spoke, not as one who throws out words to fill a pause, but as one who cannot help pouring out what presses behind the lips. They discovered, almost in the same moment, that their delight in music and dancing was mutual, and—what pleased her still more—that it arose from a conformity of judgment in all that related to either: the same tenderness to expression, the same impatience of cold execution, the same hatred of performance that is all glitter and no soul. The cottage, which had lately been only the theatre of anxieties, became the scene of a bright, intimate concord; even the small sounds of the room—the crackle of the fire, the faint chime of a spoon against china when Margaret could not keep still—seemed to fall into time with their conversation, as if the very house listened.

Encouraged by the agreement of their tastes, Marianne carried her enquiries further, and brought forward the subject of books. Her favourite authors were named and dwelt upon with such rapturous delight, such vivid recollection of passages—remembered, as she remembered melodies, by their power to move—that any young man of five-and-twenty must have been insensible indeed not to become an immediate convert to works he might once have neglected. Their taste appeared strikingly alike. The same books, the same passages were idolised by each—or if any difference arose, if he offered an objection, it lasted no longer than until her arguments gained force from her animation, and her eyes, in defending what she loved, grew brighter still. He acquiesced in all her decisions, caught her enthusiasm, and long before his visit concluded they conversed with the familiarity of a long-established acquaintance, as if a long winter of separation had merely been ended by a door opening.

When he had gone, and the cottage recovered its usual noises—Mrs. Dashwood’s careful arranging of work upon the table, Margaret’s restless step, the soft, patient turning of a page—Elinor could not resist her smile.

“Well, Marianne,” said she, “for one morning I think you have done pretty well. You have already ascertained Mr. Willoughby’s opinion in almost every matter of importance. You know what he thinks of Cowper and Scott; you are certain of his estimating their beauties as he ought, and you have received every assurance of his admiring Pope no more than is proper. But how is your acquaintance to be long supported, under such extraordinary despatch of every subject for discourse? You will soon have exhausted each favourite topic. Another meeting will suffice to explain his sentiments on picturesque beauty, and second marriages, and then you can have nothing farther to ask.”

“Elinor!” cried Marianne, stung and glowing at once. “Is this fair? is this just? are my ideas so scanty? But I see what you mean. I have been too much at my ease, too happy, too frank. I have erred against every common-place notion of decorum; I have been open and sincere where I ought to have been reserved, spiritless, dull, and deceitful—had I talked only of the weather and the roads, and had I spoken only once in ten minutes, this reproach would have been spared.”

“My love,” said her mother, who could never bear to see a generous spirit wounded by a jest, “you must not be offended with Elinor—she was only in jest. I should scold her myself, if she were capable of wishing to check the delight of your conversation with our new friend.”

Marianne was softened in a moment. There was something in Mrs. Dashwood’s voice—always rich with feeling, and now as soothing as a familiar song—that restored her ease as quickly as it had been disturbed.

Willoughby, on his side, gave every proof of the pleasure he took in their acquaintance which an eager wish of improving it could offer. He came to them every day. To enquire after Marianne was at first his excuse; but the encouragement of his reception, which every day grew kinder, made such an excuse unnecessary before it had ceased to be possible by Marianne’s perfect recovery. She was confined for some days to the house; but never had any confinement been less irksome. The cottage, within its modest walls, offered all that her heart demanded: the quiet pulse of domestic sound; the fire; the window with its changing light; and, most of all, a visitor whose presence made the air itself seem more alive.

Willoughby was a young man of good abilities, quick imagination, lively spirits, and open, affectionate manners. He was exactly formed to engage Marianne’s heart; for with all this he joined not only a captivating person, but a natural ardour of mind which was now roused and increased by the example of her own, and which recommended him to her affection beyond every thing else. His society became gradually her most exquisite enjoyment. They read, they talked, they sang together. His musical talents were considerable; and he read with all the sensibility and spirit which Edward had unfortunately wanted. When a line rose and fell on his voice, it did not sound like recitation but like confession; and even Elinor, who guarded her feelings as carefully as one guards a lamp from draughts, could not deny that there was pleasure in hearing words take breath as naturally as music.

In Mrs. Dashwood’s estimation he was as faultless as in Marianne’s. Elinor saw nothing to censure in him but a propensity—in which he strongly resembled, and peculiarly delighted, her sister—of saying too much what he thought on every occasion, without attention to persons or circumstances. In hastily forming and giving his opinion of other people, in sacrificing general politeness to the enjoyment of undivided attention where his heart was engaged, and in slighting too easily the forms of worldly propriety, he displayed a want of caution which Elinor could not approve, in spite of all that he and Marianne could say in its support.

Marianne began now to perceive that the desperation which had seized her at sixteen and a half, of ever seeing a man who could satisfy her ideas of perfection, had been rash and unjustifiable. Willoughby was all that her fancy had delineated in that unhappy hour and in every brighter period as capable of attaching her; and his behaviour declared his wishes, in that respect, to be as earnest as his abilities were strong. Her mother too—who, in whose mind not one speculative thought of their marriage had been raised by his prospect of riches—was led, before the end of a week, to hope and expect it; and secretly to congratulate herself on having gained two such sons-in-law as Edward and Willoughby.

Colonel Brandon’s partiality for Marianne, which had so early been discovered by his friends, now first became perceptible to Elinor when it ceased to be noticed by them. Their attention and wit were drawn off to his more fortunate rival; and the raillery which the other had incurred before any partiality arose was removed when his feelings began really to call for the ridicule so justly annexed to sensibility. Elinor was obliged—though unwillingly—to believe that the sentiments which Mrs. Jennings had assigned him for her own satisfaction were now actually excited by her sister; and that, however a general resemblance of disposition between the parties might forward the affection of Mr. Willoughby, an equally striking opposition of character was no hindrance to the regard of Colonel Brandon.

She saw it with concern; for what could a silent man of five-and-thirty hope, when opposed to a very lively one of five-and-twenty? and as she could not even wish him successful, she heartily wished him indifferent. Yet she liked him. In spite of his gravity and reserve, she beheld in him an object of interest. His manners, though serious, were mild; and his reserve appeared rather the result of some oppression of spirits than of any natural gloominess of temper. Sir John had dropped hints of past injuries and disappointments which justified her belief of his being an unfortunate man; and she regarded him with respect and compassion. Perhaps she pitied and esteemed him the more because he was slighted by Willoughby and Marianne, who, prejudiced against him for being neither lively nor young, seemed resolved to undervalue his merits.

“Brandon is just the kind of man,” said Willoughby one day, when they were talking of him together, “whom every body speaks well of, and nobody cares about; whom all are delighted to see, and nobody remembers to talk to.”

“That is exactly what I think of him,” cried Marianne.

“Do not boast of it, however,” said Elinor, “for it is injustice in both of you. He is highly esteemed by all the family at the park, and I never see him myself without taking pains to converse with him.”

“That he is patronised by you,” replied Willoughby, “is certainly in his favour; but as for the esteem of the others, it is a reproach in itself. Who would submit to the indignity of being approved by such a woman as Lady Middleton and Mrs. Jennings, that could command the indifference of any body else?”

“But perhaps the abuse of such people as yourself and Marianne will make amends for the regard of Lady Middleton and her mother. If their praise is censure, your censure may be praise, for they are not more undiscerning than you are prejudiced and unjust.”

“In defence of your protégé you can even be saucy.”

“My protégé, as you call him, is a sensible man; and sense will always have attractions for me. Yes, Marianne, even in a man between thirty and forty. He has seen a great deal of the world; has been abroad, has read, and has a thinking mind. I have found him capable of giving me much information on various subjects; and he has always answered my inquiries with readiness of good-breeding and good nature.”

“That is to say,” cried Marianne, with contempt, “he has told you, that in the East Indies the climate is hot, and the mosquitoes are troublesome.”

“He would have told me so, I doubt not, had I made any such inquiries; but they happened to be points on which I had been previously informed.”

“Perhaps,” said Willoughby, “his observations may have extended to the existence of nabobs, gold mohrs, and palanquins.”

“I may venture to say that his observations have stretched much further than your candour. But why should you dislike him?”

“I do not dislike him. I consider him, on the contrary, as a very respectable man, who has every body’s good word, and nobody’s notice; who has more money than he can spend, more time than he knows how to employ, and two new coats every year.”

“Add to which,” cried Marianne, “that he has neither genius, taste, nor spirit. That his understanding has no brilliancy, his feelings no ardour, and his voice no expression.”

“You decide on his imperfections so much in the mass,” replied Elinor, “and so much on the strength of your own imagination, that the commendation I am able to give of him is comparatively cold and insipid. I can only pronounce him to be a sensible man, well-bred, well-informed, of gentle address, and, I believe, possessing an amiable heart.”

“Miss Dashwood,” cried Willoughby, “you are now using me unkindly. You are endeavouring to disarm me by reason, and to convince me against my will. But it will not do. You shall find me as stubborn as you can be artful. I have three unanswerable reasons for disliking Colonel Brandon; he threatened me with rain when I wanted it to be fine; he has found fault with the hanging of my curricle, and I cannot persuade him to buy my brown mare. If it will be any satisfaction to you, however, to be told, that I believe his character to be in other respects irreproachable, I am ready to confess it. And in return for an acknowledgment, which must give me some pain, you cannot deny me the privilege of disliking him as much as ever.”

When the Dashwoods first crossed into Devonshire, Mrs. Dashwood and her daughters had imagined a quieter sort of life—hours long enough for needlework, reading, reflection; mornings not immediately portioned out by other people’s wishes. Yet the country, with its hedged lanes and talkative neighbours, took hold of them almost at once. Engagements sprang up like autumn mushrooms after rain. Invitations arrived with baskets of apples, with jars of preserved pears, with notes smudged by haste and sociable certainty. Visitors came so often that the very threshold seemed to remember footsteps.

As soon as Marianne’s health was restored, Sir John’s long-hoarded plans for their diversion poured out in a cheerful flood. The private balls at the Park commenced; and, as often as a showery October allowed, parties on the water were attempted and, with much laughter at the weather’s caprice, accomplished. The season’s damp had its own domestic music—the soft percussion of rain on leaves, the low swish of skirts brushed by wet grass, the creak of oars, the small, pleased roar of distant rivers swollen and busy. In that soundscape there was, to Elinor’s mind, something healing, a kind of honest accompaniment to life; it was the harsher noises of fashion—forced gaiety, the clatter of cards, the scrape of chairs in crowded rooms—that could turn the head and weary the heart.

In every meeting of the kind, Mr. Willoughby was included. He belonged to these parties as naturally as cider belonged to an October supper; and the ease and familiarity which attended them were exactly calculated to increase the intimacy of his acquaintance with the Dashwoods—to give him continual opportunity of witnessing Marianne’s excellencies, of marking his animated admiration of her, and of receiving, in her behaviour to himself, the most pointed assurance of her affection.

Elinor could not be surprised at their attachment. It had the inevitability of leaven at work: once introduced, it rose, warmed, and claimed the whole loaf. She only wished it were less openly shown; and once or twice, as gently as one might suggest lowering the fire rather than putting it out, she ventured to hint at the propriety of some self-command to Marianne.

But Marianne abhorred all concealment where no real disgrace could attend unreserve. To aim at the restraint of sentiments which were not in themselves illaudable appeared to her not merely an unnecessary effort, but a disgraceful subjection of reason to common-place and mistaken notions. Mr. Willoughby thought the same; and their behaviour at all times was an illustration of their opinions.

When he was present she had no eyes for any one else. Every thing he did was right; every thing he said was clever. If their evenings at the Park were concluded with cards, he cheated himself and all the rest of the party to get her a good hand, with a frank gaiety that made his injustice appear almost a sort of gallantry. If dancing formed the amusement of the night, they were partners for half the time; and when obliged to separate for a couple of dances, they were careful to stand together, and scarcely spoke a word to any body else. Even the room seemed to narrow around them, as if their attention drew the air into a smaller circle.

Such conduct made them, of course, most exceedingly laughed at; but ridicule could not shame them, and seemed hardly to provoke them. Mrs. Dashwood entered into all their feelings with a warmth which left her no inclination for checking this excessive display. To her it was but the natural consequence of a strong affection in a young and ardent mind; a bright blaze on the hearth, not to be scolded into embers for fear the neighbours should see the light through the curtains.

This was the season of happiness to Marianne. Her heart was devoted to Willoughby; and even her fond attachment to Norland, which she had brought with her from Sussex like a cherished family recipe—tenderly remembered, endlessly recited—was more likely to be softened than she had thought possible before, by the charms which his society bestowed on her present home.

Elinor’s happiness was not so great. Her heart was not so much at ease, nor her satisfaction in their amusements so pure. The engagements, for all their bustle, brought her no companion who could make amends for what she had left behind, nor any one who could teach her to think of Norland with less regret than ever. She found herself measuring voices the way one measures ingredients: the right tone, the right warmth, the right pauses that make conversation nourishing rather than merely filling. Here, most talk either came too loudly or with too little flavour.

Neither Lady Middleton nor Mrs. Jennings could supply the conversation she missed; although the latter was an everlasting talker, and from the first had regarded Elinor with a kindness which ensured her a large share of discourse. Mrs. Jennings told her history repeatedly, as a cook repeats instructions even to a careful listener—again the same details, again the same emphases, the same turns of phrase, as if repetition might preserve what time threatened to spoil. She had already related it to Elinor three or four times; and had Elinor’s memory been equal to Mrs. Jennings’s means of improvement, she might very early in their acquaintance have known all the particulars of Mr. Jennings’s last illness, and what he said to his wife a few minutes before he died.

Lady Middleton was more agreeable than her mother only in being more silent. Elinor needed little observation to perceive that her reserve was a mere calmness of manner with which sense had nothing to do. Towards her husband and mother she was the same as to others; intimacy was therefore neither to be looked for nor desired. She had nothing to say one day that she had not said the day before. Her insipidity was invariable; even her spirits never altered. Though she did not oppose the parties arranged by her husband—provided every thing were conducted in style and her two eldest children attended her—she never appeared to receive more enjoyment from them than she might have experienced in sitting at home. So little did her presence add to the pleasure of others by any share in their conversation, that they were sometimes only reminded of her being amongst them by her solicitude about her troublesome boys: sharp little cries and stamping feet that cut into the room’s softer sounds like ill-timed clanging in a church bell’s echo.

In Colonel Brandon alone, of all her new acquaintance, did Elinor find a person who could in any degree claim the respect of abilities, excite the interest of friendship, or give pleasure as a companion. Mr. Willoughby was out of the question. Marianne’s admiration and regard—even Elinor’s sisterly regard—was all his own; but he was a lover, and his attentions were wholly Marianne’s. A far less agreeable man might have been more generally pleasing, had he spared others the constant sense of being uninvited guests at an entertainment arranged for two.

Colonel Brandon, unfortunately for himself, had no such encouragement to think only of Marianne; and in conversing with Elinor he found the greatest consolation for the indifference of her sister. Elinor’s compassion for him increased, as she had reason to suspect that the misery of disappointed love had already been known to him.

This suspicion was given by some words which accidentally dropped from him one evening at the Park. The company were assembled in one of the larger rooms; the dancers moved in measured figures while the musicians—well-meaning, a little too eager—filled every corner with sound. Between the repeated strains, Elinor sometimes heard the quieter world outside: wind shifting in the trees, rain worrying at the panes, the brief hush before a new tune began. She and Colonel Brandon had seated themselves together by mutual consent, while the others were dancing.

His eyes, fixed on Marianne, followed her as she laughed and turned, her colour bright, her hair loosened slightly in the warmth of exertion. After a silence of some minutes—one of those silences that feels not empty, but laden, like a covered dish brought to table and not yet unveiled—he said, with a faint smile, “Your sister, I understand, does not approve of second attachments.”

“No,” replied Elinor, “her opinions are all romantic.”

“Or rather, as I believe, she considers them impossible to exist.”

“I believe she does. But how she contrives it without reflecting on the character of her own father, who had himself two wives, I know not. A few years, however, will settle her opinions on the reasonable basis of common sense and observation; and then they may be more easy to define and to justify than they now are, by any body but herself.”

“This will probably be the case,” he replied; “and yet there is something so amiable in the prejudices of a young mind, that one is sorry to see them give way to the reception of more general opinions.”

“I cannot agree with you there,” said Elinor. “There are inconveniences attending such feelings as Marianne’s, which all the charms of enthusiasm and ignorance of the world cannot atone for. Her systems have all the unfortunate tendency of setting propriety at nought; and a better acquaintance with the world is what I look forward to as her greatest possible advantage.”

After a short pause he resumed, as if trying to lay his thoughts more evenly, like a hand smoothing dough that will not quite come together. “Does your sister make no distinction in her objections against a second attachment? or is it equally criminal in every body? Are those who have been disappointed in their first choice—whether from the inconstancy of its object, or the perverseness of circumstances—to be equally indifferent during the rest of their lives?”

“Upon my word, I am not acquainted with the minutiae of her principles. I only know that I never yet heard her admit any instance of a second attachment’s being pardonable.”

“This,” said he, “cannot hold; but a change, a total change of sentiments—No, no, do not desire it; for when the romantic refinements of a young mind are obliged to give way, how frequently are they succeeded by such opinions as are but too common, and too dangerous! I speak from experience. I once knew a lady who in temper and mind greatly resembled your sister, who thought and judged like her, but who from an enforced change—from a series of unfortunate circumstances—”

Here he stopped suddenly. He appeared to think he had said too much; and by his countenance gave rise to conjectures which might not otherwise have entered Elinor’s head. The lady would probably have passed without suspicion, had he not convinced Miss Dashwood that what concerned her ought not to escape his lips. As it was, it required but a slight effort of fancy to connect his emotion with the tender recollection of past regard.

Elinor attempted no more. She would not press him, not with the music still sawing its way through the room, not with Marianne’s laughter rising and falling on the air like a refrain, not with Colonel Brandon’s voice—so usually composed—betraying, in those broken clauses, the existence of a story whose true language perhaps could never be properly translated into another’s understanding.

But Marianne, in her place, would not have done so little. The whole tale would have been speedily formed under her active imagination; every circumstance arranged, every sorrow seasoned to the richest poignancy; and every thing established in the most melancholy order of disastrous love.

Elinor and Marianne were abroad early the next morning, the air rinsed clean by night rain and full of those small, particular sounds that make a place feel inhabited—the drip of eaves into puddles, the soft complaint of damp leaves, the distant answering of rooks from the higher trees. Their footsteps kept time upon the gravel as they walked; and yet it was Marianne’s voice that filled the lane, bright and hurried, as if she had been holding her happiness in her mouth all night and could no longer keep it.

“I must tell you,” she said, clasping Elinor’s arm with a warmth that was half triumph, half tenderness. “Willoughby has given me a horse.”

Elinor stopped short. The news had that extravagant air which always startled her most—not because she had not long understood Marianne’s capacity for rapture, but because such raptures, once accepted, could not be unaccepted without consequence. “A horse?”

“A horse he bred himself in Somersetshire,” Marianne went on, as if naming the county were like naming an herb in a recipe—proof of quality, of origin, of care. “Exactly calculated to carry a woman. He says it moves like music—light, obedient. He intends to send his groom immediately for it. Think, Elinor—think of a gallop on these downs, every day. You shall ride it too; I insist upon it.”

The downs lay pale and open beyond them, under a sky that had not decided whether it would break or weep again. Marianne’s eyes, however, were already elsewhere: in wind, in speed, in the imagined quickening of blood. To Elinor, the vision came with other attachments—like a rich dish whose cost one tastes at once beneath the first delicious mouthful.

“My dear Marianne,” she said as gently as she could, “have you considered what it implies? Mama has no intention of keeping a horse. If she alters her plans for this—if you accept it—we must buy another for the servant, keep a man to ride it, and we have not even a stable. A gift is not only the thing itself; it draws an establishment after it.”

Marianne’s smile wavered, then returned with the impatience of one who will not have felicity measured out by the spoonful. “An additional servant would be a trifle. Mama will never object. Any horse would do for him; he might always borrow one at the Park. And as for a stable—what is a stable? The merest shed would answer.”

Elinor heard in her sister’s voice that dangerous ease with which people dismiss what they do not wish to reckon. It was like watching a cook fling handfuls of spice into the pot with a lavish wrist, enchanted by fragrance, forgetting that someone must pay for saffron.

She walked on a little, letting the sound of their steps and the thin, bright calls of birds between hedges temper her words before she said them. “There is another consideration.”

Marianne looked at her quickly. Elinor could have wished to keep her eyes upon the rain-beaded hawthorn instead of meeting that ardour, but she did not flinch. “Is it proper,” she asked, “to receive such a present—from a man so little, or at least so lately known to you?”

That touched what could not be touched without pain. Marianne’s colour rose as if a warm oven-door had opened in her breast. “You are mistaken, Elinor,” she said, and the sweetness of her earlier tone sharpened. “You suppose I know very little of Willoughby. I have not known him long indeed; but I am much better acquainted with him than I am with any other creature in the world, except yourself and mama. It is not time or opportunity that determines intimacy—it is disposition. Seven years would be insufficient to make some people acquainted with each other, and seven days are more than enough for others. I should hold myself guilty of greater impropriety in accepting a horse from my brother than from Willoughby. Of John I know very little, though we have lived together for years; but of Willoughby my judgment has long been formed.”

Elinor felt the ground give slightly beneath her, not from Marianne’s reasoning—which was all fire and no structure—but from her certainty. Opposition now would only feed that certainty, as wind feeds flame. She changed her tack, and, with that careful economy of feeling that was her own kind of devotion, appealed where Marianne’s tenderness was most steady.

“Then think of mama,” she said. “Not of whether she would object—but of what she would do, out of kindness. You know she would stretch herself, deny herself, take on trouble gladly, rather than refuse you. It is not right to tempt her to such imprudent generosity. We are settled here to live simply. We must not let any one draw her into expense and inconvenience.”

The mention of their mother—of her easy, affectionate readiness to be persuaded—worked like a cool hand laid on a fevered brow. Marianne’s expression softened; her gaze fell to the wet grass at the path’s edge. She struggled, for a moment, between her desire and her conscience; and Elinor, listening, could almost hear that struggle in the quiet of the morning, as plainly as the ticking drip of water from a leaf.

At last Marianne said, subdued, “You are right. I will not mention it to mama. I will tell him—when I see him—that it must be declined.”

Elinor, relieved, pressed her sister’s hand. Marianne’s promise had cost her something, and Elinor honoured the cost.

She kept her word. When Willoughby called at the cottage that day, Elinor—passing through the parlour with some household errand—heard Marianne’s voice lowered, as if they were trying to keep their words from the very walls. Marianne spoke of her disappointment; she named, plainly and without exaggeration, the reasons that made acceptance impossible. They were reasons no man of any delicacy could combat without becoming indelicate.

Willoughby’s concern was unmistakable. There was a quick sincerity in it, like a sudden hush falling over a room that has been too loud. Elinor could not see his face from where she stood; but she heard the altered weight of his voice as he answered, also low.

“But, Marianne—the horse is still yours, though you cannot use it now. I shall keep it only till you can claim it. When you leave Barton to form your own establishment, in a more lasting home, Queen Mab shall receive you.”

Queen Mab. The name, spoken with that intimate ease; and Marianne’s own name too, given without ceremony—Christian name only, as if there were no need for any other—told Elinor what argument and observation had been leading her toward, and told it with a directness that made her almost giddy. The promise contained a whole future: a home not borrowed but made; an establishment “more lasting;” Marianne leaving Barton—not as a guest of circumstance, but as a bride.

From that moment Elinor doubted no longer. They were engaged. The only surprise remaining was that, with tempers so frank, they could have left even their nearest friends to discover it by accident, as one stumbles upon a hidden hearth by the smell of bread.

The next day, Margaret brought her confirmation with the gravity of a child who believes herself entrusted with history.

“Oh, Elinor!” she cried, drawing her sister aside with a face so important it made Elinor smile in spite of herself. “I have such a secret to tell you about Marianne. I am sure she will be married to Mr. Willoughby very soon.”

“You have said so,” Elinor returned, “almost every day since they first met on High-church Down; and before they had known each other a week you were certain Marianne wore his picture round her neck—when it proved to be only our great-uncle.”

“But indeed this is quite another thing!” Margaret’s eyes widened. “I am sure they will be married very soon, for he has got a lock of her hair.”

Elinor could not prevent a short laugh. “Take care, Margaret. It may be only the hair of some great-uncle of his.”

“It is Marianne’s,” Margaret insisted, affronted by such injustice to her powers of observation. “I am almost sure it is, for I saw him cut it off. Last night, after tea—when you and mama went out of the room—they were whispering and talking as fast as could be, and he seemed to be begging something of her; and presently he took up her scissors and cut off a long lock of her hair, for it was all tumbled down her back; and he kissed it, and folded it up in a piece of white paper, and put it into his pocket-book.”

The detail was too exact to be merely invented. Elinor believed it at once; and she was not disposed to resist believing it, for it fell into place with what she had overheard and with all the unguarded language of Willoughby’s promise. In Marianne’s loosened hair, in the private snip of scissors and the kiss and the careful folding—there was the same kind of intimacy as in the naming of Queen Mab: a domestic confidence, like seasoning tasted from the spoon and approved without comment.

Margaret’s sagacity, however, did not always serve her sisters so well.

One evening at the Park, when the noise of company made a sort of weather of its own—voices rising and falling like gusts, chairs scraping, fireside tools clinking—Mrs. Jennings, in the midst of her comfortable raillery, turned suddenly upon Margaret with that hungry curiosity of hers, which delighted in guessing other people’s feelings aloud.

“Come, Miss Margaret,” she said, “you must tell me the name of the young man who is Elinor’s particular favourite. It has been a prodigious mystery this long while.”

Margaret, pleased to be teased and to tease in turn, looked straight at Elinor and said, with a cautious air that would have been comic if it had not been cruel, “I must not tell, may I, Elinor?”

Everybody laughed; the sound struck Elinor like a spoon against a cracked cup. She tried to laugh too, because to refuse would be to confess; but the effort was painful, and she felt the heat of it on her face as if she were too near a blaze. In an instant she was certain Margaret had fixed—by chance or childish inference—upon a name Elinor could not bear to have made a standing joke in Mrs. Jennings’s mouth.

Marianne felt for her, and yet, by the very intensity of her sympathy, made it worse. She flushed deeply and spoke sharply to Margaret. “Remember that whatever your conjectures may be, you have no right to repeat them.”

“I never had any conjectures,” Margaret retorted. “It was you who told me of it yourself.”

That only increased the company’s mirth; the room, already too loud, grew louder. Mrs. Jennings clapped her hands, delighted.

“Oh! Pray, Miss Margaret, let us know all about it. What is the gentleman’s name?”

“I must not tell, ma’am,” Margaret said, enjoying her own importance. “But I know very well what it is; and I know where he is too.”

“Yes, yes, we can guess where he is,” cried Mrs. Jennings. “At his own house at Norland to be sure. He is the curate of the parish, I dare say.”

“No, that he is not,” Margaret returned triumphantly. “He is of no profession at all.”

“Margaret,” Marianne said, warm with indignation, “you know that all this is an invention of your own, and that there is no such person in existence.”

“Well then,” Margaret cried, refusing to be crushed, “he is lately dead, Marianne; for I am sure there was such a man once, and his name begins with an F.”

Elinor’s heart seemed to stop and then go on again with a different rhythm. The air of the room grew thick; even the familiar sounds—the laugh, the rustle of silk, the crackle of the fire—felt suddenly like noise, not life, as if something fine in her had been jostled by careless hands.

At that moment Lady Middleton, with a composure that always appeared like disapproval, observed, “It rains very hard.”

Elinor could have loved her for the sentence, though she suspected the interruption owed less to any concern for Elinor than to Lady Middleton’s dislike of such coarse sport. Still, the topic—so harmless, so general—fell like a cloth thrown over a dish one is glad to hide.

Colonel Brandon, who seemed always to notice what others suffered and to step quietly between pain and the public eye, took it up at once. Much was said of the rain by him and by Lady Middleton; the company, eager for any change, let the raillery drop as easily as an emptied cup.

Willoughby opened the piano-forte and asked Marianne to sit down. The lid rose with a soft wooden sigh; the first notes, when she played, gathered the room into a more tolerable harmony. Conversation broke into smaller threads. Sound became music again instead of laughter sharpened into mockery.

But Elinor did not recover so quickly. The alarm remained in her—less the fear of discovery than the knowledge of how lightly other people could throw a name about, how a private feeling might be dragged into the common language of jest, changed by being spoken. She sat with her hands folded, listening as much to what was not said as to what was: the way Colonel Brandon’s voice kept low; the way Marianne’s playing covered awkwardness; the way Mrs. Jennings, balked, soon sought new amusement.

Before the evening ended a party was arranged for the following day: they were to go, by Colonel Brandon’s interest, to see a place some twelve miles from Barton, belonging to his brother-in-law. The owner was abroad and had left strict orders that it was not to be shown without permission; but Brandon could procure admittance. Sir John, warm in praise and loud in certainty, declared the grounds to be as fine as any in the county, and might be allowed a judge’s confidence, having made parties to visit them at least twice every summer for the last ten years.

There was, he said, a noble piece of water—a sail upon it to be the great entertainment of the morning. Cold provisions were to be taken; open carriages only employed; everything conducted in the full, bustling style of a pleasure-party.

To some of the company it appeared bold—almost wilful—considering the season and that it had rained every day for the last fortnight. The very mention of cold provisions, of damp air and open carriages, made Elinor think at once of her mother’s cough, like a faint thread of sound in the night that tells you the house is not as warm as it should be.

Mrs. Dashwood, who had already caught cold, was persuaded by Elinor to stay at home. Elinor urged it not as command but as care, imagining their mother by the fire, with tea properly made, the kettle singing softly instead of the wind; a small domestic refuge against the wet world’s ceaseless, needling noise.

Their projected expedition to Whitwell, so long talked of and so confidently promised, ended in a disappointment more mortifying than any wet stockings or jolting miles of road. Elinor had armed herself against rain, fatigue, even a frightened moment on some narrow lane; but she had not imagined that they should be cheated of the very going.

By ten o’clock the whole party was gathered at the Park, where breakfast waited, bright with the bustle of servants and the fragrance of hot bread. The night’s rain still clung to the hedges; it fell now only as a steady drip from leaf to leaf, and the gravel outside held the water in shallow, shining hollows. Yet the morning itself was tolerable—clouds loosening and sliding apart, a pale sun breaking through often enough to make them all insist the day meant well.

They were in that particular humour which belongs to companies resolved upon enjoyment: determined, as Sir John declared, to be happy if happiness could be bullied into appearing. The room rang with talk; cups met saucers; knives worked at crisp rashers; and the low, domestic music of breakfast—pouring, slicing, laughing—seemed to promise that even weather must submit.

In the midst of this, the letters were brought.

Among them was one for Colonel Brandon. He took it up, glanced at the direction, and something in him altered at once—as if colour had been wiped from his face by the very sight of the hand that wrote. Without speaking, he rose and left the room.

“What is the matter with Brandon?” cried Sir John, staring after him as though a man could not properly vanish from a breakfast table without explanation.

No one could tell.

“I hope he has had no bad news,” said Lady Middleton, with that air of propriety which made even concern seem neatly arranged.

“It must be something extraordinary,” added she, “that could make Colonel Brandon leave my breakfast table so suddenly.”

In about five minutes he returned. His manner was composed again, but it was the sort of composure that has been fastened on, not grown naturally.

“No bad news, Colonel, I hope?” cried Mrs. Jennings the instant he appeared, her curiosity already pressing forward like a well-fed spaniel.

“None at all, ma’am, I thank you.”

“Was it from Avignon? I hope it is not to say that your sister is worse.”

“No, ma’am. It came from town, and is merely a letter of business.”

“But how came the hand to discompose you so much, if it was only a letter of business? Come, come, this won’t do, Colonel; so let us hear the truth of it.”

“My dear madam,” said Lady Middleton, in a warning tone, “recollect what you are saying.”

Mrs. Jennings, who recollected nothing she did not choose to recollect, went on with perfect ease. “Perhaps it is to tell you that your cousin Fanny is married?”

“No, indeed, it is not.”

“Well, then, I know who it is from, Colonel. And I hope she is well.”

“Whom do you mean, ma’am?” said he, colouring faintly in spite of himself.

“Oh! you know who I mean.”

Colonel Brandon turned, as if glad to speak to any one whose civility demanded an answer. “I am particularly sorry, ma’am,” said he to Lady Middleton, “that I should receive this letter to-day, for it is on business which requires my immediate attendance in town.”

“In town!” Mrs. Jennings echoed it as if the words were an impertinence. “What can you have to do in town at this time of year?”

“My own loss is great,” he continued, “in being obliged to leave so agreeable a party; but I am the more concerned, as I fear my presence is necessary to gain your admittance at Whitwell.”

It was as though he had dropped cold water into the company’s warmest expectations. Their eager faces fell; plates and cups seemed suddenly to lose their pleasant importance.

“But if you write a note to the housekeeper, Mr. Brandon,” Marianne cried, too quick to bear the thought of surrender, “will it not be sufficient?”

He shook his head.

“We must go,” said Sir John stoutly. “It shall not be put off when we are so near it. You cannot go to town till tomorrow, Brandon—that is all.”

“I wish it could be so easily settled. But it is not in my power to delay my journey for one day.”

“If you would but let us know what your business is,” said Mrs. Jennings, “we might see whether it could be put off or not.”

“You would not be six hours later,” Willoughby urged, leaning forward with that spirited air which made persuasion seem a sort of sport, “if you were to defer your journey till our return.”

“I cannot afford to lose one hour.”

Elinor, who observed everything because she spoke so little, heard Willoughby bend towards Marianne and say in a low voice, sharpened with amusement, “There are some people who cannot bear a party of pleasure. Brandon is one of them. He was afraid of catching cold, I dare say, and invented this trick for getting out of it. I would lay fifty guineas the letter was of his own writing.”

“I have no doubt of it,” Marianne returned, her eyes bright with instant indignation, and her voice as quick as a struck match.

“There is no persuading you to change your mind, Brandon,” Sir John went on, with the familiarity of old acquaintance and the impatience of a host disappointed of his triumph, “when once you are determined on anything. But, however, I hope you will think better of it. Consider, here are the two Miss Careys come over from Newton; the three Miss Dashwoods walked up from the cottage; and Mr. Willoughby got up two hours before his usual time on purpose to go to Whitwell.”

Colonel Brandon repeated, with steady politeness, his regret at being the cause of disobliging them; but declared, with equal steadiness, that it was unavoidable.

“Well, then, when will you come back again?”

“I hope we shall see you at Barton,” Lady Middleton added, “as soon as you can conveniently leave town; and we must put off the party to Whitwell till you return.”

“You are very obliging. But it is so uncertain when I may have it in my power to return, that I dare not engage for it at all.”

“Oh! he must and shall come back,” cried Sir John. “If he is not here by the end of the week, I shall go after him.”

“Ay, so do, Sir John,” cried Mrs. Jennings, “and then perhaps you may find out what his business is.”

“I do not want to pry into other men’s concerns,” Sir John answered, with a rough honesty that did not always keep good company with discretion. “I suppose it is something he is ashamed of.”

At that moment, as if the house itself were impatient to hurry him away, Colonel Brandon’s horses were announced.

“You do not go to town on horseback, do you?” Sir John demanded.

“No. Only to Honiton. I shall then go post.”

“Well, as you are resolved to go, I wish you a good journey. But you had better change your mind.”

“I assure you it is not in my power.”

He took leave of the whole party with quiet gravity; to Elinor he spoke more than to Marianne. “Is there no chance of my seeing you and your sisters in town this winter, Miss Dashwood?”

“I am afraid, none at all.”

“Then I must bid you farewell for a longer time than I should wish to do.”

To Marianne he only bowed; not a word accompanied it, and the omission had the weight of meaning, whether meant or not.

“Come, Colonel,” cried Mrs. Jennings, advancing as though she could physically bar the door with inquisitiveness, “before you go, do let us know what you are going about.”

He wished her a good morning; and, attended by Sir John, left the room.

The instant the door closed, all the complaints that good breeding had kept simmering broke into open lamentation. Every voice had its own phrasing of the same offence—how provoking, how unlucky, how vexatious—to be so ready and so thwarted.

“I can guess what his business is, however,” said Mrs. Jennings, with an exultation that made mystery itself sound like triumph.

“Can you, ma’am?” was cried from nearly every side.

“Yes. It is about Miss Williams, I am sure.”

“And who is Miss Williams?” Marianne asked, with that frank ignorance which Mrs. Jennings always treated as an invitation.

“What! do not you know who Miss Williams is? I am sure you must have heard of her before. She is a relation of the Colonel’s, my dear—a very near relation. We will not say how near, for fear of shocking the young ladies.”

Then, lowering her voice to Elinor—though not so much that the words did not ride the room’s very air—she added, “She is his natural daughter.”

“Indeed!”

“Oh, yes; and as like him as she can stare. I dare say the Colonel will leave her all his fortune.”

When Sir John returned, he joined in the general regret with all the heartiness of a man who felt disappointment in the same strong way he felt appetite; and, concluding that as they were all assembled they must do something “by way of being happy,” proposed—after some consultation—that though happiness could only be enjoyed at Whitwell, they might at least procure a tolerable composure of mind by driving about the country.

Carriages were ordered. Willoughby’s curricle was first; and Marianne, stepping into it, looked as if the mere act of being beside him created its own weather. The wheels cut sharply through the softened gravel; the horse’s shoes struck bright notes against hidden stones; and the sound ran away through the park with them as they went, fast and laughing, out of sight.

Nothing more was seen of them until their return, which did not happen until after all the rest had already come back. They both seemed delighted with their drive, yet said only in general terms that they had kept in the lanes, while the others went on the downs.

It was settled, to crown the day that had been robbed of its first pleasure, that there should be a dance in the evening, and that everybody should be extremely merry all day long—as if mirth might be kneaded and baked by force, like a loaf.

More of the Careys came to dinner; and they had the satisfaction of sitting down nearly twenty to table—an abundance Sir John regarded with the contentment of a country gentleman who loved a full room almost as much as a full dish. The long table steamed with the honest produce of the season: joints that yielded richly beneath the knife, vegetables still tasting of earth and rain, and warm puddings sending up a sweet, heavy fragrance that softened even disappointment. The clink of glasses, the rustle of gowns, the steady pulse of talk—these were the household sounds that kept the spirit from sinking too far, a kind of acoustic comfort made of many small lives going on together.

Willoughby took his usual place between the two elder Miss Dashwoods. Mrs. Jennings sat at Elinor’s right hand; and they had not been long seated before she leaned behind Elinor and Willoughby, and said to Marianne, loudly enough for both to hear, “I have found you out in spite of all your tricks. I know where you spent the morning.”

Marianne coloured to the roots of her hair, and answered hastily, “Where, pray?”

“Did not you know,” Willoughby said, with an air of playful defiance, “that we had been out in my curricle?”

“Yes, yes, Mr. Impudence, I know that very well—and I was determined to find out where you had been to. I hope you like your house, Miss Marianne. It is a very large one, I know; and when I come to see you, I hope you will have new-furnished it, for it wanted it very much when I was there six years ago.”

Marianne turned away, confusion mixed with anger; Mrs. Jennings laughed heartily, delighted by the little sting she had managed to place under so much merriment.

Elinor, who could not laugh when she felt uneasiness, soon learned how Mrs. Jennings had armed herself with certainty: in her resolution to know where they had been, she had sent her own woman to enquire of Willoughby’s groom, and had thus been told that they had gone to Allenham, and spent a considerable time walking about the garden and going all over the house.

Elinor could hardly believe it. It seemed improbable, not merely because the thing was bold, but because it must have required, on Marianne’s side, a blindness to common opinion which Elinor—who heard, in every drawing-room and every lane, the subtle hum of what people would say—could not imagine in any one she loved.

As soon as they left the dining-room, Elinor drew Marianne a little apart and asked her about it. Her surprise grew only stronger when she found every circumstance Mrs. Jennings had repeated was true. Marianne was quite angry that Elinor could doubt her.

“Why should you imagine, Elinor, that we did not go there, or that we did not see the house? Is not it what you have often wished to do yourself?”

“Yes, Marianne—but I would not go while Mrs. Smith was there, and with no other companion than Mr. Willoughby.”

“Mr. Willoughby, however, is the only person who can have a right to show that house; and as he went in an open carriage it was impossible to have any other companion. I never spent a pleasanter morning in my life.”

“I am afraid,” Elinor replied, trying to keep tenderness steady against alarm, “that the pleasantness of an employment does not always evince its propriety.”

“On the contrary, nothing can be a stronger proof of it, Elinor; for if there had been any real impropriety in what I did, I should have been sensible of it at the time—for we always know when we are acting wrong; and with such a conviction I could have had no pleasure.”

“But, my dear Marianne—since it has already exposed you to some very impertinent remarks—do you not now begin to doubt the discretion of your own conduct?”

“If the impertinent remarks of Mrs. Jennings are to be the proof of impropriety in conduct, we are all offending every moment of our lives. I value not her censure any more than I should do her commendation. I am not sensible of having done anything wrong in walking over Mrs. Smith’s grounds, or in seeing her house. They will one day be Mr. Willoughby’s, and—”

“If they were one day to be your own, Marianne,” Elinor said gently, “you would not be justified in what you have done.”

Marianne blushed; the hint, though meant as warning, touched the very centre of her hopes, and the colour in her cheeks betrayed how sweetly it struck. For a time she was silent, as if listening inwardly to a language Elinor could not hear—a private dialect of feeling that would not translate into prudence.

After an interval—ten minutes of earnest thought, softened by youth and confidence—she returned to Elinor again with a sudden good humour, as if she had conceded enough to be generous without surrendering anything dear.

“Perhaps, Elinor, it was rather ill-judged in me to go to Allenham; but Mr. Willoughby wanted particularly to show me the place—and it is a charming house, I assure you.”
