And then, as if the very rooms were printed on her senses, she began to describe it with the eager exactness of one who has walked through a building and carried away its air upon her clothes: “There is one remarkably pretty sitting-room upstairs; of a nice, comfortable size for constant use—and with modern furniture it would be delightful. It is a corner room, and has windows on two sides. On one side you look across the bowling-green, behind the house, to a beautiful hanging wood; and on the other you have a view of the church and village, and beyond them those fine bold hills that we have so often admired. I did not see it to advantage, for nothing could be more forlorn than the furniture—but if it were newly fitted up—a couple of hundred pounds, Willoughby says, would make it one of the pleasantest summer-rooms in England.”

Had Elinor been allowed to listen without interruption, Marianne would have gone on from room to room, as if pacing the house again in her mind; and perhaps she would have been the happier for speaking, while Elinor—hearing under every pretty particular the louder, harsher noise of the world’s judgement—could only wish that delight itself did not so often carry, hidden within it, the seeds of future pain.

The abrupt ending of Colonel Brandon’s stay at the Park—so neatly cut off, so firmly sealed up behind the steadiness of his manner—set Mrs. Jennings to wondering as a kettle sets itself to sing the moment it feels heat. For two or three days her mind went on at it, never quite boiling over into certainty, yet never cooling into sense. She had that lively appetite for the movements of her acquaintance which some people reserve for dinner; and having once tasted a mystery, she could not leave it untasted.

“Something very melancholy must be the matter, I am sure,” she insisted, seasoning every sentence with a new conjecture, as if she were trying sauces upon the same plain joint. “I could see it in his face. Poor man! I am afraid his circumstances may be bad. Delaford was never reckoned above two thousand a year, and his brother left things sadly involved—sadly! I do think he has been sent for about money matters, for what else can it be? I would give anything to know the truth. Perhaps it is Miss Williams—and, by the bye, I dare say it is, because he looked so conscious when I mentioned her. May be she is ill in town; nothing more likely, for I have a notion she is always rather sickly. I would lay any wager it is about Miss Williams. Yet, it is not so very likely he should be distressed in his circumstances now, for he is prudent, and must have cleared the estate by this time. I wonder what it can be! May be his sister is worse at Avignon, and has sent for him over. His setting off in such a hurry seems very like it. Well, I wish him out of all his trouble with all my heart—and a good wife into the bargain.”

Thus Mrs. Jennings talked, her opinions shifting with each fresh guess, as a cook’s hand will alter its measures—more of this, less of that—till the whole bowl becomes a new thing, though the ingredients remain much the same.

Elinor, though sincerely concerned for Colonel Brandon’s welfare, could not give her whole attention to the matter, nor lend her imagination to the same restless speculation. The circumstance, in her judgement, did not justify so long a ferment. Her mind was already occupied—more quietly, but more deeply—by another silence far more extraordinary.

It was the silence of Marianne and Willoughby.

That they should know this subject must interest their family—that it could not but touch upon their common life—was obvious. Yet day after day went by, and not one frank word acknowledged what their every look, their every tone, their every habitual turn toward each other proclaimed. Their attachment did not merely exist; it spoke continually in the smallest domestic acts, like a savour that rises from the hearth and will not be denied. And still, on the very point where words would have been simplest, there was nothing.

Elinor tried to reason with it. She could easily imagine that marriage might not be immediately within their power; though Willoughby was independent, there was no certain proof that he was rich. Sir John had rated his estate at six or seven hundred a year; and yet Willoughby lived with a freedom of expense that such an income could hardly satisfy, and he had himself often complained—half in jest, half in earnest—of his poverty. But secrecy—this particular, needless, almost theatrical sort of secrecy—seemed to her wholly unaccountable. It concealed nothing, and yet it affected concealment. It was so unlike the open ardour of Marianne, so unlike the confident animation of Willoughby, that sometimes—only sometimes—a doubt entered Elinor’s mind: were they in truth engaged? And that doubt, slight as it was, checked her from questioning Marianne, for she would not draw from her sister either a confession prematurely forced, or an explanation that might wound.

In every other respect, Willoughby’s behaviour was as expressive of attachment as a man could make it. To Marianne, he brought all the distinguishing tenderness his heart could supply—an attention that had not only passion in it, but that delighted, with a lover’s vanity, in anticipating her wishes. To Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor he was affectionate as a son and brother: quick to perceive, eager to oblige, pleased with their little habits, and laying himself out to be necessary. The cottage appeared to have become his home—not by any right of possession, but by that stronger right with which the heart occupies a place. Many more hours were spent there than at Allenham; and if no general engagement collected them at the Park, the exercise that called him out in the morning was almost certain to end at Barton, where the rest of the day would be passed—he at Marianne’s side, his favourite pointer stretched contentedly at her feet, the room warmed by talk and by that comfortable stir of small domestic life.

One evening in particular, about a week after Colonel Brandon had left the country, Willoughby’s spirits seemed unusually unguarded, his feelings more ready to pour themselves out upon whatever lay nearest. The house was gathered as usual; the air held that mixed perfume of woodsmoke, candles, and the faint, homely trace of supper cleared away—warm bread and tea-leaves, a ghost of butter, a lingering sweetness from whatever preserve had been set out. Outside, the wind made a low, constant sound through the bare branches, and now and then a gust pushed at the cottage as if to listen; within, the crackle of the fire answered it. It was the sort of soundscape that steadies the mind without one’s knowing it—natural, various, and never harsh; unlike those louder, harder noises Elinor had sometimes heard in towns, where wheels, hammers, and shrill calls seemed to grind down thought itself and leave one’s nerves raw, as if the world had mistaken clamour for life.

It was then that Mrs. Dashwood happened to mention, with her usual cheerful confidence, her design of improving the cottage in the spring. She spoke of it as one might speak of adding a little spice to an old family dish—something to make it yet more agreeable, yet still the same.

Willoughby started at the very word.

“What!” he exclaimed. “Improve this dear cottage! No. That I will never consent to. Not a stone must be added to its walls, not an inch to its size, if my feelings are regarded.”

“Do not be alarmed,” said Elinor, quietly, “nothing of the kind will be done; for my mother will never have money enough to attempt it.”

“I am heartily glad of it,” he cried. “May she always be poor, if she can employ her riches no better.”

“Thank you, Willoughby,” Mrs. Dashwood returned, laughing, yet touched. “But you may be assured that I would not sacrifice one sentiment of local attachment of yours—or of any one whom I loved—for all the improvements in the world. Depend upon it, whatever unemployed sum may remain when I make up my accounts in the spring, I would even rather lay it uselessly by than dispose of it in a manner so painful to you. But are you really so attached to this place as to see no defect in it?”

“I am,” said he. “To me it is faultless. Nay, more, I consider it as the only form of building in which happiness is attainable; and were I rich enough I would instantly pull Combe down, and build it up again in the exact plan of this cottage.”

“With dark narrow stairs and a kitchen that smokes, I suppose,” said Elinor.

“Yes,” he cried, in the same eager tone, as if even inconvenience, when allied to affection, became dear. “With all and every thing belonging to it—in no one convenience or inconvenience about it should the least variation be perceptible. Then, and then only, under such a roof, I might perhaps be as happy at Combe as I have been at Barton.”

“I flatter myself,” Elinor replied, “that even under the disadvantage of better rooms and a broader staircase, you will hereafter find your own house as faultless as you now do this.”

“There certainly are circumstances,” said Willoughby, “which might greatly endear it to me; but this place will always have one claim of my affection which no other can possibly share.”

Mrs. Dashwood looked with pleasure at Marianne. Marianne’s eyes, bright and fixed, were turned toward him with an expression that needed no interpreter; she understood him, and, perhaps more dangerous still, she believed herself understood in return.

“How often did I wish,” he went on, “when I was at Allenham this time twelvemonth, that Barton cottage were inhabited! I never passed within view of it without admiring its situation, and grieving that no one should live in it. How little did I then think that the very first news I should hear from Mrs. Smith, when I next came into the country, would be that Barton cottage was taken; and I felt an immediate satisfaction and interest in the event which nothing but a kind of prescience of what happiness I should experience from it can account for. Must it not have been so, Marianne?” He spoke the last words lower, for her alone, as if he were placing something delicate into her hands—something meant to be felt rather than repeated.

Then, returning at once to his former tone, he addressed Mrs. Dashwood again. “And yet this house you would spoil? You would rob it of its simplicity by imaginary improvement! And this dear parlour, in which our acquaintance first began, and in which so many happy hours have been since spent by us together—you would degrade it to the condition of a common entrance, and everybody would be eager to pass through the room which has hitherto contained within itself more real accommodation and comfort than any other apartment of the handsomest dimensions in the world could possibly afford.”

Mrs. Dashwood again assured him that no alteration of the kind should be attempted.

“You are a good woman,” he replied warmly. “Your promise makes me easy. Extend it a little farther, and it will make me happy. Tell me that not only your house will remain the same, but that I shall ever find you and yours as unchanged as your dwelling; and that you will always consider me with the kindness which has made everything belonging to you so dear to me.”

The promise was readily given; and Willoughby’s manner through the whole of the evening declared at once his affection and his happiness—so overflowing, so certain, that it seemed as natural and inevitable as the fire’s warmth in the room, as the steady undertone of wind outside that made the cottage feel, by contrast, the safer and more intimate.

“Shall we see you tomorrow to dinner?” said Mrs. Dashwood, when at last he rose to go. “I do not ask you to come in the morning, for we must walk to the Park, to call on Lady Middleton.”

He engaged to be with them by four o’clock; and with that assurance, and with Marianne’s eyes following him to the door, the evening closed as such evenings often do—quietly, familiarly—while, beneath the ordinary sounds of a household settling itself, Elinor’s mind returned to its uneasiness: the strange silence that still held its own, like an unspoken word that will not translate itself into any language at all.

Mrs. Dashwood’s visit to Lady Middleton was made the next day, and she took with her two of her daughters. Marianne, with a carelessness in the excuse that would have deceived no one who did not wish to be deceived, pleaded some little employment at home; and her mother—who, hearing in every pause of the previous evening a promise that had not been spoken aloud, concluded that Willoughby would call while they were absent—left her behind with a mind perfectly at ease, as if she had laid a warm loaf under a clean cloth and expected to find it still rising on her return.

The morning at Barton had its accustomed sounds before they went: the scuff of a broom at the flags, the scrape of the kettle-lid, the low talk of a maid at the dairy door, and the larks outside, stitching their thin music into the air above the fields. In such a place the ear is taught to be content; no shriek of wheels upon a paved street, no iron din, but only the sober noises that keep a household and a little farm in order. Mrs. Dashwood, who loved that quiet industry, thought it the best of auguries that Marianne should remain where every familiar creak and chirp might soothe her till Willoughby came.

On returning from the park, however, they found Willoughby’s curricle and servant waiting at the cottage. Mrs. Dashwood’s confidence was instantly confirmed; this, she thought, was the very picture she had imagined—Marianne within, the visitor arrived, the house holding its breath to keep their happiness from spilling out into the lane.

So far it was all as she had foreseen; but on entering the house she saw what no foresight had taught her to expect.

They were scarcely in the passage when Marianne came hastily out of the parlour, not with the light, quick step that had lately seemed to carry her like a tune, but with the broken hurry of one fleeing her own heart. Her handkerchief was pressed to her eyes; her whole face, even through the linen, told of violent affliction; and without noticing either her mother or sisters, she rushed up-stairs, as if the very boards might accuse her of feeling too much.

Surprised and alarmed, they went directly into the room she had quitted. There they found only Willoughby. He stood leaning against the mantel-piece, his back turned towards them, as if he wished the fire—cold though it was—might burn away the necessity of speaking. At their entrance he turned; and his countenance, which had so often been all animation and easy tenderness, was now strained, and showed that he shared, strongly and unwillingly, the emotion that had overpowered Marianne.

“Is anything the matter with her?” cried Mrs. Dashwood, in the instant fear with which a mother reads grief in a child’s flight. “Is she ill?”

“I hope not,” he replied, striving after cheerfulness as a man might strive to whistle in a storm; and with a forced smile presently added, “It is I who may rather expect to be ill—for I am now suffering under a very heavy disappointment!”

“Disappointment?”

“Yes; for I am unable to keep my engagement with you. Mrs. Smith has this morning exercised the privilege of riches upon a poor dependent cousin, by sending me on business to London. I have just received my dispatches, and taken my farewell of Allenham; and by way of exhilaration I am now come to take my farewell of you.”

“To London!—and are you going this morning?”

“Almost this moment.”

The words struck Mrs. Dashwood with a shock that seemed to hush the very cottage. Even the little domestic sounds—some stirring in the scullery, the faint clink of a spoon, the distant complaint of a hinge—appeared indecently loud against so sudden a sentence.

“This is very unfortunate. But Mrs. Smith must be obliged;—and her business will not detain you from us long, I hope.”

He coloured as he replied, “You are very kind, but I have no idea of returning into Devonshire immediately. My visits to Mrs. Smith are never repeated within the twelvemonth.”

“And is Mrs. Smith your only friend? Is Allenham the only house in the neighbourhood to which you will be welcome? For shame, Willoughby, can you wait for an invitation here?”

His colour increased; and, with his eyes fixed on the ground, he only said, “You are too good.”

Mrs. Dashwood looked at Elinor with surprise; Elinor felt equal amazement. For a few moments no one spoke. It was a silence in which the smallest household note—the wind worrying the casement, the faint creak of a settling rafter—seemed to press upon the mind, as if nature herself listened and found no comfort.

Mrs. Dashwood first recovered voice. “I have only to add, my dear Willoughby, that at Barton Cottage you will always be welcome; for I will not press you to return here immediately, because you only can judge how far that might be pleasing to Mrs. Smith; and on this head I shall be no more disposed to question your judgment than to doubt your inclination.”

“My engagements at present,” replied Willoughby, confusedly, “are of such a nature—that—I dare not flatter myself—” He stopped, as if each syllable were a morsel he could neither swallow nor set down.

Mrs. Dashwood was too much astonished to speak. Another pause succeeded—one of those pauses that lengthen like shadows at evening, when the light has not yet gone and yet nothing looks as it did.

“It is folly to linger in this manner,” he said at last, with a faint smile that belonged to habit rather than to happiness. “I will not torment myself any longer by remaining among friends whose society it is impossible for me now to enjoy.”

He then hastily took leave of them all and left the room. They saw him step into his carriage; and in a minute it was out of sight, the sound of the wheels sinking quickly into the soft country distance, until the lane had swallowed it as the sea swallows a ship.

Mrs. Dashwood felt too much for speech, and instantly quitted the parlour to give way, alone, to the concern and alarm which this sudden departure occasioned.

Elinor’s uneasiness was at least equal to her mother’s; but, being Elinor, it did not spend itself in tears at once. It arranged itself into thoughts, as careful hands arrange provisions—each item examined, weighed, and found wanting. Willoughby’s embarrassment, his affectation of cheerfulness, and above all his unwillingness to accept her mother’s invitation—so unlike a lover, so unlike himself—disturbed her deeply. One moment she feared that no serious design had ever been formed on his side; the next, that some unfortunate quarrel had taken place between him and her sister. The distress in which Marianne had fled the room was such as a serious quarrel could most reasonably account for; and yet, when Elinor considered what Marianne’s love for him was, a quarrel seemed almost impossible. But whatever the particulars of their separation, her sister’s affliction was indubitable; and Elinor thought, with the tenderest compassion, of that violent sorrow which Marianne was probably not merely giving way to for relief, but feeding and encouraging as a duty—like a fire cherished, though it burns the very hands that tend it.

In about half an hour Mrs. Dashwood returned; and though her eyes were red, her countenance was not uncheerful. She sat down to her work with an air as if she meant to stitch some firmness into the day.

“Our dear Willoughby is now some miles from Barton, Elinor,” said she, “and with how heavy a heart does he travel!”

“It is all very strange. So suddenly to be gone! It seems but the work of a moment. And last night he was with us so happy, so cheerful, so affectionate—and now, after only ten minutes’ notice—gone too without intending to return! Something more than what he owned to us must have happened. He did not speak—he did not behave like himself. You must have seen the difference as well as I. What can it be? Can they have quarrelled? Why else should he have shown such unwillingness to accept your invitation here?”

“It was not inclination that he wanted, Elinor; I could plainly see that. He had not the power of accepting it. I have thought it all over, I assure you, and I can perfectly account for every thing that at first seemed strange to me as well as to you.”

“Can you, indeed!”

“Yes. I have explained it to myself in the most satisfactory way;—but you, Elinor, who love to doubt where you can, it will not satisfy you, I know; but you shall not talk me out of my trust in it. I am persuaded that Mrs. Smith suspects his regard for Marianne, disapproves of it (perhaps because she has other views for him), and on that account is eager to get him away;—and that the business which she sends him off to transact is invented as an excuse to dismiss him. This is what I believe to have happened. He is, moreover, aware that she does disapprove the connection; he dares not therefore at present confess to her his engagement with Marianne; and he feels himself obliged, from his dependent situation, to give into her schemes, and absent himself from Devonshire for a while. You will tell me, I know, that this may or may not have happened; but I will listen to no cavil, unless you can point out any other method of understanding the affair as satisfactory as this. And now, Elinor, what have you to say?”

“Nothing, for you have anticipated my answer.”

“Then you would have told me that it might or might not have happened. Oh, Elinor, how incomprehensible are your feelings! You had rather take evil upon credit than good. You had rather look out for misery for Marianne, and guilt for poor Willoughby, than an apology for the latter. You are resolved to think him blameable, because he took leave of us with less affection than his usual behaviour has shown. And is no allowance to be made for inadvertence, or for spirits depressed by recent disappointment? Are no probabilities to be accepted merely because they are not certainties? Is nothing due to the man whom we have all such reason to love, and no reason in the world to think ill of? To the possibility of motives unanswerable in themselves, though unavoidably secret for a while? And, after all, what is it you suspect him of?”

Elinor, who heard in her mother’s warmth not reproach alone but the desperate wish to keep the household’s harmony from being shattered, answered with as much steadiness as she could command.

“I can hardly tell myself. But suspicion of something unpleasant is the inevitable consequence of such an alteration as we just witnessed in him. There is great truth, however, in what you have now urged of the allowances which ought to be made for him, and it is my wish to be candid in my judgment of every body. Willoughby may undoubtedly have very sufficient reasons for his conduct, and I will hope that he has. But it would have been more like Willoughby to acknowledge them at once. Secrecy may be advisable; but still I cannot help wondering at its being practised by him.”

“Do not blame him, however, for departing from his character where the deviation is necessary. But you really do admit the justice of what I have said in his defence?—I am happy—and he is acquitted.”

“Not entirely. It may be proper to conceal their engagement (if they are engaged) from Mrs. Smith—and if that is the case, it must be highly expedient for Willoughby to be but little in Devonshire at present. But this is no excuse for their concealing it from us.”

“Concealing it from us! my dear child, do you accuse Willoughby and Marianne of concealment? This is strange indeed, when your eyes have been reproaching them every day for incautiousness.”

“I want no proof of their affection,” said Elinor; “but of their engagement I do.”

“I am perfectly satisfied of both.”

“Yet not a syllable has been said to you on the subject, by either of them.”

“I have not wanted syllables where actions have spoken so plainly. Has not his behaviour to Marianne, and to all of us, for at least the last fortnight, declared that he loved and considered her as his future wife, and that he felt for us the attachment of the nearest relation? Have we not perfectly understood each other? Has not my consent been daily asked by his looks, his manner, his attentive and affectionate respect? My Elinor, is it possible to doubt their engagement? How could such a thought occur to you? How is it to be supposed that Willoughby, persuaded as he must be of your sister’s love, should leave her, and leave her perhaps for months, without telling her of his affection;—that they should part without a mutual exchange of confidence?”

“I confess,” replied Elinor, “that every circumstance except one is in favour of their engagement; but that one is the total silence of both on the subject, and with me it almost outweighs every other.”

“How strange this is! You must think wretchedly indeed of Willoughby, if, after all that has openly passed between them, you can doubt the nature of the terms on which they are together. Has he been acting a part in his behaviour to your sister all this time? Do you suppose him really indifferent to her?”

“No, I cannot think that. He must and does love her, I am sure.”

“But with a strange kind of tenderness, if he can leave her with such indifference, such carelessness of the future, as you attribute to him.”

“You must remember, my dear mother, that I have never considered this matter as certain. I have had my doubts, I confess; but they are fainter than they were, and they may soon be entirely done away. If we find they correspond, every fear of mine will be removed.”

“A mighty concession indeed! If you were to see them at the altar, you would suppose they were going to be married. Ungracious girl! But I require no such proof. Nothing in my opinion has ever passed to justify doubt; no secrecy has been attempted; all has been uniformly open and unreserved. You cannot doubt your sister’s wishes. It must be Willoughby therefore whom you suspect. But why? Is he not a man of honour and feeling? Has there been any inconsistency on his side to create alarm? Can he be deceitful?”

“I hope not, I believe not,” cried Elinor. “I love Willoughby, sincerely love him; and suspicion of his integrity cannot be more painful to yourself than to me. It has been involuntary, and I will not encourage it. I was startled, I confess, by the alteration in his manners this morning;—he did not speak like himself, and did not return your kindness with any cordiality. But all this may be explained by such a situation of his affairs as you have supposed. He had just parted from my sister, had seen her leave him in the greatest affliction; and if he felt obliged, from a fear of offending Mrs. Smith, to resist the temptation of returning here soon, and yet aware that by declining your invitation, by saying that he was going away for some time, he should seem to act an ungenerous, a suspicious part by our family, he might well be embarrassed and disturbed. In such a case, a plain and open avowal of his difficulties would have been more to his honour, I think, as well as more consistent with his general character;—but I will not raise objections against any one’s conduct on so illiberal a foundation as a difference in judgment from myself, or a deviation from what I may think right and consistent.”

“You speak very properly. Willoughby certainly does not deserve to be suspected. Though we have not known him long, he is no stranger in this part of the world; and who has ever spoken to his disadvantage? Had he been in a situation to act independently and marry immediately, it might have been odd that he should leave us without acknowledging everything to me at once: but this is not the case. It is an engagement in some respects not prosperously begun, for their marriage must be at a very uncertain distance; and even secrecy, as far as it can be observed, may now be very advisable.”

They were interrupted by Margaret’s entrance, bringing with her a bustle of childish movement and a question that belonged to another world. For a moment the household resumed its ordinary business: the click of scissors, the soft tear of thread, the whisper of a drawer shut too carefully. Elinor, released from the necessity of answering, was left to turn over her mother’s reasoning as one turns over loaves to see which has browned best—acknowledging the probability of many points, and hoping for the justice of all.

They saw nothing of Marianne till dinner time. When she came in, she took her place at the table without speaking. Her eyes were red and swollen, and it seemed as if her tears were even then restrained with difficulty, like water held back by a trembling hand. She avoided all their looks; she could neither eat nor speak. The dishes—however wholesome, however carefully contrived by a country cook who knew the season’s produce and the proper saving of every scrap—might as well have been set before a stone. To Marianne, food had lost its language; it could not translate comfort into the palate, nor persuade her body to accept what her mind rejected.

After some time, on her mother’s silently pressing her hand with tender compassion, the small degree of fortitude she had brought to the table was quite overcome. She burst into tears and left the room.

This violent oppression of spirits continued through the whole evening. She was without any power because she was without any desire of command over herself. The slightest mention of anything relative to Willoughby overpowered her in an instant; and though her family were most anxiously attentive to her comfort, it was impossible for them—if they spoke at all—to keep clear of every subject which her feelings connected with him. Even the innocent sounds of the cottage seemed altered to Elinor’s ear: the very quiet that had been their blessing now felt like a space in which grief could echo without end, while far away, in the direction of London, there lay the imagined roar of a city—wheels, voices, and iron—ready to swallow a young man’s promises as readily as it swallowed his carriage.

Marianne would have believed herself unpardonable if she had slept at all on that first night after Willoughby’s departure. Rest would have seemed a kind of treachery—as if her body could grow calm while her heart was in uproar. Yet no such composure threatened her. The house at Allenham lay quiet under its own familiar sounds—the settling of old timbers, the faint rasp of wind in hedgerow thorns, the far-off, human-small barking of a dog—and she heard them all too clearly, as one hears in illness the ticking of a clock like an accusation. She did not close her eyes. She wept until the tears themselves felt like the only sustenance she could bear, and when morning came, it brought no relief: only a headache, a voice she could not command, and a refusal—stubborn, almost ceremonial—to take nourishment.

Her mother and sisters suffered with her at every moment. They pressed tea upon her, and toasted bread, and the smallest persuasion—“Only a little, Marianne”—as if warmth and sweetness might coax the world back into order. She turned her face away from the steam of the cup, from the buttery scent that would usually have tempted her, and would not be comforted. It was not that she did not feel their love; it was that her grief held her so entirely that even kindness seemed an intrusion on her devotion to what she had lost.

When breakfast ended, she escaped. She went out alone and wandered through the village of Allenham as if its lanes might still contain the echo of his step. The world went on with its ordinary labours. Doors opened; pails clinked; a cart-wheel complained over stones; somewhere a woman shook out linen, and the dry slap of cloth against cloth sounded like a brisk rebuke to Marianne’s ruin. To her it all felt indecently alive. She walked where she and Willoughby had walked, and each familiar turn of hedge and gate brought back a remembered happiness so sharp it became pain. She cried openly, careless of who might see. She indulged her recollections as if they were a sacred duty, and the morning slipped away in that worship of the past.

The evening did not soften her; it only changed the instruments of her grief. She returned to music with the same fierce appetite with which she had rejected food. She sat at the pianoforte and played every air she had played for Willoughby, every song where their voices had once met and braided together. The keys under her fingers were cool at first, then warmed; her own breath was unsteady. She would sing a line and break; begin again; dissolve again. And between the pieces she sat staring at the sheets of music he had copied out for her—his notes, his handwriting—till her eyes could no longer make out the lines and she had wrung from her heart all the additional sadness it could yield in one sitting. The next day she would return to it, and the next—grief, like a hungry guest, served and served again.

Books became another dish of misery she chose with intention. She would read nothing but what they had read together, as if a page, like a melody, could hold the very shape of a former hour; and each passage, once shared, now felt like a language half-killed by absence—meaning surviving, but the living voice gone. It was not that the words had changed; it was that the translation of the old experience into the present would never be faithful. What she had felt with him beside her could not be rendered into solitude without loss; and she clung to that loss as proof.

Such violence could not be maintained forever. Within a few days her anguish sank into a quieter melancholy, but it did not truly leave her. She still took solitary walks and returned to her silent meditations; and from time to time, as if grief were a spring touched by a careless hand, tears would burst forth as freshly as at first.

No letter came from Willoughby. No messenger arrived; no folded paper with his hand upon it was brought in with the morning’s post. Marianne did not even seem to expect one. That, perhaps, was what troubled the others most. Mrs. Dashwood was astonished; Elinor grew uneasy again, for silence, to her, was not a romance but a fact that demanded explanation.

Mrs. Dashwood, however, could always find explanations when she wanted them—explanations that soothed her own heart, at least. “Remember, Elinor,” she said, “how very often Sir John fetches our letters himself from the post, and carries them to it. We have already agreed that secrecy may be necessary; and we must acknowledge that it could not be maintained if their correspondence were to pass through Sir John’s hands.”

Elinor could not deny the truth of this. Sir John’s friendliness had a loudness to it; it was full of cheerful intrusion, like a hunting-horn sounded too close to one’s ear. If there were a secret, it could not thrive under such noise. Still, Elinor’s mind sought a remedy as carefully as a sensible cook seeks to correct a dish gone wrong—by taste, by method, by certainty, not by wishes.

There was one way so direct, so plain, and to Elinor so preferable, that she could not help proposing it. “Why do you not ask Marianne at once,” she said, “whether she is or she is not engaged to Willoughby? From you—her mother, and so kind, so indulgent a mother—the question could not give offence. It would be the natural result of your affection. She used to be all unreserve, and to you more especially.”

“I would not ask such a question for the world,” replied Mrs. Dashwood. “Supposing it possible that they are not engaged, what distress would not such an enquiry inflict! At any rate it would be most ungenerous. I should never deserve her confidence again, after forcing from her a confession of what is meant at present to be unacknowledged to any one. I know Marianne’s heart: I know that she dearly loves me, and that I shall not be the last to whom the affair is made known, when circumstances make the revealment of it eligible. I would not attempt to force the confidence of any one—of a child much less—because a sense of duty would prevent the denial which her wishes might direct.”

Elinor thought this generosity overstretched. Marianne was young; and what Mrs. Dashwood called delicacy seemed to Elinor like leaving a pot unattended because one was too refined to stir it. She urged the matter a little farther, but in vain. Common sense, common care, common prudence—these were drowned in Mrs. Dashwood’s romantic tenderness.

For several days, Willoughby’s name was scarcely spoken before Marianne by any of her family. Sir John and Mrs. Jennings were not so considerate; their jokes and witticisms—good-humoured in intention, loud in delivery—fell like stones dropped into deep water, making fresh disturbance. Their voices were part of the neighbourhood’s constant soundscape: jovial, bustling, never content with quiet. Marianne’s suffering had no room for such noise, and yet it had to endure it.

One evening, Mrs. Dashwood, taking up a volume of Shakespeare by accident, exclaimed, “We have never finished Hamlet, Marianne; our dear Willoughby went away before we could get through it. We will put it by, that when he comes again… But it may be months, perhaps, before that happens.”

“Months!” cried Marianne, with a shock so immediate it seemed to lift her from her languor. “No—nor many weeks.”

Mrs. Dashwood was sorry for what she had said; but Elinor felt a quiet, sharp pleasure, for Marianne’s reply was so full of confidence, so suggestive of knowledge and expectation, that it promised more certainty than all Mrs. Dashwood’s comforting inventions.

About a week after Willoughby had left the country, Marianne was at last persuaded to join her sisters on their usual walk, instead of fleeing alone. Until then she had avoided companionship with an art that was almost violent. If Elinor and Margaret proposed the downs, Marianne would steal away towards the lanes; if they spoke of the valley, Marianne would climb the hills and vanish as if the landscape itself could hide her. Elinor disapproved this constant seclusion; and at length, by steady exertion, she secured Marianne’s company.

They walked along the road through the valley, and chiefly in silence. Marianne’s mind could not be governed; and Elinor, satisfied with gaining one point, would not press for more. Their footsteps made a soft, regular sound on the road—an honest, human rhythm—while above them the wind moved through late leaves and bare twigs with a thin, restless whisper. Elinor listened to it as she listened to everything now: as a measure of the world’s health. The country, at least, was not yet drowned in any harsh, manufactured roar; its noises were the proper ones—wind, birds, distant labour—sounds that belonged, in their very textures, to living.

Beyond the entrance of the valley the land became less wild, more open, though still rich. A long stretch of the road they had travelled when first coming to Barton lay before them; and reaching a point they had never happened to attain in their walks before, they stopped to look around and examine the prospect, as if by seeing their home at a new angle they might better understand their altered lives.

Among the objects in the scene they soon discovered one that moved: a man on horseback riding towards them. In a few minutes they could distinguish him to be a gentleman. In a moment after that, Marianne, seized by hope so fierce it seemed almost like rapture, cried out, “It is he; it is indeed;—I know it is!” and would have hastened to meet him.

“Indeed, Marianne,” cried Elinor, catching at her with the urgency of both caution and love, “I think you are mistaken. It is not Willoughby. The person is not tall enough for him, and has not his air.”

“He has, he has!” cried Marianne. “I am sure he has. His air, his coat, his horse. I knew how soon he would come.”

She walked eagerly on as she spoke. Elinor, almost certain it was not Willoughby, quickened her pace to keep up with her, determined at least to shield Marianne from any particular attention the village might pay to so public a display.

They were soon within thirty yards of the gentleman. Marianne looked again; and in that second, cruelly clear look, her heart seemed to drop within her. She turned abruptly and began to hurry back.

Both her sisters called to detain her; and then a third voice, almost as well known as Willoughby’s though gentler and less vivid, joined theirs in begging her to stop. Marianne turned with surprise—and saw Edward Ferrars.

He was, at that moment, the only person in the world who could be forgiven for not being Willoughby. He was the only one who could coax even a brief smile from her. She wiped her tears and smiled on him; and in the honest warmth of her affection for her sister, she forgot—briefly—her own disappointment.

Edward dismounted, gave his horse to his servant, and walked back with them toward Barton. He had come purposely to visit them. All three sisters welcomed him with cordiality, and Marianne, in particular, showed more warmth than even Elinor did—so ready was she to be grateful for any kindness not entangled with her misery.

Yet to Marianne, the meeting between Edward and Elinor was only a continuation of that unaccountable coldness she had often observed at Norland in their behaviour toward each other. On Edward’s side especially there was a lack of everything a lover should look and say. He was confused; he seemed scarcely sensible of pleasure in seeing them; he looked neither rapturous nor gay. He said little, and what he did say was forced from him by questions. He distinguished Elinor by no particular mark of affection.

Marianne watched and listened with increasing astonishment. Her mind—always quick to compare, to feel contrasts as if they were moral truths—began almost to dislike Edward. And as every feeling with her found its natural conclusion in Willoughby, her thoughts flew back to him, whose manners, whose animation, whose ready eloquence seemed now a blazing foil to Edward’s reserve.

After a short silence that followed the first enquiries, Marianne asked Edward if he came directly from London.

“No,” he said. He had been in Devonshire a fortnight.

“A fortnight!” Marianne repeated, surprised that he had been so long in the same county with Elinor and had not come sooner.

He looked rather distressed as he added that he had been staying with some friends near Plymouth.

“Have you been lately in Sussex?” Elinor asked.

“I was at Norland about a month ago.”

“And how does dear, dear Norland look?” cried Marianne.

“Dear, dear Norland,” said Elinor, with an effort at steadiness, “probably looks much as it always does at this time of the year. The woods and walks thickly covered with dead leaves.”

“Oh,” cried Marianne, “with what transporting sensation have I formerly seen them fall! How have I delighted, as I walked, to see them driven in showers about me by the wind! What feelings have they—the season, the air altogether—inspired! Now there is no one to regard them. They are seen only as a nuisance, swept hastily off, and driven as much as possible from the sight.”

“It is not every one,” said Elinor, “who has your passion for dead leaves.”

“No; my feelings are not often shared, not often understood. But sometimes they are.” As she spoke, she sank into a reverie, as if listening to some inward music no one else could hear—an unrepeatable dialect of the heart that refused to be translated into ordinary words.

Then, rousing herself, she said, “Now, Edward,” calling his attention to the prospect, “here is Barton valley. Look up to it, and be tranquil if you can. Look at those hills! Did you ever see their equals? To the left is Barton park, amongst those woods and plantations. You may see the end of the house. And there, beneath that farthest hill, which rises with such grandeur, is our cottage.”

“It is a beautiful country,” he replied; “but these bottoms must be dirty in winter.”

“How can you think of dirt, with such objects before you?”

“Because,” he answered, smiling, “among the rest of the objects before me, I see a very dirty lane.”

“How strange!” Marianne said to herself as she walked on, and the strangeness, to her, was not merely in the words but in the whole manner—how different people named the same world, how the same scene could be spoken in such unlike languages, one choosing grandeur and feeling, another choosing inconvenience and mud.

“Have you an agreeable neighbourhood here?” Edward asked. “Are the Middletons pleasant people?”

“No, not all,” Marianne answered at once; “we could not be more unfortunately situated.”

“Marianne!” Elinor cried. “How can you say so? How can you be so unjust? They are a very respectable family, Mr. Ferrars, and towards us have behaved in the friendliest manner. Have you forgot, Marianne, how many pleasant days we have owed to them?”

“No,” said Marianne, in a low voice, “nor how many painful moments.”

Elinor took no notice of that, and, directing her attention to their visitor, endeavoured to support something like conversation by talking of their present residence—its conveniences, its arrangements—drawing from Edward occasional questions and remarks. His coldness and reserve mortified her severely. She was vexed, and half angry; but she resolved to regulate her behaviour by the past rather than the present, and avoided every appearance of resentment. Whatever his manner now, he was still connected to them; and she treated him as she believed he ought to be treated—by duty, by kindness, and by a steadiness of temper that could not, like Marianne’s, be overturned by every gust of feeling.

Mrs. Dashwood was startled only for the space of a breath when Edward Ferrars was shown into the little parlour at Barton; astonishment melted at once into that frank, maternal joy which made every guest feel, not admitted, but claimed. The room itself seemed to soften to her welcome: the fire spoke in small, sap-sweet cracklings; the kettle gave its thin, companionable song; and beyond the windows the late air moved through bare branches with a hush like distant surf. Whatever shyness or chill he had brought with him—whatever stiffness learned in richer rooms and under a colder roof—could not stand long against such warmth.

Elinor, watching as she always watched—quietly, with a patience that took note of everything—had the comfort of seeing him become, by degrees, more like the Edward she had known before: his face less guarded, his attention more natural, his kindness less an effort. His affections, which had seemed of late to sleep under some weight, revived towards them all; he asked after their comfort, admired the prospect, praised with real feeling the plain convenience of their home. Yet his praise had not the easy gaiety of content. He spoke, he smiled when he must, but his spirits did not rise with the fire.

They all perceived it. Mrs. Dashwood, who could never see a young man depressed without instantly imagining the parent who had done it, sat down to dinner with indignation already prepared against selfish mothers. The meal, though simple, had in it that domestic care which turns necessity into pleasure: broth clear as amber, bread that still carried the nutty scent of its crust, a joint carved with tenderness as much as skill, and, after, a small dish of preserved fruit whose clove and sugar seemed to gather summer into one spoonful. Even Edward’s appetite, which politeness could not entirely counterfeit, appeared restrained; he tasted rather than ate, as if his mind stood between him and the plate.

When dinner was over, and they had drawn round the fire while the servants cleared away and the last scents of roast and spice slowly cooled into the air, Mrs. Dashwood leaned forward with that affectionate frankness which made questions feel like kindness.

“What are Mrs. Ferrars’s views for you at present, Edward?” said she. “Are you still to be a great orator in spite of yourself?”

“No,” he answered, and the word came out like a relief. “I hope my mother is now convinced that I have no more talents than inclination for a public life!”

“But how is your fame to be established? for famous you must be to satisfy all your family; and with no inclination for expense, no affection for strangers, no profession, and no assurance, you may find it a difficult matter.”

“I shall not attempt it. I have no wish to be distinguished; and have every reason to hope I never shall. Thank Heaven! I cannot be forced into genius and eloquence.”

“You have no ambition, I well know. Your wishes are all moderate.”

“As moderate as those of the rest of the world, I believe. I wish as well as every body else to be perfectly happy; but, like every body else, it must be in my own way. Greatness will not make me so.”

“Strange that it would!” cried Marianne, whose whole being, even at rest, seemed tuned like an instrument ready to answer any touch. “What have wealth or grandeur to do with happiness?”

“Grandeur has but little,” said Elinor, in her composed manner; “but wealth has much to do with it.”

“Elinor, for shame!” Marianne’s colour rose as quickly as flame catches kindling. “Money can only give happiness where there is nothing else to give it. Beyond a competence, it can afford no real satisfaction, as far as mere self is concerned.”

“Perhaps,” said Elinor, smiling, “we may come to the same point. Your competence and my wealth are very much alike, I dare say; and without them, as the world goes now, we shall both agree that every kind of external comfort must be wanting. Your ideas are only more noble than mine. Come, what is your competence?”

“About eighteen hundred or two thousand a year; not more than that.”

Elinor could not help laughing; it was not unkindness, only the clear sight of a sister’s splendid imagination setting up housekeeping as easily as it composed a sonnet. “Two thousand a year! One is my wealth! I guessed how it would end.”

“And yet two thousand a-year is a very moderate income,” Marianne insisted, serious as if stating a household account. “A family cannot well be maintained on a smaller. I am sure I am not extravagant in my demands. A proper establishment of servants, a carriage, perhaps two, and hunters, cannot be supported on less.”

Elinor smiled again, for the picture was drawn with such exactness that one might almost hear the wheels of those future carriages on an unseen gravel drive, and the impatient pawing of the hunters in their stalls at Combe Magna.

“Hunt—ers!” Edward repeated, and there was in his tone both amusement and honest perplexity. “But why must you have hunters? Every body does not hunt.”

Marianne coloured, and her reply came with that proud quickness which betrayed how much she took the customs of her own imagined circle for the laws of nature. “But most people do.”

Margaret, who had been listening with the eager eye of childhood that turns talk into game, struck out a thought with the boldness of a wish. “I wish that somebody would give us all a large fortune apiece!”

“Oh that they would!” cried Marianne; her eyes brightened, her cheeks glowed, and for a moment the room seemed fuller of light, as if the very fire leapt in agreement with so intoxicating an idea.

“We are all unanimous in that wish, I suppose,” said Elinor, “in spite of the insufficiency of wealth.”

“Oh dear!” Margaret cried, already half-drunk on possibility. “How happy I should be! I wonder what I should do with it!”

Marianne looked as though she had never doubted what she would do: purchase beauty, reward genius, and make the world echo with music.

“I should be puzzled to spend so large a fortune myself,” said Mrs. Dashwood, with a laugh that carried no bitterness, “if my children were all to be rich without my help.”

“You must begin your improvements on this house,” observed Elinor; “and your difficulties will soon vanish.”

“What magnificent orders would travel from this family to London,” said Edward, and for a moment he rallied, his fancy warming like hands held to flame. “What a happy day for booksellers, music-sellers, and print-shops! You, Miss Dashwood, would give a general commission for every new print of merit to be sent you—and as for Marianne, I know her greatness of soul, there would not be music enough in London to content her. And books!—Thomson, Cowper, Scott—she would buy them all over and over again: she would buy up every copy, I believe, to prevent their falling into unworthy hands; and she would have every book that tells her how to admire an old twisted tree. Should not you, Marianne? Forgive me, if I am very saucy. But I was willing to show you that I had not forgot our old disputes.”

“I love to be reminded of the past, Edward—whether it be melancholy or gay, I love to recall it—and you will never offend me by talking of former times.” Her voice, softened by sincerity, seemed to settle the room into an attentive quiet; even the wind outside sounded, for a moment, like it was listening. “You are very right in supposing how my money would be spent—some of it, at least—my loose cash would certainly be employed in improving my collection of music and books.”

“And the bulk of your fortune would be laid out in annuities on the authors or their heirs.”

“No, Edward, I should have something else to do with it.”

“Perhaps, then, you would bestow it as a reward on that person who wrote the ablest defence of your favourite maxim, that no one can ever be in love more than once in their life—your opinion on that point is unchanged, I presume?”

“Undoubtedly. At my time of life opinions are tolerably fixed. It is not likely that I should now see or hear any thing to change them.”

“Marianne is as steadfast as ever, you see,” said Elinor; and though she spoke lightly, there was in her look a meaning that did not ask to be read aloud. “She is not at all altered.”

“She is only grown a little more grave than she was.”

“Nay, Edward,” said Marianne, turning upon him with a kind of fearless justice, “you need not reproach me. You are not very gay yourself.”

“Why should you think so!” he replied, and a sigh escaped him, as involuntary as steam from a kettle-lid. “But gaiety never was a part of my character.”

“Nor do I think it a part of Marianne’s,” said Elinor. “I should hardly call her a lively girl—she is very earnest, very eager in all she does—sometimes talks a great deal and always with animation—but she is not often really merry.”

“I believe you are right,” he returned; “and yet I have always set her down as a lively girl.”

“I have frequently detected myself in such kind of mistakes,” said Elinor, and her tone, though gentle, carried the precision of an inward habit—of weighing, naming, and keeping truth clean. “In a total misapprehension of character in some point or other: fancying people so much more gay or grave, or ingenious or stupid than they really are, and I can hardly tell why or in what the deception originated. Sometimes one is guided by what they say of themselves, and very frequently by what other people say of them, without giving oneself time to deliberate and judge.”

“But I thought it was right, Elinor,” said Marianne, her irony as bright as a sharp knife, “to be guided wholly by the opinion of other people. I thought our judgments were given us merely to be subservient to those of neighbours. This has always been your doctrine, I am sure.”

“No, Marianne, never. My doctrine has never aimed at the subjection of the understanding. All I have ever attempted to influence has been the behaviour. You must not confound my meaning. I am guilty, I confess, of having often wished you to treat our acquaintance in general with greater attention; but when have I advised you to adopt their sentiments or to conform to their judgment in serious matters?”

“You have not been able to bring your sister over to your plan of general civility,” said Edward to Elinor. “Do you gain no ground?”

“Quite the contrary,” replied Elinor, and her look at Marianne was expressive enough to make Margaret giggle and then check herself, as if the sound might be too loud.

“My judgment,” he returned, “is all on your side of the question; but I am afraid my practice is much more on your sister’s. I never wish to offend, but I am so foolishly shy, that I often seem negligent, when I am only kept back by my natural awkwardness. I have frequently thought that I must have been intended by nature to be fond of low company, I am so little at my ease among strangers of gentility!”

“Marianne has not shyness to excuse any inattention of hers,” said Elinor.

“She knows her own worth too well for false shame,” replied Edward. “Shyness is only the effect of a sense of inferiority in some way or other. If I could persuade myself that my manners were perfectly easy and graceful, I should not be shy.”

“But you would still be reserved,” said Marianne; and the words fell into the room with a weight quite disproportionate to their softness.

Edward started, as if she had touched a bruise. “Reserved! Am I reserved, Marianne?”

“Yes, very.”

“I do not understand you,” he replied, colouring. “Reserved!—how, in what manner? What am I to tell you? What can you suppose?”

Elinor looked surprised at his emotion; and, anxious to draw the sting from what had been said, tried to laugh it away. “Do not you know my sister well enough to understand what she means? Do not you know she calls every one reserved who does not talk as fast, and admire what she admires as rapturously as herself?”

Edward made no answer. The brief flicker of ease went out; his gravity returned upon him in its fullest extent, as surely as embers sink when no one stirs the fire. He sat for some time silent and dull, while the little domestic sounds—the settling of the hearth, the faint clink of a spoon forgotten on a saucer, the wind’s patient music outside—seemed to fill the spaces his words would not.

Elinor watched the dulness that had settled on her friend’s spirits with an uneasiness she could not season away. His coming ought to have been like the first savour of broth on a winter morning—comforting, restoring; yet the warmth of it reached her only in patches, as if some cold draught kept slipping in through a crack she could not find. He was attentive, he was present, and still he seemed not wholly there. One moment, a look—quick, bright, almost tender—promised the old distinction; the next, his manner drew back, reserved and careful, contradicting what his eyes had nearly confessed. If affection remained, it moved in him like a note half-muted beneath too much noise.

The next morning he came down early and joined them in the breakfast-room before the rest of the household had stirred. The cottage was quiet in the way of a place that still remembered fields: the softened creak of a stair, the faint tick of the clock, and beyond the window the hedgerows holding the wind as a sieve holds flour. Mrs. Dashwood’s breakfast things were in readiness—tea steaming in the pot, bread cut and set out, butter pale as primroses—so homely and exact that even Edward’s troubled air seemed out of place among such honest provisions.

Marianne, alive to every opportunity of advancing other people’s happiness, was scarcely seated before she contrived to leave them to themselves. She rose with an alacrity that had once been merely her nature and now, perhaps, her effort, and began to go upstairs; but before she was half-way, the parlour door opened behind her. She turned, expecting Elinor, and saw instead Edward come out with a hurried step.

“I am going into the village to see my horses,” said he. “As you are not yet ready for breakfast, I shall be back again presently.”

He was gone before Marianne could make sense of it; and Elinor, though she said nothing, felt the small wound of it as distinctly as if a pin had pricked her finger while slicing bread—nothing fatal, nothing dramatic, yet sharp enough to make one careful afterwards.

When he returned, his countenance had altered—not into ease, but into that forced brightness by which people try to persuade themselves. He spoke with fresh admiration of the country. In his walk, he had found vantage points in the valley; and the village, lying higher than the cottage, had given him a broad view of the whole which had exceedingly pleased him. It was a subject sure to catch Marianne, who heard landscape the way others hear music: instantly, thoroughly, with her whole being.

She began to answer him in kind, describing her own delight in the scenes around them, questioning him with eager minuteness about every particular that had struck him; but Edward, with a sudden laugh that sounded like a spoon against china—too light for the weight of the room—interrupted her.

“You must not enquire too far, Marianne—remember I have no knowledge in the picturesque, and I shall offend you by my ignorance and want of taste if we come to particulars. I shall call hills steep, which ought to be bold; surfaces strange and uncouth, which ought to be irregular and rugged; and distant objects out of sight, which ought only to be indistinct through the soft medium of a hazy atmosphere. You must be satisfied with such admiration as I can honestly give. I call it a very fine country—the hills are steep, the woods seem full of fine timber, and the valley looks comfortable and snug—with rich meadows and several neat farm-houses scattered here and there. It exactly answers my idea of a fine country, because it unites beauty with utility—and I dare say it is a picturesque one too, because you admire it; I can easily believe it to be full of rocks and promontories, grey moss and brushwood, but these are all lost on me. I know nothing of the picturesque.”

“I am afraid it is but too true,” said Marianne; “but why should you boast of it?”

Elinor, pouring the tea with the steadiness of one used to keeping things from boiling over, looked up at him. There was something in the very way he refused the language of admiration—too deliberate, too pointed—that suggested not simplicity but defence, as if he feared the commerce of words might commit him.

“I suspect,” said she, “that to avoid one kind of affectation, Edward here falls into another. Because he believes many people pretend to more admiration of the beauties of nature than they really feel, and is disgusted with such pretensions, he affects greater indifference and less discrimination in viewing them himself than he possesses. He is fastidious and will have an affectation of his own.”

“It is very true,” Marianne replied, warming, “that admiration of landscape scenery is become a mere jargon. Every body pretends to feel and tries to describe with the taste and elegance of him who first defined what picturesque beauty was. I detest jargon of every kind, and sometimes I have kept my feelings to myself, because I could find no language to describe them in but what was worn and hackneyed out of all sense and meaning.”

There was something in that—this fear of borrowed phrases, this hunger for a tongue that could still mean what it said—that made Elinor’s heart tighten. Words were power; words were also traps. To name a feeling in another person’s coin was to let them set the value.

“I am convinced,” said Edward, “that you really feel all the delight in a fine prospect which you profess to feel. But, in return, your sister must allow me to feel no more than I profess. I like a fine prospect, but not on picturesque principles. I do not like crooked, twisted, blasted trees. I admire them much more if they are tall, straight, and flourishing. I do not like ruined, tattered cottages. I am not fond of nettles or thistles, or heath blossoms. I have more pleasure in a snug farm-house than a watch-tower—and a troop of tidy, happy villagers please me better than the finest banditti in the world.”

Marianne stared at him, astonishment plain in her face; then her eyes shifted to Elinor with a sort of compassionate alarm, as if she had witnessed a beloved dish spoiled by an unexpected ingredient. Elinor, who could bear much but could not help her own perception of humour, laughed outright. Edward’s seriousness, so grave in everything that touched the heart, could be almost arch when he chose; and it was as if he had taken a knife to the inflated language of taste and pared it down to something plain and serviceable.

The subject went no farther. Marianne fell into a thoughtful silence, and the morning would have continued so, softened only by the domestic sounds—the cup set carefully on a saucer, the faint stirring of the fire—had not a new object suddenly caught her eye.

She sat beside Edward; and as Mrs. Dashwood passed him his tea, his hand moved so directly before Marianne that a ring, with a plait of hair in the centre, showed conspicuously on one of his fingers. In a moment, and with her usual ungoverned sincerity, she cried out—

“I never saw you wear a ring before, Edward. Is that Fanny’s hair? I remember her promising to give you some. But I should have thought her hair had been darker.”

The words, once spoken, could not be taken back. Marianne had uttered what she truly felt, careless of consequence; and the consequence came at once. Edward coloured deeply—so suddenly that Elinor saw the blood rise, as when heat catches on a pan too quickly. He gave a momentary glance at Elinor—an appeal, an apology, a fear—then answered, with an effort at steadiness:

“Yes; it is my sister’s hair. The setting always casts a different shade on it, you know.”

Elinor met his eye and could not pretend she had not understood. In that instant she was as satisfied as Marianne, and more wounded. The hair was her own. Marianne concluded, in her innocence, that it was a free gift from sister to brother; Elinor knew—by the plain fact that she had never given it—that it must have been procured by some theft or contrivance, done without her knowledge and therefore without her consent. The little plait, so domestic in its fashioning, so intimate in its nearness to his skin, became at once a token and an insult; not because Edward wore it, but because someone had taken from her what she had not chosen to surrender and turned it into an emblem.

Yet her temper was not in a humour to take offence as others might. She would not grant the triumph of her pain to anyone’s eyes. Affecting to notice nothing, she began instantly to talk of something else—of bread, of the weather, of the arrangement of the day—while inwardly she resolved to catch every opportunity of seeing that ring again, to satisfy herself beyond all doubt that the hair was exactly the shade of her own. It was not curiosity; it was the need to place certainty where words had been used to cloud meaning.

Edward’s embarrassment lasted some time and settled at last into an absence of mind still more fixed. He was particularly grave through the whole morning, as if every sound in the room—each murmur of teacup, each footstep—troubled him. Marianne, acute in feeling when she had once committed an error, severely censured herself for what she had said. Her remorse would have been shorter, perhaps, had she known how little offence her thoughtlessness had given Elinor compared with what Elinor had already been forced to swallow in silence.

Before midday was half spent, Sir John Middleton and Mrs. Jennings arrived, brisk and full, as people are who live amid plenty and think it a kindness to distribute bustle like extra servings. Hearing of a gentleman at the cottage, they came to survey the guest; and Sir John, aided by his mother-in-law, was not long in discovering that the name Ferrars began with an F. It promised them a mine of raillery against Elinor, which nothing but the newness of their acquaintance with Edward prevented from being sprung at once. Even so, Elinor learned, from certain significant looks—made more significant by Mrs. Jennings’s inability to keep anything silent—how far their penetration, founded on Margaret’s previous instructions, extended.

Sir John never entered the Dashwoods’ parlour without offering an invitation, as if hospitality were a dish that must be pressed upon everyone until accepted. On this occasion, eager to entertain their visitor and feeling himself bound to contribute to the amusement of the party, he insisted on engaging them for both tea and dinner.

“You must drink tea with us to-night,” said he, “for we shall be quite alone—and to-morrow you must absolutely dine with us, for we shall be a large party.”

Mrs. Jennings enforced the necessity with cheerful tyranny. “And who knows but you may raise a dance,” said she. “And that will tempt you, Miss Marianne.”

“A dance!” Marianne cried. “Impossible! Who is to dance?”

“Who! why yourselves, and the Careys, and Whitakers to be sure.—What! you thought nobody could dance because a certain person that shall be nameless is gone!”

“I wish with all my soul,” cried Sir John, “that Willoughby were among us again.”

The name, thrown into the room with such careless force, acted on Marianne like a sudden loud sound in a place that had been quiet. Colour rushed into her face. She tried to command herself, and could not.

This, and Marianne’s blush, gave new suspicions to Edward. His eyes, which had been heavy all morning, lifted quickly; he looked from the visitors to Marianne and then, leaning a little towards Elinor, asked in a low voice, by whom he was sitting, “And who is Willoughby?”

Elinor answered briefly, as briefly as truth and prudence would allow. Marianne’s countenance, however, told more than Elinor’s words. Edward saw enough, in the language she could not suppress, to comprehend not only the meaning of others but many of Marianne’s former expressions that had puzzled him. When their visitors at last left them, he went immediately round to Marianne and said, in a whisper, with a quiet, almost playful archness that yet carried the edge of knowledge—

“I have been guessing. Shall I tell you my guess?”

“What do you mean?” she said, startled.

“Shall I tell you?”

“Certainly.”

“Well then; I guess that Mr. Willoughby hunts.”

Marianne was surprised and confused; yet she could not help smiling at the manner in which he spoke—as if he had taken a great secret and reduced it to something ordinary, domestic, safe. After a moment’s silence, she said with warmth that was all her own, “Oh, Edward! How can you?—But the time will come I hope… I am sure you will like him.”

“I do not doubt it,” he replied, rather astonished at the earnestness of her assurance; for had he not imagined it to be a joke contrived for the benefit of general acquaintance—founded only on a something or a nothing between Mr. Willoughby and herself—he would not have ventured to mention it at all.

Edward stayed a week at Barton Cottage, and it seemed to Elinor that the days, like loaves left too near the hearth, rose too quickly and were taken away before anyone had properly tasted them. Mrs. Dashwood urged him, with all the warm hospitality that could turn a humble table into a festival, to remain longer; yet he held to his departure with a kind of stubborn severity, as though the very sweetness of ease among friends must be punished by an early retreat.

In the last two or three days his spirits had visibly mended. He grew fond of the little house and of the sheltered lane beyond it; he lingered by the window that looked upon the green court, as if he meant to memorize the light there; he never spoke of leaving without a sigh. He declared his time wholly disengaged, even owned himself uncertain where he should go when he left them—yet still, go he must. He repeated, again and again, that no week had ever passed so quickly; and he said other things too—so tender, so betraying—things which gave the lie to the act of quitting them. He had no pleasure at Norland; he detested town; but either to Norland or London he must go. He valued their kindness beyond anything, and his greatest happiness, he said, was in being with them. Yet he meant to be gone at the end of the week, in spite of their wishes and his own, without any apparent restraint upon his time.

Elinor, disappointed and vexed, placed whatever was astonishing in such behaviour to his mother’s account. It was a comfort, perhaps an unearned one, that Mrs. Ferrars’s character was imperfectly known to her; the unknown makes an easy vessel for every strange necessity. Still, even when Edward’s uncertainty stung her, Elinor was disposed to soften every judgement with allowances—those candid qualifications which her mother’s gentler persuasions had already taught her, to Willoughby’s advantage, at a cost.

Most often she attributed Edward’s want of openness and consistency to his want of independence, and to his better knowledge of Mrs. Ferrars’s disposition and designs. The shortness of his visit, the steadiness of his purpose in leaving them, seemed to spring from the same fettered inclination, the same inevitable need of temporizing with a parent whose will ruled like a dominant dialect, flattening every tender inflection of a son’s own desires into obedience. Duty against will; parent against child—the old grievance, as old as any winter proverb spoken by the fireside, and as difficult to translate into happiness.

She could not help wishing to know when such difficulties would cease—when this opposition would yield—when Mrs. Ferrars would be reformed, and her son be at liberty to be happy. But wishes so vain must be set aside like a dish too rich to be borne; Elinor turned for comfort to the renewal of her confidence in Edward’s affection, to every mark of regard in look or word that had fallen from him at Barton, and—most of all—to that flattering proof which he constantly wore round his finger, as if its very circle promised constancy while everything else wavered.

On the last morning, at breakfast, Mrs. Dashwood—who had the art of making even plain fare feel plentiful, and who attended to the comfort of others as carefully as she would have watched a pot from boiling over—looked at Edward with affectionate concern.

“I think, Edward,” said she, “you would be a happier man if you had any profession to engage your time and give an interest to your plans and actions. Some inconvenience to your friends, indeed, might result from it—you would not be able to give them so much of your time. But,” with a smile that tried to coax his gloom into levity, “you would be materially benefited in one particular at least—you would know where to go when you left them.”

“I do assure you,” he replied, “that I have long thought on this point, as you think now. It has been, and is, and probably will always be a heavy misfortune to me, that I have had no necessary business to engage me, no profession to give me employment, or afford me anything like independence. But unfortunately my own nicety, and the nicety of my friends, have made me what I am, an idle, helpless being. We never could agree in our choice of a profession. I always preferred the church, as I still do. But that was not smart enough for my family. They recommended the army. That was a great deal too smart for me. The law was allowed to be genteel enough; many young men, who had chambers in the Temple, made a very good appearance in the first circles, and drove about town in very knowing gigs. But I had no inclination for the law, even in this less abstruse study of it, which my family approved. As for the navy, it had fashion on its side, but I was too old when the subject was first started to enter it—and, at length, as there was no necessity for my having any profession at all, as I might be as dashing and expensive without a red coat on my back as with one, idleness was pronounced on the whole to be most advantageous and honourable, and a young man of eighteen is not in general so earnestly bent on being busy as to resist the solicitations of his friends to do nothing. I was therefore entered at Oxford and have been properly idle ever since.”

“The consequence of which, I suppose, will be,” said Mrs. Dashwood, “since leisure has not promoted your own happiness, that your sons will be brought up to as many pursuits, employments, professions, and trades as Columella’s.”

“They will be brought up,” said he, in a serious accent, “to be as unlike myself as is possible. In feeling, in action, in condition, in everything.”

“Come, come; this is all an effusion of immediate want of spirits, Edward. You are in a melancholy humour, and fancy that any one unlike yourself must be happy. But remember that the pain of parting from friends will be felt by everybody at times, whatever be their education or state. Know your own happiness. You want nothing but patience—or give it a more fascinating name, call it hope. Your mother will secure to you, in time, that independence you are so anxious for; it is her duty, and it will, it must ere long become her happiness to prevent your whole youth from being wasted in discontent. How much may not a few months do?”

“I think,” replied Edward, “that I may defy many months to produce any good to me.”

There was something in the cadence of his voice that made even the cottage seem, for a moment, too small to contain so much discouragement. The usual domestic sounds—the soft click of a spoon against china, the rustle of a dress, the distant, habitual chatter of sparrows in the hedge—went on, but felt subdued, as if the very air listened. This despondency could not be communicated to Mrs. Dashwood; her nature refused to take it in. Yet it gave additional pain to the parting which soon followed, and left an uncomfortable impression on them all—on Elinor especially—an impression that required trouble and time to subdue.

And Elinor did subdue it. She was resolved not to appear to suffer more than what all her family suffered at his going away. She did not, like Marianne on a similar occasion, seek to augment and fix sorrow by silence, solitude, and idleness. Their means were as different as their objects, and each method suited the advancement of each. As soon as Edward was out of the house, Elinor sat down to her drawing-table and made herself busy for the whole day. She neither sought nor avoided the mention of his name; she appeared to interest herself, almost as much as ever, in the general concerns of the family. If this discipline did not lessen her grief, it at least prevented its unnecessary increase, and spared her mother and sisters much solicitude on her account.

To Marianne, behaviour so exactly the reverse of her own appeared no more meritorious than her own had seemed faulty to Elinor. The business of self-command she settled very easily: with strong affections it was impossible; with calm ones it could have no merit. That Elinor’s affections were calm she dared not deny—though she blushed to acknowledge it—and of the strength of her own she gave a striking proof, by still loving and respecting that sister, in spite of so mortifying a conviction.

Yet Elinor’s day, however carefully filled, could not be made soundless within. Without shutting herself up from her family, without leaving the house in determined solitude, without lying awake all night to indulge meditation, she found every day afforded leisure enough to think of Edward, and of Edward’s behaviour, in every variety that different spirits could produce—tenderness, pity, approbation, censure, doubt.
