There were moments in abundance when, if not by the absence of her mother and sisters, at least by the nature of their employments, conversation was forbidden among them and every effect of solitude was produced. Her mind was at liberty; her thoughts could not be chained elsewhere; and the past and the future, on a subject so interesting, would force her attention, engross her memory, her reflection, and her fancy.

From one such reverie, as she sat at her drawing-table, she was roused one morning, soon after Edward’s leaving them, by the arrival of company. She happened to be quite alone. The closing of the little gate at the entrance of the green court drew her eyes to the window; she saw a large party walking up to the door. Among them were Sir John and Lady Middleton and Mrs. Jennings, but with them were two others—a gentleman and a lady—quite unknown to her.

Elinor sat near the window; and as soon as Sir John perceived her, he left the rest to the ceremony of knocking and stepped across the turf, obliging her to open the casement to speak to him, though the space between door and window was so short that anything said at one was hardly safe from being heard at the other.

“Well,” said he, “we have brought you some strangers. How do you like them?”

“Hush! they will hear you.”

“Never mind if they do. It is only the Palmers. Charlotte is very pretty, I can tell you. You may see her if you look this way.”

As Elinor was certain of seeing her in a couple of minutes without taking that liberty, she begged to be excused.

“Where is Marianne? Has she run away because we are come? I see her instrument is open.”

“She is walking, I believe.”

They were joined then by Mrs. Jennings, who had not patience enough to wait for the door to be opened before she told her story. She came hallooing to the window, breathlessly, her voice as full and bustling as a kitchen on baking-day.

“How do you do, my dear? How does Mrs. Dashwood do? And where are your sisters? What! all alone! you will be glad of a little company to sit with you. I have brought my other son and daughter to see you. Only think of their coming so suddenly! I thought I heard a carriage last night, while we were drinking our tea, but it never entered my head that it could be them. I thought of nothing but whether it might not be Colonel Brandon come back again; so I said to Sir John, I do think I hear a carriage; perhaps it is Colonel Brandon come back again—”

Elinor was obliged to turn from her, in the middle of this torrent, to receive the rest. Lady Middleton introduced the two strangers; Mrs. Dashwood and Margaret came down at the same time; and they all sat down to look at one another, while Mrs. Jennings continued her account as she walked through the passage into the parlour, attended by Sir John, her words carrying on ahead of her like the rattle of wheels on a hard road.

Mrs. Palmer was several years younger than Lady Middleton, and totally unlike her. She was short and plump, with a very pretty face and the finest expression of good humour that could possibly be. Her manners were not so elegant as her sister’s, but they were far more prepossessing. She came in with a smile, smiled all the time of her visit except when she laughed, and smiled again when she went away; her cheerfulness seemed to spread like the agreeable scent of something sweet set near the fire.

Her husband, on the contrary, was a grave-looking young man of five or six and twenty, with an air of more fashion and sense than his wife, but of less willingness to please or be pleased. He entered with a look of self-consequence, bowed slightly to the ladies without speaking a word, surveyed them and their apartments, and then took up a newspaper from the table and read it as long as he stayed, as if the printed column were the only conversation he meant to acknowledge.

Mrs. Palmer—formed by nature for being uniformly civil and happy—was hardly seated before her admiration of the parlour and everything in it burst forth.

“Well! what a delightful room this is! I never saw anything so charming! Only think, Mama, how it is improved since I was here last! I always thought it such a sweet place, ma’am!” turning to Mrs. Dashwood, “but you have made it so charming! Only look, sister, how delightful everything is! How I should like such a house for myself! Should not you, Mr. Palmer?”

Mr. Palmer made her no answer, and did not even raise his eyes from the newspaper.

“Mr. Palmer does not hear me,” said she, laughing; “he never does sometimes. It is so ridiculous!”

This was quite a new idea to Mrs. Dashwood; she had never been used to find wit in the inattention of anyone, and could not help looking with surprise at them both.

Mrs. Jennings, in the meantime, talked on as loud as she could, and continued her account of the surprise the evening before on seeing their friends, without ceasing until everything was told. Mrs. Palmer laughed heartily at the recollection, and everybody agreed—two or three times over—that it had been quite an agreeable surprise.

“You may believe how glad we all were to see them,” added Mrs. Jennings, leaning forward towards Elinor and speaking in a low voice, as if she meant to be heard by no one else, though they were seated on different sides of the room, “but, however, I can’t help wishing they had not travelled quite so fast, nor made such a long journey of it, for they came all round by London upon account of some business, for you know”—nodding significantly and pointing to her daughter—“it was wrong in her situation. I wanted her to stay at home and rest this morning, but she would come with us; she longed so much to see you all!”

Mrs. Palmer laughed, and said it would not do her any harm.

“She expects to be confined in February,” continued Mrs. Jennings.

Lady Middleton could no longer endure such a conversation, and therefore exerted herself to ask Mr. Palmer if there was any news in the paper.

“No, none at all,” he replied, and read on.

“Here comes Marianne,” cried Sir John. “Now, Palmer, you shall see a monstrous pretty girl.” He immediately went into the passage, opened the front door, and ushered her in himself, as if he were introducing a celebrated singer upon a stage.

Mrs. Jennings asked Marianne, as soon as she appeared, if she had not been to Allenham; and Mrs. Palmer laughed so heartily at the question as to show she understood it. Mr. Palmer looked up upon Marianne’s entering, stared at her some minutes, and then returned to his newspaper.

Mrs. Palmer’s eye was now caught by the drawings hung round the room. She got up to examine them.

“Oh! dear, how beautiful these are! Well! how delightful! Do but look, mama, how sweet! I declare they are quite charming; I could look at them for ever.”

And then, sitting down again, she very soon forgot there were any such things in the room.

When Lady Middleton rose to go away, Mr. Palmer rose also, laid down the newspaper, stretched himself, and looked at them all round.

“My love, have you been asleep?” said his wife, laughing.

He made her no answer; and only observed, after again examining the room, that it was very low pitched, and that the ceiling was crooked. He then made his bow and departed with the rest.

Sir John had been very urgent with them all to spend the next day at the park. Mrs. Dashwood—who did not choose to dine with them oftener than they dined at the cottage—absolutely refused on her own account; her daughters might do as they pleased. But they had no curiosity to see how Mr. and Mrs. Palmer ate their dinner, and no expectation of pleasure from them in any other way. They attempted therefore to excuse themselves; the weather was uncertain, and not likely to be good.

But Sir John would not be satisfied. The carriage should be sent for them; they must come. Lady Middleton too, though she did not press their mother, pressed them. Mrs. Jennings and Mrs. Palmer joined their entreaties; all seemed equally anxious to avoid a family party; and the young ladies were obliged to yield.

“Why should they ask us?” said Marianne, as soon as they were gone. “The rent of this cottage is said to be low; but we have it on very hard terms, if we are to dine at the park whenever any one is staying either with them, or with us.”

“They mean no less to be civil and kind to us now,” said Elinor, “by these frequent invitations, than by those which we received from them a few weeks ago. The alteration is not in them, if their parties are grown tedious and dull. We must look for the change elsewhere.”

The next day the Miss Dashwoods were admitted to the Park by a door that sighed on its hinges with the damp, and were hardly within the drawing-room before another door flew open as if the weather itself had pushed it—Mrs. Palmer, bright as a robin in rain, came running in, cheeks warmed, eyes shining, her laughter already a kind of music that made the room feel less chilled.

She caught them all by the hand as though she meant to keep them from being carried off by the draughts, and placed herself between Elinor and Marianne with the decision of one who would season a dish properly, no matter who protested.

“I am so glad to see you!—because it is such a bad day I was afraid you might not come, which would be shocking, for we go away again tomorrow. We must go, for the Westons come to us next week, you know. It was quite sudden our coming at all—I knew nothing of it till the carriage was coming to the door, and then Mr. Palmer asked me if I would go with him to Barton. He is so droll! He never tells me any thing!—I am so sorry we cannot stay longer; however we shall meet again in town very soon, I hope.”

They were obliged—gently, firmly—to put an end to so forward a hope.

“Not go to town!” cried Mrs. Palmer, laughing as if London were a warm kitchen and the country only a larder one visited for supplies. “I shall be quite disappointed if you do not. I could get the nicest house in the world for you, next door to ours, in Hanover-square. You must come indeed. I am sure I shall be very happy to chaperon you at any time till I am confined, if Mrs. Dashwood should not like to go into public.”

They thanked her, and resisted; and as she protested anew, her husband entered—Mr. Palmer with his usual air of taking offence at the very air. He bowed slightly, as though courtesy were an ill-fitting coat he wore for the sake of decency, and immediately began to complain of the weather.

“How horrid all this is! Such weather makes every thing and every body disgusting. Dullness is as much produced within doors as without, by rain. It makes one detest all one’s acquaintance. What the devil does Sir John mean by not having a billiard room in his house? How few people know what comfort is! Sir John is as stupid as the weather.”

The rain, drumming on the windows, made a steady, restless percussion; the wind worried the shrubs outside; and in the pauses one could hear, more than one wished, the little domestic noises within—the scrape of a chair-leg, the soft tick of the clock, the distant clink of china as servants made ready for dinner. Elinor, who always attended to what other people called trifles, thought how sound itself could soothe or sour a mind; how some tempers, like milk too near a fire, would curdle at once.

The rest of the company soon dropped in; Sir John, with that hearty bustle which filled a house more effectually than any furniture, went up to Marianne with eager kindness.

“I am afraid, Miss Marianne, you have not been able to take your usual walk to Allenham today.”

The question fell like a pebble in a still pan: small, but certain to make circles. Marianne’s face, which had brightened at Mrs. Palmer’s entrance, became suddenly grave. She said nothing. The rain spoke for her, and the harshness of the wind.

“Oh, don’t be so sly before us,” cried Mrs. Palmer, delighted with herself as if she had discovered a secret spice in a familiar dish; “for we know all about it, I assure you; and I admire your taste very much, for I think he is extremely handsome. We do not live a great way from him in the country, you know. Not above ten miles, I dare say.”

“Much nearer thirty,” said Mr. Palmer.

“Ah, well! there is not much difference. I never was at his house; but they say it is a sweet pretty place.”

“As vile a spot as I ever saw in my life,” said Mr. Palmer.

Marianne remained silent still; but the very stillness of her attention betrayed her. Elinor saw, with a tenderness that was almost pain, how a name could be a flavour on the tongue—how it could bring heat to the blood or bitterness at once, and how impossible it was to translate such sensations into safer words.

“Is it very ugly?” continued Mrs. Palmer. “Then it must be some other place that is so pretty, I suppose.”

When they were seated in the dining-room, Sir John observed with regret that they were only eight altogether—his table, like his heart, being made to expand.

“My dear,” said he to his lady, “it is very provoking we should be so few. Why did not you ask the Gilberts to come to us today?”

“Did not I tell you, Sir John,” returned Lady Middleton, with placid propriety, “when you spoke to me about it before, that it could not be done? They dined with us last.”

“You and I, Sir John,” cried Mrs. Jennings, who liked company as other people like gravy—plentiful and poured freely—“should not stand upon such ceremony.”

“Then you would be very ill-bred,” said Mr. Palmer, cutting into the conversation as if it were meat he meant to carve with contempt.

“My love, you contradict every body,” cried his wife, laughing with the unquenchable ease of a nature determined to be happy. “Do you know you are quite rude?”

“I did not know I contradicted any body in calling your mother ill-bred.”

“Ay, you may abuse me as you please,” returned the good-natured old lady; “you have taken Charlotte off my hands, and cannot give her back again. So there I have the whip hand of you.”

Charlotte laughed heartily, delighted to think that even his ill-humour could not dismiss her; and declared, with exultation, that she did not care how cross he was to her, as they must live together.

It was impossible for any one to be more thoroughly good-natured, or more resolute in the pursuit of cheerfulness, than Mrs. Palmer. Her husband’s studied indifference, insolence, and discontent seemed to slip from her as rain from oiled cloth; and when he scolded, she looked as if she had been entertained.

“Mr. Palmer is so droll!” she whispered to Elinor. “He is always out of humour.”

Elinor, after a little observation, was not inclined to believe him so genuinely and unaffectedly ill-natured—or so ill-bred—as he wished to appear. His temper might, perhaps, be a little soured by finding, like many others of his sex, that through some unaccountable bias in favour of beauty he was the husband of a very silly woman; yet that kind of mistake was too common to wound a sensible man for long. She suspected a different seasoning in his conduct: a wish of distinction, the desire of seeming above other people by despising them; as though superiority could be proved by refusing to be pleased. It was a motive too common to surprise; but the means—however they might establish his eminence in ill-breeding—were not likely to attach any one to him except his wife.

Soon after, when the first bustle of dinner had subsided into that contented murmur of voices, forks, and the soft passing of dishes—a music that, when kindly governed, makes even a rainy day tolerable—Mrs. Palmer turned again to the Dashwoods with a bright suddenness, like a cook remembering an excellent ingredient she had nearly forgotten.

“Oh, my dear Miss Dashwood, I have got such a favour to ask of you and your sister. Will you come and spend some time at Cleveland this Christmas? Now, pray do—and come while the Westons are with us. You cannot think how happy I shall be! It will be quite delightful!—My love,” applying to her husband, “don’t you long to have the Miss Dashwoods come to Cleveland?”

“Certainly,” he replied, with a sneer; “I came into Devonshire with no other view.”

“There now,” cried his lady, triumphant, “you see Mr. Palmer expects you; so you cannot refuse to come.”

They both, however, declined eagerly and resolutely.

“But indeed you must and shall come. I am sure you will like it of all things. The Westons will be with us, and it will be quite delightful. You cannot think what a sweet place Cleveland is; and we are so gay now, for Mr. Palmer is always going about the country canvassing against the election; and so many people come to dine with us that I never saw before, it is quite charming! But, poor fellow! it is very fatiguing to him! for he is forced to make every body like him.”

Elinor could hardly keep her countenance as she assented to the hardship of such an obligation.

“How charming it will be,” continued Charlotte, “when he is in Parliament!—won’t it? How I shall laugh! It will be so ridiculous to see all his letters directed to him with an M.P.—But do you know, he says he will never frank for me? He declares he won’t. Don’t you, Mr. Palmer?”

Mr. Palmer took no notice.

“He cannot bear writing, you know,” she went on, as innocently pleased as if she were describing some beloved, familiar domestic practice; “he says it is quite shocking.”

“No,” said he, “I never said any thing so irrational. Don’t palm all your abuses of language upon me.”

“There now; you see how droll he is. This is always the way with him! Sometimes he won’t speak to me for half a day together, and then he comes out with something so droll—all about any thing in the world.”

When they returned into the drawing-room, she surprised Elinor by asking, with perfect sincerity, whether she did not like Mr. Palmer excessively.

“Certainly,” said Elinor; “he seems very agreeable.”

“Well—I am so glad you do. I thought you would, he is so pleasant; and Mr. Palmer is excessively pleased with you and your sisters I can tell you, and you can’t think how disappointed he will be if you don’t come to Cleveland. I can’t imagine why you should object to it.”

Elinor was obliged again to decline; and by changing the subject, put a stop to renewed entreaties.

She had, however, an object of her own, which sat upon her mind with the steady persistence of a flavour one cannot name but cannot ignore. Mrs. Palmer lived in the same county as Willoughby; she might know—if not from intimacy, then from that wide, careless knowledge society spreads as easily as it spreads a rumour—something more particular of his general character than could be gathered from the Middletons’ partial acquaintance. Elinor longed for any confirmation of his merits, however small, that might quiet the uneasy forebodings she did not dare translate into warnings for Marianne.

She began, therefore, by inquiring whether Mrs. Palmer saw much of Mr. Willoughby at Cleveland, and whether they were intimately acquainted with him.

“Oh dear, yes; I know him extremely well,” replied Mrs. Palmer—“not that I ever spoke to him, indeed; but I have seen him for ever in town. Somehow or other I never happened to be staying at Barton while he was at Allenham. Mama saw him here once before—but I was with my uncle at Weymouth. However, I dare say we should have seen a great deal of him in Somersetshire, if it had not happened very unluckily that we should never have been in the country together. He is very little at Combe, I believe; but if he were ever so much there, I do not think Mr. Palmer would visit him, for he is in the opposition, you know, and besides it is such a way off. I know why you inquire about him very well; your sister is to marry him. I am monstrous glad of it, for then I shall have her for a neighbour, you know.”

“Upon my word,” said Elinor, “you know much more of the matter than I do, if you have any reason to expect such a match.”

“Don’t pretend to deny it, because you know it is what every body talks of. I assure you I heard of it in my way through town.”

“My dear Mrs. Palmer!”

“Upon my honour I did. I met Colonel Brandon Monday morning in Bond-street, just before we left town, and he told me of it directly.”

“You surprise me very much. Colonel Brandon tell you of it! Surely you must be mistaken. To give such intelligence to a person who could not be interested in it, even if it were true, is not what I should expect Colonel Brandon to do.”

“But I do assure you it was so for all that; and I will tell you how it happened. When we met him, he turned back and walked with us; and so we began talking of my brother and sister, and one thing and another, and I said to him, ‘So, Colonel, there is a new family come to Barton cottage, I hear, and mama sends me word they are very pretty, and that one of them is going to be married to Mr. Willoughby of Combe Magna. Is it true, pray? for of course you must know, as you have been in Devonshire so lately.’”

“And what did the Colonel say?” asked Elinor, whose mind—so careful of tones and pauses, like one listening for a particular bird in an altered landscape—caught at every shade.

“Oh—he did not say much; but he looked as if he knew it to be true, so from that moment I set it down as certain. It will be quite delightful, I declare! When is it to take place?”

Elinor, obliged to set aside the sting of her surprise, asked instead, “Mr. Brandon was very well, I hope?”

“Oh! yes, quite well; and so full of your praises, he did nothing but say fine things of you.”

“I am flattered by his commendation. He seems an excellent man; and I think him uncommonly pleasing.”

“So do I. He is such a charming man, that it is quite a pity he should be so grave and so dull. Mama says he was in love with your sister too. I assure you it was a great compliment if he was, for he hardly ever falls in love with any body.”

Elinor, wishing to bring the talk back to what most concerned her, asked, “Is Mr. Willoughby much known in your part of Somersetshire?”

“Oh! yes, extremely well; that is, I do not believe many people are acquainted with him, because Combe Magna is so far off; but they all think him extremely agreeable, I assure you. Nobody is more liked than Mr. Willoughby wherever he goes, and so you may tell your sister. She is a monstrous lucky girl to get him, upon my honour; not but that he is much more lucky in getting her, because she is so very handsome and agreeable, that nothing can be good enough for her. However, I don’t think her hardly at all handsomer than you, I assure you; for I think you both excessively pretty, and so does Mr. Palmer too, I am sure, though we could not get him to own it last night.”

The substance of Mrs. Palmer’s account was slight, full of echoes and borrowed certainty; yet even that small testimony in Willoughby’s favour was a comfort to Elinor, as a familiar scent can be—a remembered loaf, a known herb—when one is uncertain of what may be served next.

“I am so glad we are got acquainted at last,” continued Charlotte. “And now I hope we shall always be great friends. You can’t think how much I longed to see you! It is so delightful that you should live at the cottage! Nothing can be like it, to be sure! And I am so glad your sister is going to be well married! I hope you will be a great deal at Combe Magna. It is a sweet place, by all accounts.”

Then, with the easy leap of a mind that ran where it pleased, she asked Elinor, “You have been long acquainted with Colonel Brandon, have not you?”

“Yes, a great while; ever since my sister married. He was a particular friend of Sir John’s. I believe,” she added in a low voice, as if confiding a family recipe, “he would have been very glad to have had me, if he could. Sir John and Lady Middleton wished it very much. But mama did not think the match good enough for me; otherwise Sir John would have mentioned it to the Colonel, and we should have been married immediately.”

“Did not Colonel Brandon know of Sir John’s proposal to your mother before it was made? Had he never owned his affection to yourself?”

“Oh, no; but if mama had not objected to it, I dare say he would have liked it of all things. He had not seen me then above twice, for it was before I left school. However, I am much happier as I am. Mr. Palmer is the kind of man I like.”

The Palmers were gone back to Cleveland the very next day, their carriage-wheels diminishing into the damp Devon lanes until even the cawing rooks seemed to close over the vacancy they left behind. Barton, relieved of visitors and yet somehow lonelier for the relief, sank again into its accustomed interchange between Park and cottage: the same walks, the same calls, the same tea-things set out and cleared away with practised hands. Elinor, who had not yet finished marvelling—at Charlotte’s airy contentment without any visible reason; at Mr. Palmer’s odd mixture of sense and indolent plainness; at the perpetual mystery of husbands and wives so ill matched and yet so irrevocably tied—found herself, before she could settle into yesterday, pressed onward into tomorrow by Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, those indefatigable gardeners of society, forever planting new acquaintances in the beds of their neighbours’ lives.

They had been to Exeter that morning—an “excursion,” as Sir John called it, though it was as much a hunt as a drive—and there Mrs. Jennings, with the triumph of a woman whose net has at last tightened, had discovered two young ladies to be relations. That word, in her mouth, had the force of law. Sir John, to whom kinship was only a finer excuse for hospitality, immediately invited them to the Park as soon as their Exeter engagements could possibly allow.

Their engagements, whatever they had been, melted at once before so hearty an invitation. Lady Middleton, on hearing the news, was thrown into a small, well-bred panic. Two girls she had never seen; two girls of whose “elegance”—whose tolerable gentility even—she could have no proof; for her mother’s assurances were too sanguine, and her husband’s confidence too loud, to carry weight with a woman who measured worth by quiet polish and the right sort of restraint. That they were relations made it worse. Relations could not be dismissed after one visit, or mistaken for strangers and forgot.

Mrs. Jennings attempted comfort in the only manner she understood: by laughing at the anxiety and advising her daughter not to care whether they were fashionable, because cousins must put up with one another. It was consolation that stung. Since it was now impossible to prevent their coming, Lady Middleton resigned herself with the philosophy of a woman trained to smile over irritations; and she contented herself, instead, with giving Sir John a gentle reprimand on the subject five or six times every day—always in the same tone, always at the same safe distance from real feeling, as if repetition might boil down impropriety into acceptability.

When the young ladies arrived, their appearance did not justify alarm. They were smartly dressed, with that air of having taken pains and being conscious of it; their manners were civil; they were delighted with the house and in raptures with the furniture; and, above all, they professed themselves so passionately—so extravagantly—fond of children, that Lady Middleton’s opinion, which could be won by no argument so quickly as by admiration of her offspring, was engaged in their favour within the hour. She pronounced them “very agreeable girls indeed,” which, coming from her, was enthusiasm. Sir John’s faith in his own judgment rose like yeast with that faint praise. He set off directly to the cottage to proclaim the Miss Steeles.

He burst in with the same hearty bustle with which he rode to cover in the hunting season, eager to share his find as though kindness were a dish that spoiled if not served at once.

“Do come now—pray come—you must come—I declare you shall come. You can’t think how you will like them. Lucy is monstrous pretty, and so good-humoured and agreeable! The children are all hanging about her already, as if she was an old acquaintance. And they both long to see you of all things, for they have heard at Exeter that you are the most beautiful creatures in the world; and I have told them it is all very true, and a great deal more. You will be delighted with them, I am sure. They have brought the whole coach full of playthings for the children. How can you be so cross as not to come? Why, they are your cousins, you know, after a fashion. You are my cousins, and they are my wife’s, so you must be related.”

There was something almost touching in his inability to keep pleasure to himself; even a third cousin must be shared, as if affection were a pot forever on the hob, meant to be ladled out while it steamed. Yet neither Elinor nor Marianne could be prevailed on to go instantly. Sir John obtained only a promise that they would call at the Park within a day or two, and left them in genuine astonishment at their indifference—only to walk home and boast anew of the Dashwoods’ charms to the Miss Steeles, as he had already been boasting of the Miss Steeles to them.

When the promised visit took place and the introductions were made, Elinor’s first impression settled quickly. The elder Miss Steele, nearly thirty, was very plain, and her face had that want—not merely of beauty, but of sensibility—which no civility can conceal. The younger, two or three and twenty, had considerable prettiness: features that were light and pleasing, a sharp quick eye, a smartness of air that, though it could not be mistaken for true elegance or grace, gave her a certain distinctness, like a brisk spice that is noticed even when it is not delicate.

Both were particularly civil, and Elinor, watching rather than speaking, soon allowed them credit for some kind of sense—at least the sense of self-interest—when she observed with what constant, judicious attention they made themselves agreeable to Lady Middleton. Their praise fell exactly where her vanity was hungriest. They were in continual raptures with the children—extolling their beauty, courting their notice, humouring their whims with a perseverance that might have passed for devotion, had it not been so obviously strategic. When not required to applaud an infant’s shout or fetch back a toy flung across the room, they admired whatever her ladyship happened to be doing—if she happened to be doing anything—or they begged patterns of some new dress, and talked as if yesterday’s ribbon were an invention.

Lady Middleton, like many fond mothers, was insatiable in her thirst for admiration and boundless in her credulity. Her demands were enormous; but she would swallow any offering, however coarsely prepared, so long as it was sweetened with praise. She watched with complacency the encroachments and tricks to which her cousins submitted. She saw their sashes untied, their hair pulled about their ears, their work-bags searched, their knives and scissors stolen; and she never suspected that endurance could be anything but enjoyment.

“John is in such spirits to-day!” she said, when her eldest boy seized Miss Steele’s pocket-handkerchief and threw it out of the window. “He is full of monkey tricks.”

Soon after, when the second boy pinched one of the same lady’s fingers with all the eager strength of a child discovering power, Lady Middleton observed tenderly, “How playful William is!”

“And here is my sweet little Annamaria,” she added, gathering her three-year-old daughter to her with complacent affection. “And she is always so gentle and quiet—never was there such a quiet little thing!”

At that very moment, a pin in Lady Middleton’s head-dress, shifting as she bent, scratched the child’s neck. The pattern of gentleness answered with screams so violent and piercing that the room seemed suddenly all noise: a shrill, raw note cutting through the softer domestic sounds—the crackle of the fire, the rustle of muslin, the distant clink of tea-things—until everything else was drowned.

The mother’s consternation was extreme; and it could not exceed the alarm of the Miss Steeles, who flung themselves into sympathy as if sympathy were a duty to be performed with dispatch. All three did everything affection could suggest. The child was seated on her mother’s lap and covered with kisses; her tiny wound was bathed with lavender-water by one Miss Steele, kneeling as if before an altar; her mouth was stuffed with sugar-plums by the other, who searched quickly for the brightest, the sweetest, the most bribing. Rewarded so richly for her tears, Annamaria was too wise to stop. She screamed and sobbed with renewed lustiness, kicked her brothers when they offered to touch her, and rejected, with noisy contempt, all attempts at soothing.

At last Lady Middleton remembered that in a similar distress the week before, some apricot marmalade had been applied with great success to a bruised temple. The same remedy was proposed now with eager faith; and the child’s brief pause, upon hearing the word, gave them hope. She was carried from the room in her mother’s arms in search of this “medicine.” The boys chose to follow, though their mother earnestly begged them to stay behind; and in their wake the house seemed, for a moment, to exhale. The room, which had not known quiet for hours, fell into a sudden hush so deep that Elinor could hear the soft settling of fabric, the faint ticking of a clock, and, beyond the windows, the mild rural chorus: a wind brushing bare branches, a distant farm-dog answering another, and the steady, uncommercial music of life unforced.

“Poor little creatures!” said the elder Miss Steele, as soon as they were gone. “It might have been a very sad accident.”

“Yet I hardly know how,” cried Marianne, her eyes bright with impatience, “unless it had been under totally different circumstances. But this is the usual way of heightening alarm, where there is nothing to be alarmed at in reality.”

“What a sweet woman Lady Middleton is!” said Lucy Steele.

Marianne was silent. She could not say what she did not feel, even when the occasion was trivial. The entire burden of civility—of those small untruths which oil the wheels of society—fell, as it always did, to Elinor. She did what she could, praising Lady Middleton with more warmth than she truly felt, though with far less than Lucy’s extravagant zeal.

“And Sir John too,” cried Miss Steele, the elder, “what a charming man he is!”

Here Elinor’s commendation, being simple and just, came without any sparkle. She merely observed that he was perfectly good-humoured and friendly.

“And what a charming little family they have! I never saw such fine children in my life.—I declare I quite doat upon them already, and indeed I am always distractedly fond of children.”

“I should guess so,” said Elinor, with a smile, “from what I have witnessed this morning.”

“I have a notion,” said Lucy, “you think the little Middletons rather too much indulged; perhaps they may be the outside of enough; but it is so natural in Lady Middleton; and for my part, I love to see children full of life and spirits; I cannot bear them if they are tame and quiet.”

“I confess,” replied Elinor, “that while I am at Barton Park, I never think of tame and quiet children with any abhorrence.”

A short pause followed. The air seemed to settle again into domestic stillness, like flour after it has been shaken. Miss Steele, who appeared eager for talk, broke it rather abruptly.

“And how do you like Devonshire, Miss Dashwood? I suppose you were very sorry to leave Sussex.”

Elinor, surprised by the familiarity of the question—or at least by the manner of it—answered that she was.

“Norland is a prodigious beautiful place, is not it?” added Miss Steele.

“We have heard Sir John admire it excessively,” said Lucy, as if she felt an apology was needed for her sister’s freedom.

“I think every one must admire it,” replied Elinor, “who ever saw the place; though it is not to be supposed that any one can estimate its beauties as we do.”

“And had you a great many smart beaux there? I suppose you have not so many in this part of the world; for my part, I think they are a vast addition always.”

“But why should you think,” said Lucy, colouring with mortification, “that there are not as many genteel young men in Devonshire as Sussex?”

“Nay, my dear, I’m sure I don’t pretend to say that there an’t. I’m sure there’s a vast many smart beaux in Exeter; but, you know, how could I tell what smart beaux there might be about Norland? And I was only afraid the Miss Dashwoods might find it dull at Barton if they had not so many as they used to have. But perhaps you young ladies may not care about the beaux, and had as lief be without them as with them. For my part, I think they are vastly agreeable, provided they dress smart and behave civil. But I can’t bear to see them dirty and nasty. Now there’s Mr. Rose at Exeter, a prodigious smart young man, quite a beau, clerk to Mr. Simpson, you know, and yet if you do but meet him of a morning, he is not fit to be seen. I suppose your brother was quite a beau, Miss Dashwood, before he married, as he was so rich?”

“Upon my word,” replied Elinor, “I cannot tell you, for I do not perfectly comprehend the meaning of the word. But this I can say, that if he ever was a beau before he married, he is one still, for there is not the smallest alteration in him.”

“Oh! dear! one never thinks of married men’s being beaux—they have something else to do.”

“Lord! Anne,” cried Lucy, “you can talk of nothing but beaux—you will make Miss Dashwood believe you think of nothing else.” And, eager to turn the discourse, she began admiring the house and furniture again, as if objects at least could be praised without offence.

This first specimen of the Miss Steeles was enough for Elinor. The vulgar freedom and folly of the elder offered no recommendation; and Elinor, not blinded by the younger’s prettiness or her sharp look into mistaking smartness for refinement, saw too plainly the want of real elegance and artlessness beneath her civil exterior. She left Barton Park without any desire to know them better.

The Miss Steeles, however, had come from Exeter well provisioned with admiration, as if it were packed in their trunks for convenient use. Sir John, his family, all his relations—every person and thing connected with Barton Park—was praised with unmeasured readiness. To his fair cousins they dealt out no niggardly portion. Elinor and Marianne were declared the most beautiful, elegant, accomplished, agreeable girls they had ever beheld; and they professed themselves particularly anxious to be better acquainted.

And better acquainted, Elinor soon found, was to be their unavoidable fate. Sir John was entirely on the side of the Miss Steeles; his party was too strong for opposition. That kind of intimacy must be submitted to which consists of sitting an hour or two together in the same room almost every day—an intimacy measured not by sympathy or trust, but by proximity and repetition. Sir John could do no more; and he did not suspect that any more might be required. To be together, in his opinion, was to be intimate; and while his schemes for their meeting succeeded, he never doubted the friendship was established.

To do him justice, he did everything in his power to promote unreserve—by making the Miss Steeles acquainted with whatever he knew, or imagined, of his cousins’ situations in the most delicate particulars. Elinor had not seen them more than twice before the elder Miss Steele wished her joy on her sister’s having been so lucky as to make a conquest of a very smart beau since coming to Barton.

“’Twill be a fine thing to have her married so young, to be sure,” said she; “and I hear he is quite a beau, and prodigious handsome. And I hope you may have as good luck yourself soon—but perhaps you may have a friend in the corner already.”

Elinor could not doubt the source. Sir John was no more cautious in proclaiming his suspicions of her regard for Edward than he had been about Marianne. Indeed it was his favourite joke of the two, being newer, more conjectural, and therefore more amusing to him. Since Edward’s visit they had scarcely dined together without Sir John drinking to her “best affections” with such significant nods and winks as to draw every eye. The letter F— had been invariably produced and paraded, wrung for its merriment until its character as the wittiest letter in the alphabet was, for Sir John, firmly established.

The Miss Steeles now shared all the benefit of these jokes; and in the elder they raised a curiosity to know the name of the gentleman hinted at—a curiosity expressed so impertinently, so persistently, that it only matched her general habit of prying into other people’s affairs as if their private histories were recipes she meant to copy.

But Sir John did not long sport with the curiosity he delighted to raise; he had almost as much pleasure in telling the name as Miss Steele had in hearing it.

“His name is Ferrars,” said he, in a very audible whisper; “but pray do not tell it, for it’s a great secret.”

“Ferrars!” repeated Miss Steele. “Mr. Ferrars is the happy man, is he? What! your sister-in-law’s brother, Miss Dashwood? A very agreeable young man, to be sure; I know him very well.”

“How can you say so, Anne?” cried Lucy, quick as ever to correct, to soften, to amend. “Though we have seen him once or twice at my uncle’s, it is rather too much to pretend to know him very well.”

Elinor heard this with a start of attention that she took pains not to show. An uncle? Where did he live? How came they acquainted? The careless way in which Miss Steele had spoken of Edward struck her as something more than mere folly; it sounded edged, almost ill-natured—like a word pronounced in a dialect meant to dominate a conversation, to imply familiarity and power.

She wished the subject continued; yet she would not join in it herself. But nothing more was said. Mrs. Jennings, for the first time in Elinor’s experience, seemed deficient either in curiosity for petty information or in a desire to communicate it. The moment passed; the room returned to common talk; and though Sir John continued to bandy the name of Ferrars openly, the elder Miss Steele made no further remark, and Lucy let the matter drop with a composure that only sharpened Elinor’s uneasiness.

Marianne had never been fashioned for indulgence. A rough joke, a coarse turn of phrase, a complacent ignorance parading as wit—any of it set her whole spirit in arms; and now, with her feelings still raw and feverish from disappointments she would not name aloud, she could scarcely endure the Miss Steeles’ very voices. Their laugh, pitched too sharp, seemed to rasp against the quiet grain of Barton like a blunt knife over a board; their footsteps on the path had a brisk, restless tap that broke the countryside’s more honest music—wind in hawthorn, a cart’s distant creak, the small, measured talk of labourers going home. Marianne met them with a chill that needed no speech. Every attempt at intimacy on their side fell, rebuffed, before it could bloom into familiarity.

It was chiefly, Elinor thought, that Marianne’s unvarying coldness drove the sisters toward herself; for soon both showed her a preference, Lucy in particular, who missed no opening to draw Elinor into conversation, to press their acquaintance forward with a confidence that wore the mask of frankness. Lucy Steele was naturally quick: she could seize a point, turn it neatly, and, for half an hour at a time, be truly diverting. Yet her cleverness had never been ripened by any steady cultivation. She spoke with an air of knowing more than she did, and the little slips—the wrong name, the confused fact, the bold opinion built on no foundation—betrayed, in spite of her constant effort to appear at advantage, a mind left hungry. Elinor could pity that neglect; she could not so readily soften toward what she discerned besides—an ease in flattery, an eagerness to insinuate, a habit of shaping herself to each listener, which betrayed a want, not of information, but of delicacy and rectitude. Attention from such a quarter, however neatly offered, had a hollowness to it that made it less a compliment than a trial.

One morning, as they walked back from the Park toward the cottage, the lane lay steeped in late-summer warmth. A cart went by in the distance; its wheels sang a thin, intermittent song over gravel, and the hedges held the low hum of bees. Lucy kept pace at Elinor’s side, her voice pitched to be confidential without being quite a whisper.

“You will think my question a very odd one, I dare say,” said she at last, after watching her companion’s face with a cautious sidelong look; “but pray—are you personally acquainted with your sister-in-law’s mother, Mrs. Ferrars?”

Elinor did think it odd; her countenance owned it as she answered, with measured civility, that she had never seen Mrs. Ferrars.

“Indeed!” Lucy returned, with an air of gentle wonder. “I declare I thought you must have met her at Norland sometimes. Then perhaps you cannot tell me what sort of a woman she is?”

“No,” said Elinor, guarding her real opinion, and not disposed to gratify what looked very like impertinent curiosity. “I know nothing of her.”

Lucy’s eyes lingered on her as if weighing the effect of every syllable. “I am sure you think me very strange for inquiring in such a way,” she said; “but perhaps there may be reasons. I wish I might venture—but however, I hope you will do me the justice of believing I do not mean to be impertinent.”

Elinor answered with calm courtesy; and they walked on, the sound of their steps rising and falling together on the hard path. For a few minutes Lucy was silent. Then, as if her own thoughts would not let her rest, she began again, a little unsteadily, yet with the manner of one resolved to be noticed.

“I cannot bear to have you think me impertinently curious. I would rather do any thing in the world than be thought so by a person whose good opinion is so well worth having as yours. And I am sure I should not have the smallest fear of trusting you. Indeed, I should be very glad of your advice how to manage in such an uncomfortable situation as I am. But there is no occasion to trouble you. I am sorry you do not happen to know Mrs. Ferrars.”

“I am sorry I do not,” Elinor replied, struck into real astonishment, “if it could be of any use to you to know my opinion of her. But really, I never understood that you were at all connected with that family, and therefore I confess I am surprised at so serious an inquiry into her character.”

“I dare say you are,” said Lucy quickly, and she gave a little dutiful laugh, as if to soften what she meant to sharpen. “And I am sure I do not wonder at it. But if I dared tell you all, you would not be so much surprised. Mrs. Ferrars is certainly nothing to me at present—but the time may come—how soon it will come must depend upon herself—when we may be very intimately connected.”

She looked down as she spoke, bashful in her pose, yet stealing a glance up from beneath her lashes to catch the impression she had made.

Elinor’s breath caught. For an instant her mind refused the path Lucy offered it; it turned aside as from something too strange to be true. “Good heavens!” she cried at last. “What do you mean? Are you acquainted with Mr. Robert Ferrars? Can you be?” The thought of such an alliance, of such a sister-in-law, brought no pleasure with it.

“No,” Lucy answered. “Not to Mr. Robert Ferrars—I never saw him in my life; but—” and here she fixed her eyes full upon Elinor’s face, as if she would force belief by steadiness, “to his eldest brother.”

What Elinor felt in that moment was a sudden, violent astonishment—so keen it might have been agony, had not disbelief sprung up at once, like a hand flung out to keep her from falling. She turned toward Lucy in silent amazement, struggling to comprehend not only the meaning, but the motive of such a declaration. Her colour changed; but she stood firm, too practiced in self-command to be betrayed into any outward excess.

“You may well be surprised,” Lucy continued, taking Elinor’s silence for the effect she wished. “For to be sure you could have had no idea of it before; for I dare say he never dropped the smallest hint of it to you or to any of your family. Because it was always meant to be a great secret, and it has been faithfully kept so by me to this hour. Not a soul of all my relations knows of it but Anne; and I never should have mentioned it to you if I had not felt the greatest dependence in the world upon your secrecy. And besides, I really thought my behaviour in asking so many questions about Mrs. Ferrars must seem so odd that it ought to be explained. And I do not think Mr. Ferrars can be displeased when he knows I have trusted you; because I know he has the highest opinion in the world of all your family, and looks upon yourself and the other Miss Dashwoods quite as his own sisters.”

She paused, as if to allow that last phrase to settle and do its work.

Elinor, for a few moments, could not speak. The lane seemed suddenly too narrow; the air, though warm, felt thin. Then she forced herself to steadiness—forced her voice into a calm that would neither implore nor accuse.

“May I ask,” she said, “if your engagement is of long standing?”

“We have been engaged these four years.”

“Four years!”

“Yes.”

The number struck Elinor with a dull, heavy certainty, as if time itself had been used against her; and yet, even while shock ran through her, something in her mind still struggled to question, to doubt, to find an escape from believing. “I did not know,” said she, “that you were even acquainted till the other day.”

“Our acquaintance, however, is of many years’ date. He was under my uncle’s care, you know, a considerable while.”

“Your uncle!”

“Yes; Mr. Pratt. Did you never hear him talk of Mr. Pratt?”

“I think I have,” Elinor answered, with an effort that felt like holding a trembling vessel steady while it brimmed.

“He was four years with my uncle, who lives at Longstaple, near Plymouth. It was there our acquaintance began; for my sister and me was often staying with my uncle, and it was there our engagement was formed—though not till a year after he had quitted as a pupil; but he was almost always with us afterwards. I was very unwilling to enter into it, as you may imagine, without the knowledge and approbation of his mother; but I was too young, and loved him too well, to be so prudent as I ought to have been. Though you do not know him so well as me, Miss Dashwood, you must have seen enough of him to be sensible he is very capable of making a woman sincerely attached to him.”

“Certainly,” Elinor replied—she scarcely knew what she said; and then, as if her very honesty must rally to defend him, she added, with a returning grasp at the idea of his honour and of Lucy’s possible deception, “Engaged to Mr. Edward Ferrars!—I confess myself so totally surprised, that really—I beg your pardon; but surely there must be some mistake of person or name. We cannot mean the same Mr. Ferrars.”

“We can mean no other,” Lucy cried, smiling as if at an absurdity. “Mr. Edward Ferrars, the eldest son of Mrs. Ferrars, of Park Street, and brother of your sister-in-law, Mrs. John Dashwood, is the person I mean. You must allow I am not likely to be deceived as to the name of the man on who all my happiness depends.”

“It is strange,” said Elinor, in a perplexity that was pain itself held on a tight rein, “that I should never have heard him even mention your name.”

“No—considering our situation, it was not strange. Our first care has been to keep the matter secret. You knew nothing of me, or my family, and therefore there could be no occasion for ever mentioning my name to you; and as he was always particularly afraid of his sister’s suspecting any thing, that was reason enough for his not mentioning it.”

Elinor’s heart sank lower; yet her countenance did not sink with it. She repeated, because she could not help it, because the words seemed to measure her misery with arithmetic coldness, “Four years you have been engaged.”

“Yes; and heaven knows how much longer we may have to wait. Poor Edward! It puts him quite out of heart.”

As she spoke she drew from her pocket a small miniature, wrapped and handled with the practiced tenderness of possession. “To prevent the possibility of mistake, be so good as to look at this face. It does not do him justice, to be sure, but yet I think you cannot be deceived as to the person it was drew for. I have had it above these three years.”

The little painting lay in Elinor’s hand. She had seen that face in life with a thousand quiet meanings—seen it in lamplight at Barton, in the softened morning in the parlour, seen it attentive, embarrassed, kind. Here it was reduced to colour and shadow, yet unmistakable: Edward’s eyes, Edward’s line of brow and mouth, the very expression she had learned to read. Whatever her fear of deciding too hastily, whatever wish she had of detecting falsehood, could not survive that evidence. She returned it almost instantly, acknowledging the likeness in a voice that sounded, to her own ears, like someone else’s.

“I have never been able,” Lucy went on, “to give him my picture in return, which vexes me sadly; for he has always been so anxious to get it! But I am determined to set for it the very first opportunity.”

“You are quite in the right,” Elinor answered, and marvelled at her own composure, as one marvels at keeping a delicate sauce from curdling while the fire beneath is too fierce.

They walked a few paces in silence. A bird startled from the hedge; its wings made a sudden clap of sound, then the lane was quiet again but for their steps and the distant murmur of the Park. Lucy spoke first.

“I am sure I have no doubt in the world of your faithfully keeping this secret,” said she, “because you must know of what importance it is to us not to have it reach his mother; for she would never approve of it, I dare say. I shall have no fortune, and I fancy she is an exceeding proud woman.”

“I certainly did not seek your confidence,” Elinor said, and though her words were civil, there was a steadiness in them that refused to be coaxed. “But you do me no more than justice in imagining that I may be depended on. Your secret is safe with me; but pardon me if I express some surprise at so unnecessary a communication. You must at least have felt that my being acquainted with it could not add to its safety.”

As she spoke, she looked earnestly at Lucy, searching—without quite knowing what she sought—for any involuntary change, any crack in that smooth surface: guilt, malice, triumph, fear. Lucy’s countenance did not alter.

“I was afraid you would think I was taking a great liberty,” Lucy said, as if injured by the idea and yet pleased to have produced it. “I have not known you long, to be sure—personally at least—but I have known you and all your family by description a great while; and as soon as I saw you I felt almost as if you was an old acquaintance. Besides, in the present case, I really thought some explanation was due to you, after my making such particular inquiries about Edward’s mother. And I am so unfortunate that I have not a creature whose advice I can ask. Anne is the only person that knows of it, and she has no judgment at all—indeed, she does me a great deal more harm than good; for I am in constant fear of her betraying me. She does not know how to hold her tongue, as you must perceive; and I was in the greatest fright in the world t’other day, when Edward’s name was mentioned by Sir John, lest she should out with it all. You can’t think how much I go through in my mind from it altogether. I only wonder that I am alive after what I have suffered for Edward’s sake these last four years—every thing in such suspense and uncertainty; and seeing him so seldom—we can hardly meet above twice a-year. I am sure I wonder my heart is not quite broke.”

She took out her handkerchief and wiped her eyes; but Elinor, though she heard every word, felt little softness rising in answer. The soundscape of Lucy’s complaint—its rehearsed cadences, its little pauses for effect—seemed to Elinor like a song performed too loudly in a room where someone is trying to listen for truth.

“Sometimes,” Lucy continued, as if following a script of suffering, “I think whether it would not be better for us both to break off the matter entirely.” As she said this, she looked directly at Elinor, waiting.

“Pardon me,” Elinor replied, startled into a more open firmness than she intended, “but I can give you no advice under such circumstances. Your own judgment must direct you.”

“To be sure,” Lucy resumed after a silence, “his mother must provide for him sometime or other; but poor Edward is so cast down by it! Did you not think him dreadful low-spirited when he was at Barton? He was so miserable when he left us at Longstaple to go to you, that I was afraid you would think him quite ill.”

“Did he come from your uncle’s, then, when he visited us?” Elinor asked—each fresh detail, against her will, knitting the story tighter.

“Oh yes; he had been staying a fortnight with us. Did you think he came directly from town?”

“No,” Elinor answered, and the memory rose with cruel clarity, “I remember he told us he had been staying a fortnight with some friends near Plymouth.” She remembered, too, how she had wondered at his saying no more, at his silence even on their names.

“Did not you think him sadly out of spirits?” Lucy repeated.

“We did indeed,” Elinor said, and every syllable felt like it cost her. “Particularly so when he first arrived.”

“I begged him to exert himself for fear you should suspect what was the matter; but it made him so melancholy—not being able to stay more than a fortnight with us, and seeing me so much affected. Poor fellow! I am afraid it is just the same with him now; for he writes in wretched spirits. I heard from him just before I left Exeter.” And with a carelessness that was not carelessness at all, she drew a letter from her pocket and showed Elinor the direction. “You know his hand, I dare say—a charming one it is; but this is not written so well as usual. He was tired, I dare say, for he had just filled the sheet to me as full as possible.”

Elinor looked. The strokes, the turns of the letters, the very manner of the flourish—she knew them. The miniature might have been stolen, bought, come by accident; but this could not. A correspondence so intimate and so sustained did not exist without a promise. In those few moments, her strength failed her so suddenly that the world seemed to tilt: the hedge, the sky, Lucy’s bonnet ribbon—each swam a little. Her heart sank within her; her limbs felt unsteady; she could hardly stand.

But necessity, that stern household deity, demanded her composure. She made an exertion—not dramatic, not visible to any but herself—and held her face and voice in order again, as one steadies a dish one must serve, though the kitchen behind is in disorder.

“Writing to each other,” Lucy said, tucking the letter away, “is the only comfort we have in such long separations. Yes, I have one other comfort in his picture; but poor Edward has not even that. If he had but my picture, he says he should be easy. I gave him a lock of my hair set in a ring when he was at Longstaple last, and that was some comfort to him, he said, but not equal to a picture. Perhaps you might notice the ring when you saw him?”

“I did,” Elinor answered, in a voice that, to any ear but her own, would have sounded perfectly composed. Under it lay distress beyond any she had ever known—mortification, shock, confusion, a sense of having been fed, for weeks, on hopes that now turned bitter in the mouth. The past—every look, every hesitation, every gentle attentiveness—seemed to rearrange itself with a new, cruel logic, as if some old family recipe had been recited in a dialect she had never truly understood, and now the meaning, once translated into plain fact, had changed the taste of every remembered moment.

They had reached the cottage. The familiar threshold was there, the hush of the little rooms, the safer, softer sounds of home. The Miss Steeles sat with them only a few minutes before returning to the Park; their voices and their footsteps retreated down the path until the lane’s natural quiet reclaimed itself.

Then Elinor was at liberty to think—and to be wretched.

Elinor had never been lavish of belief where Lucy Steele was concerned. Even in the first heat of that morning’s disclosure, her mind—trained to test and weigh—had reached instinctively for some seam, some loosened thread by which the whole might be unpicked. Yet, when the first tumult had abated and she could sit alone with the matter, she found no such thread.

For what temptation could invite a falsehood so elaborate, so perilous, and so very much against the teller’s ease? Lucy had not spoken with the heedless extravagance of a romancer; she had produced circumstance, time, place—Mr. Pratt’s house, Plymouth, a visit, a melancholy that had once seemed to Elinor only a general dissatisfaction. Then came the small, damning proofs—knowledge the Miss Steeles should not have possessed of Norland and its connexions; a picture remembered; a letter; a ring. Each item, like a pinch of salt cast into water, vanished, and yet altered the whole. Nothing contradicted it, except the desire in Elinor’s own breast that it might be untrue.

It settled, at last, into the shape of fact; and with it came, sharp and clean, the thought that Edward had behaved ill by her. Indignation rose first—an almost selfish sting, that she had been drawn on, that she had listened, that she had been made, unwittingly, ridiculous. For a little while she could feel only this, as one feels the first bite of winter air before the lungs have learned its lesson.

But other questions followed fast, each one less angry and more wounding than the last. Had he meant to deceive? Had he put on a tenderness he did not feel? Was Lucy’s engagement an engagement of the heart?

No. Whatever Edward might once have fancied at nineteen, she could not—she dared not—think it so now. His affection was hers. It was not vanity that told her so. At Norland it had been visible—not to herself alone, but to her mother, her sisters, even to Fanny with all her watchfulness for slights. Edward’s manner had been too consistent, too involuntary, to be an invention. He loved her.

The persuasion softened her as warmth softens hardened butter—still firm, still itself, but yielding at the edges. It tempted her to forgiveness even while it sharpened the sense of his fault. He had been blameable, highly blameable, in remaining at Norland after he had begun to feel her influence; in suffering himself to linger where he ought, in honour, to have fled. That could not be excused.

And yet—if she was injured, how much more was he? Her misery might be conquered by time, by habit, by effort; but what prospect had he? His imprudence had cost her a season of anguish; it seemed to have cost him the whole shape of his future.

She found herself, with an unwilling minuteness, imagining what Lucy Steele would be as a wife—not in the broad, easy caricature her resentment would have liked, but in particular traits that offended Edward’s nature. Illiterate; artful; selfish. At nineteen, beauty and a seeming good humour might have dazzled him, might have seemed to answer all. But four years—four years which, if soberly spent, enlarge the understanding—must have opened his eyes. While he had improved (for he had improved; she knew it in his conversation, his delicacy, his informed mind), Lucy’s time in inferior society and frivolous pursuits might have rubbed away whatever simplicity had once made her pretty face more interesting than pretty. Even if Edward’s affection for Elinor were put aside entirely, could a man like him be satisfied with a woman like Lucy?

And then, as if her imagination were bent on cruel accuracy, she added the likely opposition of his family. If his mother had been severe at the thought of a match with Elinor—superior as Elinor must be in every thing that such people count—how much more severe would she be when the object was plainly inferior in connexions, and probably in fortune too? Difficulties might, in some temperaments, give energy; in Edward they would give only a mournful resignation. What a bleak relief it is, to a melancholy mind, to foresee opposition—because opposition at least is movement, while compliance would only bind him faster to what he dreaded.

As these reflections passed through her, one by one, like carts on a distant road that one hears before one sees, she wept—not for herself, but for him. It was a strange reversal, yet perfectly natural to her: supported by the conviction that she had done nothing to deserve such pain, and consoled by believing that Edward had done nothing to forfeit her esteem, she could still pity him as the greater sufferer.

And she resolved—instantly, quietly—that her mother and sisters should not know.

The necessity of silence, so far from being an added burden, came as a kind of mercy. To tell Mrs. Dashwood would be to pour out sorrow upon sorrow; to tell Marianne would be to invite that impassioned condemnation of Edward which Elinor felt she could not bear to hear. Their tenderness would only increase the distress which she must already endure; their example would not strengthen her self-command, and their praise of it would but make her feel the strain more.

She was, in truth, stronger alone.

So it was that when she joined them at dinner—only two hours after the first blow had extinguished, as by a sudden gust, all her dearest hopes—no one, from Elinor’s face or voice, could have supposed her to be secretly mourning an obstacle that must separate her for ever from the man she loved; nor, from Marianne’s glowing expectation, that her whole heart was dwelling upon the perfections of a man she believed entirely her own, and whom she looked for in every carriage that rolled within hearing of their gate.

Yet Elinor’s mind was not at rest. Much as she had suffered in that first conversation with Lucy, she soon felt an eager wish to renew it. She wanted to hear the particulars again—not because repetition would make them less true, but because the mind, when wounded, tries to map the wound. She wanted to understand more clearly what Lucy felt for Edward: whether there was any sincerity in her protestations of tenderness, or only the hard glitter of possession. And, above all, she wished to convince Lucy—by a calmness she had not fully commanded in the morning—that she was interested in the affair only as a friend. She feared her involuntary agitation had betrayed too much.

Lucy’s jealousy, indeed, was almost certain. Edward must have spoken of Elinor with warmth; not only did Lucy herself assert it, but her very act of disclosure proved it. Why else reveal a secret so important to a near stranger, unless to establish her own claim and warn Elinor away? Even Sir John’s careless jesting hints—those loud, bustling words that make sport of what they cannot measure—might have pricked Lucy into action.

Elinor understood her rival’s intention without difficulty. And though she was firmly resolved to act by every principle of honour—combating her own affection, and seeing Edward as little as possible—she could not deny herself the comfort of trying to appear unwounded. If Lucy could be persuaded that Elinor’s heart was safe, perhaps Lucy’s triumph would be less sharpened into malice.

She trusted, too, that nothing more painful remained to be heard. She had already swallowed the bitterest draught; a second taste might be endured with composure.

But opportunity did not readily offer itself. The weather was rarely fine enough for long walks in which two might drop behind the rest. And though they met at least every other evening—sometimes at the Cottage, more often at the Park—those meetings were not made for conversation. Sir John and Lady Middleton assembled people for the same reasons they set out refreshments: for noise, for movement, for occupation. Leisure, in their world, was something to be filled at once with eating, drinking, laughter, cards, consequences, and any game that made enough clamour to drown thought. No one, least of all Sir John, would have imagined any meeting could occur for the sake of a quiet chat; and thus there was little general discourse and none at all for private.

One or two such bustling evenings passed, leaving Elinor still without her chance, when Sir John appeared at the Cottage one morning with an urgency that he called charity.

They must all dine at the Park that day, he said; he was obliged to attend the club at Exeter, and Lady Middleton would otherwise be quite alone—except, indeed, for her mother and the two Miss Steeles. He spoke as if those three were a kind of emptiness that required a remedy.

Elinor, foreseeing that a party without Sir John’s boisterous direction might be quieter, more at liberty under Lady Middleton’s tranquil, well-bred governance, accepted at once. Margaret, with her mother’s permission, was equally ready. Marianne, who was always reluctant to join these visits, resisted in her usual way, but yielded to Mrs. Dashwood’s entreaties; her mother could not bear to see her shut herself away from any chance of amusement, however small.

So they went; and Lady Middleton was, as Sir John had wished, preserved from the solitude she dreaded.

The meeting proved exactly what Elinor had expected—insipid to the last degree. Not one new idea, not one lively turn of expression, enlivened their talk in dining parlour or drawing-room. When they withdrew, the children came with them; and with so many small voices, little feet, and Lady Middleton’s continual attention to nursery details, Elinor knew it would be useless to attempt fixing Lucy’s mind upon any subject of consequence.

At last the tea-things were removed. The room seemed to breathe out, as a kitchen does when the last pan is set aside and the coals settle. A card-table was brought in; and Elinor began to wonder at herself for ever imagining that the Park could furnish time for confidential conversation.

They all rose, preparing for a round game, when Lady Middleton, turning to Lucy, said, “I am glad you are not going to finish poor little Annamaria’s basket this evening; for I am sure it must hurt your eyes to work filigree by candlelight. We will make the dear little love some amends to-morrow, and then I hope she will not much mind it.”

The hint was enough. Lucy recollected herself instantly—quick as a cook who hears the step of the mistress of the house and at once arranges her face as carefully as her dish.

“Indeed you are very much mistaken, Lady Middleton,” she replied. “I am only waiting to know whether you can make your party without me; or I should have been at my filigree already. I would not disappoint the little angel for all the world: and if you want me at the card-table now, I am resolved to finish the basket after supper.”

“You are very good,” said Lady Middleton, pleased. “I hope it won’t hurt your eyes. Will you ring for some working candles? My poor little girl would be sadly disappointed, I know, if the basket was not finished to-morrow; for though I told her it certainly would not, I am sure she depends upon having it done.”

Lucy drew her work-table nearer, and reseated herself with an alacrity so studied, so bright, it was meant to signify that no greater delight could be imagined than weaving pale strips of paper into a little filigree basket for a spoiled child.

Lady Middleton proposed a rubber of Casino to the others. No one objected but Marianne, who, with that heedlessness of form which sprang in her from sincerity rather than intention to offend, exclaimed, “Your Ladyship will have the goodness to excuse me—you know I detest cards. I shall go to the piano-forte; I have not touched it since it was tuned.”

And without further ceremony, she crossed the room and sat down at the instrument.

Lady Middleton’s look was a silent thanksgiving that she had never uttered so rude a speech.

Elinor, anxious to smooth the offence, said gently, “Marianne can never keep long from that instrument, ma’am, you know; and I do not much wonder at it—for it is the very best toned piano-forte I ever heard.”

The remaining five drew their cards. Elinor, seeing her chance, continued with an ease meant to seem accidental, “Perhaps, if I should happen to cut out, I may be of some use to Miss Lucy Steele, in rolling her papers for her. There is so much still to be done to the basket, that it must be impossible, I think, for her to finish it this evening singly. I should like the work exceedingly, if she would allow me a share in it.”

“Indeed I shall be very much obliged to you for your help,” cried Lucy, with a quickness that made the gratitude sound like eagerness. “For I find there is more to be done to it than I thought there was; and it would be a shocking thing to disappoint dear Annamaria after all.”

“Oh! that would be terrible indeed,” said Miss Steele. “Dear little soul, how I do love her!”

“You are very kind,” Lady Middleton said to Elinor. “And as you really like the work, perhaps you will be as well pleased not to cut in till another rubber—or will you take your chance now?”

Elinor—joyfully profiting by the proposal, and by a little address which Marianne would never have practised—gained her end and pleased Lady Middleton too. Lucy made room with ready attention; and thus the two rivals were seated side by side at the same table, and, with the utmost harmony of hands, engaged in forwarding the same delicate labour—rolling, folding, fitting, the dry whisper of paper-splints passing between their fingers like a restrained kind of speech.

Marianne, meanwhile, had forgotten that any one was in the room besides herself. The piano-forte—new-tuned, clear-voiced—filled the air with sound. Notes rose and fell like birds over a quiet field; they covered, mercifully, the smaller noises of the room, and made a sheltered space in which words might be spoken and not carried.

Elinor, feeling that shelter, judged she might now, under the screen of Marianne’s music, venture at last to introduce the subject that lay like an undissolved bitterness at the bottom of her heart—without risk of being heard at the card-table.

Elinor waited till the room had settled into its usual evening order—the cards laid straight, the candles trimmed, the air faintly warmed by the last of the tea—and then, with a steadiness that was careful rather than bold, turned again to the confidence Lucy had pressed upon her.

“I should be unworthy of what you have honoured me with,” she said, “if I did not wish for its continuance, or if I pretended to have no further curiosity in what so deeply concerns you. I shall not apologize, therefore, for speaking of it again.”

“Thank you,” Lucy cried, all glow and gratitude at once, as if Elinor had unclasped a tight bodice for her. “You have set my heart at ease. For I was—somehow or other—afraid you had took offence at what I told you on Monday.”

“Offended!” Elinor answered with sincere astonishment, and an honesty that made her voice warmer than her face. “How could you suppose it? Nothing could be farther from my intention than to give you such an idea. Could you have any motive for trusting me, that was not—at once—honourable to you, and flattering to me?”

“And yet I do assure you,” Lucy persisted, her little sharp eyes bright with meaning, “there seemed to me a coldness—something displeased—in your manner, that made me quite uncomfortable. I felt sure you was angry with me; and I have been quarrelling with myself ever since for taking such a liberty as to trouble you with my affairs. But I am very glad to find it was only my own fancy, and that you really do not blame me. If you knew what a consolation it is to me, to relieve my heart by speaking to you of what I am thinking of every moment of my life, your compassion would make you overlook every thing else, I am sure.”

Elinor, who had listened as one listens to a soundscape one cannot leave—a steady drip, a wheel’s incessant creak—answered with gentleness, because gentleness was her habit even when her feelings were sore.

“Indeed, I can easily believe it was a great relief to you, to acknowledge your situation to me; and you shall never have reason to repent it. Your case is unfortunate. You seem surrounded with difficulties, and you will need all your mutual affection to support you under them. Mr. Ferrars, I believe, is entirely dependent on his mother.”

“He has only two thousand pounds of his own,” Lucy said quickly, as if she had counted it out coin by coin on her tongue. “It would be madness to marry upon that; though for my own part I could give up every prospect of more without a sigh. I have been always used to a small income, and could struggle with any poverty for him. But I love him too well to be the selfish means of robbing him—perhaps—of all that his mother might give him if he married to please her. We must wait—maybe for many years. With almost every other man in the world, it would be alarming; but Edward’s affection and constancy—nothing can deprive me of that, I know.”

“That conviction must be every thing to you,” Elinor said, “and he is undoubtedly supported by the same trust in yours. If the strength of your reciprocal attachment had failed—as between many people, and under many circumstances it naturally would during a four years’ engagement—your situation would have been pitiable indeed.”

Lucy lifted her eyes at this; but Elinor kept her own countenance guarded, as a cook guards a simmering pot from boiling over—watchful, silent, determined that no sudden gust of feeling should spill what must not be shown.

“Edward’s love for me,” Lucy continued, “has been put to the test by our long—very long—absence since we were first engaged; and it has stood the trial so well, that I should be unpardonable to doubt it now. I can safely say he has never gave me one moment’s alarm on that account from the first.”

Elinor hardly knew whether to smile at the complacency, or sigh at the blindness—or the skill—with which it was spoken. Lucy, encouraged by her own certainty, went on as if she were setting out proofs like ingredients upon a board.

“I am rather of a jealous temper by nature, too; and from our different situations in life—his being so much more in the world than me—and our continual separation, I was enough inclined for suspicion, to have found out the truth in an instant, if there had been the slightest alteration in his behaviour when we met, or any lowness of spirits that I could not account for; or if he had talked more of one lady than another, or seemed in any respect less happy at Longstaple than he used to be. I do not mean to say that I am particularly observant or quick-sighted in general; but in such a case, I am sure I could not be deceived.”

All this, Elinor thought, may be very prettily said; but it can impose upon neither of us.

After a short silence—filled, as so many silences are, by the small domestic noises that keep a household alive: the light scratch of a chair-leg, the soft fall of a card on felt, the crackle of a coal that remembered it had once been wood—Elinor asked, because she must.

“But what are your views? Or have you none but that of waiting for Mrs. Ferrars’s death—which is a melancholy and shocking extremity?
