Is her son determined to submit to this, and to all the tediousness of the many years of suspense in which it may involve you, rather than run the risk of her displeasure for a while by owning the truth?”

“If we could be certain it would be only for a while!” Lucy returned. “But Mrs. Ferrars is a very headstrong proud woman; and in her first fit of anger, upon hearing it, would very likely secure every thing to Robert. And the idea of that—for Edward’s sake—frightens away all my inclination for hasty measures.”

“And for your own sake too,” Elinor said, unable to leave the point untouched, “or you are carrying disinterestedness beyond reason.”

Lucy looked at her again and said nothing, the silence held between them like a dish set down and not tasted.

“Do you know Mr. Robert Ferrars?” Elinor asked at last.

“Not at all. I never saw him,” Lucy replied, with a quick little shrug. “But I fancy he is very unlike his brother—silly, and a great coxcomb.”

“A great coxcomb!” Miss Steele repeated at once, her ear catching at the words as a spoon catches at a pot’s rim. Marianne’s music had paused for a breath, and Nancy’s voice darted into it. “Oh, they are talking of their favourite beaux, I dare say.”

“No, sister,” Lucy cried, colouring with vexation, “you are mistaken there. Our favourite beaux are not great coxcombs.”

“I can answer for it that Miss Dashwood’s is not,” said Mrs. Jennings, laughing heartily, in that full-bodied, comfortable manner of hers, as if her mirth were a kind of hospitality. “For he is one of the modestest, prettiest behaved young men I ever saw. But as for Lucy—she is such a sly little creature, there is no finding out who she likes.”

“Oh,” Nancy cried, glancing round with significance, “I dare say Lucy’s beau is quite as modest and pretty behaved as Miss Dashwood’s.”

Elinor blushed in spite of all her composure. Lucy bit her lip, and turned on her sister a look sharp enough to cut thread. A mutual silence followed; and then Marianne, as if she felt the room growing coarse with talk, threw herself again into her concerto with such power that the notes rose like a wind through the trees—sound made clean, sound made shelter. Under that magnificent cover, Lucy leaned closer, and lowered her voice.

“I will honestly tell you of one scheme,” she whispered, “which has lately come into my head for bringing matters to bear. Indeed I am bound to let you into the secret, for you are a party concerned. I dare say you have seen enough of Edward to know he would prefer the church to every other profession. Now my plan is that he should take orders as soon as he can; and then, through your interest—which I am sure you would be kind enough to use out of friendship for him, and I hope out of some regard to me—your brother might be persuaded to give him Norland living; which I understand is a very good one, and the present incumbent not likely to live a great while. That would be enough for us to marry upon; and we might trust to time and chance for the rest.”

“I should always be happy,” Elinor replied, “to show any mark of my esteem and friendship for Mr. Ferrars. But do you not perceive that my interest on such an occasion would be perfectly unnecessary? He is brother to Mrs. John Dashwood—that must be recommendation enough to her husband.”

“But Mrs. John Dashwood would not much approve of Edward’s going into orders.”

“Then I rather suspect that my interest would do very little.”

Again they were silent, while the music filled the spaces between them with a kind of living hush, like the steady pulse of a well-managed kitchen where every hand knows its task. At length Lucy sighed, deep enough to seem practiced.

“I believe it would be the wisest way to put an end to the business at once by dissolving the engagement. We seem so beset with difficulties on every side, that though it would make us miserable for a time, we should be happier perhaps in the end. But you will not give me your advice, Miss Dashwood?”

“No,” Elinor answered, and forced a smile to cover feelings that struggled like a flame in draught. “On such a subject I certainly will not. You know very well my opinion would have no weight with you, unless it were on the side of your wishes.”

“Indeed you wrong me,” Lucy said, with great solemnity, as if she were pronouncing a vow. “I know nobody of whose judgment I think so highly as I do of yours; and I do really believe that if you was to say to me, ‘I advise you by all means to put an end to your engagement with Edward Ferrars; it will be more for the happiness of both of you,’ I should resolve upon doing it immediately.”

Elinor blushed—not now for herself, but for the insincerity of the woman who could speak so, and yet hold so tightly.

“This compliment,” she said, “would effectually frighten me from giving any opinion, had I formed one. It raises my influence much too high. The power of dividing two people so tenderly attached is too much for an indifferent person.”

“’Tis because you are an indifferent person,” Lucy returned, with a little pique, laying a pointed stress on the word, “that your judgment might justly have such weight with me. If you could be supposed to be biased in any respect by your own feelings, your opinion would not be worth having.”

Elinor thought it wisest to make no answer. There are moments when speech is a kind of seasoning that will only draw out bitterness; and she had no desire to provoke them both into an ease and unreserve that could end only in something unworthy. She was even half determined never to mention the subject again.

A pause followed; long enough for the room’s ordinary life to assert itself—the gentle slap of cards, Mrs. Jennings’s contented chuckle, and, beyond the walls, the more ancient sounds of the place: a distant gate’s complaint, the soft animal breath of the night wind over the park. Then Lucy, as if she had never been piqued at all, returned to her accustomed complacency.

“Shall you be in town this winter, Miss Dashwood?”

“Certainly not.”

“I am sorry for that,” Lucy said, and Elinor saw her eyes brighten at the refusal as plainly as if a candle had been raised under them. “It would have gave me such pleasure to meet you there! But I dare say you will go for all that. To be sure, your brother and sister will ask you to come to them.”

“It will not be in my power to accept their invitation, if they do.”

“How unlucky that is! I had quite depended upon meeting you there. Anne and me are to go the latter end of January to some relations who have been wanting us to visit them these several years. But I only go for the sake of seeing Edward. He will be there in February; otherwise London would have no charms for me. I have not spirits for it.”

Before Elinor could reply, the first rubber ended, and she was called to the card-table. Thus the confidential discourse concluded—not with reluctance on either side, for nothing had been said to make them dislike each other less than they had done before. Elinor sat down with a melancholy persuasion, heavier than any mere jealousy: that Edward was not only without affection for the person meant to be his wife, but had not even the chance of being tolerably happy in marriage—the sort of happiness sincere affection on her side might have given him; for self-interest alone could induce a woman to hold a man to an engagement of which she seemed so thoroughly aware that he was weary.

From this time, Elinor never revived the subject. When Lucy entered upon it—and she seldom missed an opportunity, being particularly careful to inform her confidante of her “happiness” whenever a letter came from Edward—Elinor treated it with calmness and caution, and dismissed it as soon as civility would allow. She felt such conversations to be an indulgence Lucy did not deserve, and a danger to herself.

Meanwhile, the Miss Steeles’ visit at Barton Park stretched itself far beyond the first invitation, as dough stretches under a patient hand—pressed, yielded, pressed again. Their favour increased; they could not be spared. Sir John would not hear of their going. In spite of their numerous engagements in Exeter—in spite of the absolute necessity of returning immediately, which rose in full force at the end of every week and was as regularly forgotten at the beginning of the next—they were prevailed on to stay nearly two months, and to assist in the due celebration of that season which seems to require, to proclaim its importance, more than an ordinary share of private balls and large dinners; nights of bright rooms and louder laughter, while outside the older world held to its own quieter language of wind in the trees and distant hoofbeats on winter ground.

Mrs. Jennings, for all her bustling fondness and her habit of roosting from one child’s house to another like a contented, well-fed bird, was not without a hearth that belonged solely to herself. Since her husband’s death—he who had made his money in a noisier, less genteel quarter of the town, where carts rattled and vendors cried their wares from dawn—she had kept a winter house near Portman Square. As January drew near, she began to speak of London the way a practiced cook speaks of a familiar kitchen: with certainty, appetite, and an impatience that did not permit delay.

One day, as abruptly as if she had clapped down a pot-lid mid-simmer, she asked the two elder Miss Dashwoods to go with her.

Elinor answered at once—grateful, but absolute—declining for both, and giving the reason that seemed, to her mind, sufficient and final: they were determined not to leave their mother at that season.

Marianne’s colour shifted as if a sudden heat had passed over her; her eyes brightened with a look that betrayed how little indifferent she was to London. Elinor, intent on propriety and the comfort of home, did not at first mark that quick, betraying change.

Mrs. Jennings stared, then began again, the way she always began again—by sheer force of good humour.

“Oh, Lord! your mother can spare you very well. I do beg you’ll favour me with your company, for I’ve quite set my heart upon it. Don’t fancy you’ll be any inconvenience. I shan’t put myself out of my way for you—not I. It’s only sending Betty by the coach, and I hope I can afford that. We three shall do very well in my chaise; and when we’re in town, if you don’t like to go wherever I go, why, you can go with one of my daughters. Your mother won’t object. I’ve had such good luck in getting my own children off my hands, she’ll think me very fit to have the charge of you; and if I don’t get one of you at least well married before I’ve done with you, it shan’t be my fault. I’ll speak a good word for you to all the young men—depend upon it.”

Sir John, who could no more bear quiet than some people can bear smoke, laughed out his agreement. Even in winter, Barton’s air had its gentle sounds—the wind in bare branches, the hush of fields lying fallow—yet he always seemed to hear in that hush a kind of loneliness that frightened him.

“I have a notion,” said he, “that Miss Marianne would not object, if her elder sister would come into it. It’s very hard indeed that she should not have a little pleasure because Miss Dashwood does not wish it. So I would advise you two to set off for town when you’re tired of Barton, without saying a word to Miss Dashwood about it.”

“Nay, nay,” cried Mrs. Jennings, as if scolding flour from a sleeve. “I shall be monstrous glad of Miss Marianne’s company whether Miss Dashwood goes or not—only the more the merrier, say I. I thought it would be more comfortable for them to be together; because if they got tired of me, they might talk to one another and laugh at my odd ways behind my back. But one or the other, if not both, I must have. Lord bless me! how can I live poking by myself, I who’ve always had Charlotte with me till this winter? Come, Miss Marianne—let us strike hands upon the bargain; and if Miss Dashwood changes her mind by and bye, why, so much the better.”

Marianne answered with a warmth that made her voice tremble, as if the very word London carried in it a music she could already hear through the miles.

“I thank you, ma’am—sincerely thank you. Your invitation has ensured my gratitude for ever; and it would give me such happiness—yes, almost the greatest happiness I am capable of—to be able to accept it. But my mother—my dearest, kindest mother—if she were to be made less happy, less comfortable by our absence—Oh! no. Nothing should tempt me to leave her. It should not, must not be a struggle.”

Mrs. Jennings assured her again—loudly, kindly—that Mrs. Dashwood could spare them very well. And Elinor, who now understood her sister’s sudden brightness and the eagerness beneath it—the way one might understand, from a single deep breath, what dish another has been craving—pressed her opposition no farther in direct terms. She referred it to her mother’s decision.

She scarcely expected support. Whatever Marianne desired, Mrs. Dashwood was eager to promote; she had never been taught to distrust this particular attachment, and Elinor dared not explain the private reasons that made London, for herself, a place of danger rather than pleasure.

What struck Elinor most was not only Marianne’s haste to be gone, but her willingness to swallow what she had always found distasteful. Marianne, fastidious, sensitive, often wounded by coarse speech as by a too-sour wine, was thoroughly acquainted with Mrs. Jennings’s manners and habitually disgusted by them. Yet now she was ready to overlook everything—every broad allusion, every ill-timed jest, every clatter of talk that drowned softer voices—as if the whole world’s noise could be endured for the chance of meeting one person again. To Elinor it was proof—strong, full, undeniable—of the importance of that object; proof she had not been prepared to see so plainly.

When Mrs. Dashwood heard of the invitation, she accepted it in the spirit with which she accepted most things that promised delight. She pictured the change as one pictures a new arrangement of rooms or a different seasoning in familiar fare: an improvement, an enlivening.

“I am delighted with the plan,” she cried. “It is exactly what I could wish. Margaret and I shall be as much benefited by it as yourselves. When you and the Middletons are gone, we shall go on so quietly and happily together with our books and our music! You will find Margaret so improved when you come back again! I have a little plan of alteration for your bedrooms too, which may now be performed without any inconvenience. It is very right that you should go to town; I would have every young woman of your condition in life acquainted with the manners and amusements of London. You will be under the care of a motherly, good sort of woman, of whose kindness I can have no doubt. And in all probability you will see your brother; and whatever may be his faults, or the faults of his wife—when I consider whose son he is, I cannot bear to have you so wholly estranged.”

Elinor, though she loved her mother’s cheerfulness, felt, in this moment, the same discomfort one feels when a bright tune is played too loudly in a small room: it smothers the subtler notes. She ventured, as cautiously as she could, to name one remaining objection.

“Though with your usual anxiety for our happiness you have been obviating every impediment,” said she, “there is still one objection which, in my opinion, cannot be so easily removed.”

Marianne’s face fell.

“And what,” said Mrs. Dashwood, smiling at her “dear prudent Elinor,” “is she going to suggest now? What formidable obstacle? Do not let me hear a word about the expense.”

“It is not the expense. My objection is this: though I think very well of Mrs. Jennings’s heart, she is not a woman whose society can afford us pleasure, or whose protection will give us consequence.”

“That is very true,” replied her mother; “but of her society, separately from that of other people, you will scarcely have anything at all; and you will almost always appear in public with Lady Middleton.”

“If Elinor is frightened away by her dislike of Mrs. Jennings,” cried Marianne, quick as flame catching, “at least it need not prevent my accepting her invitation. I have no such scruples, and I am sure I could put up with every unpleasantness of that kind with very little effort.”

Elinor could not help smiling—not in mockery, but with a kind of resigned tenderness—at this sudden indifference toward manners that Marianne had so often found unbearable. She resolved then, silently, that if Marianne went, she would go likewise. It did not seem proper that Marianne should be left to the sole guidance of her own judgement; nor did Elinor wish Mrs. Jennings, well-meaning as she was, to be abandoned entirely to Marianne’s variable patience for the whole comfort of her domestic hours.

The determination was easier when Elinor remembered what Lucy Steele had said—that Edward Ferrars was not expected in town before February. Their visit, without any unreasonable abridgement, might be finished first.

“I will have you both go,” declared Mrs. Dashwood. “These objections are nonsensical. You will have much pleasure in being in London, and especially in being together; and if Elinor would ever condescend to anticipate enjoyment, she would foresee it there from a variety of sources—she would, perhaps, expect some from improving her acquaintance with her sister-in-law’s family.”

Elinor, who had often wished for an opening to loosen her mother’s confidence a little—so the shock might be less when the truth must come—forced herself, even now, to begin, though the attempt felt nearly hopeless.

“I like Edward Ferrars very much,” she said, with all the calmness she could command, “and shall always be glad to see him; but as to the rest of the family, it is a matter of perfect indifference to me whether I am ever known to them or not.”

Mrs. Dashwood only smiled, and said nothing. Marianne lifted her eyes in astonishment, as if Elinor had spoken in some colder dialect, where affection had different names and fewer shades. Elinor, seeing the look, thought she might as well have been silent.

After very little more discourse, it was settled: the invitation was accepted.

Mrs. Jennings received the news with overflowing joy, and a chorus of assurances—care, kindness, attention—promised with the same hearty confidence with which she would promise a table well spread. Sir John was delighted too; to a man whose chief dread was being alone, the thought of adding two more familiar faces to London’s crowd was comfort enough to set him laughing. Even Lady Middleton took the trouble to appear delighted—a considerable exertion, as it obliged her to stir herself out of habitual composure.

As for the Miss Steeles, and Lucy in particular, they had never been so happy in their lives as this intelligence made them.

Elinor submitted to the arrangement with less reluctance than she had expected. For herself it was now a matter of unconcern whether she went to town or not; and when she saw her mother so thoroughly pleased, and Marianne so exhilarated—her whole manner revived, her voice animated, her gaiety raised above its usual pitch—Elinor could not be dissatisfied with the cause, and would scarcely allow herself to distrust the consequence.

Marianne’s joy, indeed, was almost beyond happiness; it was a restless, bright perturbation, as if every sound at Barton—the creak of a gate, the crackle of a fire, the soft step on a stair—only reminded her that time still moved too slowly. Her unwillingness to quit her mother was the only check upon that impatience; and when the moment of parting arrived, her grief on that account was excessive. Mrs. Dashwood’s affliction was hardly less; and Elinor was the only one of the three who seemed able to remember that separations, even tender ones, were not eternal.

They departed in the first week of January. The Middletons were to follow in about a week. The Miss Steeles kept their station at the park, and were to quit it only with the rest of the family.

Elinor could not seat herself beside Mrs. Jennings, and feel the carriage-wheels begin their long urging toward London, without a quiet astonishment at the turn her life had taken. It seemed but yesterday that their acquaintance with this bustling, benevolent woman had begun; that their ages, their habits, their very manner of thinking had appeared so ill matched; that Mrs. Jennings herself had raised objections—loud, repeated, and to her mind unanswerable—against carrying off the Dashwood ladies to town. Yet objections, like yesterday’s crust, had been softened by warmth and time. Mrs. Dashwood and Marianne, with that ardour which makes youth mistake eagerness for certainty, had smiled away every doubt. Elinor, though she could not always still the small, persistent misgiving that Willoughby’s attentions were too brilliant to be safely trusted, could not look on Marianne’s bright, expectant rapture—her whole soul leaning forward as if London already held out its arms—without feeling how pale her own prospect was beside it. How easily, she thought, could she embrace anxiety, if only she might also possess hope; how willingly exchange her cheerless composure for the keen suspense that animated Marianne.

A short time—very short—must decide what Willoughby intended; he was, in all likelihood, already in town. Marianne’s impatience to be gone declared plainly where she expected her happiness to be waiting. Elinor resolved, therefore, not merely to gather whatever light London might throw on his character, but to observe his behaviour to Marianne with such steady attention as should soon determine what he was, and what he meant. If her judgement proved unfavourable, she would at any cost try to open her sister’s eyes. If it proved otherwise, she must school herself into a more generous mind—must banish the selfish comparisons which stole in even when she condemned them, and allow Marianne’s joy to be unclouded by her own secret regrets.

They were three days upon the road; three days of cramped sitting, cold starts, and warmed inns; of the ceaseless accompaniment of hoof and wheel, and the changing music of England as they passed it—the thin winter wind worrying at hedges, the cawing of rooks over bare fields, the sudden roar and then hush of a coaching-yard as they swung in beneath an archway and the world became voices, chains, and stamping horses. Mrs. Jennings, hearty as a good kitchen fire, found something to remark upon in every meal and every bedchamber. If the inn’s hallway smelt of wet wool and smoke, she would lament that nobody kept a proper clear flame now; if the broth were thin, she would declare that people had grown stingy with bones; if the butter were sweet, she praised the dairy; if it were rank, she prophesied ruin to the farmer. She fretted, with the affectionate tyranny of a hostess by anticipation, that she could not make them choose their own dinners, nor extort a confession of preferring salmon to cod, boiled fowl to veal cutlets; and she treated such preferences as a matter of character, as if a woman’s heart might be read in her appetite.

Marianne, meanwhile, was a pattern of what future companionableness to Mrs. Jennings might be expected—if pattern meant a beautiful absence. She sat silent almost the whole way, wrapped in meditation and expectation, speaking scarcely at all unless a sudden glimpse of picturesque beauty—a pale river under leafless trees, a church tower rising over mist—drew from her an exclamation addressed to Elinor alone. To atone for this, Elinor took immediate possession of the post of civility she had assigned herself. She talked with Mrs. Jennings; she listened; she laughed when laughter was expected; she answered question after question about Barton and its neighbourhood, about people Mrs. Jennings half knew and wholly delighted in discussing. The very clatter of the road seemed to insist on common talk, and Elinor obliged it. If Marianne would not be drawn into the ordinary comfort of companionship, Elinor would at least make the journey tolerable to their friend.

They reached London by three o’clock on the third day, glad to escape the long confinement and to surrender stiff limbs to the luxury of a good fire. Mrs. Jennings’s house was handsome, and handsomely fitted up; they were shown at once into a comfortable apartment which had formerly belonged to Charlotte. Over the mantelpiece hung a landscape wrought in coloured silks—patient hills and a slow river—evidence that seven years at a great school in town had not passed without its expected accomplishments. Elinor looked at it with a kind of melancholy amusement; so much labour, so little life, and yet it was valued, spoken of, framed and displayed, like a proof of worth in a language some women were forced to learn.

Dinner was not to be ready in less than two hours; and Elinor, feeling that her mother must be made easy by some account of their arrival, sat down to write home. Marianne, in a few moments, took up pen and paper also.

“I am writing home, Marianne,” said Elinor. “Had not you better defer your letter for a day or two?”

“I am not going to write to my mother,” Marianne replied hastily, as if the words were a door shut to further inquiry.

Elinor said no more; but her mind made its own quick step. Marianne, then, wrote to Willoughby. The conclusion that followed—instant, almost involuntary—was that, however mysteriously they wished to manage the affair, they must be engaged. The conviction did not satisfy all her prudence; but it did, in spite of herself, give her pleasure. An engagement would explain so much; it would give honourable shape to Marianne’s confidence, and purpose to Willoughby’s apparent devotion. She returned to her letter with more alacrity.

Marianne’s was finished in a very few minutes; in length it could be no more than a note. It was folded, sealed, directed with eager rapidity. Elinor fancied she distinguished, in the bold sweep of the address, a large W; and no sooner was it complete than Marianne rang the bell and requested the footman to have it conveyed to the two-penny post. That decided the matter as far as outward signs could decide it.

Marianne’s spirits remained high, yet they fluttered with a nervous lightness that made them less pleasurable to witness. As evening came on, her agitation increased; she could scarcely eat any dinner, though Mrs. Jennings lamented the smallness of her appetite with all the earnestness of one who believed nourishment a remedy for every trouble of the heart. When they returned to the drawing-room, Marianne seemed to listen anxiously to every passing sound. In London, even in a good neighbourhood, the air is never still: there are distant wheels, the calling of chairmen, the rise and fall of voices in the street, the occasional sharp report of a carriage-door, like a hand knocking at the heart. Marianne started at each, her attention strained, as if the very city were a great instrument playing only one awaited note.

It was a great satisfaction to Elinor that Mrs. Jennings, being much engaged in her own room, saw little of what passed. The tea-things were brought in. The kettle’s low murmur, the small clink of porcelain, the comfortable breath of hot water and leaves—these homely sounds and smells, which in quieter circumstances might have settled the mind, only made Marianne’s vigilance more painful. Already she had been disappointed more than once by a rap at a neighbouring door, when a loud one was suddenly heard—so distinct, so near, that it could not be mistaken for any house but their own. Elinor felt secure it announced Willoughby’s approach. Marianne started up and moved toward the door.

Everything fell silent. Such silence could not be borne. Marianne opened the door, advanced a few steps toward the stairs, listened half a minute with her whole being, and returned to the room in all the agitation which a conviction of having heard him would naturally produce. In the ecstasy of that instant she could not help exclaiming, “Oh, Elinor, it is Willoughby—indeed it is!” and seemed almost ready to throw herself into his arms—when Colonel Brandon appeared.

The shock was too great to be met with calmness. Marianne immediately left the room.

Elinor, too, was disappointed; the hope she had not acknowledged even to herself had leapt at that rap. But regard for Colonel Brandon ensured him welcome, and something like pain followed close upon it: pain that he, so partial to Marianne, should be made to feel—must see at once—that her first emotion at the sight of him was nothing but grief and mortification. She perceived instantly that it was not unnoticed. He even looked after Marianne as she quitted the room, with such astonishment and concern as almost robbed him of recollection for what civility demanded.

“Is your sister ill?” said he.

Elinor answered, in distress, that she was; and then, hurriedly, she spoke of headaches, low spirits, fatigue—of everything to which she could decently attribute Marianne’s behaviour. He listened with earnest attention; then, as if recollecting himself and forcing a gentler composure into his features, said no more of it. He began to speak of his pleasure at seeing them in London, and made the usual inquiries about their journey, and the friends they had left behind. The talk went on in that calm way which is meant to be friendly and becomes only formal when hearts are elsewhere. Both were out of spirits. Elinor’s thoughts pursued one name; Brandon’s, she suspected, pursued another, with a heaviness he would not expose.

Elinor wished very much to ask whether Willoughby were in town; but she feared to give Colonel Brandon pain by naming his rival. At length, merely to say something, she asked if he had been in London ever since she had seen him last.

“Yes,” he replied, with some embarrassment, “almost ever since. I have been once or twice at Delaford for a few days, but it has never been in my power to return to Barton.”

The words and the manner brought back to Elinor’s mind all the circumstances of his quitting Barton, and the uneasiness and suspicions they had caused to Mrs. Jennings. She was fearful her question had implied more curiosity than she had ever truly felt.

Mrs. Jennings soon came in, full of noise and cheerfulness, as if she brought with her a whole bustling household.

“Oh! Colonel,” cried she, “I am monstrous glad to see you—sorry I could not come before—beg your pardon, but I have been forced to look about me a little, and settle my matters; for it is a long while since I have been at home, and you know one has always a world of little odd things to do after one has been away; and then I have had Cartwright to settle with. Lord! I have been as busy as a bee ever since dinner! But pray, Colonel, how came you to conjure out that I should be in town to-day?”

“I had the pleasure of hearing it at Mr. Palmer’s, where I have been dining.”

“Oh, you did? Well, and how do they all do? How does Charlotte do? I warrant you she is a fine size by this time.”

“Mrs. Palmer appeared quite well, and I am commissioned to tell you, that you will certainly see her to-morrow.”

“Ay, to be sure, I thought as much. Well, Colonel, I have brought two young ladies with me, you see—that is, you see but one of them now, but there is another somewhere. Your friend, Miss Marianne, too—which you will not be sorry to hear. I do not know what you and Mr. Willoughby will do between you about her. Ay, it is a fine thing to be young and handsome! Well! I was young once, but I never was very handsome—worse luck for me. However, I got a very good husband, and I don’t know what the greatest beauty can do more. Ah! poor man! he has been dead these eight years and better. But Colonel, where have you been to since we parted? And how does your business go on? Come, come, let’s have no secrets among friends.”

He replied with his customary mildness to all her questions, but without satisfying her in any. Elinor began to make the tea—measuring leaves, pouring the water, watching the steam rise with a momentary absorption in the small exactness of the task, as if the senses could be persuaded to quiet the mind. Marianne was obliged to appear again. After her entrance, Colonel Brandon grew more thoughtful and silent than before, and Mrs. Jennings could not prevail on him to stay long.

No other visitor came that evening. The ladies agreed, with unusual unanimity, to go early to bed. The house, settling for the night, had its own soft language: doors eased to, coals shifted and sighed, footsteps above and below grew fewer. Yet for Marianne, silence was not rest; it was only the pause in which expectation could speak loudest.

She rose the next morning with recovered spirits and happy looks. The disappointment of the evening before appeared forgotten, absorbed into the confidence of what must surely happen to-day. They had not long finished breakfast when Mrs. Palmer’s barouche stopped at the door; and in a few minutes she came laughing into the room, delighted beyond measure—so delighted that it was hard to say whether she took most pleasure in meeting her mother or the Miss Dashwoods again. She was surprised at their coming to town, though it was what she had rather expected all along; angry that they had accepted her mother’s invitation after declining her own, though she declared, with perfect sincerity, that she never would have forgiven them if they had not come.

“Mr. Palmer will be so happy to see you,” said she. “What do you think he said when he heard of your coming with Mama? I forget what it was now, but it was something so droll!”

After an hour or two spent in what Mrs. Jennings called comfortable chat—meaning, in truth, every variety of inquiry concerning all their acquaintance on her side, and laughter without cause on Mrs. Palmer’s—it was proposed by Mrs. Palmer that they should all accompany her to some shops where she had business that morning. Mrs. Jennings and Elinor readily consented, as they too had purchases to make. Marianne declined at first, but was induced to go.

Wherever they went, Marianne was evidently on the watch. In Bond Street especially, where much of their business lay, her eyes were in constant inquiry, sweeping faces, doorways, the passing carriages; and in whatever shop the party were engaged, her mind was equally abstracted from everything actually before them. There were counters bright with ribbon and gloves, cabinets where perfumed soaps were stacked like little carved blocks of luxury, baskets of oranges breathing out a sharp sweetness that cut through the city’s damp—yet Marianne saw none of it, or saw it as one sees a table set when the appetite is not for food but for news. Elinor asked her opinion on an article that concerned them both; Marianne scarcely heard. Mrs. Palmer’s attention, by contrast, was caught by everything pretty, expensive, or new; she was wild to buy all, could determine on none, and dawdled away her time in rapture and indecision. Elinor, who could have smiled at such folly on another day, found it almost unendurable now, because every delay lengthened Marianne’s suspense.

It was late in the morning before they returned home. No sooner had they entered the house than Marianne flew eagerly upstairs; and when Elinor followed, she found her turning from the table with a sorrowful countenance that declared no Willoughby had been there.

“Has no letter been left here for me since we went out?” she asked the footman, who entered at that moment with parcels.

He answered, No.

“Are you quite sure of it?” she insisted. “Are you certain that no servant, no porter, has left any letter or note?”

He repeated that none had.

“How very odd!” Marianne said in a low, disappointed voice, turning away to the window.

“How odd, indeed,” repeated Elinor within herself, and uneasiness sharpened her thought. If Marianne had not known him to be in town, she would not have written as she did; she would have written to Combe Magna. And if he was in town—how strange that he should neither come nor write! Oh, my dear mother, you must be wrong to permit an engagement—if engagement there is—between a daughter so young and a man so little known, to be carried on in so doubtful, so mysterious a manner. Elinor longed to inquire outright; and yet she foresaw how such interference would be borne. After some consideration she determined that if appearances continued, for many days longer, as unpleasant as they now were, she would represent to her mother, in the strongest manner, the necessity of serious inquiry.

Mrs. Palmer and two elderly ladies—intimate acquaintance of Mrs. Jennings, met and invited in the morning—dined with them. Mrs. Palmer left soon after tea to fulfil her evening engagements, and Elinor was obliged to assist in making a whist-table for the others. Marianne was of no use on these occasions; she would never learn the game. Though her time was thus her own, the evening brought no more pleasure to her than to Elinor. It was spent in the same anxious expectation and the same pain of disappointment.

She sometimes attempted to read; but the book was soon thrown aside, as tasteless as food to a fevered palate. She returned to the more interesting employment of walking backwards and forwards across the room, pausing whenever she came to the window, listening, listening always—her whole being drawn toward the outer sounds of London, as if somewhere in that restless tide of wheels and footsteps there must at last be the long-expected rap.

“If this open weather holds much longer,” Mrs. Jennings declared at breakfast, buttering her toast with the generous hand of one who believed good fare a moral duty, “Sir John will not like leaving Barton next week. ’Tis a sad thing for sportsmen to lose a day’s pleasure. Poor souls! I always pity them; they take it so to heart.”

“That is true,” Marianne cried, with a brightness that startled Elinor by its suddenness, and she crossed at once to the window, as if the whole question were to be settled by the look of the sky. The panes were cold under her fingers; beyond them, London breathed its damp, mild breath—no sting of winter yet, only that soft, indecisive warmth that left mud in the streets and a languor in the air. “I had not thought of that. This weather will keep many sportsmen in the country.”

The recollection was like a spoonful of cordial. Colour came back to her face; her eyes warmed. She returned to the table as if the very scent of tea—sharp, tannic, with the faint bergamot Mrs. Jennings liked—had restored her.

“It is charming weather for them indeed,” she went on, tasting her words as if they had flavour. “How much they must enjoy it! But—” and the sweetness soured, just perceptibly, like milk threatened by heat, “it cannot be expected to last long. At this time of year, and after such a series of rain, we shall certainly have very little more of it. Frosts will soon set in, and in all probability with severity. In another day or two perhaps; this extreme mildness can hardly last longer—nay, perhaps it may freeze tonight!”

“At any rate,” Elinor said quickly—because she heard, beneath Marianne’s talk of weather, the other name that weather carried for her sister—“I dare say we shall have Sir John and Lady Middleton in town by the end of next week.”

“Ay, my dear,” Mrs. Jennings answered, nodding, crumbs dropping cheerfully upon her napkin, “I’ll warrant you we do. Mary always has her own way.”

And Elinor, who understood that “Mary’s own way” often travelled by post, thought, with a silent sinking, Now she will write to Combe by this day’s letter. Yet if such a letter was written, it was done with a secrecy that escaped even Elinor’s careful watchfulness; no blotting-sand betrayed it, no hurried fold, no lingering over sealing-wax. And though Elinor could not feel content, she could not feel wholly miserable while Marianne looked even for a moment as she looked now—alive again, strung to expectation.

Marianne’s spirits, indeed, were fed all day—not by society, nor by any common amusement, but by the air itself. The morning was spent in making calls with Mrs. Jennings, leaving cards at the houses of her acquaintance to announce her being in town. In every doorway the world offered its little domestic signs: coal-smoke that clung to the curtains in the entry, a footman’s polished shoes creaking on stone, the thin chime of a distant musical clock competing with the raw scrape of wheels on wet pavement outside. London’s soundscape pressed upon Elinor like a constant hand—street cries and carriage-hubs, the hammering of a far-off builder, a sudden metallic shout as iron met iron—so different from Barton’s wind in trees and the hush that could settle like flour over a kitchen at dusk. Marianne, however, moved through all of it as if she were listening only to the sky.

She watched the direction of the wind between one house and another, lifting her face as they stepped from a warm parlour into the street’s cooler breath; she studied clouds as others studied faces.

“Don’t you find it colder than it was in the morning, Elinor?” she asked, drawing her muff up as if it could keep not only her hands but her heart warm. “There seems to me a very decided difference. I can hardly keep my hands warm even in my muff. It was not so yesterday, I think. The clouds seem parting too; the sun will be out in a moment, and we shall have a clear afternoon.”

Elinor was alternately diverted and pained. Marianne persevered, and in everything she met—the brightness of the fire at night, the clearer look of the atmosphere at morning—she tasted, with desperate faith, “certain symptoms of approaching frost,” as if frost were an ally, as if winter’s hard clean hand could force some long-delayed truth to the surface.

In the arrangement of Mrs. Jennings’s household there was nothing stingy, nothing pinched. Meals were plentiful, the tea-table always ready, the sideboard never bare; she managed her kitchen like a benevolent sovereign who thought hospitality part of good government. Elinor could not dislike that liberality, even when she found its forms loud. And Mrs. Jennings, though she carried about with her a few old city friends whom Lady Middleton regretted and would gladly have seen dropped, had sense enough not to drag Elinor and Marianne into any company that might truly distress them. Elinor, pleased to find herself more comfortably placed in that particular than she had expected, made her peace with the rest—though their evenings, whether at home or abroad, were generally those industrious gatherings formed only for cards, where the shuffling of hands and the smack of laid-down counters became the chief music.

Colonel Brandon had a general invitation and came almost every day. His entrance always altered the room’s tone, as if a deeper instrument had joined a thin tune. He came to look at Marianne and to talk with Elinor. Elinor often derived more satisfaction from conversing with him than from any other daily occurrence, for his mind had a steadiness that felt, in London’s racket, like stepping into a pantry where all is clean, ordered, and quietly necessary. Yet she saw, with real concern, his continued regard for her sister. She feared it was a strengthening regard. There was an earnestness in the way his eyes followed Marianne—as if he listened for her in the room the way one listens for a familiar voice in a crowded street—and his spirits were certainly worse than when at Barton.

About a week after their arrival, it became certain that Willoughby was also arrived. His card lay on the table when they came in from their morning drive, stiff and white against the polished wood, like a small thing that could cut.

“Good God!” Marianne cried. “He has been here while we were out.”

Elinor’s heart sprang—not with the same wild leap as Marianne’s, but with relief, as if a knot might at last be loosened. “Depend upon it, he will call again tomorrow,” she ventured.

Marianne scarcely heard her. When Mrs. Jennings came in, Marianne slipped away with the precious card as if it were food to be hidden, a morsel to be hoarded in famine.

From that moment, her mind never rested. The expectation of seeing him every hour made her unfit for anything. It was like waiting for a dish that has been promised—your appetite sharpened until it is pain, every sound from the street making you lift your head, certain that now, now the thing will arrive that will restore you. She insisted on being left behind the next morning when the others went out.

Elinor’s thoughts, during the drive, stayed fixed on Berkeley Street, on what might be passing there in their absence—the knocker struck, the door opened, the voice asking for Miss Dashwood. But the moment she returned, one glance at Marianne told her all. Willoughby had not come.

A note was brought in just then, and laid upon the table.

“For me!” Marianne cried, stepping forward with such haste that the paper seemed already hers by right.

“No, ma’am, for my mistress,” the servant answered.

Marianne snatched it up nevertheless. “It is indeed for Mrs. Jennings; how provoking!”

“You are expecting a letter, then?” Elinor said, no longer able to keep silence.

“Yes, a little—not much.”

There was a pause, in which the fire snapped and the street outside sent up its constant, wearying rumble, the world always moving, always loud, indifferent to one girl’s waiting.

“You have no confidence in me, Marianne.”

“Nay, Elinor, this reproach from you—you who have confidence in no one!”

“Me!” Elinor returned, colouring with the confusion of a person accused where she cannot defend herself. “Indeed, Marianne, I have nothing to tell.”

“Nor I,” Marianne answered, with a kind of fierce purity, “our situations then are alike. We have neither of us anything to tell; you, because you do not communicate, and I, because I conceal nothing.”

The injustice of it stung Elinor; yet she could not wholly repel it. There were things—one thing—she could not speak of, and Marianne’s sharp sincerity found the tender spot. She knew not, under such circumstances, how to press her sister to greater openness.

Mrs. Jennings appeared, and taking the note, read it aloud. It was from Lady Middleton, announcing their arrival in Conduit Street the night before, and requesting the company of her mother and cousins the following evening. Business on Sir John’s part, and a violent cold on her own, had prevented their calling in Berkeley Street.

The invitation was accepted. But when the hour drew near, necessary as it was in common civility that both should attend Mrs. Jennings, Elinor had difficulty in persuading Marianne to go. She had still seen nothing of Willoughby; and she was not only indisposed for any amusement abroad, but unwilling to risk his calling in her absence, as if the mere act of stepping out might betray her into missing the only moment that mattered.

Elinor found, when the evening was over, that disposition is not materially altered by a change of abode. Sir John, scarcely settled in town, had contrived to gather nearly twenty young people and to amuse them with a ball. The rooms were bright, warmed by crowded bodies and many candles; the air carried the mingled scents of wax, pomatum, damp woollen cloaks drying by the heat, and the thin sharpness of wine from the sideboard. Two violins scraped their way through country-dance measures, the sound trying hard to make itself merry against the thick London hush that exists even inside gaiety—the constant awareness of streets outside, the coach-wheels always waiting.

It was, however, an affair of which Lady Middleton did not approve. In the country, an unpremeditated dance was very allowable; but in London, where the reputation of elegance was more important and less easily attained, it was risking too much, for the gratification of a few girls, to have it known that Lady Middleton had given a small dance of eight or nine couple, with two violins, and a mere side-board collation.

Mr. and Mrs. Palmer were among the party. From Mr. Palmer, whom they had not seen since their arrival in town, they received no mark of recognition on entering; careful to avoid the appearance of attention to his mother-in-law, he looked at them slightly, as if they were strangers, and merely nodded to Mrs. Jennings from the other side of the room.

Marianne, the instant she entered, gave one swift glance around the apartment. It was enough—he was not there. She sat down, equally ill-disposed to receive or to communicate pleasure. The music might have been water poured upon stone; the laughter, the talk, the clapping of hands, all seemed to pass her without wetting her.

After they had been assembled about an hour, Mr. Palmer sauntered toward the Miss Dashwoods to express surprise on seeing them in town—though Colonel Brandon had been first informed of their arrival at his house, and Mr. Palmer himself had said something “very droll” on hearing they were to come.

“I thought you were both in Devonshire,” said he.

“Did you?” Elinor replied.

“When do you go back again?”

“I do not know.”

And thus ended their discourse.

Never had Marianne been so unwilling to dance, and never so fatigued by the exercise when at last she did. The very act of moving through the figures, of turning and stepping while her mind stood still, seemed to exhaust her. She complained of it as they returned to Berkeley Street, her voice low and tight, as if the cold she feared had taken hold of her from within.

“Aye, aye,” said Mrs. Jennings, who could not resist the comfort of her own conjectures, “we know the reason of all that very well; if a certain person who shall be nameless had been there, you would not have been a bit tired. And to say the truth, it was not very pretty of him not to give you the meeting when he was invited.”

“Invited!” Marianne cried, stopping short on the word as if it were something bitter in her mouth.

“So my daughter Middleton told me; for it seems Sir John met him somewhere in the street this morning.”

Marianne said no more; but her face, in the street-light, looked as if it had been struck.

Elinor, impatient to do anything that might lead to her sister’s relief, resolved to write the next morning to her mother. If she could not command events, she might at least summon inquiry. She hoped, by awakening her mother’s fears for Marianne’s health, to procure those questions which had been so long delayed. And she was the more eager in this measure because, after breakfast, she perceived Marianne writing again—writing with that hurried absorption which belonged now to only one name. Elinor could not suppose it was to any other person than Willoughby.

About the middle of the day, Mrs. Jennings went out alone on business. The house fell quieter. Even London’s noise seemed, by contrast, more insistent—cart-wheels grinding, a hawker’s call rising and fading, the hard knock of a neighbour’s door. Elinor began her letter directly. Marianne, too restless for employment and too anxious for conversation, walked from one window to another, or sat by the fire in melancholy meditation, staring into the coals as if she could read her fate in their shifting red.

Elinor wrote earnestly. She related what had passed, her suspicions of Willoughby’s inconstancy, and urged her mother by every plea of duty and affection to demand from Marianne an account of her real situation with respect to him. She chose her words as carefully as if she were seasoning a dish that must be strong enough to rouse, but not so strong as to offend; yet her hand trembled all the same.

The letter was scarcely finished when a rap sounded below—a sharp interruption, the house answering it with the quick echo of footsteps on stairs. Colonel Brandon was announced.

Marianne, who had seen him from the window and who hated company of any kind in her present state, left the room before he entered.

He looked more than usually grave; and though he expressed satisfaction at finding Miss Dashwood alone, as if he had something in particular to tell her, he sat for some time without speaking. The silence was filled with small noises that become loud when hearts are strained: the settling of the fire, the faint creak of leather as he moved, the distant thud of London beyond the walls.

Elinor, persuaded he had some communication to make in which Marianne was concerned, waited with impatience. It was not the first time she had felt such a conviction; for more than once before, beginning with “your sister looks unwell today,” or “your sister seems out of spirits,” he had appeared on the point either of disclosing or inquiring something particular.

After several minutes, he broke the silence, and in a voice of some agitation asked her when he was to congratulate her on the acquisition of a brother.

Elinor, struck and unprepared, could only ask what he meant.

He tried to smile. “Your sister’s engagement to Mr. Willoughby is very generally known.”

“It cannot be generally known,” Elinor returned quickly, “for her own family do not know it.”

He looked surprised. “I beg your pardon. I am afraid my inquiry has been impertinent; but I had not supposed any secrecy intended, as they openly correspond, and their marriage is universally talked of.”

“How can that be? By whom can you have heard it mentioned?”

“By many—by some of whom you know nothing, by others with whom you are most intimate: Mrs. Jennings, Mrs. Palmer, and the Middletons. But still I might not have believed it—for where the mind is perhaps rather unwilling to be convinced, it will always find something to support its doubts—if I had not, when the servant let me in today, accidentally seen a letter in his hand, directed to Mr. Willoughby, in your sister’s writing. I came to inquire; but I was convinced before I could ask the question. Is everything finally settled? Is it impossible to—? But I have no right, and I could have no chance of succeeding. Excuse me, Miss Dashwood. I believe I have been wrong in saying so much, but I hardly know what to do, and on your prudence I have the strongest dependence. Tell me that it is all absolutely resolved on; that any attempt—that in short concealment, if concealment be possible—is all that remains.”

The words, with their broken restraint and their helpless honour, conveyed to Elinor a direct avowal of his love for Marianne, and they affected her deeply. For a moment she could not speak. When she recovered, she debated what answer it would be most proper to give. The real state of things between Willoughby and her sister was so little known even to herself that in explaining it she might say too much or too little. Yet she was convinced that Marianne’s affection for Willoughby left no hope of Colonel Brandon’s success, whatever the event might be; and she wished also, tenderly, to shield Marianne from censure.

She therefore said more than she truly knew or believed. She acknowledged that, though she had never been informed by themselves of the terms on which they stood, of their mutual affection she had no doubt; and of their correspondence she was not astonished to hear.

He listened in silence. Then he rose directly, and after saying, in a voice shaken with emotion, “To your sister I wish all imaginable happiness; to Willoughby that he may endeavour to deserve her,” he took leave and went away.

Elinor was left with no comfort from the conversation to lessen her other uneasiness. Instead, a melancholy impression of Colonel Brandon’s unhappiness settled upon her like the lingering smell of extinguished candle-wick—sad, clinging, impossible to sweeten. And she was prevented even from wishing it removed by the very anxiety that must confirm it: the dread and hope bound together, tightening around Marianne’s name whenever the street below sent up the sound of another arriving carriage.

For three or four days after Elinor had, with a reluctant candour, laid her fears before her mother, nothing occurred to make her repent it; for Willoughby neither came nor wrote. The hours went by with a sameness almost cruel in its quiet—breakfast things cleared away, the dull thud of carriage-wheels below in Berkeley Street, the chink of the tea-spoon against china at evening—each domestic sound marking time, each one asking, in its small unanswerable way, why a voice so lately expected at the door had fallen out of the household’s daily music.

At the end of that interval they were engaged to attend Lady Middleton to a party. Mrs. Jennings was detained at home by the indisposition of her youngest daughter; and Marianne, so far from being roused by the thought of company, prepared with a listlessness that pained Elinor more than any tears could have done. She was not, as she once had been, alive to the smallest distinctions of dress, the soft persuasion of colour against her cheek, the agreeable oppression of a well-warmed room after a winter walk. She seemed careless whether she went or stayed; and after tea she sat by the drawing-room fire without once shifting her posture, as if the crackle of coals were the only language she could bear, and even that she scarcely heard.

When Lady Middleton’s carriage was announced, Marianne started—startled not like one who has been waiting, but like one who has forgotten that the world still expects anything of her. They went down, took their places, and were carried through the thickened streets where every sound was multiplied—hoofs striking stone, harness-jingle, a distant coachman’s call—till they reached their destination. There was the usual ceremony of arrival: the slow patience in the line of carriages, the press of bodies at the foot of the stairs, names called out from landing to landing in voices sharpened by repetition, and then the opening into a room splendidly lit, crowded, and insufferably hot—heat that had the stale, sweet breath of too many perfumes and too many candles, as if the air itself had been over-seasoned and left too long to stand.

They paid their tribute of politeness to the lady of the house and were then permitted, like every other guest, to take their share of inconvenience. After an interval of saying little and doing less, Lady Middleton sat down to Cassino; and as Marianne was not in spirits to move about, the sisters were fortunate enough to secure two chairs not far from the card-table. Elinor, who could not help listening to the room as one listens to a kitchen where something has gone wrong—straining for the clatter that tells of a dropped dish, the hiss that warns of milk boiling over—was surveying faces, tones, movements, all those minute signals by which society advertises its secrets, when she perceived Willoughby.

He stood within a few yards of them, in earnest conversation with a very fashionable-looking young woman. The sight came upon Elinor with that odd, immediate shock of recognition that is almost bodily: as if the room’s murmur changed key. She soon caught his eye. He bowed at once—but he did not attempt to speak to her, nor to approach Marianne, though he could not but see her. He continued his discourse with the same lady, as if nothing in his world had altered by the presence of those two sisters.

Elinor turned involuntarily to Marianne. At the moment Marianne first perceived him; and her whole countenance, so lately fixed and dim, kindled with a sudden delight so vivid that it seemed to put colour back into the air about her. She would have moved towards him instantly had not Elinor caught hold of her hand.

“Good heavens!” Marianne cried. “He is there—he is there—Oh! why does he not look at me? why cannot I speak to him?”

“Pray—pray be composed,” Elinor whispered, bending towards her with urgent tenderness. “Do not betray what you feel to everybody present. Perhaps he has not observed you yet.”

Yet Elinor could not persuade herself of it. He had observed; he had chosen. And to be composed was not only beyond Marianne’s reach—it was beyond her wish. She sat in an agony of impatience that altered every feature, as if the pain had its own expression and insisted on being seen. At last Willoughby turned again and regarded them both. Marianne started up, and pronouncing his name with the warmth of old certainty, held out her hand.

He approached; but he addressed himself rather to Elinor than to Marianne, as if he wished to avoid Marianne’s eye and was determined not to notice the attitude of trust in which she stood. In a hurried manner he inquired after Mrs. Dashwood, and asked how long they had been in town.

Elinor, struck into helplessness by such an address, could not at first command a word. Marianne’s feelings, however, found instant utterance. Her face crimsoned; her voice, when she spoke, was all emotion.

“Good God! Willoughby, what is the meaning of this? Have you not received my letters? Will you not shake hands with me?”

He could not then avoid it; but her touch seemed painful to him, and he held her hand only for a moment. During all this time he was evidently struggling for composure, as a man does who has been forced to swallow something bitter and must still converse as if the taste were not in his mouth. Elinor watched his countenance and saw its expression becoming more tranquil—as if, having resolved on cruelty, he found steadiness in the resolution.

After a moment’s pause he spoke with calmness. “I did myself the honour of calling in Berkeley Street last Tuesday, and very much regretted that I was not fortunate enough to find yourselves and Mrs. Jennings at home. My card was not lost, I hope.”

“But have you not received my notes?” Marianne cried, in the wildest anxiety. “Here is some mistake, I am sure—some dreadful mistake. What can be the meaning of it? Tell me, Willoughby—for heaven’s sake tell me—what is the matter?”

He made no reply. His complexion changed; all his embarrassment returned. But, catching the eye of the young lady with whom he had been previously talking, he seemed to feel the necessity of instant exertion; he recovered himself again, and after saying—forced and distinct—“Yes, I had the pleasure of receiving the information of your arrival in town, which you were so good as to send me,” he turned hastily away with a slight bow and joined his friend.

The words fell like cold drops down Marianne’s spine. She looked dreadfully white, and, unable to stand, sank into her chair. Elinor, expecting every moment to see her faint, tried to screen her from observation—bending closer, shifting her own position—while she revived her with lavender water. The sharp, clean scent rose between them like a frail barrier; it cut through the room’s stale sweetness for an instant, a small domestic remedy doing what it could against a public wound.

“Go to him, Elinor,” Marianne cried as soon as she could speak. “Force him to come to me. Tell him I must see him again—must speak to him instantly. I cannot rest—I shall not have a moment’s peace till this is explained—some dreadful misapprehension or other. Oh, go to him this moment.”

“How can that be done?” Elinor answered, keeping her voice low with the desperation of prudence. “No, my dearest Marianne—you must wait. This is not the place for explanations. Wait only till tomorrow.”

With difficulty could she prevent Marianne from following him herself. To persuade her to check her agitation, to wait—at least with the appearance of composure—till she might speak to him with more privacy and more effect, was impossible; for Marianne continued, in a low voice, to give way to the misery of her feelings by exclamations of wretchedness that were the more dreadful for being half-stifled, like sobs smothered in a pillow.

In a short time Elinor saw Willoughby quit the room by the door towards the staircase. She told Marianne that he was gone and urged the impossibility of speaking to him again that evening as a fresh argument for her to be calm. Marianne, as if the last thread of endurance had snapped, begged Elinor to entreat Lady Middleton to take them home; she was too miserable, she said, to stay a minute longer.

Lady Middleton, though in the middle of a rubber, on being informed that Marianne was unwell, was too polite to object. She made over her cards to a friend; and they departed as soon as the carriage could be found.

Scarcely a word was spoken during their return to Berkeley Street. The city’s night-noises came in at the windows—wheels over ruts, a shout carried and lost, the restless metallic ring of London—yet to Elinor it seemed as if the world were loudly alive while her sister was sinking into a silence too heavy for tears. Marianne sat in a mute agony, oppressed beyond weeping.

Mrs. Jennings was luckily not come home; they could go directly to their own room. There, away from the contaminated gaiety of candles and cards, hartshorn restored Marianne a little to herself. She was soon undressed and in bed; and as she seemed desirous of being alone, Elinor left her.

Waiting for Mrs. Jennings’s return, Elinor had leisure enough for thinking over the past. That some kind of engagement had subsisted between Willoughby and Marianne she could not doubt; and that Willoughby was weary of it seemed equally clear. Marianne might still feed her own wishes—might still insist on mistake, on misapprehension—but Elinor could not. Nothing but a thorough change of sentiment could account for such behaviour.

Her indignation would have been stronger than it was had she not witnessed his embarrassment—that struggle which seemed to confess a consciousness of misconduct, and prevented her from believing him so wholly unprincipled as to have been sporting with Marianne’s affections from the first without any design that could bear investigation. Absence might have weakened his regard; convenience might have determined him to overcome it. But that such a regard had once existed, she could not bring herself to doubt.

As for Marianne—on the pangs which so unhappy a meeting must already have given her, and on those still more severe which might await her in its probable consequence—Elinor could not reflect without the deepest concern. Her own situation gained in the comparison; for while she could esteem Edward as much as ever, however they might be divided in future, her mind might still be supported. But every circumstance that could embitter evil seemed uniting to heighten Marianne’s misery: a final separation from Willoughby—an immediate and irreconcilable rupture with him.

Before the maid had stirred the grate, before even the thin January sun could make a bargain with the fog and win an ounce of warmth from it, Marianne was already awake—if that tossing, tearful fever of mind could be called waking. Half dressed, her hair loose upon her shoulders, she had drawn herself to the window-seat, as though the faintest gray light might serve her for inkstand and courage both. The room was cold enough that Elinor, starting from sleep at the sound of suppressed sobbing, felt it in her lungs; yet Marianne, kneeling, wrote with a desperate speed, pausing only when tears blotted her words and she had to lift her hand to her mouth as if to stifle a cry.

Elinor watched in silence a few moments, her own heart tightening with each shaking breath Marianne drew, then spoke in a voice softened by dread.

“Marianne, may I ask—?”

“No, Elinor,” she said, without turning; and the steadiness of it was more alarming than any wail. “Ask nothing; you will soon know all.”

The calm broke as soon as it was uttered. Grief returned like a wave, and the pen halted again and again. Elinor, moving as gently as if any louder sound might fracture her sister, offered every quiet service—reaching for a ribbon, setting out what clothes she could, standing ready if summoned; but Marianne, in the quick irritability of anguish, begged her—almost with fury—not to speak to her for the world.

So Elinor withdrew, because remaining would only make them both raw with pain, and because Marianne’s mind seemed to require at once solitude and motion, as a sick body craves both heat and air. Marianne dressed at last and began to wander through the house like a spirit that cannot rest—down the passage and back again, to the landing, to the parlour door, away again—avoiding every face.

At breakfast she sat, but did not eat. The table smoked with a comfortable domestic abundance—Mrs. Jennings’s favourite hour, her tea fragrant and dark, toast crisping at the edge of the dish, butter softening into gloss in its little saucer—yet Marianne’s plate remained untouched, the scent of it all seeming only to mock her. Elinor’s whole attention, therefore, turned upon a different sort of management: she tried, by the look of her eyes and the turn of her answers, to keep Mrs. Jennings engaged with herself alone, to draw away notice from Marianne’s white face and fixed silence. Mrs. Jennings lingered, as she liked to do, taking her time with her meal and her cheerful commentary, as steady as the ticking of a clock.

They were scarcely settled afterwards about the common working-table, Mrs. Jennings already measuring out lengths of worsted with the solemnity of an old ritual, when a letter was brought in and placed in Marianne’s hand. She seized it as if it were bread to a starving creature—then the colour fled her cheeks so suddenly that it was like watching a candle go out. Without a word she fled the room.

Elinor needed no direction on the envelope to know whose hand had written. A sickness rose in her, and her limbs trembled so that she feared Mrs. Jennings must see it. But Mrs. Jennings saw only what pleased her notion of romance and humour.

“Upon my word,” she cried, laughing, “I never saw a young woman so desperately in love in my life! My girls were nothing to her, and yet they used to be foolish enough; but as for Miss Marianne, she is quite an altered creature. I hope, from the bottom of my heart, he won’t keep her waiting much longer, for it is quite grievous to see her look so ill and forlorn. Pray, when are they to be married?”

Elinor forced her lips into something like a smile, though it felt stiff as ice.

“And have you really, ma’am,” she said, “talked yourself into a persuasion of my sister’s being engaged to Mr. Willoughby? I thought it had been only a joke, but so serious a question seems to imply more; and I must beg, therefore, that you will not deceive yourself any longer. I do assure you that nothing would surprise me more than to hear of their being going to be married.”

“For shame, for shame, Miss Dashwood! how can you talk so?” Mrs. Jennings cried, not at all checked. “Don’t we all know that it must be a match, that they were over head and ears in love with each other from the first moment they met? Did not I see them together in Devonshire every day, and all day long; and did not I know that your sister came to town with me on purpose to buy wedding clothes? Come, come, this won’t do. Because you are so sly about it yourself, you think nobody else has any senses; but it is no such thing, I can tell you, for it has been known all over town this ever so long. I tell every body of it and so does Charlotte.”

“Indeed, ma’am,” Elinor said, her seriousness cutting through any pretence, “you are mistaken. Indeed, you are doing a very unkind thing in spreading the report, and you will find that you have—though you will not believe me now.”

Mrs. Jennings only laughed again, her hands busy with the worsted, the soft scrape and pull of it through her fingers a homely sound in a morning suddenly made dreadful.

Elinor had no strength for more. She hurried away.

In their room Marianne lay stretched upon the bed as if the bones beneath her skin had gone slack. Her breath came in choking bursts. In one hand she clutched a letter; two or three others lay scattered near her, as though they had fallen from her grasp in the violence of her despair.

Elinor crossed the room and sat beside her without speaking. She took Marianne’s hand and kissed it—once, and again—until her own restraint gave way and tears poured down, answering Marianne’s grief with a grief of her own. For a time they could do nothing but weep, the sound of it filling the cold chamber more completely than any fire could have filled it with heat.

At length Marianne, still unable to form words, gathered the letters with shaking hands and thrust them into Elinor’s lap. Then she covered her face with her handkerchief and cried out, not like a girl disappointed, but like a creature wounded in the vital part.

Elinor waited, because such suffering must spend itself; and when Marianne’s sobs lessened by the smallest degree, Elinor opened the letter that had come that morning.

It was as follows:

“Bond Street, January.

“MY DEAR MADAM,

“I have just had the honour of receiving your letter, for which I beg to return my sincere acknowledgments. I am much concerned to find there was anything in my behaviour last night that did not meet your approbation; and though I am quite at a loss to discover in what point I could be so unfortunate as to offend you, I entreat your forgiveness of what I can assure you to have been perfectly unintentional. I shall never reflect on my former acquaintance with your family in Devonshire without the most grateful pleasure, and flatter myself it will not be broken by any mistake or misapprehension of my actions. My esteem for your whole family is very sincere; but if I have been so unfortunate as to give rise to a belief of more than I felt, or meant to express, I shall reproach myself for not having been more guarded in my professions of that esteem. That I should ever have meant more you will allow to be impossible, when you understand that my affections have been long engaged elsewhere, and it will not be many weeks, I believe, before this engagement is fulfilled. It is with great regret that I obey your commands in returning the letters with which I have been honoured from you, and the lock of hair, which you so obligingly bestowed on me.

“I am, dear Madam,

“Your most obedient humble servant,

“JOHN WILLOUGHBY.”

Elinor’s indignation was so sharp it was almost clarifying, like the sting of spirits on a raw cut. She had expected confession; she had even braced herself for coldness. But this—this careful cruelty, this pretence of distance and decorum, this turning of intimacy into a formal insult—was beyond what she had believed possible in him.

She read it again, and again; and with each perusal her abhorrence grew. Yet she did not dare speak what she felt, because to call it a deliverance—true as it might be—would have been to trample on Marianne’s heart while it still bled.

In the midst of that bitter contemplation, the rumble of wheels came from below—unreasonably early. Elinor went to the window, astonished to see Mrs. Jennings’s chariot at the door though it had not been ordered till one. The street’s winter noise—hooves on hard ground, a coachman’s voice, the brief clank of harness—rose and faded again, as if the world persisted in its daily music without caring what tragedy lay upstairs.

Determined not to quit Marianne, Elinor went down only long enough to excuse herself from attending Mrs. Jennings, pleading her sister’s indisposition.

Mrs. Jennings, in a thoroughly good-humoured concern, admitted the excuse with readiness. Elinor saw her safe off, then returned quickly.

She arrived in time to catch Marianne as she attempted to rise and swayed, faint and giddy, toward the floor. Long want of rest and food had thinned her strength to nothing; for days she had scarcely eaten, and nights had brought her no real sleep. Now that suspense was gone, the body, no longer held up by feverish hope, sank into aching head, weakened stomach, and a general trembling faintness.

Elinor procured a glass of wine at once and persuaded her to take it. The warmth of it—sharp, living—seemed to call her back a little. Marianne’s eyes, swollen and wild, softened into a moment of human sense.

“Poor Elinor,” she whispered, “how unhappy I make you!”

“I only wish,” Elinor answered, “there were any thing I could do, which might be of comfort to you.”

It was too much kindness to bear.

“Oh! Elinor, I am miserable, indeed,” Marianne cried, and her voice broke again into sobbing.

Elinor could not watch it in silence any longer.

“Exert yourself, dear Marianne,” she cried, “if you would not kill yourself and all who love you. Think of your mother; think of her misery while you suffer: for her sake you must exert yourself.”

“I cannot, I cannot,” Marianne sobbed, turning away as if Elinor’s words were a bright light to her sore eyes. “Leave me, leave me, if I distress you; leave me, hate me, forget me! but do not torture me so. Oh! how easy for those, who have no sorrow of their own to talk of exertion! Happy, happy Elinor, you cannot have an idea of what I suffer.”

“Do you call me happy, Marianne? Ah! if you knew!—And can you believe me to be so, while I see you so wretched!”

“Forgive me, forgive me,” Marianne said, flinging her arms around her sister’s neck, clinging as if Elinor were the one steady thing left in a world that had shifted. “I know you feel for me; I know what a heart you have; but yet you are—you must be happy; Edward loves you—what, oh what, can do away such happiness as that?”

“Many, many circumstances,” Elinor said, very gravely.

“No, no, no!” Marianne cried wildly. “He loves you, and only you. You can have no grief.”

“I can have no pleasure while I see you in this state.”

“And you will never see me otherwise. Mine is a misery which nothing can do away.”

“You must not talk so, Marianne. Have you no comforts? no friends? Is your loss such as leaves no opening for consolation? Much as you suffer now, think of what you would have suffered if the discovery of his character had been delayed to a later period—if your engagement had been carried on for months and months, as it might have been, before he chose to put an end to it. Every additional day of unhappy confidence, on your side, would have made the blow more dreadful.”

“Engagement!” Marianne cried, and even in her despair there was a sudden defiance. “There has been no engagement.”

“No engagement!”

“No—he is not so unworthy as you believe him. He has broken no faith with me.”

“But he told you that he loved you.”

“Yes—no—never absolutely. It was every day implied, but never professedly declared. Sometimes I thought it had been, but it never was.”

“Yet you wrote to him?”

“Yes: could that be wrong after all that had passed? But I cannot talk.”

Elinor said no more then. The other letters—three of them now—burned with a stronger curiosity than before. She unfolded them and read quickly, while Marianne lay turned away, exhausted by her own sobbing.

The first, written on their arrival in town, ran thus:

“Berkeley Street, January.

“How surprised you will be, Willoughby, on receiving this; and I think you will feel something more than surprise, when you know that I am in town. An opportunity of coming hither, though with Mrs. Jennings, was a temptation we could not resist. I wish you may receive this in time to come here to-night, but I will not depend on it. At any rate I shall expect you to-morrow. For the present, adieu.

“M.D.”

The second, written the morning after the dance at the Middletons’, said:

“I cannot express my disappointment in having missed you the day before yesterday, nor my astonishment at not having received any answer to a note which I sent you above a week ago.
