I have been expecting to hear from you, and still more to see you, every hour of the day. Pray call again as soon as possible, and explain the reason of my having expected this in vain. You had better come earlier another time, because we are generally out by one. We were last night at Lady Middleton’s, where there was a dance. I have been told that you were asked to be of the party. But could it be so? You must be very much altered indeed since we parted, if that could be the case, and you not there. But I will not suppose this possible, and I hope very soon to receive your personal assurance of its being otherwise.

“M.D.”

The last, written after the affront of the previous evening, was this:

“What am I to imagine, Willoughby, by your behaviour last night? Again I demand an explanation of it. I was prepared to meet you with the pleasure which our separation naturally produced, with the familiarity which our intimacy at Barton appeared to me to justify. I was repulsed indeed! I have passed a wretched night in endeavouring to excuse a conduct which can scarcely be called less than insulting; but though I have not yet been able to form any reasonable apology for your behaviour, I am perfectly ready to hear your justification of it. You have perhaps been misinformed, or purposely deceived, in something concerning me, which may have lowered me in your opinion. Tell me what it is, explain the grounds on which you acted, and I shall be satisfied, in being able to satisfy you. It would grieve me indeed to be obliged to think ill of you; but if I am to do it, if I am to learn that you are not what we have hitherto believed you, that your regard for us all was insincere, that your behaviour to me was intended only to deceive, let it be told as soon as possible. My feelings are at present in a state of dreadful indecision; I wish to acquit you, but certainty on either side will be ease to what I now suffer. If your sentiments are no longer what they were, you will return my notes, and the lock of my hair which is in your possession.

“M.D.”

Elinor, though furious at Willoughby’s reply, could not read these without a different pain—one made of shame on Marianne’s behalf, of fear at what such unguarded tenderness had risked. The words were so open, so confident, written as if affection itself were a language everyone honoured and interpreted fairly; yet the world did not always speak that tongue, and it could be twisted—coldly, precisely—into an accusation.

Marianne, sensing Elinor had finished, turned her head slightly and said, with a bitterness that tried to disguise itself as certainty, that the letters contained nothing but what any one would have written in the same situation.

“I felt myself,” she added, “to be as solemnly engaged to him, as if the strictest legal covenant had bound us to each other.”

“I can believe it,” Elinor said; “but unfortunately he did not feel the same.”

“He did feel the same, Elinor—for weeks and weeks he felt it. I know he did. Whatever may have changed him now, (and nothing but the blackest art employed against me can have done it), I was once as dear to him as my own soul could wish. This lock of hair, which now he can so readily give up, was begged of me with the most earnest supplication. Had you seen his look, his manner, had you heard his voice at that moment! Have you forgot the last evening of our being together at Barton? The morning that we parted too! When he told me that it might be many weeks before we met again—his distress—can I ever forget his distress?”

Her breath failed her. Then, with a sudden firmness—as if she would rather be wounded again than surrender her belief—she said, “Elinor, I have been cruelly used; but not by Willoughby.”

“Dearest Marianne, who but himself? By whom can he have been instigated?”

“By all the world, rather than by his own heart. I could rather believe every creature of my acquaintance leagued together to ruin me in his opinion, than believe his nature capable of such cruelty. This woman of whom he writes—whoever she be—or any one, in short, but your own dear self, mama, and Edward, may have been so barbarous to bely me. Beyond you three, is there a creature in the world whom I would not rather suspect of evil than Willoughby, whose heart I know so well?”

Elinor would not contend. She only tried to offer the one consolation she could find.

“Whoever may have been so detestably your enemy,” she said, “let them be cheated of their malignant triumph, my dear sister, by seeing how nobly the consciousness of your own innocence and good intentions supports your spirits. It is a reasonable and laudable pride which resists such malevolence.”

“No, no,” Marianne cried. “Misery such as mine has no pride. I care not who knows that I am wretched. The triumph of seeing me so may be open to all the world. Elinor, Elinor, they who suffer little may be proud and independent as they like—may resist insult, or return mortification—but I cannot. I must feel—I must be wretched—and they are welcome to enjoy the consciousness of it that can.”

“But for my mother’s sake and mine—”

“I would do more than for my own. But to appear happy when I am so miserable—Oh! who can require it?”

Silence followed—heavy, restless. Elinor walked from the fire to the window and back again without truly feeling the heat or seeing the day. The household sounds—the distant opening of a door, a servant’s step on the stairs, the faint murmur of London beyond the glass—seemed all at once intrusive, as if the world’s ordinary noise were an offence against private sorrow.

Marianne, seated now at the foot of the bed with her head against one of its posts, took up Willoughby’s letter again. She shuddered over each sentence as if it carried chill on it, and then broke out:

“It is too much! Oh, Willoughby, Willoughby, could this be yours! Cruel, cruel—nothing can acquit you. Elinor, nothing can. Whatever he might have heard against me—ought he not to have suspended his belief? ought he not to have told me of it, to have given me the power of clearing myself? ‘The lock of hair,’” she repeated from the letter, “‘which you so obligingly bestowed on me’—That is unpardonable. Willoughby, where was your heart when you wrote those words? Oh, barbarously insolent!—Elinor, can he be justified?”

“No, Marianne,” Elinor answered, “in no possible way.”

“And yet this woman—who knows what her art may have been?—how long it may have been premeditated, and how deeply contrived by her!—Who is she?—Who can she be?—Whom did I ever hear him talk of as young and attractive among his female acquaintance?—Oh! no one, no one—he talked to me only of myself.”

Another pause, and then Marianne, as if fleeing the very air of London, exclaimed:

“Elinor, I must go home. I must go and comfort mama. Can not we be gone to-morrow?”

“To-morrow, Marianne!”

“Yes—why should I stay here? I came only for Willoughby’s sake—and now who cares for me? Who regards me?”

“It would be impossible to go to-morrow. We owe Mrs. Jennings much more than civility; and civility of the commonest kind must prevent such a hasty removal as that.”

“Well then, another day or two, perhaps; but I cannot stay here long, I cannot stay to endure the questions and remarks of all these people. The Middletons and Palmers—how am I to bear their pity? The pity of such a woman as Lady Middleton! Oh, what would he say to that!”

Elinor urged her to lie down again. Marianne obeyed for a moment, but no posture could keep her still. She shifted, restless in body as in mind, and her agitation grew so violent—so near hysteria—that Elinor feared she might be forced to call for help. At last Marianne allowed herself to take some lavender drops. The sharp, clean scent—like bruised stems, like a garden remembered in winter—seemed to steady her breathing. From then until Mrs. Jennings returned, she lay quiet and motionless on the bed, while Elinor sat near, keeping watch over her as one watches a beloved flame in a draughty room, afraid it will go out.

Mrs. Jennings returned to their apartments with the haste of a woman whose heart always outran ceremony. She did not wait for permission; the latch clicked, the door swung wide, and she came in upon them with a face so honestly troubled that even her bustling presence seemed softened, as if the very air about her had been muffled.

“How do you do, my dear?” she cried in a voice made tender by real pity, and she bent toward Marianne as though warmth could be pressed into her by leaning close.

Marianne, lying with her cheek turned away, gave no answer—only a small, involuntary movement, like a person shrinking from light.

Mrs. Jennings wheeled at once to Elinor. “How is she, Miss Dashwood? Poor thing! she looks very bad. No wonder. Ay—’tis but too true. He is to be married very soon—a good-for-nothing fellow! I have no patience with him. Mrs. Taylor told me of it half an hour ago, and she was told it by a particular friend of Miss Grey herself, else I’m sure I should not have believed it; and I was almost ready to sink as it was. Well, said I, all I can say is, if this be true, he has used a young lady of my acquaintance abominably ill, and I wish with all my soul his wife may plague his heart out. And so I shall always say, my dear, you may depend on it. I have no notion of men’s going on in this way; and if ever I meet him again, I will give him such a dressing as he has not had this many a day.”

Her indignation, so hearty and so loud, stirred the curtains slightly as if they too were startled; yet beneath it ran a note of domestic impulse, of wanting at once to set something right—like a cook snatching at salt when the soup has been spoiled.

“But there is one comfort,” she added, dropping her voice into coaxing consolation, “he is not the only young man in the world worth having; and with your pretty face you will never want admirers. Well, poor thing! I won’t disturb her any longer, for she had better have her cry out at once and have done with. The Parrys and the Sandersons luckily are coming tonight, you know, and that will amuse her.”

Then, as if she believed sorrow were a skittish creature that noise might frighten into a fit, she retreated on tiptoe, easing the door almost shut behind her.

To Elinor’s surprise, Marianne soon said she would dine with them.

Elinor advised against it; her sister’s hands were cold, her eyes fever-bright, and her whole figure seemed too light, as though grief had eaten away substance itself. But Marianne persisted: no—she would go down; she could bear it very well; the bustle about her would be less.

Elinor, grateful to see her governed for one moment by any motive but the stubbornness of pain, said no more. She smoothed Marianne’s hair with the careful fingers of a sister accustomed to make do with what comfort she could, adjusted her gown as well as the circumstances allowed, and helped her rise from the bed when they were summoned.

Downstairs, amid the warmth of the dining-room, Marianne looked wretched enough to chill the very blaze. Yet she ate more than Elinor expected, and she was calmer—calmer, at least, in the terrible way of those whose thoughts have retreated so far inward that the outer world can no longer reach them. Had she attempted speech, had she been forced to meet even half of Mrs. Jennings’s well-meant, ill-judged attentions, she could not have kept that brittle quiet. But not a syllable escaped her. She sat as if she had put herself beyond sound—beyond the clink of knives, the low hum of servants, the soft crackle of coals; as if all London’s living noises had turned into a distant surf she could not understand.

Elinor, who did justice to Mrs. Jennings’s kindness even when its effusions distressed her and sometimes bordered on the ridiculous, made the acknowledgments and returned the civilities her sister could not. Their good friend saw only that Marianne was unhappy, and felt that everything was due her that might make her, if not happy, at least less miserable. She treated her with indulgent fondness—like a parent spoiling a favourite child, determined to heap pleasures on the last day of holidays.

Marianne was placed in the warmest seat by the fire; she was urged, in a tone that might have tempted an appetite into being, to taste every delicacy the house could provide. Mrs. Jennings pressed sweetmeats upon her with the faith of a woman to whom comfort had always been edible: preserved fruits bright as jewels in their syrup, olives glistening with oil, little candied things that stuck to the fingers and promised sweetness even to a bitter mouth. She praised the good blaze, the snug chair, the excellent cook; she offered news with the same generosity she offered food, as if tidings, too, could be nourishment.

Elinor, had she not seen the check in Marianne’s face that forbade all mirth, might have been entertained by these attempts to cure a disappointment in love with sugar, salt, and a cheerful fire.

But at last repetition forced consciousness upon Marianne; she could no longer endure being tempted to eat, begged to taste, entreated to take “just one more”—as if her heart were merely faint for want of a restorative. With a hurried exclamation—“Misery!”—and a sign to Elinor not to follow, she started up and left the room.

“Poor soul!” cried Mrs. Jennings as soon as she was gone. “How it grieves me to see her! And I declare if she is not gone away without finishing her wine! And the dried cherries too! Lord! nothing seems to do her any good. I am sure if I knew of any thing she would like, I would send all over the town for it. Well, it is the oddest thing to me that a man should use such a pretty girl so ill! But when there is plenty of money on one side and next to none on the other—Lord bless you! they care no more about such things!”

“The lady then—Miss Grey, I think you called her—is very rich?” Elinor asked, because she had nothing else to do with Mrs. Jennings’s agitation but guide it into facts.

“Fifty thousand pounds, my dear. Did you ever see her? A smart, stylish girl they say, but not handsome. I remember her aunt very well—Biddy Henshawe; she married a very wealthy man. But the family are all rich together. Fifty thousand pounds! and by all accounts, it won’t come before it’s wanted; for they say he is all to pieces. No wonder! dashing about with his curricle and hunters! Well, it don’t signify talking; but when a young man, be who he will, comes and makes love to a pretty girl, and promises marriage, he has no business to fly off from his word only because he grows poor, and a richer girl is ready to have him.”

The words “promises marriage” fell like a dropped utensil—sharp, unavoidable. Elinor’s face did not change; she would not let it.

“Why don’t he,” Mrs. Jennings went on, warming to the reforming spirit she always applied to other people’s lives, “in such a case, sell his horses, let his house, turn off his servants, and make a thorough reform at once? I warrant you, Miss Marianne would have been ready to wait till matters came round. But that won’t do now-a-days; nothing in the way of pleasure can ever be given up by the young men of this age.”

“Do you know what kind of a girl Miss Grey is? Is she said to be amiable?” Elinor asked.

“I never heard any harm of her; indeed I hardly ever heard her mentioned, except that Mrs. Taylor did say this morning that one day Miss Walker hinted to her that she believed Mr. and Mrs. Ellison would not be sorry to have Miss Grey married, for she and Mrs. Ellison could never agree.”

“And who are the Ellisons?”

“Her guardians, my dear. But now she is of age and may choose for herself; and a pretty choice she has made!” Then, pausing and looking toward the door Marianne had fled through, she added with sudden anxious pity, “What now—your poor sister is gone to her own room, I suppose, to moan by herself. Is there nothing one can get to comfort her? Poor dear, it seems quite cruel to let her be alone. Well, by-and-by we shall have a few friends, and that will amuse her a little. What shall we play at? She hates whist I know; but is there no round game she cares for?”

“Dear ma’am, this kindness is quite unnecessary. Marianne, I dare say, will not leave her room again this evening. I shall persuade her if I can to go early to bed, for I am sure she wants rest.”

“Aye, I believe that will be best for her. Let her name her own supper, and go to bed. Lord! no wonder she has been looking so bad and so cast down this last week or two, for this matter I suppose has been hanging over her head as long as that. And so the letter that came today finished it! Poor soul! I am sure if I had had a notion of it, I would not have joked her about it for all my money. But then, you know, how should I guess such a thing? I made sure of its being nothing but a common love letter, and you know young people like to be laughed at about them.”

Elinor heard in those words the whole careless commerce of the world: how a name becomes a toy in mouths that do not know its weight; how a private suffering is dragged toward public amusement with a laugh. She kept her composure, because Marianne had no strength to do it for herself.

“Lord! how concerned Sir John and my daughters will be when they hear it!” Mrs. Jennings continued. “If I had my senses about me I might have called in Conduit Street in my way home, and told them of it. But I shall see them tomorrow.”

“It would be unnecessary, I am sure, for you to caution Mrs. Palmer and Sir John against ever naming Mr. Willoughby, or making the slightest allusion to what has passed, before my sister,” said Elinor. “Their own good-nature must point out to them the real cruelty of appearing to know any thing about it when she is present; and the less that may ever be said to myself on the subject, the more my feelings will be spared—as you, my dear madam, will easily believe.”

“Oh! Lord! yes, that I do indeed. It must be terrible for you to hear it talked of; and as for your sister, I am sure I would not mention a word about it to her for the world. You saw I did not all dinner time. No more would Sir John, nor my daughters, for they are all very thoughtful and considerate—especially if I give them a hint, as I certainly will. For my part, I think the less that is said about such things, the better; the sooner ’tis blown over and forgot. And what good does talking ever do, you know?”

“In this affair it can only do harm,” Elinor replied, “more so perhaps than in many cases of a similar kind, for it has been attended by circumstances which, for the sake of every one concerned in it, make it unfit to become the public conversation. I must do this justice to Mr. Willoughby—he has broken no positive engagement with my sister.”

“Law, my dear! Don’t pretend to defend him. No positive engagement indeed! after taking her all over Allenham House, and fixing on the very rooms they were to live in hereafter!”

Elinor, for Marianne’s sake, could not press the subject farther. The truth, exact and careful as she could make it, would buy Willoughby little and cost Marianne much; and so she let Mrs. Jennings keep her anger, because anger at least was on Marianne’s side.

There was a short silence, in which the small domestic sounds of the room seemed louder—the settling of coals, the faint rattle of a spoon, the distant roll of a carriage outside, like an indifferent world passing on.

Then Mrs. Jennings, whose spirits, like her housekeeping, always rallied into activity, burst out again with her natural hilarity.

“Well, my dear, ’tis a true saying about an ill-wind, for it will be all the better for Colonel Brandon. He will have her at last; aye, that he will. Mind me now, if they an’t married by Midsummer. Lord! how he’ll chuckle over this news! I hope he will come tonight. It will be all to one a better match for your sister. Two thousand a year without debt or drawback—except the little love-child indeed; aye, I had forgot her; but she may be ’prenticed out at a small cost, and then what does it signify?”

Elinor listened as one listens to a tune played too loudly—recognizing it, disliking it, unable to stop it.

“Delaford is a nice place, I can tell you; exactly what I call a nice old-fashioned place, full of comforts and conveniences; quite shut in with great garden walls that are covered with the best fruit-trees in the country; and such a mulberry tree in one corner! Lord! how Charlotte and I did stuff the only time we were there! Then there is a dove-cote, some delightful stew-ponds, and a very pretty canal; and every thing, in short, that one could wish for; and, moreover, it is close to the church, and only a quarter of a mile from the turnpike-road, so ’tis never dull, for if you only go and sit up in an old yew arbour behind the house, you may see all the carriages that pass along. Oh! ’tis a nice place! A butcher hard by in the village, and the parsonage-house within a stone’s throw. To my fancy, a thousand times prettier than Barton Park, where they are forced to send three miles for their meat, and have not a neighbour nearer than your mother. Well, I shall spirit up the Colonel as soon as I can. One shoulder of mutton, you know, drives another down. If we can but put Willoughby out of her head!”

“Ay, if we can do that, ma’am,” said Elinor, “we shall do very well with or without Colonel Brandon.”

And rising, she went away to Marianne.

She found her, as expected, in her own room, leaning in silent misery over the small remains of a fire which—until Elinor’s entrance—had been her only light. The room was hushed; even the street sounds seemed far away, as if the walls held them back out of respect for her grief.

“You had better leave me,” Marianne said—nothing else, no appeal, no anger; only that flat sentence that was like a barred door.

“I will leave you,” Elinor answered, “if you will go to bed.”

From the momentary perverseness of impatient suffering Marianne at first refused. But Elinor’s earnest, gentle persuasion—so steady, so untheatrical, so unlike the whirlwind of Marianne’s own feelings—softened her by degrees into compliance. Elinor saw her lay her aching head on the pillow; and, as she hoped, she lay at last in a way to get some quiet rest.

Elinor returned to the drawing-room. Mrs. Jennings soon followed, carrying a wine-glass filled with something that caught the firelight in a deep, warm glow.

“My dear,” said she, “I have just recollected that I have some of the finest old Constantia wine in the house that ever was tasted, so I have brought a glass of it for your sister. My poor husband! how fond he was of it! Whenever he had a touch of his old colicky gout, he said it did him more good than any thing else in the world. Do take it to your sister.”

“Dear ma’am,” Elinor replied, smiling in spite of herself at the distance between colic and heartbreak, “how good you are! But I have just left Marianne in bed, and, I hope, almost asleep; and as I think nothing will be of so much service to her as rest, if you will give me leave, I will drink the wine myself.”

Mrs. Jennings, though regretting she had not been five minutes earlier, accepted the compromise with good humour. Elinor, swallowing the chief of it, felt the rich sweetness and foreign fragrance spread through her—sun caught and bottled, warmth made liquid—and reflected that though its effects on colicky gout were, at present, of little importance to her, its healing powers on a disappointed heart might as reasonably be tried on herself as on her sister.

Colonel Brandon came in while they were at tea. The moment he entered, his eyes moved round the room, seeking Marianne with an anxiety too restrained to be mistaken. Elinor instantly fancied that he neither expected nor wished to see her there—and, in short, that he was already aware of what occasioned her absence.

Mrs. Jennings was not struck by the same thought. Soon after his entrance she crossed to the tea-table where Elinor presided and whispered, with an air of eager management, “The Colonel looks as grave as ever you see. He knows nothing of it; do tell him, my dear.”

But he soon drew a chair close to Elinor’s, and with a look which perfectly assured her of his good information, inquired after her sister.

“Marianne is not well,” said Elinor. “She has been indisposed all day, and we have persuaded her to go to bed.”

“Perhaps, then,” he replied hesitatingly, “what I heard this morning may be—there may be more truth in it than I could believe possible at first.”

“What did you hear?”

“That a gentleman, whom I had reason to think—in short, that a man whom I knew to be engaged—but how shall I tell you? If you know it already, as surely you must, I may be spared.”

“You mean,” answered Elinor, with forced calmness, “Mr. Willoughby’s marriage with Miss Grey. Yes, we do know it all. This seems to have been a day of general elucidation, for this very morning first unfolded it to us. Mr. Willoughby is unfathomable! Where did you hear it?”

“In a stationer’s shop in Pall Mall, where I had business. Two ladies were waiting for their carriage, and one of them was giving the other an account of the intended match, in a voice so little attempting concealment, that it was impossible for me not to hear all. The name of Willoughby—John Willoughby—frequently repeated, first caught my attention; and what followed was a positive assertion that every thing was now finally settled respecting his marriage with Miss Grey—it was no longer to be a secret—it would take place even within a few weeks, with many particulars of preparations and other matters.”

His voice lowered, as if the memory itself offended him—the careless public way in which a name, a life, a wound, could be spoken aloud among paper and ink and the ordinary transactions of the day.

“One thing, especially, I remember, because it served to identify the man still more: as soon as the ceremony was over, they were to go to Combe Magna, his seat in Somersetshire. My astonishment!—but it would be impossible to describe what I felt. The communicative lady I learnt, on inquiry—for I stayed in the shop till they were gone—was a Mrs. Ellison, and that, as I have been since informed, is the name of Miss Grey’s guardian.”

“It is. But have you likewise heard that Miss Grey has fifty thousand pounds? In that, if in any thing, we may find an explanation.”

“It may be so; but Willoughby is capable—at least I think—” He stopped, as if distrustful of his own generosity or his own indignation; then added, in a voice that seemed to doubt itself, “And your sister—how did she—”

“Her sufferings have been very severe,” said Elinor. “I have only to hope that they may be proportionately short. It has been—it is—a most cruel affliction. Till yesterday, I believe, she never doubted his regard; and even now, perhaps—but I am almost convinced that he never was really attached to her. He has been very deceitful; and, in some points, there seems a hardness of heart about him.”

“Ah!” said Colonel Brandon. “There is, indeed! But your sister does not—I think you said so—she does not consider quite as you do?”

“You know her disposition, and may believe how eagerly she would still justify him if she could.”

He made no answer. The tea-things were removed; the tables were arranged; cards were brought out, and conversation was forced into other channels, as a river is pushed into new banks by human hands.

Mrs. Jennings had watched Elinor’s and the Colonel’s talk with pleasure, expecting to see in him, the moment he heard the news, a kind of instant, youthful gaiety—hope springing up as if disappointment were a door opening conveniently for his happiness. Instead, to her amazement, he remained the whole evening more serious and thoughtful than usual; and in that gravity Elinor read something she trusted more than any triumph—the sound of a man who did not turn another’s grief into his own advantage, not even in his secret heart.

Marianne had slept more, in those few hours, than Elinor had dared to hope; but the gift of sleep was like a lid laid briefly over a pot already boiling. She woke into the very same misery with which she had lain down—no new shape to it, no softening, only the raw certainty that pain could keep its place through darkness and dawn as faithfully as any household duty.

Elinor, who had watched the morning light steal between the curtains with the same steady patience she gave to everything that must be endured, drew her sister gently, again and again, to speech. Before the breakfast-tray had even been carried up, they had gone over the same ground repeatedly: Elinor’s quiet conviction, her affectionate counsel, her careful questions; Marianne’s impetuous turns, now generous to Willoughby, now unable to breathe except in the certainty of his guilt. One moment she could picture him unfortunate, ensnared, as wounded as herself; the next she could find no refuge from the plain impossibility of excusing him. She swore herself indifferent to the world’s notice, then shuddered at the thought of ever being seen again, then roused herself to defy it—all within the space of a few sentences, as if her feelings, like a restless fire, changed colour with every gust of memory.

In one particular, however, she was steady: she avoided Mrs. Jennings whenever she could, and when avoidance failed, she shut herself up in a silence so determined it looked like a kind of pride.

“No, no, no—it cannot be,” she cried, as if her own words might build a wall. “She cannot feel. Her kindness is not sympathy; her good-nature is not tenderness. All she wants is gossip; she only likes me now because I supply it.”

Elinor, who had long known how readily Marianne’s fine sensibility sharpened into injustice, could only grieve at this harshness without being surprised by it. Marianne demanded in other people the very mould of her own feelings; she weighed motives by how an action struck herself; and thus even a well-meaning impulse could become, by the angle of her pain, a new offence.

So it was that, after breakfast, while they were together in their room, a small incident sank Mrs. Jennings even lower in Marianne’s estimation—because it struck Marianne at precisely the point where hope, absurd as it might have seemed to anyone watching from without, still lay tender.

Mrs. Jennings came in without ceremony, holding a letter out as if it were a warm roll fresh from the oven and meant to comfort a hungry soul. Her face wore that gaily smiling confidence of one who believes she brings relief.

“Now, my dear,” she said, “I bring you something that I am sure will do you good.”

Marianne heard only that—something—good—and her imagination, quicker than reason, leapt to the one thing in the world it craved. For one piercing instant she had him again: a letter from Willoughby, full of tenderness and contrition; a clearing of every dark corner; explanations made sweet by the very hand that wrote them; and then—because Marianne’s mind never stopped at the first pleasure—Willoughby himself, breathless, rushing in to seal every written assurance with the eloquence of his eyes at her feet.

The next instant destroyed the first. The handwriting—so familiar, so beloved, so little suspected of ever bringing pain—was her mother’s.

If the first flash had been too bright, the drop into disappointment was like tasting honey and then biting into bitterness so sharp it smarted through the palate and up behind the eyes. She felt, with an astonishment that was itself a torment, as if she had never truly suffered until that second. The tears that burst from her were violent enough to be a reproach; but it was a reproach wholly wasted on Mrs. Jennings, who, having no imagination to guess what Marianne had expected, understood only that her poor young friend was unhappy and ought to be consoled.

After many exclamations of pity, she withdrew, still referring Marianne to “the letter of comfort” as if comfort lay folded there as neatly as the page.

But when Marianne could read at last, there was little comfort to be found. Willoughby filled every line without meaning to; her mother’s whole tenderness ran towards him as well as Marianne. Mrs. Dashwood, still confident of an engagement, still relying warmly on his constancy, had only been roused by Elinor’s previous letter to entreat greater openness from Marianne—greater openness to them both. She wrote with that affectionate blindness which makes love generous and makes error feel like faith. Her conviction of Marianne’s future happiness with Willoughby was so entire, so lovingly assumed, that Marianne’s heart seemed to split beneath it. She wept over every expression; for the more her mother trusted, the more cruel the truth felt which Marianne herself could not yet name.

All her impatience to be at home returned with a force almost wild. Barton, with its familiar rooms, its honest sounds—the rustle of trees, the known cadence of voices—rose before her like a country air after a long choking day in town. Her mother was dearer than ever; dearer, too, through the very excess of her mistaken confidence. Marianne was urgent—almost frantic—to be gone.

Elinor, who could not decide whether London’s constant motion was worse for Marianne than the quiet of home, offered no counsel beyond patience. They must wait, she insisted, until their mother’s wishes were known. At length Marianne consented, not because she believed it wise, but because Elinor’s steadiness gave her something to lean upon.

Mrs. Jennings left them earlier than usual. She could not rest, it seemed, until she had carried her grief into other drawing-rooms and made the Middletons and Palmers “grieve as much as herself.” She refused Elinor’s offered attendance and went out alone, her steps brisk with that kind of benevolence which must be active even when it is clumsy.

Elinor, her heart heavy with what she had to do, sat down to write to her mother—an account of what had passed, and a request for directions. Marianne came into the drawing-room as Mrs. Jennings went away and remained fixed at the table near Elinor, following the progress of the pen as if each stroke were a sound she could not bear to hear and yet could not endure to miss. She grieved for Elinor in having to write such a letter; she grieved still more, with a tenderness almost childlike, over the pain it must give their mother.

They had been thus perhaps a quarter of an hour when a sudden rap at the door made Marianne start as if the noise struck her nerves like a sharp utensil against porcelain.

“Who can this be?” cried Elinor. “So early too! I thought we had been safe.”

Marianne moved quickly to the window. The street below had its usual London chorus—wheels, footsteps, distant calls—a ceaseless murmur that never allowed a thought to settle in quiet.

“It is Colonel Brandon!” she said, and there was vexation in her tone, as if his very presence were an additional sound to her already over-full world. “We are never safe from him.”

“He will not come in,” Elinor answered, “as Mrs. Jennings is from home.”

“I will not trust to that,” Marianne said, retreating to her own room with the stiffness of injured feeling. “A man who has nothing to do with his own time has no conscience in his intrusion on that of others.”

The event proved her conjecture right, though it sprang from injustice. Colonel Brandon did come in. Elinor rose to meet him; and at once she read solicitude in his disturbed and melancholy look, in his anxious, brief inquiry after Marianne. Whatever Marianne could say of him, his concern was not a thing to be mistaken.

“I met Mrs. Jennings in Bond Street,” he said, when the first salutations were over. “She encouraged me to come on; and I was the more easily encouraged because I thought it probable I might find you alone, which I was very desirous of doing.”

He paused, as if the words were difficult to set in their right order.

“My object—my wish—my sole wish in desiring it—I hope, I believe it is—to be a means of giving comfort; no, I must not say comfort—not present comfort—but conviction—lasting conviction to your sister’s mind. My regard for her, for yourself, for your mother—will you allow me to prove it by relating some circumstances which nothing but a very sincere regard—nothing but an earnest desire of being useful—would lead me to speak of? I think I am justified—though where so many hours have been spent in convincing myself that I am right, is there not some reason to fear I may be wrong?”

He stopped, his chest rising with a controlled breath, as if even speaking strained something tender within him.

Elinor’s face steadied.

“I understand you. You have something to tell me of Mr. Willoughby that will open his character farther. Your telling it will be the greatest act of friendship that can be shown Marianne. My gratitude will be insured immediately by any information tending to that end, and hers must be gained by it in time. Pray—pray let me hear it.”

“You shall,” he said; “and to be brief—when I quitted Barton last October—but that will give you no idea—I must go farther back.” He gave a short, rueful motion of the head. “You will find me a very awkward narrator, Miss Dashwood. I hardly know where to begin. A short account of myself, I believe, will be necessary; and it shall be a short one. On such a subject,”—he sighed heavily—“I can have little temptation to be diffuse.”

He stood a moment, collecting himself, and then began again, the words coming as if pulled out with effort, each one weighted with years.

“You have probably entirely forgotten a conversation between us one evening at Barton Park—it was the evening of a dance—in which I alluded to a lady I had once known, as resembling, in some measure, your sister Marianne.”

“Indeed,” Elinor answered, “I have not forgotten it.”

He looked pleased—genuinely, quietly pleased—by her remembrance.

“If I am not deceived by the uncertainty—the partiality—of tender recollection, there is a very strong resemblance between them, as well in mind as person. The same warmth of heart, the same eagerness of fancy and spirits. This lady was one of my nearest relations; an orphan from her infancy, and under the guardianship of my father. Our ages were nearly the same; and from our earliest years we were playfellows and friends. I cannot remember the time when I did not love Eliza; and my affection for her, as we grew up, was such as perhaps—judging from my present forlorn and cheerless gravity—you might think me incapable of having ever felt.”

His voice faltered into a softer register on her name, as if the syllables carried an old music no longer sung in ordinary life.

“Hers for me was, I believe, fervent—as fervent as the attachment of your sister to Mr. Willoughby—and it was, though from a different cause, no less unfortunate. At seventeen she was lost to me for ever. She was married—married against her inclination—to my brother. Her fortune was large, and our family estate much encumbered. And this, I fear, is all that can be said for the conduct of one who was at once her uncle and guardian.”

Elinor sat still, her hands folded, her whole attention fixed, as if the room had narrowed to his voice alone. Outside, London’s noise ran on; within, his recollection created its own solemn quiet.

“My brother did not deserve her,” he went on. “He did not even love her. I had hoped her regard for me would support her under any difficulty, and for some time it did; but at last the misery of her situation—for she experienced great unkindness—overcame all her resolution, and though she had promised me that nothing—” He stopped abruptly, shaken. “But how blindly I relate! I have never told you how this was brought on. We were within a few hours of eloping together for Scotland. The treachery—or the folly—of my cousin’s maid betrayed us. I was banished to the house of a relation far distant, and she was allowed no liberty, no society, no amusement, till my father’s point was gained.”

His words grew tighter, as if spoken through a restrained bitterness.

“I had depended on her fortitude too far, and the blow was a severe one—but had her marriage been happy, so young as I then was, a few months must have reconciled me to it, or at least I should not now have to lament it. This, however, was not the case. My brother had no regard for her; his pleasures were not what they ought to have been; and from the first he treated her unkindly. The consequence of this upon a mind so young, so lively, so inexperienced as Mrs. Brandon’s, was but too natural.”

He did not soften the fact by any indulgent phrasing. Elinor felt the delicacy of it, the shame wrapped about it like a veil, and yet she heard in his tone not condemnation, but grief that had been living a long time.

“She resigned herself at first to all the misery of her situation; and happy had it been if she had not lived to overcome those regrets which the remembrance of me occasioned. But can we wonder that, with such a husband to provoke inconstancy, and without a friend to advise or restrain her—for my father lived only a few months after their marriage, and I was with my regiment in the East Indies—she should fall? Had I remained in England, perhaps—but I meant to promote the happiness of both by removing from her for years, and for that purpose had procured my exchange.”

His agitation rose, audible now, as if the very air in the room had become more difficult to draw in.

“The shock which her marriage had given me,” he continued, “was of trifling weight—was nothing—to what I felt when I heard, about two years afterwards, of her divorce. It was that which threw this gloom,—even now the recollection of what I suffered—”

He could say no more. He rose hastily and walked a few minutes about the room, as if movement could carry him past the worst of the memory.

Elinor, affected by the story and still more by the pain of telling it, could not speak. When he returned, he saw her concern; and, with an earnestness that seemed less like gallantry than like gratitude for being listened to, he took her hand, pressed it, and kissed it with respectful warmth. Elinor coloured, but did not withdraw it; there was too much sincerity in his manner to make the action anything but touching.

A few minutes of silent struggle brought him again to composure.

“It was nearly three years after this unhappy period before I returned to England. My first care, when I did arrive, was of course to seek for her; but the search was as fruitless as it was melancholy. I could not trace her beyond her first seducer, and there was every reason to fear she had removed from him only to sink deeper in a life of sin. Her legal allowance was not adequate to her fortune, nor sufficient for her comfortable maintenance; and I learnt from my brother that the power of receiving it had been made over some months before to another person. He imagined—calmly could he imagine it—that her extravagance, and consequent distress, had obliged her to dispose of it for some immediate relief.”

His disdain for his brother’s calmness sharpened his pronunciation, then faded into weariness.

“At last, however, and after I had been six months in England, I did find her. Regard for a former servant of my own, who had since fallen into misfortune, carried me to visit him in a spunging-house, where he was confined for debt; and there, in the same house, under a similar confinement, was my unfortunate sister. So altered—so faded—worn down by acute suffering of every kind! hardly could I believe the melancholy and sickly figure before me to be the remains of the lovely, blooming, healthful girl on whom I had once doted.”

He drew in a breath, as if the memory had a physical taste—bitter and metallic—on his tongue.

“What I endured in so beholding her—but I have no right to wound your feelings by attempting to describe it. I have pained you too much already. That she was, to all appearance, in the last stage of a consumption, was—yes, in such a situation it was my greatest comfort. Life could do nothing for her beyond giving time for a better preparation for death; and that was given. I saw her placed in comfortable lodgings, and under proper attendants; I visited her every day during the rest of her short life: I was with her in her last moments.”

Again he stopped. Elinor, her heart full, could only exclaim, in a low voice, her tender concern for the fate of his unhappy friend.

“Your sister, I hope, cannot be offended,” he said, “by the resemblance I have fancied between her and my poor disgraced relation. Their fates—their fortunes—cannot be the same; and had the natural sweet disposition of the one been guarded by a firmer mind, or a happier marriage, she might have been all that you will live to see the other be.”

He checked himself, as if fearing he had wandered too far.

“But to what does all this lead? I seem to have been distressing you for nothing. Ah, Miss Dashwood—a subject such as this—untouched for fourteen years—it is dangerous to handle it at all! I will be more collected—more concise.”

He fixed his eyes upon a point beyond Elinor, as if reading the rest of his story from a page he could not alter.

“She left to my care her only child, a little girl—the offspring of her first guilty connection—who was then about three years old. She loved the child, and had always kept it with her. It was a valued—a precious—trust to me; and gladly would I have discharged it in the strictest sense, by watching over her education myself, had the nature of our situations allowed it; but I had no family, no home; and my little Eliza was therefore placed at school. I saw her there whenever I could; and after the death of my brother—which happened about five years ago, and which left to me the possession of the family property—she visited me at Delaford. I called her a distant relation; but I am well aware that I have in general been suspected of a much nearer connection with her.”

Elinor listened, understanding now the quiet reserve about his domestic life that had always wrapped him like a cloak.

“It is now three years ago—she had just reached her fourteenth year—that I removed her from school, to place her under the care of a very respectable woman in Dorsetshire, who had the charge of four or five other girls of about the same time of life; and for two years I had every reason to be pleased with her situation. But last February—almost a twelvemonth back—she suddenly disappeared.”

Elinor’s breath caught.

“I had allowed her—imprudently, as it has since turned out—at her earnest desire, to go to Bath with one of her young friends, who was attending her father there for his health. I knew him to be a very good sort of man, and I thought well of his daughter—better than she deserved; for with a most obstinate and ill-judged secrecy, she would tell nothing, would give no clue, though she certainly knew all. He—her father—a well-meaning, but not a quick-sighted man, could really, I believe, give no information; for he had been generally confined to the house while the girls were ranging over the town and making what acquaintance they chose; and he tried to convince me, as thoroughly as he was convinced himself, of his daughter’s being entirely unconcerned in the business.”

His voice hardened on the word business, as if the blandness of it insulted the ruin it named.

“In short, I could learn nothing but that she was gone. All the rest, for eight long months, was left to conjecture. What I thought—what I feared—may be imagined; and what I suffered too.”

“Good heavens!” Elinor cried, unable now to keep her composure smooth. “Could it be—could Willoughby—”

“The first news that reached me of her,” he continued, “came in a letter from herself last October. It was forwarded to me from Delaford, and I received it on the very morning of our intended party to Whitwell; and this was the reason of my leaving Barton so suddenly, which I am sure must at the time have appeared strange to everybody, and which I believe gave offence to some.”

Elinor remembered it perfectly: his abrupt departure, his visible disturbance, the way Willoughby’s looks had judged him.

“Little did Mr. Willoughby imagine,” Colonel Brandon went on, “when his looks censured me for incivility in breaking up the party, that I was called away to the relief of one whom he had made poor and miserable; but had he known it, what would it have availed? Would he have been less gay or less happy in the smiles of your sister? No. He had already done that which no man who can feel for another would do.”

His controlled tone shook, not into loudness, but into a deeper severity.

“He had left the girl whose youth and innocence he had seduced in a situation of the utmost distress, with no creditable home, no help, no friends, ignorant of his address. He had left her, promising to return; he neither returned, nor wrote, nor relieved her.”

“This is beyond everything!” Elinor exclaimed, and the room seemed suddenly closer, the air heavier, as if this knowledge itself had changed its quality.

“His character is now before you,” said Colonel Brandon. “Expensive, dissipated, and worse than both. Knowing all this, as I have now known it many weeks, guess what I must have felt on seeing your sister as fond of him as ever, and on being assured that she was to marry him: guess what I must have felt for all your sakes. When I came to you last week and found you alone, I came determined to know the truth; though irresolute what to do when it was known. My behaviour must have seemed strange to you then; but now you will comprehend it.”

Elinor did comprehend it; and with comprehension came a new weight. She thought of Marianne upstairs—of her proud silences, her quick tears, her insistence on Willoughby’s possible innocence—and felt the cruel necessity of breaking those last defences.

“To suffer you all to be so deceived,” he continued, “to see your sister—but what could I do? I had no hope of interfering with success; and sometimes I thought your sister’s influence might yet reclaim him. But now, after such dishonourable usage, who can tell what were his designs on her? Whatever they may have been, however, she may now, and hereafter doubtless will, turn with gratitude towards her own condition, when she compares it with that of my poor Eliza—when she considers the wretched and hopeless situation of this poor girl, and pictures her to herself, with an affection for him so strong, still as strong as her own, and with a mind tormented by self-reproach which must attend her through life.”

His voice softened, not to excuse, but to urge mercy and perspective.

“Surely this comparison must have its use with her. She will feel her own sufferings to be nothing. They proceed from no misconduct, and can bring no disgrace. On the contrary, every friend must be made still more her friend by them. Concern for her unhappiness, and respect for her fortitude under it, must strengthen every attachment.”

He looked at Elinor with a humility that seemed almost strange in a man so upright.

“Use your own discretion, however, in communicating to her what I have told you. You must know best what will be its effect; but had I not seriously, and from my heart believed it might be of service—might lessen her regrets—I would not have suffered myself to trouble you with this account of my family afflictions, with a recital which may seem to have been intended to raise myself at the expense of others.”

Elinor’s thanks came with grateful earnestness; and with them, the assurance that she did expect material good to Marianne from this dreadful enlightenment.

“I have been more pained,” she said, “by her endeavours to acquit him than by all the rest; for it irritates her mind more than the most perfect conviction of his unworthiness can do. Now, though at first she will suffer much, I am sure she will soon become easier.”

After a short silence, she asked, carefully, “Have you ever seen Mr. Willoughby since you left him at Barton?”

“Yes,” he replied gravely. “Once I have. One meeting was unavoidable.”

Elinor, startled by his manner, looked at him anxiously. “What? have you met him to—”

“I could meet him no other way. Eliza had confessed to me, though most reluctantly, the name of her lover; and when he returned to town—which was within a fortnight after myself—we met by appointment, he to defend, I to punish his conduct. We returned unwounded, and the meeting, therefore, never got abroad.”

Elinor sighed over what she imagined the necessity of such a scene must have been; but she presumed not to censure it.

“Such,” Colonel Brandon added, after a pause, “has been the unhappy resemblance between the fate of mother and daughter; and so imperfectly have I discharged my trust!”

“Is she still in town?” Elinor asked.

“No. As soon as she recovered from her lying-in—for I found her near her delivery—I removed her and her child into the country, and there she remains.”

The words lying-in, child, country fell into Elinor’s mind with a strange heaviness, like items in a ledger of sorrow: the cost of one man’s pleasure tallied in another woman’s lifelong burden.

Soon after, recollecting that he was probably dividing Elinor from her sister at a time when every moment might be required upstairs, Colonel Brandon rose to go. He ended his visit with the same quiet consideration with which he had begun it, receiving again Elinor’s grateful acknowledgements, and leaving her full of compassion and esteem—her thoughts already turning, with reluctant courage, to the cruel task before her: to carry this knowledge, like a bitter draught, to Marianne, in the hope that bitterness might at last purge away illusion.

When Elinor, as soon as she could do so without betraying either haste or cruelty, repeated to her sister the substance of what she had learnt, she had imagined—foolishly, perhaps—that truth, once set upon the table like plain bread, might at least be broken and shared with less anguish than a feast of conjecture. Marianne heard her as one hears the turning of a knife on a board: steadily, without flinching, with eyes that did not once seek escape, and with lips that offered neither defence nor denial. She made no plea for Willoughby; she did not even attempt that delicate art by which the heart tries to sweeten bitterness with a remembered kindness. Only tears, silently gathered, declared how impossible it was for her to receive the story and yet to live unhurt beneath it.

Elinor was not deceived. The conviction went home. She saw it in Marianne’s altered bearing when Colonel Brandon called—no longer the shrinking that had been half resentment, half dread, but a subdued, compassionate respect, as if she had suddenly learnt what it is to carry an old wound and walk nevertheless. She saw, too, that Marianne’s spirits were less violently shaken; the sharp feverishness was gone. Yet wretchedness remained, only changed in its manner—as a fire, when the flames are beaten down, still burns in sullen coals.

For Marianne had lost more than a lover. The fall of Willoughby’s character—his seduction and desertion of Miss Williams, the misery of that injured girl, and the dreadful doubt of what his designs might once have been toward herself—preyed on her with a kind of sickened horror. His heart, gone, was a grief; his honour, found false, was a contamination. And this, which she could scarcely bear within her own mind, she could not bring herself to speak of even to Elinor. She brooded in silence, and that silence, like a room without air, oppressed her sister more than any outcry could have done.

From Barton, their mother’s letters followed quickly, long and full, like winter stores sent up to town—carefully packed, overflowing with feeling, and meant to sustain. To repeat Mrs. Dashwood’s language would have been only to echo what her daughters already knew: disappointment scarcely less painful than Marianne’s, indignation even greater than Elinor’s, and that anxious, tender solicitude which, in a mother’s hand, turns every word into a touch. Yet Elinor, as she read, could not help being struck by one mortifying proof of the depth of Marianne’s humiliation: Mrs. Dashwood spoke of fortitude. Bad indeed must be the affliction, when her mother—who had so long indulged the poetry of sorrow—could entreat her child not to indulge regret.

Mrs. Dashwood had determined, against her own comfort, that Marianne should not be at Barton now. There, every accustomed sound and smell—each gate’s creak, each wind in the trees, each familiar path where a step had once matched another step—would bring back the past with cruel fidelity, setting Willoughby before her not as he truly was, but as she had always seen him there. In London, by contrast, the day had other noises to impose themselves: wheels on stone, the far-off calls of sellers, the thin music that escaped from windows when someone practiced without passion. Variety of objects, of company, of occupations—those common, domestic distractions by which people are sometimes “cheated,” as her mother expressed it, into interest beyond themselves—might yet, at moments, draw Marianne’s mind from its own devouring circle.

Nor did Mrs. Dashwood fear any danger of their meeting Willoughby. His acquaintance, she was persuaded, must now be dropped by all who called themselves their friends. Design could never bring him to them; negligence could never leave them exposed; chance, in the crowd of London, had less in its favour than in the retirement of Devonshire, where it might one day force him before Marianne’s eyes while paying a visit at Allenham on his marriage—an event Mrs. Dashwood, having once only foreseen as probable, had now brought herself to expect as certain.

She had yet another reason—one of family, and therefore, in her mind, sacred. A letter from her son-in-law told her that he and his wife would be in town before the middle of February. It seemed right that her daughters should sometimes see their brother.

Marianne had promised to be guided by her mother’s opinion, and she submitted without open opposition; yet she felt it wrong, founded on mistaken grounds, and cruel in its effect. It deprived her of what she believed the only possible alleviation—the personal sympathy of her mother—and doomed her to such society and such scenes as must prevent her ever knowing a moment’s rest. Still, even in her misery, she found one strange consolation: what brought evil to herself might bring good to Elinor. And Elinor, who suspected—though she did not say so—that a longer stay might make it impossible to avoid Edward entirely, quieted her own apprehensions by thinking that, however it might try her, it was better for Marianne than an immediate return into Devonshire.

Elinor’s watchfulness over her sister was unceasing. As a careful cook keeps smoke from tainting a delicate dish, she guarded Marianne from the very name of Willoughby, knowing how a single syllable might set all the bitterness rising again. Her care was not thrown away. Marianne, without knowing it, reaped the whole advantage; for neither Mrs. Jennings, nor Sir John, nor even Mrs. Palmer ever spoke of him before her.

Elinor wished such forbearance could have been extended to herself; but that was impossible. Day after day she was obliged to listen to the indignation of their friends, poured out with as much noise as if sound itself could scour a stain from a man’s conduct.

Sir John could hardly contain himself. He had always thought well of Willoughby—such a good-natured fellow, such a bold rider; and now it was “an unaccountable business,” a thing past belief. He wished him at the devil with all his heart. He would not speak another word to him, meet him where he might, for all the world—not even if it were by the side of Barton covert and they were kept watching for two hours together. Such a scoundrel of a fellow, such a deceitful dog! Why, it was only the last time they met that Willoughby had offered him one of Folly’s puppies—and this was the end of it!

Mrs. Palmer, in her own manner, was equally enraged. She was determined to drop his acquaintance immediately; she was thankful she had never been acquainted with him at all; she wished Combe Magna were not so near Cleveland—though it did not signify, for it was far too far off to visit. She hated him so much, she said, that she was resolved never to mention his name again; and she should tell everybody she saw how good-for-nothing he was.

Yet Mrs. Palmer’s sympathy, always eager for particulars, found its occupation. Having sworn never to mention his name, she nevertheless took pains to procure all she could of the approaching marriage, and communicated it all to Elinor with the complacency of one laying out a table of curiosities: at what coachmaker’s the new carriage was building; by what painter Mr. Willoughby’s portrait was drawn; at what warehouse Miss Grey’s clothes might be seen. Elinor received these details as one takes an unwanted spice—aware it will overpower everything else, and unable, from politeness, to refuse it.

In this clamour of kindness, the calm, polite unconcern of Lady Middleton was, to Elinor, a relief like cool water. It comforted her to be sure of exciting no interest in at least one person; to know there was one who would meet her without curiosity after particulars, without anxiety for Marianne’s health, without the craving to hear grief described as if it were a fashionable entertainment. There are hours in which every qualification is raised, by the circumstances of the moment, beyond its real value; and Elinor, so often worried down by officious condolence, sometimes rated good-breeding as more indispensable than good-nature.

Lady Middleton expressed her sense of the affair about once every day, or twice if the subject occurred often, by saying, with perfect composure, “It is very shocking, indeed!” And by this continual though gentle vent, she was enabled not only to see the Miss Dashwoods from the first without the smallest emotion, but very soon to see them without recollecting a word of the matter. Having thus supported, in her own opinion, the dignity of her sex—censured what was wrong in the other—she considered herself at liberty to attend to the interest of her assemblies, and therefore determined (though rather against Sir John’s opinion) that, as Mrs. Willoughby would at once be a woman of elegance and fortune, she would leave her card as soon as she married.

Colonel Brandon’s enquiries were of another kind—so delicate and unobtrusive that they never pressed, never jarred. They were never unwelcome to Elinor. He had earned the privilege of intimate discussion by the friendly zeal with which he had endeavoured to soften Marianne’s disappointment; and Elinor, who had been made the unwilling repository of painful history, could talk with him in confidence. His chief reward for the exertion of disclosing old sorrows and present humiliations was given without ceremony: the pitying eye with which Marianne sometimes observed him, and the gentleness of her voice whenever—though it did not often happen—she was obliged, or could oblige herself, to speak to him. Those slight tokens assured him that his effort had not been thrown away, that it had won him an increase of goodwill; and they gave Elinor hopes that it might be farther augmented hereafter.

Mrs. Jennings, who knew nothing of all this fine interior change, saw only that the Colonel continued as grave as ever, and that she could neither prevail upon him to make the offer himself nor commission her to make it for him. At the end of two days she began to think that, instead of Midsummer, they would not be married till Michaelmas; by the end of a week she concluded it would not be a match at all. The good understanding between the Colonel and Miss Dashwood seemed rather to declare that the honours of the mulberry-tree, the canal, and the yew arbour would all be made over to Elinor; and Mrs. Jennings had for some time ceased to think at all of Mrs. Ferrars.

Early in February—within a fortnight of receiving Willoughby’s letter—Elinor had the painful office of informing Marianne that he was married. She had taken care to have the intelligence conveyed to herself as soon as the ceremony was over, because Marianne, every morning, eagerly examined the public papers, and Elinor could not bear the thought of her learning it first from that cold, printed line, surrounded by advertisements and trifles, as if a heart could be broken between notices of new goods and theatrical amusements.

Marianne received the news with a resolute composure that frightened Elinor more than any immediate passion would have done. She made no observation, and at first shed no tears. But after a short time, the tears burst forth with a force that seemed to come from deeper than sorrow—like a spring long dammed, suddenly freed. For the rest of the day she was hardly less pitiable than when she had first learnt to expect the event.

The Willoughbys left town as soon as they were married; and Elinor, now that there could be no danger of Marianne seeing either of them, hoped to prevail on her sister—who had never once left the house since the blow first fell—to go out again by degrees, as she had done before, to let air and motion, if not heal, at least keep life from becoming wholly stagnant.

About this time, the two Miss Steeles, lately arrived at their cousin’s house in Bartlett’s Buildings, Holborn, presented themselves again before their more grand relations in Conduit and Berkeley Streets, and were welcomed by all with great cordiality. Elinor alone was sorry to see them. Their presence always brought her pain; and she hardly knew how to make even a tolerably gracious return to the overpowering delight of Lucy in finding her still in town.

“I should have been quite disappointed if I had not found you here still,” Lucy said repeatedly, laying a strong emphasis on the word, as if it were a claim. “But I always thought I should. I was almost sure you would not leave London yet awhile, though you told me, you know, at Barton, that you should not stay above a month. But I thought at the time you would most likely change your mind when it came to the point. It would have been such a great pity to have went away before your brother and sister came. And now to be sure you will be in no hurry to be gone. I am amazingly glad you did not keep to your word.”

Elinor perfectly understood her meaning—each “still” and “word” was like the sharp clink of coins, counting out possession. She was forced to use all her self-command to make it appear that she did not understand.

“Well, my dear,” cried Mrs. Jennings, “and how did you travel?”

“Not in the stage, I assure you,” replied Miss Steele with quick exultation. “We came post all the way, and had a very smart beau to attend us. Dr. Davies was coming to town, and so we thought we’d join him in a post-chaise; and he behaved very genteelly, and paid ten or twelve shillings more than we did.”

“Oh, oh!” cried Mrs. Jennings. “Very pretty indeed! and the Doctor is a single man, I warrant you.”

“There now,” said Miss Steele, affectedly simpering, “everybody laughs at me so about the Doctor, and I cannot think why. My cousins say they are sure I have made a conquest; but for my part I declare I never think about him from one hour’s end to another. ‘Lord! here comes your beau, Nancy,’ my cousin said t’other day, when she saw him crossing the street to the house. My beau indeed! said I—I cannot think who you mean. The Doctor is no beau of mine.”

“Aye, aye,” said Mrs. Jennings, “that is very pretty talking—but it won’t do—the Doctor is the man, I see.”

“No, indeed!” replied her cousin, with affected earnestness; “and I beg you will contradict it, if you ever hear it talked of.”

Mrs. Jennings directly gave her the gratifying assurance that she certainly would not, and Miss Steele was made completely happy.

Lucy, after a short cessation of her pointed civilities, returned to the subject she loved best—not sympathy, but situation.

“I suppose you will go and stay with your brother and sister, Miss Dashwood, when they come to town.”

“No, I do not think we shall.”

“Oh, yes, I dare say you will.”

Elinor would not humour her by farther opposition.

“What a charming thing it is that Mrs. Dashwood can spare you both for so long a time together!”

“Long a time indeed!” interposed Mrs. Jennings. “Why, their visit is but just begun!”

Lucy was silenced.

“I am sorry we cannot see your sister, Miss Dashwood,” said Miss Steele. “I am sorry she is not well—” for Marianne had left the room on their arrival.

“You are very good. My sister will be equally sorry to miss the pleasure of seeing you; but she has been very much plagued lately with nervous head-aches, which make her unfit for company or conversation.”

“Oh, dear, that is a great pity! but such old friends as Lucy and me!—I think she might see us; and I am sure we would not speak a word.”

Elinor, with great civility, declined the proposal. Her sister was perhaps laid down upon the bed, or in her dressing gown, and therefore not able to come to them.

“Oh, if that’s all,” cried Miss Steele, “we can just as well go and see her.”

Elinor began to find this impertinence too much for her temper; but she was saved the trouble of checking it by Lucy’s sharp reprimand—one of those corrections which, though it did not add sweetness to Lucy’s manners, was of advantage in governing her sister’s.

Marianne had resisted at first with all the silent firmness of a heart that wished to feel nothing but what it already felt. Yet Elinor’s quiet perseverance, and Mrs. Jennings’s bustling good-humour—so full of plans, so sure that air and motion must do something—won by degrees what argument never could. At last Marianne consented to go out for half an hour one morning; but she bargained for it like one agreeing to a medicine: there should be no visits, no long calls, no being made to sit and smile where she could not. Only Gray’s in Sackville Street, where Elinor meant to exchange a few of their mother’s old-fashioned jewels—little relics whose value lay as much in remembered hands as in gold.

The street itself had its London chorus—wheels grating, hawkers crying, the unceasing tread of feet on stone—an acoustic thickening that Marianne seemed to draw around her like a veil. Even so, the bell at Gray’s door chimed with a clean, domestic note, and within, the shop held that peculiar hush of money and finery: the muted rustle of paper, the soft closing of drawers, the low, precise voices that weighed worth in ounces and guineas. The air smelled faintly of metal warmed by human touch, of leather cases, of perfume clinging to gloves—nothing nourishing, nothing that could be made into comfort.

Mrs. Jennings, no more able to pass a door with an acquaintance behind it than to pass a kitchen without lifting the lid of a pot, suddenly recollected a lady “just at the other end of the street” on whom she must call. As she had no business at Gray’s, it was agreed she should go and return for them.

Upstairs they found the room crowded; there were so many customers before them that no one was at liberty to attend to their orders. They sat down at the end of the counter which promised the quickest succession, where only one gentleman was being served. Elinor, with that gentle reliance on common politeness which had so often been disappointed, might have hoped his presence would hasten their turn. But the correctness of his eye, and the delicacy of his taste, proved superior to any civility.

He was ordering a toothpick-case.

It became, for the next quarter of an hour, as grave a business as if the fate of an estate depended on it. Ivory and gold were produced; pearls were considered and rejected, then considered again. He debated size with the solemnity of a man discussing a treaty, demanded a curve here, a clasp there, tried one in his hand as if tasting it for seasoning, and could not be satisfied till every ornament had been appointed by his own inventive fancy. Between these deliberations he bestowed on the Miss Dashwoods three or four broad stares—an inspection so unblushing it fixed his face in Elinor’s memory at once: strong, natural, sterling insignificance, dressed in the first style of fashion.

Marianne was spared contempt or resentment by being wholly unconscious of it. She sat as she might have done in her own chamber, her thoughts gathered close, her senses refusing to take hold of anything around her. The shop’s low murmur, the clink of metal on glass, the sigh of opened cases—everything seemed to pass her by as sound passes a sleeper.

At last the toothpick-case was decided. The gentleman named the last day on which his existence could continue without possessing it, drew on his gloves with leisurely care, cast another look at the sisters—less a compliment than a demand for admiration—and went away with the satisfied air of real conceit and affected indifference.

Elinor lost no time. She brought forward her jewels—those ornaments whose fashion had gone, but not the tenderness attached to them—and was on the point of concluding the exchange when another gentleman presented himself at her side. She turned and saw, with a surprise that overcame even her composure, her brother.

Their affection and pleasure in meeting was just enough to make a very creditable appearance in Mr. Gray’s shop. John Dashwood was really far from being sorry to see his sisters again; he was respectful in his inquiries after their mother, attentive in his manner, and perfectly satisfied that they could be in town and yet not have been instantly received at his door.

Elinor learned he and Fanny had been in London two days.

“I wished very much to call upon you yesterday,” said he, “but it was impossible; we were obliged to take Harry to see the wild beasts at Exeter Exchange, and we spent the rest of the day with Mrs. Ferrars. Harry was vastly pleased. This morning I had fully intended to call in Berkeley Street, if I could possibly find a spare half hour; but one has always so much to do on first coming to town. I am come here to bespeak Fanny a seal. But tomorrow, I think I shall certainly be able to call, and be introduced to your friend Mrs. Jennings. I understand she is a woman of very good fortune. And the Middletons too—you must introduce me to them. As my mother-in-law’s relations, I shall be happy to show them every respect. They are excellent neighbours to you in the country, I understand.”

“Excellent indeed,” said Elinor. “Their attention to our comfort, their friendliness in every particular, is more than I can express.”

“I am extremely glad to hear it—upon my word, extremely glad indeed. But so it ought to be; they are people of large fortune, they are related to you, and every civility and accommodation that can serve to make your situation pleasant might reasonably be expected. And so you are most comfortably settled in your little cottage and want for nothing! Edward brought us a most charming account of the place—the most complete thing of its kind, he said, that ever was—and you all seemed to enjoy it beyond anything. It was a great satisfaction to us to hear it, I assure you.”

Elinor felt a little ashamed—less for herself than for the ease with which he could speak of duty performed while doing so little. She was spared the necessity of answering by the arrival of Mrs. Jennings’s servant, come to say his mistress waited at the door. Mr. Dashwood attended them down stairs, was introduced to Mrs. Jennings at the carriage, repeated his hope of calling the next day, and took his leave.

His visit was, in due time, paid.

He came with what he called an apology from their sister-in-law, who did not attend him—“but she was so much engaged with her mother, that really she had no leisure for going anywhere.” Mrs. Jennings assured him directly she should not stand on ceremony, for they were all cousins, or something like it, and she would certainly wait on Mrs. John Dashwood very soon, and bring Elinor and Marianne to see her.

To the sisters, his manners were calm, perfectly kind; to Mrs. Jennings, most attentively civil. When Colonel Brandon came in soon after, Mr. Dashwood eyed him with a curiosity that seemed to ask only one question—was he rich?—that he might know how much civility to offer in return.

After staying half an hour, he asked Elinor to walk with him to Conduit Street and introduce him to Sir John and Lady Middleton. The weather was remarkably fine—the sort of mild brightness that makes even London’s noise seem less brutal—and she readily consented.

As soon as they were out of the house, his enquiries began.

“Who is Colonel Brandon? Is he a man of fortune?”

“Yes; he has very good property in Dorsetshire.”

“I am glad of it. He seems a most gentlemanlike man; and I think, Elinor, I may congratulate you on the prospect of a very respectable establishment in life.”

“Me, brother! What do you mean?”

“He likes you. I observed him narrowly, and am convinced of it. What is the amount of his fortune?”

“I believe about two thousand a year.”

“Two thousand a-year!” And then, working himself up to a pitch of enthusiastic generosity, he added, “Elinor, I wish with all my heart it were twice as much, for your sake.”

“Indeed I believe you,” said Elinor; “but I am very sure Colonel Brandon has not the smallest wish of marrying me.”

“You are mistaken—very much mistaken. A very little trouble on your side secures him. Perhaps just at present he may be undecided; the smallness of your fortune may make him hang back; his friends may all advise him against it. But some of those little attentions and encouragements which ladies can so easily give will fix him, in spite of himself. And there can be no reason why you should not try for him. It is not to be supposed that any prior attachment on your side—in short, you know as to an attachment of that kind, it is quite out of the question; the objections are insurmountable—you have too much sense not to see all that. Colonel Brandon must be the man; and no civility shall be wanting on my part to make him pleased with you and your family. It is a match that must give universal satisfaction. In short, it is a kind of thing that”—lowering his voice to an important whisper—“will be exceedingly welcome to all parties.”

Recollecting himself, he added with a more ordinary tone, “That is—I mean to say—your friends are all truly anxious to see you well settled; Fanny particularly, for she has your interest very much at heart, I assure you. And her mother too—Mrs. Ferrars—a very good-natured woman; I am sure it would give her great pleasure. She said as much the other day.”

Elinor would not vouchsafe any answer; and the London din, passing in waves—coach-wheels, hooves, a distant hammer—seemed to take up her silence and carry it on.

“It would be something remarkable now,” he continued, “something droll, if Fanny should have a brother and I a sister settling at the same time. And yet it is not very unlikely.”

“Is Mr. Edward Ferrars,” said Elinor, forcing steadiness into her voice, “going to be married?”

“It is not actually settled, but there is such a thing in agitation. He has a most excellent mother. Mrs. Ferrars, with the utmost liberality, will come forward, and settle on him a thousand a year, if the match takes place. The lady is the Hon. Miss Morton—only daughter of the late Lord Morton—with thirty thousand pounds. A very desirable connection on both sides, and I have not a doubt of its taking place in time.
