A thousand a-year is a great deal for a mother to give away—to make over for ever; but Mrs. Ferrars has a noble spirit. To give you another instance of her liberality—the other day, as soon as we came to town, aware that money could not be very plenty with us just now, she put bank-notes into Fanny’s hands to the amount of two hundred pounds. And extremely acceptable it is, for we must live at a great expense while we are here.”

He paused, waiting for Elinor’s compassion, or at least her assent; and she forced herself to say, “Your expenses both in town and country must certainly be considerable; but your income is a large one.”

“Not so large, I dare say, as many people suppose. I do not mean to complain; it is undoubtedly a comfortable one, and I hope will in time be better. The enclosure of Norland Common, now carrying on, is a most serious drain. And then I have made a little purchase within this half year—East Kingham Farm—you must remember the place, where old Gibson used to live. The land was so very desirable for me in every respect, so immediately adjoining my own property, that I felt it my duty to buy it. I could not have answered it to my conscience to let it fall into any other hands. A man must pay for his convenience; and it has cost me a vast deal of money.”

“More than you think it really and intrinsically worth.”

“Why, I hope not that. I might have sold it again, the next day, for more than I gave. But with regard to the purchase-money, I might have been very unfortunate indeed; for the stocks were at that time so low, that if I had not happened to have the necessary sum in my banker’s hands, I must have sold out to very great loss.”

Elinor could only smile—an expression that in her had learned to serve as defence.

“Other great and inevitable expenses too we have had on first coming to Norland. Our respected father, as you well know, bequeathed all the Stanhill effects that remained at Norland—and very valuable they were—to your mother. Far be it from me to repine at his doing so; he had an undoubted right to dispose of his own property as he chose; but, in consequence of it, we have been obliged to make large purchases of linen, china, &c. to supply the place of what was taken away. You may guess, after all these expenses, how very far we must be from being rich, and how acceptable Mrs. Ferrars’s kindness is.”

“Certainly,” said Elinor; “and assisted by her liberality, I hope you may yet live to be in easy circumstances.”

“Another year or two may do much towards it,” he gravely replied; “but however there is still a great deal to be done. There is not a stone laid of Fanny’s green-house, and nothing but the plan of the flower-garden marked out.”

“Where is the green-house to be?”

“Upon the knoll behind the house. The old walnut trees are all come down to make room for it. It will be a very fine object from many parts of the park, and the flower-garden will slope down just before it, and be exceedingly pretty. We have cleared away all the old thorns that grew in patches over the brow.”

Elinor kept her concern and her censure to herself. She saw, in the mind’s eye, those old walnut trees—grown slowly, season by season, their shade and nut-harvest part of a quiet rural memory—felled for glass and show; and she was very thankful Marianne was not present, to feel the blow as a personal injury.

Having now said enough to make his poverty clear, and to do away the necessity of buying a pair of ear-rings for each of his sisters at his next visit to Gray’s, his thoughts took a cheerfuller turn; and he began to congratulate Elinor on having such a friend as Mrs. Jennings.

“She seems a most valuable woman indeed. Her house, her style of living, all bespeak an exceeding good income; and it is an acquaintance that has not only been of great use to you hitherto, but in the end may prove materially advantageous. Her inviting you to town is certainly a vast thing in your favour; and indeed it speaks altogether so great a regard for you, that in all probability when she dies you will not be forgotten. She must have a great deal to leave.”

“Nothing at all, I should rather suppose,” said Elinor, “for she has only her jointure, which will descend to her children.”

“But it is not to be imagined that she lives up to her income. Few people of common prudence will do that; and whatever she saves, she will be able to dispose of.”

“And do you not think it more likely that she should leave it to her daughters than to us?”

“Her daughters are both exceedingly well married, and therefore I cannot perceive the necessity of her remembering them farther. Whereas, in my opinion, by her taking so much notice of you, and treating you in this kind of way, she has given you a sort of claim on her future consideration, which a conscientious woman would not disregard. Nothing can be kinder than her behaviour; and she can hardly do all this without being aware of the expectation it raises.”

“But she raises none in those most concerned. Indeed, brother, your anxiety for our welfare and prosperity carries you too far.”

“Why, to be sure,” said he, seeming to recollect himself, “people have little—very little—in their power. But, my dear Elinor, what is the matter with Marianne? She looks very unwell; has lost her colour, and is grown quite thin. Is she ill?”

“She is not well. She has had a nervous complaint on her for several weeks.”

“I am sorry for that. At her time of life, anything of an illness destroys the bloom for ever! Hers has been a very short one! She was as handsome a girl last September as I ever saw; and as likely to attract the man. There was something in her style of beauty to please them particularly. I remember Fanny used to say she would marry sooner and better than you did; not but what she is exceedingly fond of you, but so it happened to strike her. She will be mistaken, however. I question whether Marianne now will marry a man worth more than five or six hundred a-year, at the utmost; and I am very much deceived if you do not do better. Dorsetshire! I know very little of Dorsetshire; but, my dear Elinor, I shall be exceedingly glad to know more of it; and I think I can answer for your having Fanny and myself among the earliest and best pleased of your visitors.”

Elinor tried—very seriously, very patiently—to convince him there was no likelihood of her marrying Colonel Brandon. But it was an expectation of too much pleasure to himself to be relinquished. He was resolved on seeking an intimacy with that gentleman, and promoting the marriage by every possible attention; having just compunction enough for doing nothing for his sisters himself to be exceedingly anxious that everybody else should do a great deal. An offer from Colonel Brandon, or a legacy from Mrs. Jennings, was the easiest means of atoning for neglect that had become habitual.

They were lucky enough to find Lady Middleton at home, and Sir John came in before their visit ended. Abundance of civilities passed on all sides. Sir John was ready to like anybody; and though Mr. Dashwood did not seem to know much about horses, he soon set him down as a very good-natured fellow. Lady Middleton saw enough of fashion in his appearance to think his acquaintance worth having. Mr. Dashwood went away delighted with both.

“I shall have a charming account to carry to Fanny,” said he, as he walked back with his sister. “Lady Middleton is really a most elegant woman! Such a woman as I am sure Fanny will be glad to know. And Mrs. Jennings too—an exceedingly well-behaved woman—though not so elegant as her daughter. Your sister need not have any scruple even of visiting her; which, to say the truth, has been a little the case, and very naturally—for we only knew that Mrs. Jennings was the widow of a man who had got all his money in a low way; and Fanny and Mrs. Ferrars were both strongly prepossessed that neither she nor her daughters were such kind of women as Fanny would like to associate with. But now I can carry her a most satisfactory account of both.”

Mrs. John Dashwood, being as devoted to her husband’s opinion as she was to her own consequence, waited the very next day upon Mrs. Jennings and her daughter. The experiment rewarded her exactly as she had hoped: Mrs. Jennings—though the very woman who sheltered those poor Dashwood girls—proved, upon nearer inspection, not wholly beneath notice; and Lady Middleton, she declared, was one of the most charming women in the world.

Lady Middleton, for her part, was equally taken with Mrs. John Dashwood. There was, on both sides, a cool self-regard—polished, well-bred, and empty as a covered dish that arrives grandly at table and yields nothing but steam—that drew them together at once. They met, they approved, they smiled with the same measured propriety; and each found in the other a gratifying absence of troublesome understanding.

Yet the very manners which made Mrs. John Dashwood so agreeable to Lady Middleton were ill-seasoned to Mrs. Jennings’s taste. To her, Fanny seemed only a small proud-looking woman with an address too stiff to be cordial, who sat down among her husband’s sisters as if affection were an inconvenience and conversation an expense. Of the quarter of an hour she bestowed on Berkeley Street, she passed at least half of it in silence—like a kitchen clock that will not strike though everyone is waiting to hear it.

Elinor, all the while, had one question beating time beneath every civil word and every practiced smile. Was Edward in town? She wished it exceedingly to know, but would not ask. Fanny would not have spoken his name for any persuasion; not until she could announce, with a triumph she had long been airing in imagination, that his marriage with Miss Morton was resolved on, or until John’s expectations from Colonel Brandon were answered. She believed, with that busy certainty which flatters itself into prophecy, that Edward and Elinor were still so attached, they must be divided—divided in word, divided in deed—on every occasion.

But what Fanny withheld, another tongue supplied with eager industry. Lucy Steele came, very shortly, to claim Elinor’s compassion—poor Lucy, who could not see Edward, though he had arrived in town with Mr. and Mrs. Dashwood. He dared not come to Bartlett’s Buildings for fear of detection; and though their mutual impatience was too tender and too guilty to be proclaimed, they could do nothing at present but write.

Edward himself, in his quiet, painful honesty, confirmed his being in town in a way that pierced Elinor more surely than any spoken intelligence. Twice he called in Berkeley Street. Twice his card lay upon the table when they returned from their morning engagements—small, stiff, and mute as a folded napkin, set down where it ought not to be and yet cannot be ignored. Elinor was pleased that he had called; and still more pleased that she had missed him.

The Dashwoods, prodigiously delighted with the Middletons, determined—though not much in the habit of giving anything—to give them a dinner. They had taken, for three months, a very good house in Harley Street; and thither they invited Lady Middleton and her husband, Mrs. Jennings, and their sisters. John Dashwood, careful as a man can be when he means to profit by his civility, secured Colonel Brandon also. The Colonel, always glad to be where the Miss Dashwoods were, received this eager attention with some surprise and more pleasure.

They were to meet Mrs. Ferrars. Elinor could not learn whether her sons were to be of the party; but the expectation of seeing Edward’s mother was enough to fix her mind upon the engagement. Once, such an introduction would have filled her with dread; now, though the old curiosity still pricked her—what was Mrs. Ferrars like?—she could anticipate meeting her with an indifference as to her opinion of herself, which felt almost like a strange new language upon Elinor’s tongue.

That interest, however, was soon increased—more strongly than pleasantly—by hearing that the Miss Steeles were also to be of the party. They had recommended themselves to Lady Middleton with that patient assiduity which clings like perfume: not always delicate, not always natural, but persistent. Lucy was certainly not elegant, and her sister not even genteel; yet Lady Middleton, as ready as Sir John to be surrounded by those who would admire her arrangements, asked them to spend a week or two in Conduit Street. It happened to be particularly convenient to the Miss Steeles—particularly convenient, indeed—that their visit should begin a few days before the party took place.

Their claims to Mrs. John Dashwood’s notice, as nieces of the gentleman who for many years had had the care of her brother, might not have carried them far toward her table; but as Lady Middleton’s guests they must be received. And Lucy, who had long wanted to be personally known to Edward’s family—to have a nearer view of their tempers, their habits, and her own difficulties, and to find the tender places where she might press—had seldom been happier than on receiving Mrs. John Dashwood’s card. She took it almost as one might take a place at a great table: not merely a seat, but an opening.

On Elinor the effect was very different. She began immediately to determine that Edward, who lived with his mother, must be asked as his mother was, to a party given by his sister; and to see him for the first time, after all that had passed, in the company of Lucy—Lucy beside him, Lucy smiling, Lucy certain—she hardly knew how she could bear it.

These apprehensions were not founded entirely on reason, and certainly not at all on truth. They were relieved, however, not by Elinor’s own recollection, but by Lucy’s good will; for Lucy, believing herself to be inflicting a severe disappointment, told her that Edward certainly would not be in Harley Street on Tuesday. She even hoped to sharpen the wound by persuading her that he was kept away by the extreme affection for herself, which he could not conceal when they were together—an insinuation delivered with that careful sweetness which is meant to be tasted and remembered.

The important Tuesday came.

“Pity me, dear Miss Dashwood!” said Lucy, as they walked up the stairs together; for the Middletons arrived so directly after Mrs. Jennings that they all followed the servant at the same time, their footsteps and the rustle of their clothes making a kind of domestic overture in the narrow passage. “There is nobody here but you that can feel for me. I declare I can hardly stand. Good gracious! In a moment I shall see the person that all my happiness depends on—that is to be my mother!”

Elinor could have given her immediate relief by suggesting the possibility of its being Miss Morton’s mother, rather than her own, whom they were about to behold; but instead of doing that, she assured her—and with great sincerity—that she did pity her. Lucy, though really uncomfortable herself, had at least hoped to be an object of irrepressible envy; and Elinor’s pity astonished her more than any coldness could have done.

Mrs. Ferrars was a little, thin woman, upright even to formality, in her figure, and serious even to sourness, in her aspect. Her complexion was sallow; her features small, without beauty, and naturally without expression; but a lucky contraction of the brow rescued her countenance from the disgrace of insipidity by giving it the strong characters of pride and ill nature. She was not a woman of many words; unlike people in general, she proportioned her speech to the number of her ideas. Of the few syllables that escaped her, not one fell to the share of Miss Dashwood, whom she eyed with the spirited determination of disliking her at all events—as if dislike were an heirloom, handed down with the silver and the name, and requiring only a proper object to keep it bright.

Elinor could not now be made unhappy by such behaviour. A few months ago it would have hurt her exceedingly; but it was not in Mrs. Ferrars’s power to distress her by it now. The difference of her manners to the Miss Steeles—a difference which seemed purposely made to humble Elinor more—only amused her. She could not but smile to see the graciousness of both mother and daughter toward the very person—Lucy was particularly distinguished—whom, of all others, had they known as much as Elinor did, they would have been most anxious to mortify; while she herself, who had comparatively no power to wound them, sat pointedly slighted by both.

But while she smiled at a graciousness so misapplied, she could not reflect on the mean-spirited folly from which it sprang, nor observe the studied attentions with which the Miss Steeles courted its continuance, without thoroughly despising them all four. Lucy was all exultation on being so honorably noticed; and Miss Steele wanted only to be teased about Dr. Davies to be perfectly happy.

Dinner was a grand one. The servants were numerous; the plate shone; every course arrived with a practiced punctuality; and everything bespoke the mistress’s inclination for show and the master’s ability to support it. In spite of the improvements and additions making to the Norland estate—and in spite of its owner having once been within some thousand pounds of being obliged to sell out at a loss—nothing in Harley Street gave the least symptom of that indigence which John Dashwood had tried to infer from it. No poverty appeared—except of conversation; and there the deficiency was considerable. John Dashwood had not much to say for himself that was worth hearing, and his wife still less; but there was no peculiar disgrace in this, for it was much the case with the chief of their visitors, who almost all laboured under one or other disqualification for being agreeable: want of sense, either natural or improved; want of elegance; want of spirits; or want of temper.

When the ladies withdrew to the drawing-room after dinner, this poverty was particularly evident; for the gentlemen had supplied the discourse with some variety—the variety of politics, inclosing land, and breaking horses—but then it was all over. One subject only engaged the ladies till coffee came in: the comparative heights of Harry Dashwood and Lady Middleton’s second son William, who were nearly of the same age.

Had both the children been there, the question might have been determined too easily by measuring them at once; but as Harry only was present, all was conjectural assertion on both sides. Everyone had a right to be equally positive, and to repeat that positiveness as often as it pleased them—until the very air of the room seemed filled with the thin, tapping sound of opinions being set down and set down again, like spoons on china.

The parties stood thus: the two mothers, though each really convinced her own son the tallest, politely decided in favour of the other. The two grandmothers, with not less partiality but more sincerity, were equally earnest for their own descendant. Lucy, hardly less anxious to please one parent than the other, thought both boys remarkably tall for their age, and could not conceive that there could be the smallest difference in the world between them. Miss Steele, with yet greater address, gave her opinion as fast as she could in favour of each.

Elinor, having once delivered hers on William’s side—by which she offended Mrs. Ferrars and Fanny still more—did not see the necessity of enforcing it by any farther assertion. Marianne, when called upon, offended them all by declaring she had no opinion to give, as she had never thought about it.

Before removing from Norland, Elinor had painted a very pretty pair of screens for her sister-in-law. They were now just mounted and brought home, and ornamented the present drawing-room. These screens caught John Dashwood’s eye as he followed the other gentlemen in, and with the officiousness of a man eager to display his connexions, he handed them to Colonel Brandon for admiration.

“These are done by my eldest sister,” said he. “And you, as a man of taste, will, I dare say, be pleased with them. I do not know whether you have ever happened to see any of her performances before, but she is in general reckoned to draw extremely well.”

The Colonel, disclaiming all pretensions to connoisseurship, warmly admired the screens—as he would have admired anything painted by Miss Dashwood; and as curiosity was of course excited, they were handed round.

Mrs. Ferrars, not aware of their being Elinor’s work, particularly requested to look at them; and after they had received gratifying testimony of Lady Middleton’s approbation, Fanny presented them to her mother, considerately informing her at the same time that they were done by Miss Dashwood.

“Hum,” said Mrs. Ferrars. “Very pretty.” And without regarding them at all, returned them to her daughter.

Perhaps Fanny thought for a moment that her mother had been quite rude enough—for colouring a little, she immediately said, “They are very pretty, ma’am—an’t they?” But then, the dread of having been too civil—too encouraging—came over her, for she presently added, “Do you not think they are something in Miss Morton’s style of painting, ma’am? She does paint most delightfully! How beautifully her last landscape is done!”

“Beautifully indeed! But she does every thing well.”

Marianne could not bear this. She was already greatly displeased with Mrs. Ferrars; and such ill-timed praise of another at Elinor’s expense—though she had not any notion of what was principally meant by it—provoked her immediately to say, with warmth, “This is admiration of a very particular kind! What is Miss Morton to us? Who knows, or who cares, for her? It is Elinor of whom we think and speak.” And so saying, she took the screens out of her sister-in-law’s hands, to admire them herself as they ought to be admired.

Mrs. Ferrars looked exceedingly angry; and drawing herself up more stiffly than ever, pronounced, in retort, this bitter philippic: “Miss Morton is Lord Morton’s daughter.”

Fanny looked very angry too; her husband was all in a fright at his sister’s audacity. Elinor was much more hurt by Marianne’s warmth than she had been by what produced it; but Colonel Brandon’s eyes, fixed on Marianne, declared that he noticed only what was amiable in it—the affectionate heart which could not bear to see a sister slighted in the smallest point.

Marianne’s feelings did not stop there. The cold insolence of Mrs. Ferrars’s general behaviour to Elinor seemed, to her, to foretell such difficulties and distresses as her own wounded heart taught her to think of with horror. Urged by a strong impulse of affectionate sensibility, she moved, after a moment, to Elinor’s chair; and putting one arm round her neck and one cheek close to hers, said in a low but eager voice, “Dear, dear Elinor, don’t mind them. Don’t let them make you unhappy.”

She could say no more. Her spirits were quite overcome; and hiding her face on Elinor’s shoulder, she burst into tears.

Everybody’s attention was called; almost everybody was concerned. Colonel Brandon rose and went to them without knowing what he did. Mrs. Jennings, with a very intelligent “Ah! poor dear,” immediately gave her her salts. Sir John felt so desperately enraged against the author of this nervous distress that he instantly changed his seat to one close by Lucy Steele, and gave her in a whisper a brief account of the whole shocking affair—as if whispering could soften its harshness, or as if the sound of indignation, kept low, were less injurious to the room.

In a few minutes Marianne was recovered enough to put an end to the bustle and sit down among the rest; though her spirits retained the impression of what had passed the whole evening, as a room retains the smell of spilt wine long after the cloth is changed.

“Poor Marianne!” said her brother to Colonel Brandon, in a low voice, as soon as he could secure his attention. “She has not such good health as her sister,—she is very nervous,—she has not Elinor’s constitution;—and one must allow that there is something very trying to a young woman who has been a beauty in the loss of her personal attractions. You would not think it perhaps, but Marianne was remarkably handsome a few months ago; quite as handsome as Elinor. Now you see it is all gone.”

Elinor’s curiosity—so long kept in check by prudence, by necessity, by that habit of swallowing disappointment without so much as a grimace—had, at last, been fed. She had seen Mrs. Ferrars; and the taste it left was one she should not soon forget: sharp as alum, mean as watered broth, with that after-bite of pride which clings to the palate and makes even a rich table seem barren. In one short visit she had read enough in Mrs. Ferrars’s eye, in the rigid economy of her civility, in the way her words were weighed as if affection itself were a coin to be hoarded, to understand what storms must have beaten upon Edward’s secret engagement—how every domestic hour, every letter, every prospective comfort, would have been soured and stinted, had he been free to marry where he loved.

She had almost, for her own sake, felt a strange gratitude that one great obstacle—one that hurt her most—kept her from a lesser bondage: from dependence on Mrs. Ferrars’s caprice, from the humiliating hunger of waiting upon that woman’s good opinion as if it were bread. If Lucy had been kinder, if she had possessed even a little honest warmth, Elinor told herself she ought to have rejoiced at Edward’s being fettered to her; for then her pain would have had the decency of being the price of another person’s happiness. But Lucy was Lucy; and Elinor could not make virtue out of mere necessity.

What surprised her was not Mrs. Ferrars’s determined prejudice—of that she had seen enough—but the ease with which Lucy’s spirits rose under a civility that was, in truth, little more than a pointed exclusion. It was as if a guest, served the best cut only because the rightful one had been denied a place at table, should call the meal a triumph of personal merit. Lucy’s vanity made her hunger, and hunger made her blind: the attention paid to her because she was not Elinor appeared, to Lucy, as devotion to herself.

This blindness returned the next morning, even more insistently; for at Lucy’s particular desire, Lady Middleton set her down in Berkeley Street, on the chance of finding Elinor alone. Chance favoured her. A message from Mrs. Palmer soon after Lucy’s arrival drew Mrs. Jennings away; the door had scarcely shut upon the brisk shuffle of her departure when Lucy, as if she had been holding her breath for it, turned to Elinor with an eagerness that would not be denied.

“My dear friend,” she cried, leaning forward as if happiness were a secret to be whispered, “I come to talk to you of my happiness. Could anything be so flattering as Mrs. Ferrars’s way of treating me yesterday? So exceeding affable as she was! You know how I dreaded the thoughts of seeing her; but the very moment I was introduced, there was such an affability in her behaviour as really should seem to say she had quite took a fancy to me. Now was not it so? You saw it all; and was not you quite struck with it?”

Elinor, whose mind, like a careful housekeeper, could not help sorting impressions by their true value, answered as steadily as she could.

“She was certainly very civil to you.”

“Civil!” Lucy repeated, as if Elinor had named a poor dish when she expected praise of a feast. “Did you see nothing but only civility? I saw a vast deal more. Such kindness as fell to the share of nobody but me! No pride, no hauteur, and your sister just the same—all sweetness and affability!”

Elinor wished, with a kind of physical longing, to turn the conversation upon any safer subject—upon the weather, the state of the streets, the price of butter in London compared to Devonshire—but Lucy clung to her triumph as a cook clings to an admired receipt, insisting it be acknowledged perfect.

Lucy pressed, and Elinor was obliged to go on.

“Undoubtedly, if they had known your engagement,” said she, forcing truth through politeness, “nothing could be more flattering than their treatment of you;—but as that was not the case—”

“I guessed you would say so,” Lucy cut in quickly, as if she had heard this objection rehearsed already in her own mind and was prepared to season it away. “But there was no reason in the world why Mrs. Ferrars should seem to like me if she did not; and her liking me is every thing. You shan’t talk me out of my satisfaction. I am sure it will all end well, and there will be no difficulties at all, to what I used to think. Mrs. Ferrars is a charming woman, and so is your sister. They are both delightful women indeed!—I wonder I should never hear you say how agreeable Mrs. Dashwood was!”

There were questions that were meant less to be answered than to place a person in the wrong. Elinor felt this one settle upon her like smoke in a room with no draught; she had no clean reply, and she did not attempt any.

Lucy, who could not endure silence unless it served her, watched Elinor’s face with a little anxious sharpness. “Are you ill, Miss Dashwood?—you seem low—you don’t speak;—sure you an’t well.”

“I never was in better health,” Elinor returned; and if her voice carried any flatness, it was the fatigue of holding herself steady, not the least tremor of sickness.

“I am glad of it with all my heart; but really you did not look it. I should be sorry to have you ill; you, that have been the greatest comfort to me in the world!—Heaven knows what I should have done without your friendship.”

Elinor tried to produce a civil answer, as one forces a thin soup into the appearance of nourishment; and though she doubted her own success, it seemed to satisfy Lucy. Lucy’s vanity fed easily; it required only the shape of assent, not its substance.

“Indeed I am perfectly convinced of your regard for me,” Lucy continued, with a composed air of possession, “and next to Edward’s love it is the greatest comfort I have. Poor Edward! But now there is one good thing; we shall be able to meet, and meet pretty often, for Lady Middleton’s delighted with Mrs. Dashwood, so we shall be a good deal in Harley Street, I dare say, and Edward spends half his time with his sister—besides, Lady Middleton and Mrs. Ferrars will visit now;—and Mrs. Ferrars and your sister were both so good to say more than once they should always be glad to see me. They are such charming women!—I am sure if ever you tell your sister what I think of her, you cannot speak too high.”

Elinor, guarding her own heart as carefully as she might guard a small store of precious spice—meant for her mother, for Marianne, for the few she loved—gave no encouragement to the hope that she should repeat Lucy’s praises to Mrs. Dashwood.

Lucy went on, warming to the subject as if reciting the pleasures of a dinner still to come. “I am sure I should have seen it in a moment if Mrs. Ferrars had took a dislike to me. If she had only made me a formal courtesy, for instance, without saying a word, and never after had took any notice of me, and never looked at me in a pleasant way—you know what I mean—if I had been treated in that forbidding sort of way, I should have gave it all up in despair. I could not have stood it. For where she does dislike, I know it is most violent.”

Elinor had hardly time to consider what answer politeness demanded—if any answer could be made without agreeing to Lucy’s whole delusion—when the door was thrown open. The servant announced Mr. Ferrars; and Edward walked in.

It was the very arrangement each of them would have prayed to avoid: all three together, and without the shelter of any other presence to diffuse embarrassment. The room seemed suddenly too small; even the ordinary sounds of the house—the distant murmur of a carriage in the street, the faint click of a fire settling—grew too distinct, as if the air itself had sharpened its hearing to bear witness against them.

They all looked exceedingly foolish. Edward, in particular, stood as if he had stepped into a kitchen at the wrong hour and found every hand stopped, every eye on him, waiting for an explanation he could not give. He seemed as much inclined to retreat as to advance.

The ladies recovered themselves first. Lucy, whose business it was to preserve appearances, and who could practise secrecy with the calm of long habit, did not put herself forward. She could only look what she meant to suggest; after slightly addressing him, she said no more.

Elinor had more to do. Her mind, trained by daily discipline to serve others even when her own appetite was gone, forced itself into action. For Edward’s sake—and for her own dignity—she summoned a look and manner almost easy, almost open; and by another effort, another struggle, she improved them. She would not allow Lucy’s presence, nor the bitter knowledge of injustice to herself, to deter her from attentions Edward was due as a friend and almost a relation.

She told him she was happy to see him; she said she had regretted being from home when he had called before in Berkeley Street. She spoke of her mother’s health, of their coming to town—things Edward ought to have asked, but did not. Lucy’s eyes watched her narrowly, as if measuring every word for hidden meaning; yet Elinor persisted, refusing to let Lucy’s silent scrutiny dictate her civility.

Elinor’s steadiness reassured Edward enough to sit down; but his embarrassment still exceeded that of either lady. It was natural. Lucy’s heart had the indifference of self-interest; Elinor’s conscience had, at least, the comfort of having acted with honour. Edward had neither refuge; his feelings were too tender to be callous, his principles too alive to be easy.

Lucy, with a demure and settled air, made no contribution to the comfort of the others; she would not say a word. Almost every thing that was said proceeded from Elinor, who had to keep conversation moving as one keeps a failing fire alive—by small additions, by patient tending, while the room grows colder all the same.

At length Elinor felt herself so heroically disposed—so determined to give Edward even the small mercy of a moment unobserved—that she resolved, under pretence of fetching Marianne, to leave the others by themselves. She did it in the handsomest manner, and then, once outside, loitered for several minutes on the landing-place with a fortitude that had nothing to display itself upon but the sound of her own breath and the distant stir of the household. The very boards beneath her feet seemed to remember, in their small creaks and settled groans, other footsteps, other visits—how often a house becomes the keeper of secrets that the tongue cannot utter.

But when Marianne heard, raptures could not be delayed. Her joy hurried her into the drawing-room immediately, as sudden as a kettle beginning to sing when one has forgotten it on the hob. Her pleasure in seeing Edward was like every other feeling of hers—strong in itself, and strongly spoken. She met him with a hand that would be taken and a voice that carried all the affection of a sister.

“Dear Edward!” she cried, “this is a moment of great happiness!—This would almost make amends for every thing!”

Edward tried to return her kindness as it deserved; but before such witnesses he dared not say half what he felt. They sat down again; and for a moment or two all were silent—Marianne looking with speaking tenderness, now at Edward, now at Elinor, regretting only that delight should be checked by Lucy’s unwelcome presence.

Edward spoke first. He noticed Marianne’s altered looks, and expressed fear that London did not agree with her.

“Oh, don’t think of me!” Marianne replied with spirited earnestness; yet her eyes filled with tears as she spoke, and her voice shook like water in a glass carried too quickly. “Don’t think of my health. Elinor is well, you see. That must be enough for us both.”

The remark, meant as affection, fell as a weight. It did not make Edward or Elinor easier; nor did it conciliate Lucy, who looked up at Marianne with an expression far from benign.

“Do you like London?” Edward asked, eager for any subject that might carry them away from dangerous feeling.

“Not at all. I expected much pleasure in it, but I have found none. The sight of you, Edward, is the only comfort it has afforded; and thank Heaven! you are what you always were!”

She paused. No one spoke. The air in the room felt strained, as if even the curtains listened.

“I think, Elinor,” Marianne added presently, “we must employ Edward to take care of us in our return to Barton. In a week or two, I suppose, we shall be going; and I trust Edward will not be very unwilling to accept the charge.”

Poor Edward muttered something—what it was nobody knew, not even himself. But Marianne, seeing his agitation, traced it without hesitation to whatever cause best pleased her own generous interpretation, and was satisfied. She soon talked of something else.

“We spent such a day, Edward, in Harley Street yesterday! So dull, so wretchedly dull!—But I have much to say to you on that head, which cannot be said now.” With admirable discretion she deferred, until privacy, the assurance that she found their mutual relatives more disagreeable than ever, and that she was particularly disgusted with his mother.

“But why were you not there, Edward?—Why did you not come?”

“I was engaged elsewhere.”

“Engaged! But what was that, when such friends were to be met?”

“Perhaps, Miss Marianne,” Lucy cried, eager to take a small revenge where she could, “you think young men never stand upon engagements, if they have no mind to keep them, little as well as great.”

Elinor felt a flash of anger—hot, quick, and swallowed down—yet Marianne seemed wholly insensible of the sting. She replied calmly, with that fearless sincerity which made her truth a kind of blade.

“Not so, indeed; for, seriously speaking, I am very sure that conscience only kept Edward from Harley Street. And I really believe he has the most delicate conscience in the world; the most scrupulous in performing every engagement, however minute, and however it may make against his interest or pleasure. He is the most fearful of giving pain, of wounding expectation, and the most incapable of being selfish, of any body I ever saw. Edward, it is so, and I will say it. What! are you never to hear yourself praised!—Then you must be no friend of mine; for those who will accept of my love and esteem, must submit to my open commendation.”

In any other room, with any other company, such praise might have been warm as good bread, opening the heart by its very scent. Here it was ill-suited to two thirds of those who heard it, and unexhilarating to Edward in the extreme; it struck his conscience where it was most tender, and made his constraint visible. He rose very soon to go away.

“Going so soon!” Marianne exclaimed. “My dear Edward, this must not be.” Drawing him a little aside, she whispered her persuasion that Lucy could not stay much longer.

Even that encouragement failed. He would go.

Lucy, who would have outstayed him had his visit lasted two hours, soon afterwards went away as well; not from delicacy, but because her triumph could be postponed and rehearsed again another day.

When she had left them, Marianne’s impatience broke out.

“What can bring her here so often?” she said. “Could not she see that we wanted her gone!—how teazing to Edward!”

Elinor, forced by habit into the defence of appearances, answered with an effort at composure. “Why so?—we were all his friends, and Lucy has been the longest known to him of any. It is but natural that he should like to see her as well as ourselves.”

Marianne looked at her steadily, as if listening for a familiar voice spoken in a foreign dialect—words whose sense she understood and yet could not bear because they did not belong to Elinor’s true language of feeling.

“You know, Elinor,” she said, “that this is a kind of talking which I cannot bear. If you only hope to have your assertion contradicted, as I must suppose to be the case, you ought to recollect that I am the last person in the world to do it. I cannot descend to be tricked out of assurances that are not really wanted.”

And with that she left the room.

Elinor did not dare follow. Bound as she was by her promise of secrecy to Lucy, she could give no information that would convince Marianne; and painful as the consequences of Marianne’s continuing in error might be, she was obliged to submit to it. All she could hope was that Edward would not often expose her—or himself—to the distress of hearing Marianne’s mistaken warmth, nor to the repetition of any part of the pain that had attended their recent meeting; and she had every reason to expect it.

Within a few days of their last little excursion, the public prints—those brisk, rustling sheets that arrived with the morning’s clatter of wheels and the sharp slap of a boy’s palm against a knocker—announced, with all the importance of empire, that the lady of Thomas Palmer, Esq. had been safely delivered of a son and heir. It was an item that, to strangers, might have read like any other fragment of fashionable news; but to Mrs. Jennings it tasted like triumph itself, rich and warming as strong broth after an anxious night. She repeated it as though it were a blessing said over the cradle, and set about making her joy useful.

For Charlotte’s sake she altered the whole ordering of her days. Every morning, as soon as her cap was pinned and her gown settled, she was off to Cleveland House; and she did not return until late, when the street outside her own door had quieted into that thin London hush in which even the distant hooves seem to tread more softly. The consequence was that Elinor and Marianne, at the particular request of the Middletons, spent the whole of every day in Conduit Street.

For their own comfort they would much rather have remained, at least through the morning, in Mrs. Jennings’s house, where there was at least the homely certainty of regular tea and a fire that smelled properly of coal instead of show; but it was not a thing to be urged against the wishes of everybody. Their hours were therefore made over to Lady Middleton and the two Miss Steeles—company by whom their presence was professedly sought and in fact scarcely valued. Elinor’s quiet sense and Marianne’s delicate reserve were not the sort of relish Lady Middleton cared for; and to the Miss Steeles they were regarded with a jealous appetite, as if they were intruders at a table where kindness was to be carved and portioned out, and where Lucy had meant to keep the choicest slices for herself.

Nothing could be more polite than Lady Middleton’s behaviour. Her voice was always smooth; her smile always ready; her hand always extended to direct them toward the sofa, as though the arrangement of bodies in a drawing-room were the chief virtue of civil life. Yet she did not really like them at all. Because they neither flattered her nor her children, she could not believe them good-natured; and because they were fond of reading, she fancied them satirical—perhaps without exactly knowing what it was to be satirical; but that did not signify. It was a censure in common use, easily given, like sprinkling too much salt over a dish one has not tasted.

Their presence was a restraint both on her and on Lucy. It checked the idleness of one, and the business of the other. Lady Middleton, who could lounge for hours with the contented stillness of a cat by the hearth, was ashamed of doing nothing before them; and Lucy, who could busy herself with praise as another woman might with pastry—folding and shaping it into pretty forms meant to please—feared that Elinor and Marianne would despise her for offering such sweetmeats. Miss Steele, meanwhile, was the least discomposed. She had a cheerful hardiness that could live on scanty rations of attention; and it was in the power of the Dashwood sisters to reconcile her entirely to their being there.

Would either of them only have given her a full and minute account of the whole affair between Marianne and Mr. Willoughby, she would have thought herself amply paid for the sacrifice of the best place by the fire after dinner which their arrival occasioned. But this conciliation was not granted; for though she often threw out expressions of pity for her sister to Elinor, and more than once dropped a reflection on the inconstancy of beaux before Marianne, no effect was produced but a look of indifference from the former, or of disgust in the latter. An effort even lighter might have made her their friend. Would they only have laughed at her about the Doctor! But so little were they, any more than the others, inclined to oblige her, that if Sir John dined from home she might spend a whole day without hearing any other raillery on the subject than what she was kind enough to bestow on herself—laughing in her own way, loudly, to fill up the spaces of silence as a kitchen-maid might bang a pan to prove she is not idle.

All these jealousies and discontents, however, were so totally unsuspected by Mrs. Jennings that she thought it a delightful thing for the girls to be together; and she generally congratulated her young friends every night on having escaped the company of a stupid old woman so long. Sometimes she joined them at Sir John’s, sometimes she drew them back to her own house; but wherever she was, she always came in excellent spirits, full of delight and importance, attributing Charlotte’s well-doing to her own care, and ready to give so exact, so minute a detail of her situation as only Miss Steele had curiosity enough to desire. She spoke of the lying-in room, of the nurse, of the draughts, of the very linen and the airing of it, with such relish and certainty that one might have imagined she could tell by scent alone whether a sheet had been properly dried before the fire.

One thing did disturb her, and of that she made her daily complaint. Mr. Palmer maintained the common but unfatherly opinion among his sex of all infants being alike; and though she could plainly perceive, at different times, the most striking resemblance between this baby and every one of his relations on both sides, there was no convincing his father of it—no persuading him to believe that it was not exactly like every other baby of the same age; nor could he even be brought to acknowledge the simple proposition of its being the finest child in the world.

It was about this time that a misfortune befell Mrs. John Dashwood; and like many misfortunes among the well-appointed, it was not of sickness or death, but of being forced into decent behaviour by accident. It so happened that while her two sisters with Mrs. Jennings were first calling on her in Harley Street, another of her acquaintance dropped in—a circumstance in itself not apparently likely to produce evil. But while the imaginations of other people will carry them away to form wrong judgments of our conduct, and to decide upon it by slight appearances, one’s happiness must in some measure always be at the mercy of chance.

In the present instance, this last-arrived lady allowed her fancy to outrun truth and probability so far that, on merely hearing the name of the Miss Dashwoods and understanding them to be Mr. Dashwood’s sisters, she immediately concluded they were staying in Harley Street; and this misconstruction produced, within a day or two afterwards, cards of invitation for them as well as for their brother and sister, to a small musical party at her house.

The consequence was, that Mrs. John Dashwood was obliged to submit not only to the exceedingly great inconvenience of sending her carriage for the Miss Dashwoods, but—what was still worse—must be subject to all the unpleasantness of appearing to treat them with attention. And who could tell that they might not expect to go out with her a second time? The power of disappointing them, it was true, must always be hers. But that was not enough; for when people are determined on a mode of conduct which they know to be wrong, they feel injured by the expectation of anything better from them.

Marianne had now been brought by degrees so much into the habit of going out every day, that it had become a matter of indifference to her whether she went or not. She prepared quietly and mechanically for every evening’s engagement, without expecting the smallest amusement from any, and very often without knowing, till the last moment, where it was to take her. To her dress and appearance she was grown so perfectly indifferent as not to bestow half the consideration on it, during the whole of her toilet, which it received from Miss Steele in the first five minutes of their being together when it was finished.

Nothing escaped Miss Steele’s minute observation and general curiosity. She saw everything and asked everything; was never easy till she knew the price of every part of Marianne’s dress; could have guessed the number of her gowns altogether with better judgment than Marianne herself; and was not without hopes of finding out before they parted how much her washing cost per week, and how much she had every year to spend upon herself. Her attention ran over Marianne’s attire the way an officious housekeeper runs over a market-basket—pinching, estimating, longing to know what every morsel cost and who supplied it—only here the basket was ribbon, muslin, and shoe-leather.

The impertinence of these scrutinies was generally concluded with a compliment; and though meant as its douceur, it was considered by Marianne as the greatest impertinence of all. For after undergoing an examination into the value and make of her gown, the colour of her shoes, and the arrangement of her hair, she was almost sure of being told that, upon her word, she looked vastly smart, and that Miss Steele dared to say she would make a great many conquests.

With such encouragement was she dismissed on the present occasion to her brother’s carriage; they were ready to enter five minutes after it stopped at the door—a punctuality not very agreeable to their sister-in-law, who had preceded them to the house of her acquaintance, and was there hoping for some delay on their part that might inconvenience either herself or her coachman.

The events of the evening were not very remarkable. The party, like other musical parties, comprehended a great many people who had real taste for the performance, and a great many more who had none at all; and the performers themselves were, as usual, in their own estimation, and that of their immediate friends, the first private performers in England. The room swelled with sound—the brisk chitter of voices rising and falling like sparrows under the eaves, the scraping of chairs, the bright insistence of the pianoforte, and, underneath, that constant London under-hum, as if the city itself breathed through the cracks of windows and the seams of curtains. To ears that longed for honest quiet—the creek of a pump-handle, the wind in bare branches—this cultivated noise could feel like a kind of oppression, art made into another fashionable duty.

As Elinor was neither musical nor affecting to be so, she made no scruple of turning her eyes from the grand pianoforte whenever it suited her; and unrestrained even by the presence of a harp and violoncello, would fix them at pleasure on any other object in the room. In one of these excursive glances she perceived, among a group of young men, the very he who had given them a lecture on toothpick-cases at Gray’s. She perceived him soon afterwards looking at herself and speaking familiarly to her brother; and had just determined to find out his name from the latter, when they both came towards her, and Mr. Dashwood introduced him as Mr. Robert Ferrars.

He addressed her with easy civility, and twisted his head into a bow which assured her as plainly as words could have done that he was exactly the coxcomb she had heard him described to be by Lucy. Happy had it been for her, if her regard for Edward had depended less on his own merit than on the merit of his nearest relations! For then his brother’s bow must have given the finishing stroke to what the ill-humour of his mother and sister would have begun. But while she wondered at the difference of the two young men, she did not find that the emptiness and conceit of the one put her out of all charity with the modesty and worth of the other.

Why they were different, Robert explained to her himself in the course of a quarter of an hour’s conversation; for, talking of his brother and lamenting the extreme gaucherie which he really believed kept him from mixing in proper society, he candidly and generously attributed it much less to any natural deficiency than to the misfortune of a private education; while he himself—though probably without any particular, any material superiority by nature—merely from the advantage of a public school, was as well fitted to mix in the world as any other man.

“Upon my soul,” he added, “I believe it is nothing more; and so I often tell my mother when she is grieving about it. ‘My dear Madam,’ I always say to her, ‘you must make yourself easy. The evil is now irremediable, and it has been entirely your own doing. Why would you be persuaded by my uncle, Sir Robert, against your own judgment, to place Edward under private tuition, at the most critical time of his life? If you had only sent him to Westminster as well as myself, instead of sending him to Mr. Pratt’s, all this would have been prevented.’ This is the way in which I always consider the matter, and my mother is perfectly convinced of her error.”

Elinor would not oppose his opinion; because, whatever might be her general estimation of the advantage of a public school, she could not think of Edward’s abode in Mr. Pratt’s family with any satisfaction.

“You reside in Devonshire, I think,” was his next observation, “in a cottage near Dawlish.”

Elinor set him right as to its situation; and it seemed rather surprising to him that anybody could live in Devonshire without living near Dawlish. He bestowed his hearty approbation, however, on their species of house.

“For my own part,” said he, “I am excessively fond of a cottage; there is always so much comfort, so much elegance about them. And I protest, if I had any money to spare, I should buy a little land and build one myself, within a short distance of London, where I might drive myself down at any time, and collect a few friends about me, and be happy. I advise everybody who is going to build, to build a cottage. My friend Lord Courtland came to me the other day on purpose to ask my advice, and laid before me three different plans of Bonomi’s. I was to decide on the best of them. ‘My dear Courtland,’ said I, immediately throwing them all into the fire, ‘do not adopt either of them, but by all means build a cottage.’ And that I fancy will be the end of it.

“Some people imagine that there can be no accommodations, no space in a cottage; but this is all a mistake. I was last month at my friend Elliott’s, near Dartford. Lady Elliott wished to give a dance. ‘But how can it be done?’ said she; ‘my dear Ferrars, do tell me how it is to be managed. There is not a room in this cottage that will hold ten couple, and where can the supper be?’ I immediately saw that there could be no difficulty in it, so I said, ‘My dear Lady Elliott, do not be uneasy. The dining parlour will admit eighteen couple with ease; card-tables may be placed in the drawing-room; the library may be open for tea and other refreshments; and let the supper be set out in the saloon.’ Lady Elliott was delighted with the thought. We measured the dining-room, and found it would hold exactly eighteen couple, and the affair was arranged precisely after my plan. So that, in fact, you see, if people do but know how to set about it, every comfort may be as well enjoyed in a cottage as in the most spacious dwelling.”

Elinor agreed to it all, for she did not think he deserved the compliment of rational opposition. She could not help observing, as he spoke, how the word cottage meant to him only a fashionable arrangement of rooms and entertainments—space to be managed for display, supper to be stationed as if it were scenery—while to others it meant a hearth that truly warmed, a pantry with honest stores, the comfort of a quiet corner where one could hear the difference between rain and wind. Here, even music was made into furniture; and the very sound in the room—loud, restless, overlapping—seemed a kind of proof that art, when commercialised into an evening’s amusement, loses the inward tenderness by which it once spoke in private.

As John Dashwood had no more pleasure in music than his eldest sister, his mind was equally at liberty to fix on anything else; and a thought struck him during the evening, which he communicated to his wife for her approbation when they got home. The consideration of Mrs. Dennison’s mistake, in supposing his sisters their guests, had suggested the propriety of their being really invited to become such, while Mrs. Jennings’s engagements kept her from home. The expense would be nothing, the inconvenience not more; and it was altogether an attention which the delicacy of his conscience pointed out as requisite to its complete enfranchisement from his promise to his father.

Fanny was startled at the proposal.

“I do not see how it can be done,” said she, “without affronting Lady Middleton; for they spend every day with her; otherwise I should be exceedingly glad to do it. You know I am always ready to pay them any attention in my power, as my taking them out this evening shows. But they are Lady Middleton’s visitors. How can I ask them away from her?”

Her husband, but with great humility, did not see the force of her objection.

“They had already spent a week in this manner in Conduit Street,” he said, “and Lady Middleton could not be displeased at their giving the same number of days to such near relations.”

Fanny paused a moment, and then, with fresh vigour, said, “My love, I would ask them with all my heart, if it was in my power. But I had just settled within myself to ask the Miss Steeles to spend a few days with us. They are very well behaved, good kind of girls; and I think the attention is due to them, as their uncle did so very well by Edward. We can ask your sisters some other year, you know; but the Miss Steeles may not be in town any more. I am sure you will like them; indeed, you do like them, you know, very much already, and so does my mother; and they are such favourites with Harry!”

Mr. Dashwood was convinced. He saw the necessity of inviting the Miss Steeles immediately; and his conscience was pacified by the resolution of inviting his sisters another year—at the same time, however, slyly suspecting that another year would make the invitation needless, by bringing Elinor to town as Colonel Brandon’s wife, and Marianne as their visitor.

Fanny, rejoicing in her escape and proud of the ready wit that had procured it, wrote the next morning to Lucy to request her company and her sister’s for some days in Harley Street, as soon as Lady Middleton could spare them.

This was enough to make Lucy really and reasonably happy. Mrs. Dashwood seemed actually working for her—cherishing all her hopes and promoting all her views. Such an opportunity of being with Edward and his family was, above all things, the most material to her interest; and such an invitation the most gratifying to her feelings. It was an advantage that could not be too gratefully acknowledged, nor too speedily made use of; and the visit to Lady Middleton, which had not before had any precise limits, was instantly discovered to have been always meant to end in two days’ time.

When the note was shown to Elinor—as it was within ten minutes after its arrival—it gave her, for the first time, some share in the expectations of Lucy; for such a mark of uncommon kindness, vouchsafed on so short an acquaintance, seemed to declare that the good-will towards her arose from something more than merely malice against herself, and might be brought, by time and address, to do everything that Lucy wished. Her flattery had already subdued the pride of Lady Middleton, and made an entry into the close heart of Mrs. John Dashwood; and these were effects that laid open the probability of greater.

The Miss Steeles removed to Harley Street; and all that reached Elinor of their influence there strengthened her expectation of the event. Sir John, who called on them more than once, brought home such accounts of the favour they were in as must be universally striking. Mrs. Dashwood had never been so much pleased with any young women in her life as she was with them; had given each of them a needle-book made by some emigrant; called Lucy by her Christian name; and did not know whether she should ever be able to part with them.

By the close of a fortnight, Mrs. Palmer had gathered herself back into health with the brisk elasticity of youth; the household alarms sank into their usual channels, and Mrs. Jennings—who could never live long upon sickbeds when there were drawing-rooms to animate—declared it no longer necessary to give up every hour to her daughter. She contented herself with walking, once or twice a day, through the familiar London clamour to Cleveland House, and then returned to Berkeley Street with the air of one who resumes her proper element: conversation, visitors, and a table that could be made to look hospitable at a moment’s warning.

In that restored routine, Elinor and Marianne fell again into their former share of her hours. The house had its domestic noises—the faint rasp of a maid’s broom in the passage, the soft click of a tea-tray set down, the distant coach-wheels grinding like a continual undertone beneath the windows, as if the street itself insisted on being heard. Even in quiet moments, London never offered true silence; it offered only a thinner fabric of sound, through which one could still hear the city breathing.

On the third or fourth morning after they were thus resettled, Mrs. Jennings came in from her ordinary visit to Mrs. Palmer with such precipitate importance in her step that Elinor, seated alone in the drawing-room, had scarce time to take the measure of it before the news burst forth.

“Lord! my dear Miss Dashwood! have you heard the news?”

“No, ma’am. What is it?”

“Something so strange! But you shall hear it all. When I got to Mr. Palmer’s, I found Charlotte quite in a fuss about the child. She was sure it was very ill—it cried, and fretted, and was all over pimples. So I looked at it directly, and, ‘Lord! my dear,’ says I, ‘it is nothing in the world, but the red gum;’ and nurse said just the same. But Charlotte, she would not be satisfied, so Mr. Donavan was sent for; and luckily he happened to just come in from Harley Street, so he stepped over directly, and as soon as ever he saw the child, he said just as we did, that it was nothing in the world but the red gum, and then Charlotte was easy.”

Mrs. Jennings, as she always did, made even a physician’s visit sound like an event of state. Elinor could almost smell, from the earnestness of the recital, the warm starch of nursery linen and the faint sour-sweet of milk about an infant’s mouth; could almost hear, too, the fretful little cries that had set the whole household in motion. Domestic life—its simmering pots, its swaddled bodies, its alarms and remedies—went on, indifferent to romance, indifferent to pride.

“And so,” Mrs. Jennings pursued, not pausing for breath, “just as he was going away again, it came into my head, I am sure I do not know how I happened to think of it, but it came into my head to ask him if there was any news. So upon that, he smirked, and simpered, and looked grave, and seemed to know something or other, and at last he said in a whisper, ‘For fear any unpleasant report should reach the young ladies under your care as to their sister’s indisposition, I think it advisable to say, that I believe there is no great reason for alarm; I hope Mrs. Dashwood will do very well.’”

Elinor’s attention sharpened. “What! is Fanny ill?”

“That is exactly what I said, my dear. ‘Lord!’ says I, ‘is Mrs. Dashwood ill?’ So then it all came out; and the long and the short of the matter, by all I can learn, seems to be this. Mr. Edward Ferrars,—the very young man I used to joke with you about (but however, as it turns out, I am monstrous glad there was never any thing in it),—Mr. Edward Ferrars, it seems, has been engaged above this twelvemonth to my cousin Lucy!—There’s for you, my dear! And not a creature knowing a syllable of the matter, except Nancy!”

The words fell with a weight that ought to have made the room ring; yet the world outside went on just as before—cart-iron on cobble, a hawker’s call washed thin by distance, the muffled closing of a door. Elinor felt, with a strange clearness, how private catastrophe could be swallowed by public sound.

Mrs. Jennings delighted in the astonishment she produced, and hurried onward.

“Could you have believed such a thing possible? There is no great wonder in their liking one another; but that matters should be brought so forward between them, and nobody suspect it! That is strange! I never happened to see them together, or I am sure I should have found it out directly. Well, and so this was kept a great secret, for fear of Mrs. Ferrars, and neither she nor your brother or sister suspected a word of the matter: till this very morning, poor Nancy,—who, you know, is a well-meaning creature, but no conjurer,—popt it all out. ‘Lord!’ thinks she to herself, ‘they are all so fond of Lucy, to be sure they will make no difficulty about it;’ and so, away she went to your sister, who was sitting all alone at her carpet-work, little suspecting what was to come—for she had just been saying to your brother, only five minutes before, that she thought to make a match between Edward and some Lord’s daughter or other, I forget who.”

There was, in Mrs. Jennings’s relish of detail, the same appetite with which she would speak of a rich gravy, or a syllabub that had been whipped to the right airy firmness: she must tell the whole process, every stirring and skimming, the very moment the change began.

“So you may think what a blow it was to all her vanity and pride. She fell into violent hysterics immediately, with such screams as reached your brother’s ears, as he was sitting in his own dressing-room down stairs, thinking about writing a letter to his steward in the country. So up he flew directly, and a terrible scene took place, for Lucy was come to them by that time, little dreaming what was going on. Poor soul! I pity her. And I must say, I think she was used very hardly; for your sister scolded like any fury, and soon drove her into a fainting fit. Nancy, she fell upon her knees, and cried bitterly; and your brother, he walked about the room, and said he did not know what to do.”

Elinor could picture it all with unwilling exactness: the close room heated by sudden passion, the sharpness of voices ricocheting off polished furniture; the crash of emotion in a house where everything else—plate, linen, servants’ tread—was trained to quiet regularity. And in the midst of it, no doubt, a fire made up too high, a tea-kettle singing uselessly, breakfast waiting to be cleared away—ordinary sustenance grown cold while pride boiled over.

“Mrs. Dashwood declared they should not stay a minute longer in the house, and your brother was forced to go down upon his knees too, to persuade her to let them stay till they had packed up their clothes. Then she fell into hysterics again, and he was so frightened that he would send for Mr. Donavan, and Mr. Donavan found the house in all this uproar. The carriage was at the door ready to take my poor cousins away, and they were just stepping in as he came off; poor Lucy in such a condition, he says, she could hardly walk; and Nancy, she was almost as bad. I declare, I have no patience with your sister; and I hope, with all my heart, it will be a match in spite of her. Lord! what a taking poor Mr. Edward will be in when he hears of it! To have his love used so scornfully! for they say he is monstrous fond of her, as well he may.”

And then, as if she had set a joint to roast and must now turn it on every side, Mrs. Jennings went on—basting her narrative with conjecture, seasoning it with indignation.

“I should not wonder, if he was to be in the greatest passion!—and Mr. Donavan thinks just the same. He and I had a great deal of talk about it; and the best of all is, that he is gone back again to Harley Street, that he may be within call when Mrs. Ferrars is told of it, for she was sent for as soon as ever my cousins left the house, for your sister was sure she would be in hysterics too; and so she may, for what I care. I have no pity for either of them. I have no notion of people’s making such a to-do about money and greatness. There is no reason on earth why Mr. Edward and Lucy should not marry; for I am sure Mrs. Ferrars may afford to do very well by her son, and though Lucy has next to nothing herself, she knows better than any body how to make the most of every thing; I dare say, if Mrs. Ferrars would only allow him five hundred a-year, she would make as good an appearance with it as any body else would with eight.”

Mrs. Jennings’s imagination always returned, as naturally as a cook’s hand to the pantry, to the question of how people were to live: what they would keep in the house, how many mouths would be fed, how far a shilling might be made to go if one knew where to buy a good joint and how to stretch a broth.

“Lord! how snug they might live in such another cottage as yours—or a little bigger—with two maids, and two men; and I believe I could help them to a housemaid, for my Betty has a sister out of place, that would fit them exactly.”

Here she stopped at last. Elinor, who had been given time enough—by the very abundance of Mrs. Jennings’s talk—to collect herself, now replied with what the occasion required: such astonishment as was natural, such comments as would not betray too much, and such composure as her own long discipline supplied.

She was glad—almost with gratitude—that Mrs. Jennings seemed not to suspect her of any extraordinary interest. The older lady, it appeared, had ceased to imagine her attached to Edward at all; and, more than that, Marianne’s absence at this first moment of revelation spared Elinor the double labour of feeling and managing another’s feelings at once. She spoke therefore without visible embarrassment, and with a steadiness that surprised even herself.

Yet within, her thoughts moved in a circle that would not break. A secret, kept so long, once named aloud, altered the air of everything; it was as if a familiar dish had been discovered to contain a bitter herb, and now the tongue could not forget it. She could not decide what she expected of the end. That Edward should marry Lucy—however she tried to drive the idea away—seemed the likely, perhaps the inevitable conclusion. What Mrs. Ferrars would say and do, she had no doubt in kind, only in degree; but she was most anxious to learn how Edward would conduct himself. For him, compassion rose fresh and sharp. For Lucy, she felt very little; and even that little cost her an effort. For the rest—those who could afford to make a tragedy of pride and money—she had none.

Because Mrs. Jennings could speak of nothing else, Elinor soon saw she must prepare Marianne for the subject, and quickly. The truth could not be allowed to meet Marianne first as gossip in another’s mouth. No time was to be lost in undeceiving her; in bringing her to hear it discussed—by Mrs. Jennings, by their brother, by the world—without betraying uneasiness for her sister, or resentment against Edward.

It was a painful office. Elinor was going to strip away what she believed had been Marianne’s chief consolation: the belief in Edward’s constancy as a moral anchor in a world where Willoughby had proved a storm. She must tell particulars that might ruin Edward for ever in Marianne’s good opinion; and by a resemblance between their situations—one which Marianne’s imagination would seize on fiercely—make her feel her own disappointment all over again.

Unwelcome as the task was, it must be done. Elinor hastened to it.

She did not dwell on herself. She did not paint her own suffering, except as the self-command she had practised these four months might offer Marianne a model of what endurance could be. Her narration was clear, simple, restrained. Emotion coloured it, as warmth colours wine, but it did not break into sobs and violence. That belonged rather to the listener.

Marianne heard with horror; the colour fled, returned, fled again; tears came in a flood. Elinor, who had so long been her sister’s comforter, found herself the same now—offering, in her own distress, comfort to another.

She vindicated Edward from every charge but imprudence; she assured Marianne of her own composure; she begged her not to suffer on Elinor’s account. But Marianne, for some time, would give credit to neither. In her mind Edward turned at once into a second Willoughby; and because Elinor admitted that she had loved him sincerely, Marianne could not conceive how she could be so calm.

As for Lucy Steele—Marianne found her so unamiable, so absolutely incapable, in her imagination, of attaching a sensible man, that she could not at first be persuaded to believe it, and afterwards could not pardon Edward’s former affection. She would not even allow it to have been natural; and Elinor, who knew better the pliancy of human hearts and the power of early attachment, left Marianne to be convinced by the only teacher that would persuade her: experience, and a more accurate knowledge of mankind.

Elinor had gone no farther, at first, than the fact of the engagement and the length of time it had existed. Marianne’s feelings then broke in and put an end to all regular detail. For a while Elinor could do nothing but soothe, lessen alarms, and combat resentment. The first question Marianne asked which led again to particulars was uttered between sobs.

“How long has this been known to you, Elinor? has he written to you?”

“I have known it these four months. When Lucy first came to Barton Park last November, she told me in confidence of her engagement.”

Marianne stared as if her eyes must speak what her lips could not. Then, after a pause of incredulous wonder—

“Four months!—Have you known of this four months?”

Elinor confirmed it.

“What! while attending me in all my misery, has this been on your heart? And I have reproached you for being happy!”

“It was not fit that you should then know how much I was the reverse.”

“Four months!” Marianne cried again. “So calm! so cheerful! How have you been supported?”

“By feeling that I was doing my duty.—My promise to Lucy obliged me to be secret. I owed it to her, therefore, to avoid giving any hint of the truth; and I owed it to my family and friends, not to create in them a solicitude about me, which it could not be in my power to satisfy.”

Marianne looked much struck—like one who, tasting something familiar, discovers at last the hidden spice.

“I have very often wished to undeceive yourself and my mother,” Elinor added; “and once or twice I have attempted it;—but without betraying my trust, I never could have convinced you.”

“Four months! and yet you loved him!”

“Yes. But I did not love only him; and while the comfort of others was dear to me, I was glad to spare them from knowing how much I felt. Now, I can think and speak of it with little emotion. I would not have you suffer on my account; for I assure you I no longer suffer materially myself. I have many things to support me. I am not conscious of having provoked the disappointment by any imprudence of my own, I have borne it as much as possible without spreading it farther. I acquit Edward of essential misconduct. I wish him very happy; and I am so sure of his always doing his duty, that though now he may harbour some regret, in the end he must become so. Lucy does not want sense, and that is the foundation on which every thing good may be built.”

Elinor’s voice did not falter, but the effort of saying “sense” with fairness felt like swallowing something too dry without water.

“And after all, Marianne,” she continued, “after all that is bewitching in the idea of a single and constant attachment, and all that can be said of one’s happiness depending entirely on any particular person, it is not meant—it is not fit—it is not possible that it should be so. Edward will marry Lucy; he will marry a woman superior in person and understanding to half her sex; and time and habit will teach him to forget that he ever thought another superior to her.”

“If such is your way of thinking,” Marianne said, wounded even in her admiration, “if the loss of what is most valued is so easily to be made up by something else, your resolution, your self-command, are, perhaps, a little less to be wondered at.—They are brought more within my comprehension.”

“I understand you,” Elinor replied, and now the truth that had been held so long pressed for utterance. “You do not suppose that I have ever felt much. For four months, Marianne, I have had all this hanging on my mind, without being at liberty to speak of it to a single creature; knowing that it would make you and my mother most unhappy whenever it were explained to you, yet unable to prepare you for it in the least. It was told me,—it was in a manner forced on me by the very person herself, whose prior engagement ruined all my prospects; and told me, as I thought, with triumph.”

She had listened—again and again—to Lucy’s hints, Lucy’s exultation, as one listens in forced politeness to a song sung too loudly in a small room; and the noise of it had been all the worse for being wrapped in smiles and soft words.

“This person’s suspicions, therefore, I have had to oppose, by endeavouring to appear indifferent where I have been most deeply interested; and it has not been only once; I have had her hopes and exultation to listen to again and again. I have known myself to be divided from Edward for ever, without hearing one circumstance that could make me less desire the connection. Nothing has proved him unworthy; nor has anything declared him indifferent to me. I have had to contend against the unkindness of his sister, and the insolence of his mother; and have suffered the punishment of an attachment, without enjoying its advantages. And all this has been going on at a time, when, as you know too well, it has not been my only unhappiness.”

Her voice, still controlled, held nevertheless the weight of what had been borne in silence, like a pot kept at the simmer hour after hour, the heat never turned off.

“If you can think me capable of ever feeling,” she went on, “surely you may suppose that I have suffered now. The composure of mind with which I have brought myself at present to consider the matter, the consolation that I have been willing to admit, have been the effect of constant and painful exertion; they did not spring up of themselves; they did not occur to relieve my spirits at first. No, Marianne. Then, if I had not been bound to silence, perhaps nothing could have kept me entirely—not even what I owed to my dearest friends—from openly showing that I was very unhappy.”

Marianne was subdued at once. The pride of sensibility—so ready to think itself the only true suffering—fell away.

“Oh!
