Elinor,” she cried, “you have made me hate myself for ever.—How barbarous have I been to you!—you, who have been my only comfort, who have borne with me in all my misery, who have seemed to be only suffering for me!—Is this my gratitude?—Is this the only return I can make you?—Because your merit cries out upon myself, I have been trying to do it away.”

She clung to Elinor with the tenderest caresses. In this softened frame, Elinor had no difficulty in obtaining the promises she required. Marianne engaged never to speak of the affair with bitterness; to meet Lucy without betraying any increase of dislike; and even to see Edward, if chance brought them together, without any diminution of her usual cordiality.

These were great concessions; but where Marianne felt she had injured, no reparation could be too much.

She performed her promise with admirable discretion. She listened to all that Mrs. Jennings had to say on the subject with an unchanging complexion; dissented in nothing; and was heard three times to say, “Yes, ma’am.” She heard Lucy praised and only moved from one chair to another; and when Mrs. Jennings talked of Edward’s affection, it cost Marianne only a spasm in her throat.

Such advances toward self-command in her sister made Elinor feel equal to anything herself.

The next morning brought a further trial: a visit from their brother. John Dashwood came with a face arranged into the most serious solemnity, as if grief were another sort of propriety to be worn for company. The street-door shut behind him with its ordinary thud, and immediately the drawing-room seemed to fill with that peculiar hush which follows the arrival of someone who believes he brings the weight of the world.

“You have heard, I suppose,” said he, as soon as he was seated, “of the very shocking discovery that took place under our roof yesterday.”

They all assented; it seemed too awful a moment for speech.

“Your sister,” he continued, “has suffered dreadfully. Mrs. Ferrars too—in short it has been a scene of such complicated distress—but I will hope that the storm may be weathered without our being any of us quite overcome. Poor Fanny! she was in hysterics all yesterday. But I would not alarm you too much. Donavan says there is nothing materially to be apprehended; her constitution is a good one, and her resolution equal to any thing. She has borne it all, with the fortitude of an angel!”

Elinor listened, feeling the strange incongruity of such language when the true sufferers were not those he pitied most. Outside, a cart rumbled by; its ironwork jarred, and the sound seemed to scrape at her nerves. How much of what people called “distress,” she thought, was only the offence of pride at being made to hear an unwelcome truth?

“She says,” John went on, “she never shall think well of anybody again; and one cannot wonder at it, after being so deceived!—meeting with such ingratitude, where so much kindness had been shown, so much confidence had been placed! It was quite out of the benevolence of her heart, that she had asked these young women to her house; merely because she thought they deserved some attention, were harmless, well-behaved girls, and would be pleasant companions; for otherwise we both wished very much to have invited you and Marianne to be with us, while your kind friend there, was attending her daughter. And now to be so rewarded! ‘I wish, with all my heart,’ says poor Fanny in her affectionate way, ‘that we had asked your sisters instead of them.’”

He paused, as if gratitude were an expected course in the conversation.

They thanked him; and he, having received what he came for, proceeded with renewed gravity.

“What poor Mrs. Ferrars suffered, when first Fanny broke it to her, is not to be described. While she with the truest affection had been planning a most eligible connection for him, was it to be supposed that he could be all the time secretly engaged to another person!—such a suspicion could never have entered her head! If she suspected any prepossession elsewhere, it could not be in that quarter. ‘There, to be sure,’ said she, ‘I might have thought myself safe.’ She was quite in an agony.”

John’s tone made “safe” sound like a word of property, of enclosures and title-deeds—one of those dominant terms that claim moral authority by pretending to be natural law.

“We consulted together, however, as to what should be done, and at last she determined to send for Edward. He came. But I am sorry to relate what ensued. All that Mrs. Ferrars could say to make him put an end to the engagement, assisted too as you may well suppose by my arguments, and Fanny’s entreaties, was of no avail. Duty, affection, every thing was disregarded. I never thought Edward so stubborn, so unfeeling before.”

Marianne’s hands flew together. “Gracious God! can this be possible!”

“Well may you wonder, Marianne,” John replied, mistaking entirely the direction of her indignation. “Your exclamation is very natural.”

Marianne, remembering her promises, swallowed the retort that rose burning to her tongue, and turned away.

John continued, luxuriating in sums and settlements as if money were the only language worth speaking.

“His mother explained to him her liberal designs, in case of his marrying Miss Morton; told him she would settle on him the Norfolk estate, which, clear of land-tax, brings in a good thousand a-year; offered even, when matters grew desperate, to make it twelve hundred; and in opposition to this, if he still persisted in this low connection, represented to him the certain penury that must attend the match. His own two thousand pounds she protested should be his all; she would never see him again; and so far would she be from affording him the smallest assistance, that if he were to enter into any profession with a view of better support, she would do all in her power to prevent him advancing in it.”

Elinor’s heart tightened at the cold precision of it: it was the rhetoric of power—the same power that named “low” and “proper” as if those words were facts like flour and water, rather than judgments baked into the very dialect of wealth.

“All this, however, was urged in vain,” John went on. “Edward said very little; but what he did say, was in the most determined manner. Nothing should prevail on him to give up his engagement. He would stand to it, cost him what it might.”

“Then,” cried Mrs. Jennings, who had sat swelling with impatience, “he has acted like an honest man! I beg your pardon, Mr. Dashwood, but if he had done otherwise, I should have thought him a rascal. I have some little concern in the business, as well as yourself, for Lucy Steele is my cousin, and I believe there is not a better kind of girl in the world, nor one who more deserves a good husband.”

John Dashwood looked greatly astonished; but he was too calm, and too cautious of giving offence—especially to those with money—to show resentment.

“I would by no means speak disrespectfully of any relation of yours, madam. Miss Lucy Steele is, I dare say, a very deserving young woman, but in the present case you know, the connection must be impossible. And to have entered into a secret engagement with a young man under her uncle’s care, the son of a woman especially of such very large fortune as Mrs. Ferrars, is perhaps, altogether a little extraordinary. In short, I do not mean to reflect upon the behaviour of any person whom you have a regard for, Mrs. Jennings. We all wish her extremely happy; and Mrs. Ferrars’s conduct throughout the whole, has been such as every conscientious, good mother, in like circumstances, would adopt. It has been dignified and liberal. Edward has drawn his own lot, and I fear it will be a bad one.”

Marianne sighed—she feared it too, but for reasons that had nothing to do with “lot” in the sense John meant. Elinor’s heart wrung for Edward: for the loneliness of his choice, for the harshness of a mother’s sentence, for the bravery of standing firm for a woman who, Elinor could not help believing, would never repay him in kind.

“Well, sir,” said Mrs. Jennings, “and how did it end?”

“I am sorry to say, ma’am, in a most unhappy rupture:—Edward is dismissed for ever from his mother’s notice. He left her house yesterday, but where he is gone, or whether he is still in town, I do not know; for we of course can make no inquiry.”

“Poor young man!—and what is to become of him?”

“What, indeed, ma’am!” John answered. “It is a melancholy consideration. Born to the prospect of such affluence! I cannot conceive a situation more deplorable. The interest of two thousand pounds—how can a man live on it?—and when to that is added the recollection, that he might, but for his own folly, within three months have been in the receipt of two thousand, five hundred a-year (for Miss Morton has thirty thousand pounds,) I cannot picture to myself a more wretched condition. We must all feel for him; and the more so, because it is totally out of our power to assist him.”

Mrs. Jennings, who thought first of roofs and dinners, broke in with practical warmth.

“Poor young man! I am sure he should be very welcome to bed and board at my house; and so I would tell him if I could see him. It is not fit that he should be living about at his own charge now, at lodgings and taverns.”

Elinor’s heart thanked her, though she could not help a faint smile at the manner of it—Edward Ferrars, with his quiet dignity, transplanted into Mrs. Jennings’s bustling hospitality like a delicate herb put into too rich a stew.

“If he would only have done as well by himself,” said John, “as all his friends were disposed to do by him, he might now have been in his proper situation, and would have wanted for nothing. But as it is, it must be out of anybody’s power to assist him. And there is one thing more preparing against him, which must be worse than all—his mother has determined, with a very natural kind of spirit, to settle that estate upon Robert immediately, which might have been Edward’s, on proper conditions. I left her this morning with her lawyer, talking over the business.”

“Well!” said Mrs. Jennings, “that is her revenge. Everybody has a way of their own. But I don’t think mine would be, to make one son independent, because another had plagued me.”

Marianne rose and walked about the room, as if motion might shake off the oppression of hearing cruelty called “spirit” and selfishness called “nature.”

“Can anything be more galling to the spirit of a man,” John continued, “than to see his younger brother in possession of an estate which might have been his own? Poor Edward! I feel for him sincerely.”

Elinor, silent, heard again the everlasting wheel-grind outside, and thought how the city’s noise—unceasing, indifferent—was like the world’s judgment: always rolling on, crushing the softest feelings under hard names. Yet somewhere within that din, Edward had chosen, stubbornly and quietly, a language of his own: not the dominant lexicon of estates and eligibility, but the plain, unornamented word duty.

Mrs. Jennings praised Edward Ferrars with a warmth that filled the little drawing-room like the breath of something sweet left too long near the fire; yet the true savor of his conduct—its bitter rind and honest core—was tasted only by Elinor and Marianne. Mrs. Jennings admired the act because it looked, at a distance, like virtue; Elinor and Marianne knew how little there had been to bribe him into disobedience, and how dear the payment must be now that he had lost, for a plain duty, both fortune and the favor of those whose voices had once made his home.

Elinor, who carried her feelings as carefully as a cook carries a pan that might slop and scorch, took a grave, secret pride in his integrity. Marianne—wounded, restless, and still raw with the memory of her own ardors—forgave him all former offences out of pure compassion for the punishment he endured.

Yet though confidence between the sisters was restored by this public unmasking, the subject itself lay between them like a dish too highly seasoned for convalescence. Elinor avoided it upon principle; Marianne’s eager assurances—that Edward’s affection for Elinor must surely remain—were too hot, too pressing, and threatened to fix what Elinor was striving to loosen from her thoughts. Marianne, for her part, found her courage fail whenever she attempted the topic; it always ended by setting Elinor’s steadiness beside her own impetuosity, and that comparison left her more ashamed than strengthened. She felt the full sting of it, not as a spur to present exertion, but as the slow torture of penitence. Regret had become her daily bread—dry, hard, and swallowed without comfort. Her mind, weakened by long fever of emotion, still fancied any new effort impossible, and so each attempt only sank her lower.

For a day or two, nothing new came from Harley Street or Bartlett’s Buildings. Mrs. Jennings, however, had resolved from the first to pay her cousins a visit of comfort and inquiry as soon as she could, and only the hindrance of more visitors than usual had delayed her. Then came the third day after their first knowledge of the particulars—a Sunday so fine, so unexpectedly mild in the second week of March, that half the town seemed drawn, as if by instinct, to Kensington Gardens.

Mrs. Jennings and Elinor were among the number. Marianne, who knew the Willoughbys were again in town and lived in constant dread of meeting them, preferred to remain at home rather than venture into so public a place. Elinor did not blame her. There are wounds that cannot bear the rasp of chance encounters; even the sound of a familiar step on gravel can turn the stomach like rancid butter.

In the Gardens, the city’s winter loosened its hold. The bare branches clicked overhead; the earth exhaled a cold, clean scent where damp had been warmed by sun; and beyond the trees, London’s distant rumble persisted—wheels, calls, the thin metallic chatter of a modern world that could not keep silence even on a Sabbath. Elinor, who had grown sensitive to the way noise could wear upon the mind—how the ceaseless clack and shout seemed to erase one’s inward dialect and replace it with a harsher, public vocabulary—was grateful when an intimate acquaintance of Mrs. Jennings joined them soon after they entered. The lady’s company engaged all Mrs. Jennings’s conversation, and Elinor, left a little apart, found a rare moment for quiet reflection.

She saw nothing of the Willoughbys; nothing of Edward; for some time, indeed, nothing of anybody who could be interesting to her whether from gravity or mirth. The Gardens were full and yet empty—people moving like figures in a tapestry whose meaning she no longer cared to read.

At last, however, she was surprised by being accosted by Miss Steele. Miss Steele looked a little shy, as if her own conscience had pinched her, but she expressed great satisfaction in meeting them, and, encouraged by Mrs. Jennings’s particular kindness, left her own party for a short time to join theirs.

Mrs. Jennings immediately whispered to Elinor, “Get it all out of her, my dear. She will tell you any thing if you ask. You see I cannot leave Mrs. Clarke.”

It proved lucky for Mrs. Jennings’s curiosity—and for Elinor’s, though Elinor would have disowned the word—that Miss Steele would tell anything without being asked. She took Elinor familiarly by the arm.

“I am so glad to meet you,” said she, “for I wanted to see you of all things in the world.” Then, lowering her voice, “I suppose Mrs. Jennings has heard all about it. Is she angry?”

“Not at all, I believe, with you.”

“That is a good thing. And Lady Middleton, is she angry?”

“I cannot suppose it possible that she should be.”

“I am monstrous glad of it. Good gracious! I have had such a time of it! I never saw Lucy in such a rage in my life. She vowed at first she would never trim me up a new bonnet, nor do any thing else for me again, so long as she lived; but now she is quite come to, and we are as good friends as ever. Look, she made me this bow to my hat, and put in the feather last night. There now, you are going to laugh at me too. But why should not I wear pink ribbons? I do not care if it is the Doctor’s favourite colour. I am sure, for my part, I should never have known he did like it better than any other colour, if he had not happened to say so. My cousins have been so plaguing me! I declare sometimes I do not know which way to look before them.”

Elinor, who could have found no answer even if she had wished, let that stream run itself out until she could guide it back, as one steers boiling milk away from spilling. “But—Miss Steele—about Mr. Ferrars?”

Miss Steele brightened with triumph, as if this were the best sweetmeat in the basket.

“Well, but Miss Dashwood—people may say what they chuse about Mr. Ferrars’s declaring he would not have Lucy, for it is no such thing I can tell you; and it is quite a shame for such ill-natured reports to be spread abroad. Whatever Lucy might think about it herself, you know, it was no business of other people to set it down for certain.”

“I never heard any thing of the kind hinted at before, I assure you,” Elinor said.

“Oh, did not you? But it was said, I know, very well, and by more than one; for Miss Godby told Miss Sparks, that nobody in their senses could expect Mr. Ferrars to give up a woman like Miss Morton, with thirty thousand pounds to her fortune, for Lucy Steele that had nothing at all; and I had it from Miss Sparks myself. And besides that, my cousin Richard said himself, that when it came to the point he was afraid Mr. Ferrars would be off; and when Edward did not come near us for three days, I could not tell what to think myself; and I believe in my heart Lucy gave it up all for lost; for we came away from your brother’s Wednesday, and we saw nothing of him not all Thursday, Friday, and Saturday, and did not know what was become of him.

“Once Lucy thought to write to him, but then her spirits rose against that. However this morning he came just as we came home from church; and then it all came out, how he had been sent for Wednesday to Harley Street, and been talked to by his mother and all of them, and how he had declared before them all that he loved nobody but Lucy, and nobody but Lucy would he have.

“And how he had been so worried by what passed, that as soon as he had went away from his mother’s house, he had got upon his horse, and rid into the country, some where or other; and how he had stayed about at an inn all Thursday and Friday, on purpose to get the better of it.

“And after thinking it all over and over again, he said, it seemed to him as if, now he had no fortune, and no nothing at all, it would be quite unkind to keep her on to the engagement, because it must be for her loss, for he had nothing but two thousand pounds, and no hope of any thing else; and if he was to go into orders, as he had some thoughts, he could get nothing but a curacy, and how was they to live upon that?—He could not bear to think of her doing no better, and so he begged, if she had the least mind for it, to put an end to the matter directly, and leave him shift for himself.

“I heard him say all this as plain as could possibly be. And it was entirely for her sake, and upon her account, that he said a word about being off, and not upon his own. I will take my oath he never dropt a syllable of being tired of her, or of wishing to marry Miss Morton, or any thing like it.

“But, to be sure, Lucy would not give ear to such kind of talking; so she told him directly (with a great deal about sweet and love, you know, and all that—Oh, la! one can’t repeat such kind of things you know)—she told him directly, she had not the least mind in the world to be off, for she could live with him upon a trifle, and how little so ever he might have, she should be very glad to have it all, you know, or something of the kind. So then he was monstrous happy, and talked on some time about what they should do, and they agreed he should take orders directly, and they must wait to be married till he got a living.

“And just then I could not hear any more, for my cousin called from below to tell me Mrs. Richardson was come in her coach, and would take one of us to Kensington Gardens; so I was forced to go into the room and interrupt them, to ask Lucy if she would like to go, but she did not care to leave Edward; so I just run up stairs and put on a pair of silk stockings and came off with the Richardsons.”

Elinor stopped short, as if Miss Steele had dropped grit into a sauce meant to be smooth. “I do not understand what you mean by interrupting them. You were all in the same room together, were not you?”

“No, indeed, not us. La! Miss Dashwood, do you think people make love when any body else is by? Oh, for shame!—To be sure you must know better than that.” She laughed, not naturally, but as if she had learned the sound of laughter the way a shop learns a fashionable tune. “No, no; they were shut up in the drawing-room together, and all I heard was only by listening at the door.”

“How!” cried Elinor. “Have you been repeating to me what you only learnt yourself by listening at the door? I am sorry I did not know it before; for I certainly would not have suffered you to give me particulars of a conversation which you ought not to have known yourself. How could you behave so unfairly by your sister?”

“Oh, la! there is nothing in that. I only stood at the door, and heard what I could. And I am sure Lucy would have done just the same by me; for a year or two back, when Martha Sharpe and I had so many secrets together, she never made any bones of hiding in a closet, or behind a chimney-board, on purpose to hear what we said.”

Elinor tried to lead the talk elsewhere; but Miss Steele’s mind returned, like a spoon to the pot it has already tasted.

“Edward talks of going to Oxford soon,” said she; “but now he is lodging at No.—, Pall Mall. What an ill-natured woman his mother is, an’t she? And your brother and sister were not very kind! However, I shan’t say anything against them to you; and to be sure they did send us home in their own chariot, which was more than I looked for. And for my part, I was all in a fright for fear your sister should ask us for the huswifes she had gave us a day or two before; but, however, nothing was said about them, and I took care to keep mine out of sight.

“Edward have got some business at Oxford, he says; so he must go there for a time; and after that, as soon as he can light upon a Bishop, he will be ordained. I wonder what curacy he will get! Good gracious!”—and she giggled—“I’d lay my life I know what my cousins will say, when they hear of it. They will tell me I should write to the Doctor, to get Edward the curacy of his new living. I know they will; but I am sure I would not do such a thing for all the world. ‘La!’ I shall say directly, ‘I wonder how you could think of such a thing? I write to the Doctor, indeed!’”

“Well,” said Elinor, with a composure that cost her something, “it is a comfort to be prepared against the worst. You have got your answer ready.”

Miss Steele was ready to continue, but the approach of her own party made another duty more urgent.

“Oh, la! here come the Richardsons. I had a vast deal more to say to you, but I must not stay away from them not any longer. I assure you they are very genteel people. He makes a monstrous deal of money, and they keep their own coach. I have not time to speak to Mrs. Jennings about it myself, but pray tell her I am quite happy to hear she is not in anger against us, and Lady Middleton the same; and if anything should happen to take you and your sister away, and Mrs. Jennings should want company, I am sure we should be very glad to come and stay with her for as long a time as she likes. I suppose Lady Middleton won’t ask us any more this bout. Good-by; I am sorry Miss Marianne was not here. Remember me kindly to her. La! if you have not got your spotted muslin on!—I wonder you was not afraid of its being torn.”

That was her parting concern. After paying her farewell compliments to Mrs. Jennings, she was claimed by Mrs. Richardson; and Elinor was left with knowledge enough to occupy thought, though it added little to what she had already foreseen and, in her own mind, prepared.

Edward’s marriage with Lucy was as firmly determined on as ever; the time of its taking place remained as absolutely uncertain. Everything depended, exactly as Elinor had expected, upon his obtaining preferment—and at present there seemed not the smallest prospect of it. The future hung like an empty larder: one might plan meals, name dishes, speak of feasts, and yet have nothing but a little flour and a scanty candle.

As soon as they returned to the carriage, Mrs. Jennings was eager for information. Elinor, unwilling to spread what had first been gathered so unfairly—snatched, as it were, by the ear at a keyhole—confined herself to simple particulars, such as she felt assured Lucy, for the sake of her own consequence, would choose to have known: that the engagement continued, and that Edward meant to take orders, and that they must wait for a living.

This produced from Mrs. Jennings the natural remark—half pity, half bustling calculation, like a housekeeper counting jars before winter.

“Wait for his having a living!—ay, we all know how that will end:—they will wait a twelvemonth, and finding no good comes of it, will set down upon a curacy of fifty pounds a-year, with the interest of his two thousand pounds, and what little matter Mr. Steele and Mr. Pratt can give her. Then they will have a child every year! and Lord help ’em! how poor they will be! I must see what I can give them towards furnishing their house. Two maids and two men, indeed! as I talked of t’ other day. No, no, they must get a stout girl of all works. Betty’s sister would never do for them now.”

The next morning brought Elinor a letter by the two-penny post from Lucy herself. The paper carried the faint smell of London—ink, dust, and the close air of many rooms—but Lucy’s tone, as always, aimed to perfume every line with gratitude, importance, and the language of feeling, as if fine words could preserve a precarious future the way sugar preserves fruit.

“Bartlett’s Building, March.

“I hope my dear Miss Dashwood will excuse the liberty I take of writing to her; but I know your friendship for me will make you pleased to hear such a good account of myself and my dear Edward, after all the troubles we have went through lately, therefore will make no more apologies, but proceed to say that, thank God! though we have suffered dreadfully, we are both quite well now, and as happy as we must always be in one another’s love.

“We have had great trials, and great persecutions, but however, at the same time, gratefully acknowledge many friends, yourself not the least among them, whose great kindness I shall always thankfully remember, as will Edward too, who I have told of it.

“I am sure you will be glad to hear, as likewise dear Mrs. Jennings, I spent two happy hours with him yesterday afternoon, he would not hear of our parting, though earnestly did I, as I thought my duty required, urge him to it for prudence sake, and would have parted for ever on the spot, would he consent to it; but he said it should never be, he did not regard his mother’s anger, while he could have my affections; our prospects are not very bright, to be sure, but we must wait, and hope for the best; he will be ordained shortly; and should it ever be in your power to recommend him to any body that has a living to bestow, am very sure you will not forget us, and dear Mrs. Jennings too, trust she will speak a good word for us to Sir John, or Mr. Palmer, or any friend that may be able to assist us.—Poor Anne was much to blame for what she did, but she did it for the best, so I say nothing; hope Mrs. Jennings won’t think it too much trouble to give us a call, should she come this way any morning, ’twould be a great kindness, and my cousins would be proud to know her.—My paper reminds me to conclude; and begging to be most gratefully and respectfully remembered to her, and to Sir John, and Lady Middleton, and the dear children, when you chance to see them, and love to Miss Marianne,

“I am, &c.”

When Elinor had finished, she did at once what she concluded to be the writer’s true design: she placed the letter in Mrs. Jennings’s hands. Mrs. Jennings read it aloud with many comments of satisfaction and praise; her voice—so hearty, so unguarded—rose and fell among the domestic sounds of the house, the shifting of chairs, the distant shutting of doors, the little creaks and murmurs that make a home’s own music.

“Very well indeed!—how prettily she writes!—aye, that was quite proper to let him be off if he would. That was just like Lucy. Poor soul! I wish I could get him a living, with all my heart. She calls me dear Mrs. Jennings, you see. She is a good-hearted girl as ever lived. Very well upon my word. That sentence is very prettily turned. Yes, yes, I will go and see her, sure enough. How attentive she is, to think of every body!—Thank you, my dear, for showing it me. It is as pretty a letter as ever I saw, and does Lucy’s head and heart great credit.”

The Miss Dashwoods had now been rather more than two months in town, and Marianne’s impatience to be gone gathered upon her like a pot left too long at the boil—quiet at first, then fretting, then threatening to spill over at the slightest touch. Every day she longed more fiercely for the country air, for the unbought liberty of lanes and hedgerows, for the honest hush that falls at Barton when the wind drops and only a distant cartwheel speaks. London had its own incessant clatter—the iron-shod hooves, the calling of vendors, the thin music that was made to be heard through walls and coaches and crowds—and to Marianne it had become not life but noise, an assault that left her nerves raw, as if she were made to dine perpetually on nothing but smoke and stale bread.

Elinor was hardly less anxious to remove; only she carried her desire as she carried every other strong feeling—carefully, with an attention to practicalities as exact as measuring flour. She could not forget that a long journey was not a sigh made audible and then gone, but hours of jolting wheels, cold inns, late suppers, and a sister whose spirits could not be commanded by reason. Marianne would not be brought to acknowledge any difficulty; she spoke of returning to Barton as if it were as easy as stepping from one room to another, as if longing itself were a carriage and could bear them in a moment over leagues of road.

Yet Elinor had begun, in earnest, to turn her thoughts toward their departure, and had already hinted their wishes to their kind hostess. Mrs. Jennings resisted with all the eager eloquence of good-will—pressing them to stay as one presses a guest to take one more helping, convinced that affection must surely be proved by quantity. While this was in dispute, a plan was suggested which, though it detained them from home a few weeks longer, seemed to Elinor far more eligible than any other.

The Palmers were to remove to Cleveland about the end of March for the Easter holidays; and Mrs. Jennings, with both her friends, received a very warm invitation from Charlotte to go with them. In itself this would not have satisfied Miss Dashwood’s delicacy; but it was enforced with so much real politeness by Mr. Palmer himself—whose manners toward them had softened greatly since Marianne’s unhappiness had been known—that Elinor accepted with pleasure. It promised a safer, more orderly progress toward home: Cleveland lay within a few miles of Bristol; from there, Barton might be reached in a long day, and their mother’s servant could come to attend them down. They need not stay above a week at Cleveland; they might be home in little more than three weeks.

But when Elinor told Marianne what she had done, her sister’s first answer came like a knife set suddenly against the grain.

“Cleveland!” she cried, and the name itself seemed to strike her breath away. “No, I cannot go to Cleveland.”

“You forget,” said Elinor gently, “that its situation is not—that it is not in the neighbourhood of—”

“But it is in Somersetshire. I cannot go into Somersetshire.—There, where I looked forward to going…” The words broke upon themselves, as if she had bitten into some remembered bitterness. “No, Elinor, you cannot expect me to go there.”

Elinor would not reason her out of a pain that could not be argued down. She attempted instead to draw forth another feeling and set it against the first. She spoke of their mother—of that dear home Marianne so passionately wished to see—and represented this plan as the most comfortable, the most certain means of fixing the time of their return. The mind that cannot bear one association will sometimes be carried by another; and Marianne’s affection for her mother was sincere. It triumphed, after not much struggle, over the imaginary evils she had started; and in yielding, she snatched at the only consolation she would allow herself—certainty. She found relief in drawing up, with almost feverish minuteness, a statement of the hours that were yet to divide her from Barton, as if time could be portioned and tasted like a broth that must be endured until the richer dish arrives.

Mrs. Jennings, far from being weary of her guests, pressed them earnestly to return with her again from Cleveland. Elinor was grateful, but could not be moved from her design. Their mother’s concurrence was readily gained; everything relative to their return was arranged as far as it could be; and so the days in town, though still noisy, began to take on a shape—an end in view, like a meal planned in courses.

It was soon after their leaving Mrs. Jennings had been settled, that Colonel Brandon first called on her.

“Ah! Colonel, I do not know what you and I shall do without the Miss Dashwoods,” was Mrs. Jennings’s address to him; “for they are quite resolved upon going home from the Palmers;—and how forlorn we shall be, when I come back!—Lord! we shall sit and gape at one another as dull as two cats.”

Perhaps she hoped, by sketching their coming emptiness so vigorously, to provoke him into an offer that would save him from it—an offer which, in her imagination, could have but one form. If so, she was not long without what she took for confirmation. Elinor moved to the window, to take with quicker light the dimensions of a print she meant to copy for a friend; and the Colonel followed. He spoke to her there with a look of particular meaning, conversing for several minutes. Mrs. Jennings was too honourable to listen; she even changed her seat on purpose that she might not hear, drawing herself close by the pianoforte where Marianne was playing. Yet she could not keep herself from seeing.

Elinor changed colour. There was agitation in her manner. She was too intent on what he said to pursue her employment. A woman who lives much in other people’s prospects has an eye sharpened for the smallest signs; and Mrs. Jennings, watching as she would watch a sauce thicken, judged that something decisive was happening at the window.

In the interval of Marianne’s turning from one lesson to another, a few words of the Colonel’s inevitably reached her ear. He seemed to be apologising for the badness of his house.

This, to Mrs. Jennings, set the matter beyond a doubt. She wondered, indeed, why he should find it necessary to do so—since love, in her creed, ought to be able to dine happily off whatever was set before it—but supposed it to be proper etiquette. What Elinor replied she could not distinguish; but from the motion of her lips she judged that she did not think that any material objection. Mrs. Jennings commended her in her heart for being so honest.

They continued a few minutes longer without her catching another syllable; then, by another fortunate pause in Marianne’s performance, she heard the Colonel’s calm voice say, “I am afraid it cannot take place very soon.”

Astonished and shocked at so unlover-like a sentence, she was almost ready to cry out, Lord! what should hinder it?—but she checked herself and confined her indignation to silence. This is very strange!—sure he need not wait to be older.

Yet this delay did not seem to offend or mortify his fair companion in the least. When the conference soon after ended and they moved apart, Mrs. Jennings very plainly heard Elinor say, and with a voice which showed her to feel what she said, “I shall always think myself very much obliged to you.”

Mrs. Jennings was delighted with her gratitude, and only wondered that, after hearing such a sentence, the Colonel could take leave with the utmost sang-froid, and go away without making Elinor any reply. She had not thought her old friend could be so indifferent a suitor.

What had really passed between them was this.

“I have heard,” said he, with great compassion, “of the injustice your friend Mr. Ferrars has suffered from his family; for if I understand the matter right, he has been entirely cast off by them for persevering in his engagement with a very deserving young woman. Have I been rightly informed? Is it so?”

Elinor told him that it was.

“The cruelty, the impolitic cruelty,” he replied, with feeling, “of dividing, or attempting to divide, two young people long attached to each other, is terrible. Mrs. Ferrars does not know what she may be doing—what she may drive her son to. I have seen Mr. Ferrars two or three times in Harley Street, and am much pleased with him. He is not a young man with whom one can be intimately acquainted in a short time, but I have seen enough of him to wish him well for his own sake; and as a friend of yours, I wish it still more. I understand that he intends to take orders. Will you be so good as to tell him that the living of Delaford, now just vacant, as I am informed by this day’s post, is his, if he think it worth his acceptance; but that, perhaps, so unfortunately circumstanced as he is now, it may be nonsense to appear to doubt; I only wish it were more valuable. It is a rectory, but a small one; the late incumbent, I believe, did not make more than two hundred pounds per annum, and though it is certainly capable of improvement, I fear not to such an amount as to afford him a very comfortable income. Such as it is, however, my pleasure in presenting it to him will be very great. Pray assure him of it.”

Elinor’s astonishment at this commission could hardly have been greater had he truly offered her his hand. Two days before, any preferment for Edward had seemed hopeless, like a table spread in imagination only; and now, in the space of a morning, the very thing that could make his future possible was provided—and she, of all people in the world, was made the instrument to bestow it. The suddenness of such relief had the disorienting effect of a fragrance that brings back a whole season at once—one is transported, yet does not know whether to weep or to laugh.

Her emotion was what Mrs. Jennings had mistaken for romantic confusion; but whatever lesser feelings, less pure, less pleasing, might mingle with it, Elinor’s esteem for Colonel Brandon’s benevolence, and her gratitude for his friendship, were strong and sincere. She thanked him with all her heart; she spoke of Edward’s principles and disposition with the praise she knew them to deserve; and she promised to undertake the commission with pleasure, if it were truly his wish to put off so agreeable an office to another. Yet she could not help thinking no one could perform it so well as himself. It was an office from which—unwilling to give Edward the pain of receiving an obligation from her—she would have been very glad to be spared; but Colonel Brandon, on motives of equal delicacy, declined it likewise. He appeared so desirous that it should be given through her means that she would not make further opposition.

Edward, she believed, was still in town; and, fortunately, she had heard his address from Miss Steele. She could therefore inform him in the course of the day.

After this had been settled, Colonel Brandon spoke of his own advantage in securing so respectable and agreeable a neighbour; and then it was he mentioned, with regret, that the house was small and indifferent—an evil which Elinor made very light of, at least as far as it regarded size.

“The smallness of the house,” said she, “I cannot imagine any inconvenience to them, for it will be in proportion to their family and income.”

By this the Colonel was surprised to find that she considered Mr. Ferrars’s marriage as the certain consequence of the presentation; for he did not suppose it possible that the Delaford living could supply such an income as anybody in Edward’s style of life would venture to settle on, and he said so.

“This little rectory can do no more than make Mr. Ferrars comfortable as a bachelor; it cannot enable him to marry. I am sorry to say that my patronage ends with this; and my interest is hardly more extensive. If, however, by an unforeseen chance it should be in my power to serve him farther, I must think very differently of him from what I now do, if I am not as ready to be useful to him then as I sincerely wish I could be at present. What I am now doing indeed seems nothing at all, since it can advance him so little towards what must be his principal, his only object of happiness. His marriage must still be a distant good; at least, I am afraid it cannot take place very soon.”

Such was the sentence which, misunderstood, had so justly offended Mrs. Jennings’s delicate feelings. And thus the gratitude Elinor expressed at parting—“I shall always think myself very much obliged to you”—may appear not less reasonably excited, nor less properly worded than if it had arisen from an offer of marriage.

“Well, Miss Dashwood,” cried Mrs. Jennings the moment the gentleman’s step had died upon the stair, and the door had settled back into its frame, “I don’t ask what the Colonel has been saying, for though I protest I tried to keep my ears on my own side of the room, words will travel as they please; and I heard enough to know his meaning. And I declare I never was better pleased in my life. I wish you joy of it with all my heart.”

“Thank you, ma’am,” said Elinor. Her voice was steady, but there was in her composure the strain of a string drawn too tight. “It is, indeed, a matter of joy to me; and I feel Colonel Brandon’s goodness most sensibly. There are not many men who would act as he has done—few with so compassionate a heart. I never was more astonished.”

“Lord, my dear, modest as ever! Astonished? I am not astonished a bit. I have been thinking this good while there was nothing more likely.”

“You judged from his general benevolence,” Elinor returned; “but you could not foresee that the opportunity would occur so soon.”

“Opportunity!” repeated Mrs. Jennings, as if the word were a spice she did not mean to be cheated of. “When a man has once made up his mind, somehow or other he will find the opportunity, as sure as cook finds a hot corner in a cold hearth. Well—joy to you again and again; and if ever there was to be a happy couple, I shall soon know where to look.”

“You mean to go to Delaford after them, I suppose,” said Elinor, forcing a faint smile.

“Aye, that I do indeed. And as to the house being bad, I don’t know what the Colonel can be at. It’s as good a one as ever I saw.”

“He spoke of its being out of repair.”

“Well, and whose fault is that? Why doesn’t he repair it? Who should do it but himself?”

At that instant the servant entered to announce the carriage at the door. Mrs. Jennings, already gathering her gloves as if they were part of her very argument, began to bustle out with a final gust of words.

“Well, my dear, I must be gone before I have had half my talk out; but we shall be quite alone this evening, and then I’ll have it all. I don’t ask you to go with me; I dare say your mind’s too full for company—and besides, you must be longing to tell your sister.”

Marianne had slipped away before the talk began; her departure had been as quiet as a kettle removed from the hob before it sings.

“Certainly, ma’am, I shall tell Marianne of it,” Elinor said; “but I shall not mention it at present to anybody else.”

“Oh!—very well,” returned Mrs. Jennings, visibly balked, like a hostess told she must not set out the sweetmeats. “Then you would not have me tell it to Lucy? For I think of going as far as Holborn to-day.”

“No, ma’am—not even Lucy, if you please. One day’s delay will not be very material; and till I have written to Mr. Ferrars, I think it ought not to be mentioned to any body else. I shall do that directly. It is of importance that no time should be lost with him, for he will of course have much to do relative to his ordination.”

That last word—ordination—fell upon Mrs. Jennings’s understanding like a dropped ladle: it made a sound, but she did not immediately know what it meant for the whole dinner. She stared, reflected, and then brightened with the sudden triumph of a solution.

“Oh, ho! I understand you. Mr. Ferrars is to be the man. Well, so much the better for him. Ay, to be sure, he must be ordained in readiness; and I am very glad to find things are so forward between you. But, my dear, is not this rather out of character? Should not the Colonel write himself?—sure he is the proper person.”

Elinor, not comprehending the beginning and refusing—by long habit—to chase every flying notion of Mrs. Jennings’s, answered only the close.

“Colonel Brandon is so delicate a man, that he rather wished any one to announce his intentions to Mr. Ferrars than himself.”

“And so you are forced to do it. Well, that is an odd kind of delicacy! However, I won’t disturb you.” She saw Elinor draw paper to her and take up the pen as if it were a knife that must make one clean cut and no more. “You know your own concerns best. So good-bye, my dear. I have not heard of any thing to please me so well since Charlotte was brought to bed.”

She went; but returned again almost directly, her mind unable to quit the domestic train of thought that always ran alongside her grandest conclusions.

“I have just been thinking of Betty’s sister, my dear. I should be very glad to get her so good a mistress. But whether she would do for a lady’s maid, I can’t tell. She’s an excellent housemaid, and works very well at her needle. However, you will think of all that at your leisure.”

“Certainly, ma’am,” Elinor answered—though she heard little. She wanted solitude, not servants.

When the door closed upon Mrs. Jennings at last, the house seemed to change its tone. London was never truly silent; there was always the far-off grind of wheels, the clipped cry of a vendor, a hammer somewhere that would not be reasoned with, and, nearer at hand, the low shifting of a coal in the grate. Yet for Elinor the room had the hush of a pantry at dawn, when all is laid out and the day’s labour is waiting to begin.

How should she begin? How say enough, and not too much? The very plainness of what she had to do made it difficult; certain duties are like simple dishes that demand more care than feasts, because there is no abundance of seasoning to hide a mistake. She sat with her pen poised, as if the ink might betray her heart in a blot, deliberating over a blank sheet until she was broken in upon by the entrance of Edward himself.

He had met Mrs. Jennings at the door, as she hurried to her carriage; and she, apologising for not returning his farewell card, had nevertheless almost pushed him within, declaring that Miss Dashwood was above, and wanted to speak to him on very particular business.

Elinor had been congratulating herself that, however hard it was to shape such news into a letter, it was still better than saying it aloud; and now, by the mere opening of a door, she found herself compelled to the very exertion she most dreaded.

Her astonishment and confusion were great. Since his engagement had become public she had not seen him—therefore not since he knew her to be acquainted with it; and the consciousness of what she had been thinking, and what she must tell him, made her feel for a moment as if the air itself had thickened.

Edward was distressed too. They sat down in a silence so awkward that even the small sounds of the room—the faint snap of the fire, the soft fall of a cinder—seemed to speak for them, as if the house would not consent to be entirely mute.

Whether he had asked pardon for his intrusion at first, he could not recollect; and, determined to be on the safe side, he made his apology in form as soon as he could compel his tongue to work.

“Mrs. Jennings told me,” said he, “that you wished to speak with me—at least I understood her so—or I certainly should not have intruded in such a manner; though at the same time I should have been extremely sorry to leave London without seeing you and your sister—especially as it will most likely be some time. It is not probable I should soon have the pleasure of meeting you again. I go to Oxford tomorrow.”

“You would not have gone, however,” Elinor said, recovering herself with the resolution of a person who will finish a task before she allows herself to feel it, “without receiving our good wishes, even if we had not been able to give them in person. Mrs. Jennings was quite right in what she said. I have something of consequence to inform you, which I was on the point of communicating by paper. I am charged with a most agreeable office.”

She found herself breathing faster, but pushed on.

“Colonel Brandon, who was here only ten minutes ago, has desired me to say, that, understanding you mean to take orders, he has great pleasure in offering you the living of Delaford, now just vacant, and only wishes it were more valuable. Allow me to congratulate you on having so respectable and well-judging a friend; and to join in his wish that the living—it is about two hundred a-year—were much more considerable, and such as might better enable you to—as might be more than a temporary accommodation to yourself—such, in short, as might establish all your views of happiness.”

What Edward felt—what the sudden lifting of such a weight did to him—no other mouth could properly tell. His face said what words would not manage: astonishment, gratitude, the bewildered disbelief of a man who has lived on scant fare and is unexpectedly offered a plentiful table. He uttered only,

“Colonel Brandon!”

“Yes,” Elinor continued, gathering more firmness now that the worst had been spoken, “Colonel Brandon means it as a testimony of his concern for what has lately passed—for the cruel situation in which the unjustifiable conduct of your family has placed you—a concern which I am sure Marianne, myself, and all your friends must share; and likewise as a proof of his high esteem for your general character, and his particular approbation of your behaviour on the present occasion.”

“Colonel Brandon give me a living!—Can it be possible?”

“The unkindness of your own relations has made you astonished to find friendship any where.”

“No,” he replied, with sudden consciousness, “not to find it in you; for I cannot be ignorant that to you—to your goodness—I owe it all. I feel it—I would express it if I could—but as you well know, I am no orator.”

“You are very much mistaken. I do assure you you owe it entirely—at least almost entirely—to your own merit, and Colonel Brandon’s discernment of it. I have had no hand in it. I did not even know, till I understood his design, that the living was vacant; nor had it ever occurred to me that he might have such a living in his gift. As a friend of mine—of my family—he may perhaps—indeed I know he has—still greater pleasure in bestowing it; but, upon my word, you owe nothing to my solicitation.”

Truth required her to admit some small share; yet she did it with such reluctance, as if she feared to season his gratitude with the least suspicion of obligation. That hesitation seemed only to settle something in Edward’s mind. He sat for a short time in deep thought—silent, fixed, as if turning the whole matter over like a hard crust he must break in order to eat.

At last, as though speaking were an effort, he said, “Colonel Brandon seems a man of great worth and respectability. I have always heard him spoken of as such; and your brother I know esteems him highly. He is undoubtedly a sensible man, and in his manners perfectly the gentleman.”

“Indeed,” replied Elinor, “I believe you will find him, on farther acquaintance, all that you have heard him to be; and as you will be such very near neighbours—for I understand the parsonage is almost close to the mansion-house—it is particularly important that he should be all this.”

Edward made no answer; but when she turned away her head, he looked at her—serious, earnest, uncheerful—so plainly expressing, without speech, that he might hereafter wish the distance between parsonage and mansion-house much greater, that Elinor felt the glance like the sudden change of air when a door is opened upon a cold passage.

“Colonel Brandon, I think, lodges in St. James Street,” said he soon afterwards, rising.

Elinor told him the number.

“I must hurry away then, to give him those thanks which you will not allow me to give you—to assure him that he has made me a very—an exceedingly happy man.”

Elinor did not attempt to detain him. They parted with her earnest assurance of unceasing good wishes for his happiness, in every change of situation that might befall him; and on his side, rather an endeavour to return the same good will than the ability to clothe it in proper words.

When the door shut him out, Elinor remained standing a moment, listening—absurdly, as she would have called it—to the sound of his steps retreating, as if each footfall carried away some part of her composure. London’s noise flowed back in at once: a cart-rattle, a call in the street, the distant throb of life that never paused. She sank into her chair.

“When I see him again,” she said to herself, “I shall see him the husband of Lucy.”

And with that pleasing anticipation—pleasing, because it promised an end to hopes she had no right to nourish—she sat down to reconsider the past: to recall his words, to measure his looks, to attempt comprehension of his feelings; and, as naturally, to reflect on her own with discontent.

When Mrs. Jennings returned—though she came from visiting people she had never seen before, and therefore had, in common course, a great deal to say—her mind was far more occupied by the important secret she believed herself to carry. She reverted to it as soon as Elinor appeared, as a cook returns to the principal dish, however many small plates have been tasted.

“Well, my dear!” she cried. “I sent you up the young man. Did not I do right? And I suppose you had no great difficulty—You did not find him very unwilling to accept your proposal?”

“No, ma’am; that was not very likely,” said Elinor.

“Well—and how soon will he be ready? For it seems all to depend upon that.”

“Really,” replied Elinor, “I know so little of these kind of forms, that I can hardly even conjecture as to the time, or the preparation necessary; but I suppose two or three months will complete his ordination.”

“Two or three months!” Mrs. Jennings cried. “Lord! my dear, how calmly you talk of it! And can the Colonel wait two or three months! Bless me! it would put me quite out of patience! And though one would be very glad to do a kindness by poor Mr. Ferrars, I do think it is not worth while to wait two or three months for him. Sure somebody else might be found that would do as well; somebody that is in orders already.”

“My dear ma’am,” said Elinor, “what can you be thinking of? Colonel Brandon’s only object is to be of use to Mr. Ferrars.”

“Bless you, my dear! Sure you do not mean to persuade me that the Colonel only marries you for the sake of giving ten guineas to Mr. Ferrars!”

There was no maintaining the deception after that. Elinor explained at once; and in the clearing up of meanings—like flour shaken from a cloth and leaving the pattern plain—both found momentary amusement, with no real loss of happiness to either. Mrs. Jennings merely exchanged one form of delight for another, and did not even relinquish her expectation of the first, but carried it forward, as she did all her pleasures, in spite of contradiction.

“Aye, aye, the parsonage is but a small one,” said she, when her first surprise and satisfaction had bubbled over and subsided, “and very likely may be out of repair; but to hear a man apologising—as I thought—for a house that, to my knowledge, has five sitting-rooms on the ground floor, and I think the housekeeper told me could make up fifteen beds!—and to you too, that had been used to live in Barton Cottage! It seems quite ridiculous. But, my dear, we must touch up the Colonel to do something to the parsonage, and make it comfortable for them, before Lucy goes to it.”

“But Colonel Brandon does not seem to have any idea of the living’s being enough to allow them to marry,” Elinor observed.

“The Colonel is a ninny, my dear; because he has two thousand a-year himself, he thinks nobody else can marry on less. Take my word for it—if I am alive, I shall be paying a visit at Delaford Parsonage before Michaelmas; and I am sure I shan’t go if Lucy an’t there.”

Elinor was quite of her opinion, as to the probability of their not waiting for any thing more.

Edward carried his gratitude to Colonel Brandon as faithfully as if it were a sealed dish he dared not spill, and by the time he reached Bartlett’s Buildings his happiness had risen and aerated like batter left in a warm kitchen. Lucy received it greedily, as she received every advantage, and when Mrs. Jennings came the next day with congratulations, Lucy could say—eyes bright, voice honeyed—that she had never seen him in such spirits in her life.

Her own spirits, at least, were beyond dispute. She spoke of Delaford Parsonage as if it were already aired and swept for them, as if before Michaelmas she should be directing the servants, tasting the cream, counting the poultry, and taking the carriage as naturally as she took breath. And though Edward, in his honest fashion, insisted that Elinor must have her due for what she had done, Lucy was in no danger of being backward where gratitude cost her nothing and promised to be heard. She talked of her friendship for Miss Dashwood with a warmth ready at command, declared herself under obligation to them both, and vowed that no exertion, present or future, on Elinor’s part would ever surprise her—because she believed her capable of anything in the world for those she really valued.

Colonel Brandon, too, was raised by Lucy’s tongue to sainthood, and not merely in sentiment. She was anxious—piously anxious—that he should be treated as a saint in all worldly concerns; that the tithes should be pushed to the utmost; and in the privacy of her own mind she had already measured how far Delaford might yield up its servants, its cows, its poultry, its convenience, into her careful hands. The future, to her, was a larder already inventoried.

It was now more than a week since John Dashwood had called in Berkeley Street; and as, since that time, no notice had been taken by them of his wife’s indisposition beyond one verbal enquiry, Elinor began to feel it necessary to pay a visit. It was a duty that went against her inclination in every particular, and it had none of that softening encouragement which friendly companions sometimes lend to a disagreeable errand.

Marianne was not content with refusing to go herself; she pressed her refusal on Elinor, urgent that she should not go at all. Mrs. Jennings, though her carriage stood always at Elinor’s service, so thoroughly disliked Mrs. John Dashwood that even her curiosity—sharp as a knife when it came to family commotions—could not persuade her to endure Fanny’s company again. She wanted to see how Mrs. Dashwood looked after the late discovery; she wanted to affront her by taking Edward’s part; yet her dislike prevailed.

So Elinor set out alone—alone to a visit no one could have less pleasure in making, alone to the hazard of a tête-à-tête with a woman whom neither Marianne nor Mrs. Jennings had half so much reason to dislike.

Mrs. Dashwood was denied. Elinor had scarcely time to let disappointment settle, like tea-leaves at the bottom of a cup, before the carriage could turn from the house—when her brother accidentally came out. John Dashwood’s face wore that ready satisfaction which required so little cause.

He expressed great pleasure in meeting her; said he had been just going to call in Berkeley Street; and, assuring her that Fanny would be very glad to see her, invited her to come in. They went up stairs into the drawing-room. Nobody was there.

“Fanny is in her own room, I suppose,” said he. “I will go to her presently, for I am sure she will not have the least objection in the world to seeing you. Very far from it, indeed. Now especially there cannot be—but however, you and Marianne were always great favourites. Why would not Marianne come?”

Elinor made what excuse she could.

“I am not sorry to see you alone,” he replied, with a kind of importance that began to thicken his words. “For I have a good deal to say to you. This living of Colonel Brandon’s—can it be true? Has he really given it to Edward? I heard it yesterday by chance, and was coming to you on purpose to enquire farther about it.”

“It is perfectly true. Colonel Brandon has given the living of Delaford to Edward.”

“Really! Well, this is very astonishing! No relationship! No connection between them!—and now that livings fetch such a price! What was the value of this?”

“About two hundred a year.”

“Very well—and for the next presentation to a living of that value—supposing the late incumbent to have been old and sickly, and likely to vacate it soon—he might have got, I dare say, fourteen hundred pounds. And how came he not to have settled that matter before this person’s death? Now, indeed, it would be too late to sell it, but a man of Colonel Brandon’s sense! I wonder he should be so improvident in a point of such common, such natural, concern!”

He shook his head with the air of a man tasting a sauce and finding it foolishly wasted.

“Well, I am convinced that there is a vast deal of inconsistency in almost every human character. I suppose, however—on recollection—that the case may probably be this. Edward is only to hold the living till the person to whom the Colonel has really sold the presentation is old enough to take it. Aye, aye, that is the fact, depend upon it.”

Elinor contradicted him very positively; and by relating that she had herself been employed in conveying the offer from Colonel Brandon to Edward, and must therefore understand the terms on which it was given, obliged him at last to submit to her authority.

“It is truly astonishing!” he cried, after hearing what she said. “What could be the Colonel’s motive?”

“A very simple one—to be of use to Mr. Ferrars.”

“Well, well; whatever Colonel Brandon may be, Edward is a very lucky man. You will not mention the matter to Fanny, however, for though I have broke it to her, and she bears it vastly well,—she will not like to hear it much talked of.”

Elinor, for an instant, felt the sharp temptation to observe that Fanny might have borne with composure an acquisition to her brother by which neither she nor her child could possibly be impoverished. She resisted it.

“Mrs. Ferrars,” added he, lowering his voice to the tone becoming so important a subject, “knows nothing about it at present, and I believe it will be best to keep it entirely concealed from her as long as may be. When the marriage takes place, I fear she must hear of it all.”

“But why should such precaution be used?” Elinor asked. “Though it is not to be supposed that Mrs. Ferrars can have the smallest satisfaction in knowing that her son has money enough to live upon—for that must be quite out of the question—yet why, upon her late behaviour, is she supposed to feel at all? She has done with her son,—she cast him off for ever, and has made all those over whom she had any influence cast him off likewise. Surely, after doing so, she cannot be imagined liable to any impression of sorrow or of joy on his account. She cannot be interested in any thing that befalls him. She would not be so weak as to throw away the comfort of a child, and yet retain the anxiety of a parent!”

“Ah! Elinor,” said John, as if he were explaining a recipe she had never learned, “your reasoning is very good, but it is founded on ignorance of human nature. When Edward’s unhappy match takes place, depend upon it his mother will feel as much as if she had never discarded him; and therefore every circumstance that may accelerate that dreadful event must be concealed from her as much as possible. Mrs. Ferrars can never forget that Edward is her son.”

“You surprise me; I should think it must nearly have escaped her memory by this time.”

“You wrong her exceedingly. Mrs. Ferrars is one of the most affectionate mothers in the world.”

Elinor was silent. The room—handsomely furnished, securely cushioned against the street—seemed to swallow sound, so that even John’s earnestness landed without echo; and in that muffled quiet she felt, more strongly than ever, how affection could be spoken in the dominant family dialect until the word meant its opposite.

After a short pause, he resumed, with a new, weighty satisfaction. “We think now of Robert’s marrying Miss Morton.”

Elinor smiled at the grave decisiveness of his tone, and answered calmly, “The lady, I suppose, has no choice in the affair.”

“Choice!—how do you mean?”

“I only mean that I suppose, from your manner of speaking, it must be the same to Miss Morton whether she marry Edward or Robert.”

“Certainly, there can be no difference; for Robert will now, to all intents and purposes, be considered as the eldest son;—and as to any thing else, they are both very agreeable young men. I do not know that one is superior to the other.”

Elinor said no more. John, too, fell silent, collecting his reflections until they arranged themselves into a confidence he evidently believed would nourish her.

“Of one thing, my dear sister,” said he, kindly taking her hand and speaking in an awful whisper, “I may assure you;—and I will do it, because I know it must gratify you. I have good reason to think—indeed I have it from the best authority, or I should not repeat it, for otherwise it would be very wrong to say any thing about it,—but I have it from the very best authority,—not that I ever precisely heard Mrs. Ferrars say it herself—but her daughter did, and I have it from her,—that in short, whatever objections there might be against a certain—a certain connection, you understand me,—it would have been far preferable to her,—it would not have given her half the vexation that this does.”

He paused as if waiting for Elinor’s gratitude to rise like steam.

“I was exceedingly pleased to hear that Mrs. Ferrars considered it in that light; a very gratifying circumstance you know to us all. ‘It would have been beyond comparison,’ she said, ‘the least evil of the two, and she would be glad to compound now for nothing worse.’ But however, all that is quite out of the question,—not to be thought of or mentioned—as to any attachment you know, it never could be: all that is gone by. But I thought I would just tell you of this, because I knew how much it must please you. Not that you have any reason to regret, my dear Elinor. There is no doubt of your doing exceedingly well,—quite as well, or better, perhaps, all things considered. Has Colonel Brandon been with you lately?”

Elinor had heard enough—if not to gratify vanity, at least to agitate her nerves and fill her mind with that peculiar fatigue which comes of listening to feelings translated into the wrong language and then offered back as kindness. She was therefore glad to be spared the necessity of much reply, and glad to be saved from hearing more, by the entrance of Mr. Robert Ferrars.

After a few moments’ chat, John Dashwood—recollecting that Fanny was yet uninformed of her sister’s being there—quitted the room in quest of her. Elinor was left to improve her acquaintance with Robert.

He came in with that easy, shining unconcern which belongs to a man who hears only the pleasing parts of the world’s noise: the soft click of approval, the rustle of banknotes, the flattering hum of a mother’s partiality. In him, the unfair division of Mrs. Ferrars’s love and liberality—so cruel to Edward, so indulgent to himself—produced no shame to roughen the surface of his spirits. His very comfort confirmed Elinor’s most unfavourable opinion of his head and heart.

They had scarcely been two minutes alone before he began to speak of Edward; for he too had heard of the living, and was inquisitive on the subject. Elinor repeated the particulars as she had given them to John. Their effect on Robert, though very different, was not less striking.

He laughed most immoderately. The idea of Edward as a clergyman, lodged in a small parsonage-house, diverted him beyond measure; and when he added, with a relish for images that cost him no compassion, the fancy of Edward reading prayers in a white surplice, and publishing the banns of marriage between John Smith and Mary Brown, he could conceive nothing more ridiculous.

Elinor waited in silence, gravity fixed and unyielding, until such folly should exhaust itself. Her eyes, in spite of herself, rested on him with a look that spoke all the contempt she felt. It was a look well bestowed: it eased her own feelings, and communicated nothing to him.

He was recalled from wit to wisdom, not by any reproof of hers, but by his own sensibility to the effect he wished to produce.

“We may treat it as a joke,” said he at last, recovering from the affected laugh which had prolonged the genuine gaiety, “but, upon my soul, it is a most serious business. Poor Edward! he is ruined for ever. I am extremely sorry for it; for I know him to be a very good-hearted creature; as well-meaning a fellow perhaps, as any in the world. You must not judge of him, Miss Dashwood, from your slight acquaintance. Poor Edward! His manners are certainly not the happiest in nature. But we are not all born, you know, with the same powers,—the same address. Poor fellow! to see him in a circle of strangers! To be sure it was pitiable enough; but upon my soul, I believe he has as good a heart as any in the kingdom.”

He warmed to his own performance.

“And I declare and protest to you I never was so shocked in my life, as when it all burst forth. I could not believe it. My mother was the first person who told me of it; and I, feeling myself called on to act with resolution, immediately said to her, ‘My dear madam, I do not know what you may intend to do on the occasion, but as for myself, I must say, that if Edward does marry this young woman, I never will see him again.’ That was what I said immediately. I was most uncommonly shocked, indeed! Poor Edward! he has done for himself completely,—shut himself out for ever from all decent society! But, as I directly said to my mother, I am not in the least surprised at it; from his style of education, it was always to be expected. My poor mother was half frantic.”

“Have you ever seen the lady?” Elinor asked.

“Yes; once, while she was staying in this house, I happened to drop in for ten minutes; and I saw quite enough of her. The merest awkward country girl, without style, or elegance, and almost without beauty. I remember her perfectly. Just the kind of girl I should suppose likely to captivate poor Edward.”

He spoke with the complacent authority of a man who believes his own taste a standard measure.

“I offered immediately, as soon as my mother related the affair to me, to talk to him myself, and dissuade him from the match; but it was too late then, I found, to do any thing, for unluckily, I was not in the way at first, and knew nothing of it till after the breach had taken place, when it was not for me, you know, to interfere. But had I been informed of it a few hours earlier, I think it is most probable that something might have been hit on. I certainly should have represented it to Edward in a very strong light. ‘My dear fellow,’ I should have said, ‘consider what you are doing. You are making a most disgraceful connection, and such a one as your family are unanimous in disapproving.’ I cannot help thinking, in short, that means might have been found. But now it is all too late. He must be starved, you know, that is certain; absolutely starved.”

He had just settled Edward’s fate with serene finality when the door opened and Mrs. John Dashwood entered, putting an end to the subject as if she had shut a lid upon a saucepan boiling over.

Though she never spoke of it out of her own family, Elinor could see its influence in the something like confusion of her countenance, and in an attempt at cordiality that seemed practised as one practises a difficult civility—measured, cautious, and never quite natural. She even went so far as to say she was concerned to find that Elinor and her sister were so soon to leave town, as she had hoped to see more of them.

In this exertion her husband, attending her into the room and hanging enamoured over her accents, appeared to distinguish every thing that was most affectionate and graceful—listening as if her words were music worth defending from the world, while the deeper discord of their conduct remained, like an unwanted sound outside a closed window, ignored because it was inconvenient to name.

One other short call in Harley Street—no more than the time it took a footman to announce them and the street-door to breathe out its cold, coach-scented air—completed all that passed between the Dashwood sisters and their brother in town. John congratulated Elinor, with the easy triumph of a man pleased to be economical at another’s expense, on their having travelled so far towards Barton “without costing a shilling,” and on Colonel Brandon’s intention of following them to Cleveland in a day or two. Fanny, with that languid civility which always sounded as if she were doing one a favour by permitting one to exist, let fall a faint invitation to Norland—whenever it should happen to be in their way; which, of all contingencies, was the least likely. John, warmer in private than his wife could endure in public, pressed Elinor’s hand, and assured her—almost with earnestness—that the moment she was settled at Delaford, he would come and see her there.

It amused Elinor, even while it vexed her, to observe how all her friends appeared determined to send her to Delaford; to plant her, like a useful herb, in soil which everyone praised, and which she herself had least desire to tread. Not only did her brother and Mrs. Jennings speak of that parsonage as if her name were already written on its pantry shelves and linen-press, but Lucy too—Lucy, whose friendship had clung to Elinor in London with the persistence of a sweetmeat paper that will not be shaken off—when they parted, had pressed an invitation to visit her there; as if Delaford were a market-town of her own creation, and Elinor’s presence a tribute due.

Very early in April, and early enough in the day for the sun to have some pale strength through the spring mist, the two parties from Hanover Square and Berkeley Street set out from their respective doors, to meet by appointment on the road. For Charlotte’s convenience, and the child’s—whose wants, like a little drum, beat time to every mile—they were to be more than two days upon the journey. Mr. Palmer, travelling more expeditiously with Colonel Brandon, was to join them at Cleveland soon after their arrival.

Marianne, though she had enjoyed few hours of comfort in London, and had long been eager to quit it, could not, when the moment came, bid adieu to the house where she had last been happy in hope—where she had fed her heart on confidence in Willoughby—without pain sharp enough to make every familiar stair and doorway a reproach. Nor could she leave the place in which he remained—busy in new engagements and new schemes, in which she could have no part—without shedding many tears; tears that seemed to her the only language still left that could say what no voice would be allowed to utter.

Elinor’s satisfaction at removal was more positive, more cleanly felt; like the relief of a room aired after a long confinement. She had no such object for lingering thought to fasten on; she left behind no creature whose absence promised even a moment’s regret. She was grateful to be free from the persecution of Lucy’s endearments, grateful, too, to have brought Marianne away without another chance encounter with Willoughby since his marriage; and she looked forward with hope—quiet, practical hope—to what a few months of tranquillity at Barton might do towards restoring Marianne’s peace, and strengthening her own.

Their journey was safely performed. The second day brought them into the cherished—or the prohibited—county of Somerset, for so it was called by turns in Marianne’s imagination; beloved because it led to Barton, forbidden because it led also too near to Combe Magna. In the forenoon of the third day they drove up to Cleveland.

Cleveland was a spacious modern-built house, seated upon a sloping lawn. It had no park; but the pleasure-grounds were tolerably extensive, and arranged with that orderly pretence of wildness which belongs to places that must look “natural” by design. There was an open shrubbery and a closer wood-walk; a road of smooth gravel wound round a plantation and brought the carriage to the front. The lawn was dotted with timber, and the house itself stood as if under the guardianship of fir and mountain-ash and acacia; a thick screen of them together, interspersed with tall Lombardy poplars, shut out the offices, and with them the busy noises and savory breath of work—those homely sounds and smells that make a house feel inhabited.

Marianne entered with a heart swelling from the consciousness of being only eighty miles from Barton, and not thirty from Combe Magna; and before five minutes had passed, while the others were occupied in helping Charlotte exhibit her child to the housekeeper, Marianne slipped away. She stole out through the winding shrubberies, just beginning to be in beauty—those tender leaf-buds like green promises—till she gained a distant eminence. There stood a little Grecian temple, white and cold as an ornament is meant to be, and from it her eye wandered over a wide tract of country to the south-east, resting—fondly, cruelly—on the farthest ridge of hills upon the horizon, and fancying that from their summits Combe Magna might be seen.

In such moments of precious, invaluable misery, she rejoiced to be at Cleveland, because it allowed her to be wretched at liberty. Returning by a different circuit, tasting all the happy privilege of country freedom—the power to wander without being watched, to move from place to place in solitary indulgence—she resolved to spend almost every hour, of every day of her stay, in such rambles; to make of the grounds a kind of silent companion that would never correct her feeling, never argue her out of her grief.
