She came back in time to join the others as they quitted the house for an excursion through the immediate premises; and the rest of the morning was easily whiled away in lounging round the kitchen-garden, examining the bloom upon its warm walls, and listening to the gardener’s lamentations upon blights. There were borders where the earth lay dark and crumbling, smelling richly of last year’s decay and this year’s beginnings; there were trained branches and espaliered fruit-trees, tied and guided like domestic passions made obedient. They dawdled afterwards through the green-house, where a few favourite plants—unwarily exposed and nipped by lingering frost—gave Charlotte an opportunity to laugh, as if the very tenderness of the leaves were ridiculous. They visited the poultry-yard too; and in the dairy-maid’s disappointed hopes—hens forsaking their nests, eggs stolen by a fox, a promising young brood rapidly diminished—Charlotte found fresh sources of merriment.

Elinor observed it all with a mind that could appreciate the comfort of such employments, even if her heart was not at leisure to take delight in them. There was a particular pleasure in hearing the small, honest sounds of a place well managed: the scratch and flutter of fowls; the creak of a gate; the faint watery drip from a trough; and, from far off, the steady rhythm of a spade. How different, she thought, from London’s incessant clatter—wheels, cries, and that harsh metallic din that presses upon the spirits till one forgets what quiet feels like. Here, even laughter seemed obliged to make room for birdsong and the wind’s soft brushing through young leaves; the very air had a gentler acoustics.

The morning had been fine and dry, and Marianne, in her plan of employment abroad, had not calculated for any change of weather during their stay. With great surprise, therefore, she found herself prevented, after dinner, by a settled rain from going out again. She had depended on a twilight walk to the Grecian temple—perhaps all over the grounds—and an evening merely cold or damp would not have deterred her; but a heavy, steady rain, that fell with that patient insistence which soaks through even the bravest resolve, could not be fancied into pleasantness.

Their party was small, and the hours passed quietly away. Mrs. Palmer had her child; Mrs. Jennings her carpet-work; they talked of the friends they had left behind, arranged Lady Middleton’s engagements, and wondered whether Mr. Palmer and Colonel Brandon would get farther than Reading that night. Elinor, however little concerned in the gossip, joined in discourse when it was necessary; and Marianne—who had the knack, in every house, of finding her way to the library, however the family in general might avoid it—soon procured herself a book, as if printed pages might lend her a borrowed language for feelings she could not translate into speech.

Nothing was wanting, on Mrs. Palmer’s side, that constant and friendly good-humour could do to make them welcome. The openness and heartiness of her manner atoned for that want of recollection and elegance which often made her deficient in forms; her kindness, recommended by so pretty a face, was engaging; her folly, though evident, was not disgusting, because it was not conceited. Elinor could have forgiven everything—almost everything—but her laugh; for it broke upon the ear with a suddenness and sameness that flattened every surrounding sound, as if the delicate gradations of the room’s quiet—rain upon glass, the soft rasp of carpet-work, the turning of a page—were nothing compared with that one loud note.

The next day the two gentlemen arrived, to a very late dinner; and their coming enlarged the party with real pleasure, and gave a welcome variety to conversation, which a long morning of the same continued rain had reduced very low. Elinor had seen so little of Mr. Palmer, and in that little had seen such variety in his address to Marianne and herself, that she did not know what to expect of him in his own family. She found him, however, perfectly the gentleman in his behaviour to all his visitors, and only occasionally rude to his wife and her mother. He was capable of being a pleasant companion, and only prevented from being so always by too great an aptitude to fancy himself as much superior to people in general as he must feel himself to be to Mrs. Jennings and Charlotte.

In the rest of his character and habits, as far as Elinor could perceive, there were no traits unusual in his sex and time of life. He was nice in his eating, uncertain in his hours; fond of his child, though affecting to slight it; and he idled away the mornings at billiards which ought to have been devoted to business. He spoke of dishes with a fastidious authority, picking at what he praised, and praising what he hardly tasted; as if appetite were a performance and not a sense. Yet Elinor liked him, upon the whole, much better than she had expected; and in her heart was not sorry that she could like him no more—not sorry to be driven, by the observation of his epicurism, selfishness, and conceit, to rest with complacency on the remembrance of Edward’s generous temper, simple taste, and diffident feelings. With Edward, there had always been something unstudied, a quiet gratitude for what was placed before him, as if he heard—in the clink of a spoon, in the crackle of a fire—the domestic music others were too proud to notice.

Of Edward, or at least of some concerns connected with him, she now received intelligence from Colonel Brandon, who had lately been in Dorsetshire; and who, treating Elinor at once as the disinterested friend of Mr. Ferrars and the kind confidante of himself, talked to her a great deal of the parsonage at Delaford. He described its deficiencies with the same careful exactness with which a conscientious steward might speak of a larder too small or a roof that let in weather; and he told her what he meant to do himself towards removing them—what should be mended, what improved, what made more fit for comfort. There was, in his manner, none of the vulgar eagerness of a man displaying his generosity, but the steady gravity of one who values a home as a place where daily wants are honorably met.

His behaviour to her in this, as in every other particular—his open pleasure in meeting her again after an absence of only ten days, his readiness to converse with her, and his deference for her opinion—might very well justify Mrs. Jennings’s persuasion of his attachment, and might perhaps have been enough to make Elinor suspect it herself, had she not, as from the first, believed Marianne his real favourite. As it was, the notion scarcely entered her head except by Mrs. Jennings’s suggestion; and she could not help believing herself the nicer observer of the two. She watched his eyes while Mrs. Jennings thought only of his behaviour. And when Marianne, from imprudence rather than ill-luck, began to complain—only by look and a slight change of voice—of a heaviness in her head and throat, Elinor saw instantly what Mrs. Jennings did not: the quick feelings and needless alarm of a lover in Colonel Brandon’s anxious solicitude.

Two delightful twilight walks on the third and fourth evenings of Marianne’s being there—not merely on the dry gravel of the shrubbery, but all over the grounds, and especially in the most distant parts, where there was something more of wildness than in the rest, where the trees were oldest and the grass longest and wettest—had, assisted by the still greater imprudence of sitting in wet shoes and stockings, given her a cold so violent as, though for a day or two denied and trifled with, would force itself by increasing ailments on the concern of everybody and the notice of herself.

Prescriptions poured in from all quarters, and as usual were all declined. Though heavy and feverish, with pain in her limbs, a cough, and a sore throat, a good night’s rest was to cure her entirely; the simplest nursing was met with a romantic obstinacy, as if to accept care were to admit defeat. It was with difficulty that Elinor prevailed on her, when she went to bed, to try one or two of the plainest remedies—those humble domestic helps that ask no imagination, only patience: warmth well wrapped about the feet, a mild draught, a quiet room where the rain’s steady tapping might be heard without being answered by the reckless step of one determined to wander out into it.

Marianne rose the next morning at her usual hour, and to every question returned the same faint answer—that she was better—then tried to prove it by taking up the small occupations which had once been her pleasure. But there is a kind of diligence that exposes a body’s falsehood: she sat drawn close to the fire, a book open in her hand, yet her eyes slid over the lines as if the print had lost its language; when she attempted to walk, she shivered as though the very air upon her skin were an unkind draught. By evening she yielded early to bed, more indisposed than she would confess.

Colonel Brandon could not understand how Elinor maintained so even a composure, though she had watched and tended all day in spite of Marianne’s impatience, and at night had insisted—gently, firmly—on the draughts and powders thought proper. Elinor trusted, as Marianne did, to the old household hope that sleep, like a warm broth, might set everything right. But the night was restless, feverish, and thin with waking.

In the morning Marianne persisted in rising, then confessed—at last, and as if to spare them the struggle—she could not sit up, and returned of her own accord to bed. Elinor, now ready to take Mrs. Jennings’s counsel, sent for the Palmers’ apothecary.

Mr. Harris came, examined his patient with practised fingers and a cool attention, and spoke encouragement—only a few days, he said, and she would be restored. Yet he let fall words that turned the room colder than the winter panes: the disorder had a putrid tendency; there might be infection. At that, Mrs. Palmer’s whole mind flew, with the fierce swiftness of maternal terror, to her baby. The house itself seemed suddenly altered to her ear: every footstep in the corridor a threat; every closed door the muffling of danger.

Mrs. Jennings, who had looked on Marianne’s complaint as serious from the first, grew grave indeed under Mr. Harris’s report. She urged Charlotte’s fears, insisted on caution, and pressed that Mrs. Palmer must remove herself and her infant immediately. Mr. Palmer laughed at apprehension as he always did, as if fear were a vulgar noise best silenced by wit, yet his wife’s distress beat upon him without ceasing; and even his obstinacy could not stand against the crying importunity of a young mother with her child in her arms.

So within an hour of the apothecary’s arrival, Mrs. Palmer was gone—her little boy and his nurse carried off to the house of a relation on the far side of Bath. Mr. Palmer promised, under repeated entreaty, to follow in a day or two, and she had been nearly as urgent that Mrs. Jennings should accompany her.

Mrs. Jennings would not stir.

With a kindness that made Elinor truly love her, she declared herself fixed at Cleveland while Marianne remained ill, determined to supply—by sheer constancy and bustling care—the place of the mother they had taken Marianne from. Elinor found her, in every hour, a willing and active helpmate; there was a comfort in the very sound of Mrs. Jennings’s presence—the soft thump of a cushion set right, the quick rustle of skirts, the kettle beginning its low, domestic song—proof that the world still knew how to nurse. Her experience was often of real use; she could fold a warmed flannel with a sure hand, coax a reluctant patient to swallow a draught, and order the sick-room like a well-run kitchen where nothing is wasted and every minute matters.

Poor Marianne, languid and low, feeling herself universally ill, could no longer persuade herself that tomorrow would be recovery. And what tomorrow had been meant to bring—homeward travel, the surprise of their mother—made each ache sharper. They were to have begun their journey that day; they were to have been at Barton the next forenoon, with a servant from Mrs. Jennings to smooth the way. Marianne spoke little, but what she said was lamentation for the delay, for the disappointment of that imagined moment when their mother’s voice would have met them at the door. Elinor tried to rally her, tried to sweeten the thought as one sweetens a bitter cordial—“only a short delay,” she insisted, because she truly believed it.

The next day changed little. Marianne did not appear worse; she was certainly not better. Their party dwindled again. Mr. Palmer, though unwilling to go—unwilling from real humanity, and from a dislike of seeming frightened away by his wife—was at last persuaded by Colonel Brandon to keep his promise and follow Charlotte. While he prepared to depart, Colonel Brandon himself began to speak of going likewise, as though he could not endure the helpless waiting and must find motion, any motion, to keep his mind from dark imaginings.

Here Mrs. Jennings interposed with a shrewd tenderness. To send the Colonel away, she insisted, would be to rob them all of comfort; and besides, she declared, she should want him of an evening to play at piquet while Miss Dashwood sat upstairs. Her plea, half domestic comedy, half true necessity, pressed him to remain; and he, whose heart was gratified to obey, did not long affect reluctance—especially as Mr. Palmer seconded the request warmly, plainly relieved to leave behind someone able to advise in any emergency.

Marianne knew nothing of these arrangements. It never occurred to her that she had driven the owners of Cleveland away within seven days of their arrival. Mrs. Palmer’s absence surprised her not at all; and as it gave her no concern, she never named her.

Two days after Mr. Palmer’s departure, Marianne remained in much the same state. Mr. Harris attended daily, speaking boldly of speedy recovery; and Elinor, clinging to his confidence, was equally sanguine. The others were not. Mrs. Jennings had decided almost from the beginning that Marianne would never get over it, and Colonel Brandon—chiefly useful, now, in listening to Mrs. Jennings’s forebodings—had not strength of mind to resist them. He tried to reason himself out of fear, remembering the apothecary’s assurances; but long hours alone, when the house fell into its quieter sounds and the wind took up the louder ones, admitted every melancholy thought. In the hush between gusts he seemed to hear absence in advance, and could not drive away the persuasion that he should see Marianne no more.

On the morning of the third day, those gloomy anticipations were nearly undone. Mr. Harris arrived and declared his patient materially better—pulse stronger, symptoms more favourable. Elinor’s hope, confirmed, brightened into cheerfulness. She rejoiced that in her letters to her mother she had followed her own judgment rather than Mrs. Jennings’s, making light of the indisposition; she began almost to fix upon the day Marianne might travel.

But evening unmade the promise of the morning. Marianne became ill again—heavier, more restless, more uncomfortable than before. Elinor, still determined to hope, persuaded herself it was only fatigue from having sat up while the bed was made. She administered the cordials prescribed—those sharp, warming drops with their spirituous scent, meant to coax life back to the surface—and watched with a kind of hungry gratitude as Marianne sank at last into slumber.

The sleep was not as quiet as Elinor wished; yet it lasted long enough to tempt belief in its benefit. She resolved to sit by Marianne through it. Mrs. Jennings, knowing nothing of this change, went early to bed; her maid, one of the principal nurses, was downstairs taking her ease in the housekeeper’s room. Elinor was left alone with her sister.

The repose became more disturbed. Marianne shifted continually, her breath catching and slipping, and at intervals little sounds of complaint escaped her—broken, inarticulate, like a language half-forgotten. Elinor, bent over her, watched every movement, listening with the sharpened attention of love in fear; even the house’s smallest noises—the settling of wood, the distant chink of a hearth-tool—seemed too loud, too capable of startling a mind already unsteady.

Then some accidental sound in the house—an indistinct bump, a door somewhere caught by the wind—snapped Marianne awake. She started up with feverish wildness.

“Is mama coming?”

“Not yet,” Elinor answered, hiding her terror as one hides a flame with a hand, and easing Marianne back. “But she will be here, I hope, before it is long. It is a great way, you know, from hence to Barton.”

“But she must not go round by London,” Marianne cried, the words tumbling fast. “I shall never see her, if she goes by London.”

Elinor felt at once that Marianne was not quite herself. She tried to soothe her, and at the same time reached for her pulse. It was lower—and quicker—than ever. Marianne still spoke wildly of their mother; and Elinor’s alarm rose so fast it became decision. Mr. Harris must be sent for instantly; and a messenger dispatched to Barton for Mrs. Dashwood.

To consult Colonel Brandon on how best to effect the latter followed immediately. Elinor rang for the maid to take her place by Marianne, and hurried down to the drawing-room where she knew the Colonel often remained late.

There was no time for hesitation. Her fears and difficulties stood plain before him. He did not attempt to argue away her dread; he listened in silent despondence. But her difficulty—the messenger—was met at once. With a readiness that made it seem as if he had long prepared himself for this service, he offered to go himself and fetch Mrs. Dashwood.

Elinor made no resistance that could not be overcome. She thanked him briefly, fervently; and while he hurried off his servant with a message for Mr. Harris and orders for post-horses, she wrote a few lines to her mother.

The comfort of such a friend—such a companion for her mother—was felt with a gratitude too deep for ornament. His judgment would guide; his attendance would relieve; his steadiness might soften the shock of a summons like this. He, meanwhile, whatever storm was in him, acted with the firmness of a collected mind—arranged everything with swift exactness, calculated the time of his return. No moment was lost. The horses arrived even before they were expected. He pressed Elinor’s hand, looked at her with solemnity, murmured a few words too low to be caught, and hurried into the carriage.

It was about twelve o’clock. Elinor went back to Marianne’s room to wait for the apothecary and watch through the night.

It became a night of almost equal suffering to both. Hour after hour passed in sleepless pain and delirium for Marianne, and cruel anxiety for Elinor. Mr. Harris did not appear for long. Once Elinor’s fears were raised, they made payment—by excess—for all her former security. The servant who sat up with her, since she would not allow Mrs. Jennings to be called, tortured rather than soothed, with hints of what her mistress had always thought.

Marianne’s wandering returned again and again to her mother. Each time the name was spoken it struck Elinor like a blow. She reproached herself for having trifled with days of illness, for trusting too easily, for delaying what might now be irreparable. She pictured their mother arriving too late—too late to see Marianne living, or too late to see her rational.

She was on the point of sending again for Mr. Harris—or for someone else—when at last, not until after five o’clock, he came. His opinion made some amends for his delay. He acknowledged an unexpected and unpleasant alteration, but would not admit the danger to be material. A fresh mode of treatment, he declared, must procure relief; and though his confidence did not wholly enter Elinor’s heart, it steadied her. He promised to call again within three or four hours and left patient and attendant a little more composed than he had found them.

With strong concern and many reproaches for not being called sooner, Mrs. Jennings heard in the morning what had passed. Her old apprehensions returned with new force; she did not doubt the event. She tried to speak comfort, but her conviction would not allow her the comfort of hope. Her grief was real. The rapid decay, the early death of a girl so young and lovely would have struck even a stranger; and Marianne was not a stranger to her. Marianne had been her companion for three months, was under her care, and was known to have been injured and long unhappy. Elinor’s distress—especially dear to her—was before her; and when Mrs. Jennings thought what Marianne might be to her mother, as Charlotte was to herself, her sympathy for Mrs. Dashwood became sincere and aching.

Mr. Harris was punctual in his second visit, but he came to be disappointed. His medicines had failed. The fever was unabated. Marianne was quieter—yet not more herself—sunk in a heavy stupor. Elinor, catching in an instant all that he feared and more, proposed calling in further advice. He judged it unnecessary: he still had something more to try, another fresh application, and spoke of its success with the same confidence as before. His assurances reached Elinor’s ear but could not enter her heart. She remained calm except when she thought of her mother; but she was almost hopeless.

Thus she continued until noon, scarcely stirring from Marianne’s bed. Her thoughts wandered from one suffering friend to another, while Mrs. Jennings’s talk—well-meant, unguarded—pressed upon her the idea that this severity came from the many weeks of prior indisposition brought on by Marianne’s disappointment. Elinor felt the reasonableness of it, and it sharpened her misery.

About noon, however, she began—cautiously, with a dread of being deceived—to fancy a slight amendment in her sister’s pulse. She waited, watched, touched it again and again; and at last, with an agitation she could hardly conceal beneath outward steadiness, ventured to communicate her hope.

Mrs. Jennings, forced to acknowledge a temporary revival, still tried to keep Elinor from indulging any belief in its continuance. Elinor repeated to herself every injunction of distrust, told herself not to hope. But hope had already entered; and with its anxious flutter in her, she bent over Marianne as if watching for some small sign in a saucepan that the simmer had steadied—that the boil of danger had at last broken.

Half an hour passed, and the favourable symptom remained. Others followed: her breath, her skin, her lips all flattered Elinor with signs of amendment. Marianne opened her eyes and fixed them on her with a rational, though languid gaze. Anxiety and hope now weighed on Elinor together and left her no tranquillity until Mr. Harris arrived at four o’clock—when his assurances, his felicitations, his declaration that Marianne’s recovery surpassed even his expectation, gave Elinor confidence, comfort, and tears of joy. Marianne was materially better in every respect; she was entirely out of danger.

Mrs. Jennings, perhaps satisfied that her forebodings had found some justification in the late alarm, allowed herself to trust the apothecary now; she admitted, with unfeigned joy, and soon with plain cheerfulness, the probability of complete recovery.

Elinor could not be cheerful. Her joy was of another kind, leading to anything but gaiety. Marianne restored—to life, to health, to friends, and to her doting mother—filled her heart with exquisite comfort and fervent gratitude; yet it produced no smiles, no words of exultation. It was satisfaction silent and strong.

All the afternoon she remained by her sister with little intermission—calming fears, answering faint inquiries, supplying every small succour, watching almost every look and breath. The thought of relapse would sometimes rise of itself, to remind her what anxiety was; but each minute examination confirmed recovery. When, at six o’clock, Marianne sank into a quiet, steady, comfortable sleep, Elinor silenced every doubt.

The hour approached when Colonel Brandon might be expected back. At ten, she trusted—or not much later—her mother would be relieved from the dreadful suspense in which she must now be travelling. The Colonel too—how slow time moved while they knew nothing! The house’s sounds seemed to mark the delay: the wind in the chimneys, the low creak of a settling stair, each one distinct in the cold air, each one too present.

At seven o’clock, leaving Marianne still sweetly asleep, Elinor joined Mrs. Jennings in the drawing-room for tea. Fear had kept her from breakfast; the sudden reverse from dinner; so the warmth and fragrance of the tray were peculiarly welcome—the faint astringency of tea, the comforting breath of milk, the homely clink of cups that made a kind of peace in the ear. Mrs. Jennings would have persuaded her afterward to rest and let her take Elinor’s place by Marianne; but Elinor had no sense of fatigue, no capability of sleep. She could not be kept away an unnecessary instant.

Mrs. Jennings therefore went upstairs with her to see that all continued right, then left her again to her charge and thoughts, retiring to her own room to write letters and sleep.

The night was cold and stormy. The wind roared round the house; rain beat hard against the windows with a steady drumming that might have been terrible, had Elinor not felt such happiness within. Marianne slept through every blast. The travellers—whoever they were—had a rich reward in store for every present inconvenience.

The clock struck eight. Had it been ten, Elinor would have been convinced she heard a carriage drive up; so strong was the persuasion that, in spite of the almost impossibility of their being already come, she moved into the adjoining dressing-closet and opened a shutter to satisfy herself.

Her ears had not deceived her. The flaring lamps of a carriage were in view. By their uncertain light she thought she could discern four horses; and the excess of her poor mother’s alarm, which such haste proclaimed, explained such unexpected rapidity.

Never had Elinor found it so difficult to be calm. She knew what her mother must be feeling as the carriage stopped—the doubt, the dread, perhaps despair—and what she herself had to tell. With such knowledge, calm was impossible. All that remained was speed. She stayed only long enough to leave Mrs. Jennings’s maid with Marianne, then hurried downstairs.

The bustle in the vestibule, heard as she passed along an inner lobby, assured her they were already in the house. She rushed to the drawing-room—entered it—and saw only Willoughby.

Elinor started as if the air itself had turned bitter, and the sight of him were an offence to the very light in the room. Instinct carried her at once to the door; her fingers had found the lock, eager to free her from him as one snatches a hand from a hot pan.

“Miss Dashwood—half an hour—for ten minutes,” he said, hurrying after her, his voice not pleading so much as commanding, like a man who has driven hard and will not be denied. “I entreat you to stay.”

“No, sir,” she returned, steady as a well-set tray. “I shall not stay. Your business cannot be with me. The servants, I suppose, forgot to tell you that Mr. Palmer is not in the house.”

“Had they told me,” he cried, all heat and edge, “that Mr. Palmer and every relation he has were at the devil, it would not have turned me from the door. My business is with you—and only you.”

“With me!” Her astonishment was so complete it was almost speechless; then, recovering herself, she added with cold impatience, “Well, sir—be quick—and if you can, less violent.”

“Sit down, and I will be both.”

She stood doubting. Any moment might bring Colonel Brandon; any moment might bring discovery and confusion upon a house already worn raw with watching. Yet she had pledged herself to hear him; and curiosity, like a sharp scent on the wind, drew her against her will. Dispatch, too, was prudence. After a brief struggle she went back to the table and sat. He took the opposite chair. For half a minute the room held them in a silence that seemed to make even the distant household sounds—some soft door-hinge, a step upon a stair, the faint clink of porcelain from a lower room—more distinct, as if the house itself listened.

“Pray be quick, sir,” Elinor said at last. “I have no time to spare.”

He had folded himself into thought so deeply that he appeared not to hear. Then, abruptly, as if a knife had slipped free of its sheath—

“Your sister,” he said, “is out of danger. I heard it from the servant. God be praised!—But is it true? Is it really true?”

Elinor would not answer. He repeated it, sharper, more urgent, as if he could force certainty by sound.

“For God’s sake tell me—out of danger, or not?”

“We hope she is.”

He rose and paced across the room, the boards giving a subdued reply beneath his steps, as a well-built house answers footfall without complaint. When he returned to his chair, a forced brightness was dragged over his face like a cloth over a poor dish meant to pass for good.

“Had I known as much half an hour ago—but since I am here,” he went on, with a gaiety too deliberate to be real, “what does it signify? For once, Miss Dashwood—perhaps for the last time—let us be cheerful together. I am in a fine mood for gaiety.” His cheeks flushed deeper, and his eyes fixed on her with a reckless steadiness. “Tell me honestly—do you think me most a knave or a fool?”

The question, the visit, the whole scene, were so unlike reason that her mind seized at once on the simplest explanation. He must be in liquor. It was the only thing that made such behaviour intelligible. She rose immediately.

“Mr. Willoughby, I advise you at present to return to Combe. I am not at leisure to remain with you longer. Whatever your business may be, it will be better recollected and explained to-morrow.”

“I understand you,” he said, and a smile, expressive and controlled, crossed his mouth. “Yes, I am very drunk. A pint of porter with my cold beef at Marlborough was enough to overset me.”

“At Marlborough!” Elinor exclaimed, more bewildered than soothed.

“Yes. I left London this morning at eight. The only ten minutes I have spent out of my chaise since then procured me a nuncheon at Marlborough.”

The steadiness of his voice, the clear intelligence in his eye, swept away her notion of intoxication. Whatever madness had brought him to Cleveland, it was not that. She sat again, not from willingness but necessity.

“Mr. Willoughby, you ought to feel—and I certainly do—that after what has passed, your coming here in this manner, and forcing yourself upon my notice, requires a very particular excuse. What is it you mean by it?”

“I mean,” he answered, with a grave force that shook off all his earlier flourish, “if I can, to make you hate me one degree less than you do now. I mean to offer some kind of explanation—some kind of apology—to open my whole heart to you; and by convincing you that though I have always been a blockhead, I have not always been a rascal, to obtain something like forgiveness from Ma—from your sister.”

“Is this the real reason of your coming?”

“Upon my soul it is.” The warmth with which he spoke brought back, against her judgement, the first Willoughby—eager, animated, persuasive—and in spite of herself she believed he meant what he said.

“If that is all, you may be satisfied already; for Marianne does—she has long forgiven you.”

“Has she?” The eagerness rushed out of him like breath after a long restraint. “Then she has forgiven me before she ought. But she shall forgive me again—and on more reasonable grounds. Now will you listen to me?”

Elinor bowed. She would not grant him kindness, but she would grant him hearing; duty could be as unyielding as anger.

“I do not know,” he began after a pause in which he seemed to taste each word before allowing it out, “how you may have accounted for my behaviour to your sister, or what diabolical motive you may have imputed to me. Perhaps you will hardly think the better of me—it is worth the trial, however, and you shall hear everything. When I first became intimate in your family, I had no other intention—no other view—than to pass my time pleasantly while I was obliged to remain in Devonshire—more pleasantly than I had ever done before. Your sister’s lovely person and interesting manners could not but please me; and her behaviour to me, almost from the first, was of a kind—” His face altered with a shame he could not command. “It is astonishing, when I reflect on what it was, and what she was, that my heart should have been so insensible! But at first, I must confess, my vanity only was elevated by it. Careless of her happiness, thinking only of my own amusement, giving way to feelings which I had always been too much in the habit of indulging, I endeavoured, by every means in my power, to make myself pleasing to her—without any design of returning her affection.”

Elinor’s eyes turned on him with a contempt so sharp it might have been felt. She cut him short.

“It is hardly worth while, Mr. Willoughby, for you to relate, or for me to listen any longer. Such a beginning as this cannot be followed by anything. Do not let me be pained by hearing more.”

“I insist on your hearing the whole,” he said, and the insistence was not insolent so much as desperate. “My fortune was never large; I had always been expensive; always in the habit of associating with people of better income than myself. Every year since my coming of age—or even before, I believe—had added to my debts; and though the death of my old cousin, Mrs. Smith, was to set me free, yet that event being uncertain and possibly far distant, it had been for some time my intention to re-establish my circumstances by marrying a woman of fortune. To attach myself to your sister, therefore, was not a thing to be thought of; and with a meanness—selfishness—cruelty—which no indignant, no contemptuous look of yours, Miss Dashwood, can ever reprobate too much—I was acting in this manner, trying to engage her regard, without a thought of returning it.”

He leaned forward, the confession pressed out of him as if each sentence were drawn like a thorn.

“But one thing may be said for me: even in that horrid state of selfish vanity, I did not know the extent of the injury I meditated, because I did not then know what it was to love. But have I ever known it? Well may it be doubted; for had I really loved, could I have sacrificed my feelings to vanity—to avarice? or, what is more, could I have sacrificed hers? But I have done it. To avoid a comparative poverty—which her affection and her society would have deprived of all its horrors—I have, by raising myself to affluence, lost every thing that could make it a blessing.”

“You did then,” Elinor said, and against her will the hard edge of her tone softened a little, “believe yourself at one time attached to her?”

“To have resisted such attractions—to have withstood such tenderness! Is there a man on earth who could have done it?” His voice rose, not theatrically, but as if the memory itself struck him. “Yes, I found myself, by insensible degrees, sincerely fond of her; and the happiest hours of my life were what I spent with her when I felt my intentions strictly honourable, and my feelings blameless. Even then, however, when fully determined on paying my addresses to her, I allowed myself most improperly to put off, from day to day, the moment of doing it—from an unwillingness to enter into an engagement while my circumstances were so greatly embarrassed. I will not reason here—nor will I stop for you to expatiate on the absurdity, and the worse than absurdity, of scrupling to engage my faith where my honour was already bound. The event has proved that I was a cunning fool, providing with great circumspection for a possible opportunity of making myself contemptible and wretched for ever.

“At last, however, my resolution was taken; and I had determined, as soon as I could engage her alone, to justify the attentions I had so invariably paid her, and openly assure her of an affection I had already taken such pains to display. But in the interim—in the interim of the very few hours that were to pass before I could have an opportunity of speaking with her in private—a circumstance occurred—an unlucky circumstance—to ruin all my resolution, and with it all my comfort. A discovery took place—”

He stopped, looked down. The pause was heavy as a lidded pot set back from the fire.

“Mrs. Smith had somehow or other been informed—I imagine by some distant relation whose interest it was to deprive me of her favour—of an affair, a connection—but I need not explain myself farther,” he added, lifting his eyes to Elinor with a colour rising in his face, an enquiring look. “Your particular intimacy—you have probably heard the whole story long ago.”

“I have,” Elinor returned; she coloured too, but the blush had no softness in it. It was the heat of offended propriety. “I have heard it all. And how you will explain away any part of your guilt in that dreadful business, I confess is beyond my comprehension.”

“Remember,” Willoughby cried, “from whom you received the account. Could it be an impartial one? I acknowledge her situation and her character ought to have been respected by me. I do not mean to justify myself; but at the same time I cannot leave you to suppose that I have nothing to urge—that because she was injured she was irreproachable, and because I was a libertine, she must be a saint. If the violence of her passions, the weakness of her understanding—” He checked himself, as if the very sound of his own defence sickened him. “I do not mean, however, to defend myself. Her affection for me deserved better treatment; and I often, with great self-reproach, recall the tenderness which, for a very short time, had the power of creating any return. I wish—I heartily wish it had never been. But I have injured more than herself; and I have injured one, whose affection for me (may I say it?) was scarcely less warm than hers; and whose mind—Oh! how infinitely superior!”

Elinor would not allow him to gild his wrongs with praise of Marianne.

“Your indifference, however, towards that unfortunate girl—I must say it, unpleasant as the discussion may be—your indifference is no apology for your cruel neglect of her. Do not think yourself excused by any weakness, any natural defect of understanding on her side, in the wanton cruelty so evident on yours. You must have known, that while you were enjoying yourself in Devonshire pursuing fresh schemes, always gay, always happy, she was reduced to the extremest indigence.”

“But, upon my soul, I did not know it,” he replied, warmth returning at once, less like anger than astonishment. “I did not recollect that I had omitted to give her my direction; and common sense might have told her how to find it out.”

“Well, sir—and what said Mrs. Smith?”

“She taxed me with the offence at once, and my confusion may be guessed. The purity of her life, the formality of her notions, her ignorance of the world—everything was against me. The matter itself I could not deny, and vain was every endeavour to soften it. She was previously disposed, I believe, to doubt the morality of my conduct in general, and was moreover discontented with the very little attention—the very little portion of my time—that I had bestowed on her in my present visit. In short, it ended in a total breach.

“By one measure I might have saved myself. In the height of her morality—good woman!—she offered to forgive the past, if I would marry Eliza. That could not be—and I was formally dismissed from her favour and her house. The night following this affair—I was to go the next morning—was spent by me in deliberating on what my future conduct should be. The struggle was great—but it ended too soon. My affection for Marianne, my thorough conviction of her attachment to me—it was all insufficient to outweigh that dread of poverty, or get the better of those false ideas of the necessity of riches which I was naturally inclined to feel, and expensive society had increased. I had reason to believe myself secure of my present wife, if I chose to address her; and I persuaded myself to think that nothing else in common prudence remained for me to do.

“A heavy scene, however, awaited me before I could leave Devonshire. I was engaged to dine with you on that very day; some apology therefore was necessary for my breaking the engagement. But whether I should write this apology, or deliver it in person, was a point of long debate. To see Marianne, I felt, would be dreadful; and I even doubted whether I could see her again and keep to my resolution. In that point, however, I undervalued my own magnanimity, as the event declared; for I went—I saw her—and saw her miserable—and left her miserable—and left her hoping never to see her again.”

“Why did you call, Mr. Willoughby?” Elinor asked, reproach sharp in her voice. “A note would have answered every purpose. Why was it necessary to call?”

“It was necessary to my own pride. I could not bear to leave the country in a manner that might lead you, or the rest of the neighbourhood, to suspect any part of what had really passed between Mrs. Smith and myself—and I resolved therefore on calling at the cottage, in my way to Honiton. The sight of your dear sister, however, was really dreadful; and, to heighten the matter, I found her alone. You were all gone, I do not know where. I had left her only the evening before, so fully, so firmly resolved within myself on doing right! A few hours were to have engaged her to me for ever; and I remember how happy—how gay—were my spirits, as I walked from the cottage to Allenham, satisfied with myself, delighted with every body!

“But in this—our last interview of friendship—I approached her with a sense of guilt that almost took from me the power of dissembling. Her sorrow—her disappointment—her deep regret, when I told her I was obliged to leave Devonshire so immediately—I never shall forget it—united too with such reliance, such confidence in me! Oh, God! what a hard-hearted rascal I was!”

Silence fell again. From somewhere beyond the room came a low domestic murmur—water poured, perhaps, or a pan set down carefully—those small household noises that go on, faithful and indifferent, even when a heart is breaking upstairs. Elinor spoke first.

“Did you tell her that you should soon return?”

“I do not know what I told her,” he answered, impatient with his own memory; “less than was due to the past, beyond a doubt, and in all likelihood much more than was justified by the future. I cannot think of it—it won’t do. Then came your dear mother to torture me farther, with all her kindness and confidence. Thank Heaven! it did torture me. I was miserable. Miss Dashwood, you cannot have an idea of the comfort it gives me to look back on my own misery. I owe such a grudge to myself for the stupid, rascally folly of my own heart, that all my past sufferings under it are only triumph and exultation to me now.

“Well. I went; left all that I loved; and went to those to whom, at best, I was only indifferent. My journey to town—travelling with my own horses, and therefore so tediously—no creature to speak to—my own reflections so cheerful—when I looked forward everything so inviting—when I looked back at Barton, the picture so soothing—oh, it was a blessed journey!”

He stopped as if the irony choked him. Elinor, whose pity had begun to stir and whose patience still kept watch beside it, could not allow him to wander.

“Well, sir,” she said, “and this is all?”

“All!—No. Have you forgot what passed in town? That infamous letter? Did she show it you?”

“Yes. I saw every note that passed.”

“When the first of hers reached me—(as it immediately did, for I was in town the whole time)—what I felt is—in the common phrase—not to be expressed; in a more simple one—perhaps too simple to raise any emotion—my feelings were very, very painful. Every line, every word was—in the hackneyed metaphor which their dear writer, were she here, would forbid—a dagger to my heart. To know that Marianne was in town was—in the same language—a thunderbolt. Thunderbolts and daggers!—what a reproof would she have given me! Her taste, her opinions—I believe they are better known to me than my own—and I am sure they are dearer.”

The tenderness in his tone—tenderness made insolent by circumstance—struck at Elinor’s sense of what was due.

“This is not right, Mr. Willoughby. Remember that you are married. Relate only what in your conscience you think necessary for me to hear.”

He bowed his head a little, as if the rebuke were deserved and yet could not be borne.

“Marianne’s note, by assuring me that I was still as dear to her as in former days—that in spite of the many, many weeks we had been separated, she was as constant in her own feelings, and as full of faith in the constancy of mine as ever—awakened all my remorse. I say awakened, because time and London, business and dissipation, had in some measure quieted it; and I had been growing a fine hardened villain, fancying myself indifferent to her, and choosing to fancy that she too must have become indifferent to me; talking to myself of our past attachment as a mere idle trifling business, shrugging up my shoulders in proof of its being so, and silencing every reproach, overcoming every scruple, by secretly saying now and then, ‘I shall be heartily glad to hear she is well married.’

“But this note made me know myself better. I felt she was infinitely dearer to me than any other woman in the world—and that I was using her infamously. But everything was then just settled between Miss Grey and me. To retreat was impossible. All that I had to do was to avoid you both. I sent no answer to Marianne, intending by that to preserve myself from her farther notice; and for some time I was even determined not to call in Berkeley Street—but at last, judging it wiser to affect the air of a cool, common acquaintance than anything else, I watched you all safely out of the house one morning, and left my name.”

Elinor’s head lifted at that, indignation gathering itself like a tightened knot.

“Watched us out of the house!”

“Even so. You would be surprised to hear how often I watched you—how often I was on the point of falling in with you. I have entered many a shop to avoid your sight, as the carriage drove by. Lodging as I did in Bond Street, there was hardly a day in which I did not catch a glimpse of one or other of you; and nothing but the most constant watchfulness on my side—a most invariably prevailing desire to keep out of your sight—could have separated us so long. I avoided the Middletons as much as possible, as well as everybody else likely to prove an acquaintance in common.

“Not aware of their being in town, however, I blundered on Sir John, I believe, the first day of his coming, and the day after I had called at Mrs. Jennings’s. He asked me to a party—a dance at his house in the evening. Had he not told me, as an inducement, that you and your sister were to be there, I should have felt it too certain a thing to trust myself near him.

“The next morning brought another short note from Marianne—still affectionate, open, artless, confiding—everything that could make my conduct most hateful. I could not answer it. I tried—but could not frame a sentence. But I thought of her, I believe, every moment of the day. If you can pity me, Miss Dashwood, pity my situation as it was then. With my head and heart full of your sister, I was forced to play the happy lover to another woman! Those three or four weeks were worse than all.

“Well—at last, as I need not tell you, you were forced on me; and what a sweet figure I cut! what an evening of agony it was! Marianne—beautiful as an angel on one side—calling me Willoughby in such a tone! Oh, God! holding out her hand to me, asking me for an explanation, with those bewitching eyes fixed in such speaking solicitude on my face! and Sophia, jealous as the devil on the other hand, looking all that was—Well, it does not signify; it is over now.

“Such an evening! I ran away from you all as soon as I could; but not before I had seen Marianne’s sweet face as white as death. That was the last—last look I ever had of her; the last manner in which she appeared to me. It was a horrid sight! Yet when I thought of her to-day as really dying, it was a kind of comfort to me to imagine that I knew exactly how she would appear to those who saw her last in this world. She was before me—constantly before me—as I travelled, in the same look and hue.”

For a little while, both were held silent by the weight of what could not be altered. The house, alive around them, continued its subdued music: somewhere, a distant voice lowered itself; somewhere, a coal shifted in a grate. Willoughby shook himself, as if time were a thing he had stolen and must now repay.

“Well—let me make haste and be gone. Your sister is certainly better—certainly out of danger?”

“We are assured of it.”

“Your poor mother, too!—doting on Marianne.”

“But the letter, Mr. Willoughby,” Elinor said, bringing him back with a steadiness that was almost stern kindness. “Your own letter. Have you anything to say about that?”

“Yes—yes—that in particular. Your sister wrote to me again, you know, the very next morning. You saw what she said. I was breakfasting at the Ellisons—and her letter, with some others, was brought to me there from my lodgings. It happened to catch Sophia’s eye before it caught mine—and its size, the elegance of the paper, the hand-writing altogether, immediately gave her a suspicion. Some vague report had reached her before of my attachment to some young lady in Devonshire; and what had passed within her observation the preceding evening had marked who the young lady was, and made her more jealous than ever.

“Affecting that air of playfulness, therefore, which is delightful in a woman one loves, she opened the letter directly—and read its contents. She was well paid for her impudence. She read what made her wretched. Her wretchedness I could have borne; but her passion—her malice—at all events it must be appeased. And, in short—what do you think of my wife’s style of letter-writing? Delicate—tender—truly feminine—was it not?”

“Your wife!” Elinor cried. “The letter was in your own hand-writing.”

“Yes; but I had only the credit of servilely copying such sentences as I was ashamed to put my name to. The original was all her own—her own happy thoughts and gentle diction. But what could I do? We were engaged; everything in preparation; the day almost fixed—but I am talking like a fool. Preparation! Day! In honest words, her money was necessary to me; and in a situation like mine, anything was to be done to prevent a rupture. And after all, what did it signify to my character in the opinion of Marianne and her friends, in what language my answer was couched? It must have been only to one end. My business was to declare myself a scoundrel—and whether I did it with a bow or a bluster was of little importance.

“‘I am ruined for ever in their opinion,’ said I to myself. ‘I am shut out for ever from their society. They already think me an unprincipled fellow; this letter will only make them think me a blackguard one.’ Such were my reasonings, as, in a sort of desperate carelessness, I copied my wife’s words, and parted with the last relics of Marianne. Her three notes—unluckily they were all in my pocketbook, or I should have denied their existence, and hoarded them for ever—I was forced to put them up, and could not even kiss them. And the lock of hair—that too I had always carried about me in the same pocket-book, which was now searched by Madam with the most ingratiating virulence—the dear lock— all—every memento—was torn from me.”

Elinor stood where he had left her, as though the air still carried the imprint of his voice. Even when the last wheel of his carriage had thinned into silence down the avenue—first a rumble, then a soft diminishing hiss upon gravel, then nothing but the house’s own breathing—she could not move with ease. Her thoughts came like dishes set down in haste upon a crowded table: some scalding, some bitter, some too sweet to bear. The prevailing flavour was sadness.

Willoughby—Willoughby, whom, half an hour earlier, she had held in her mind as unworthy of any feeling but abhorrence—had contrived, by the very sufferings that sprang from his faults, to draw from her a reluctant pity. It was not pity proportioned to his deserts, she knew; it was pity measured against his wishes, his visible regret, his still-ardent attachment to Marianne—an attachment that had no right to live and yet lived, like a stubborn plant kept green by a careless hand.

She reproached herself for it. What merit was there in a countenance the world called attractive, in an open affectionate manner that seemed to promise warmth and honesty as readily as a well-laid hearth promises comfort? These were gifts, not virtues. And yet, as the house’s smaller sounds returned to her—footsteps on the landing, the faint clink of a spoon somewhere below, the steady tick that the clock insisted on keeping—she felt his influence linger. It would be long, long before it faded.

At last she went back to Marianne.

She found her sister just stirring from sleep, that long, sweet, restoring sleep which had been prayed for with such desperation that its arrival felt like a miracle accepted with trembling hands. Marianne’s lashes fluttered; colour, though faint, had returned enough to soften the sharpness of her face. Elinor’s heart, already full, swelled painfully at the sight. Everything pressed upon her at once—the past and its mistakes, the present and its fragile safety, the future and all it might yet demand; Willoughby’s sudden visit; Marianne’s recovery; their mother’s expected arrival. Her spirits were in such agitation that fatigue could not touch her; she feared only that her own countenance might betray too much to Marianne’s newly awakened eyes.

That fear had scarcely time to settle, for within half an hour of Willoughby’s departure, another carriage announced itself. The sound came first as a distant, urgent drumming, then rose and thickened as it approached, scattering the quiet of Cleveland like birds startled from a hedge. Elinor, catching the meaning before any servant could speak, ran downstairs. She reached the outer door just as it opened, just in time to receive her mother into her arms.

Mrs. Dashwood had been driven toward the house in such terror that she had almost convinced herself Marianne was already lost. There was no voice left in her for questions—no voice even for Elinor. Her breath came in broken pulls, as if each was fought for. Elinor did not wait for greeting or inquiry; she gave the relief at once, as one might press a cup of warm broth into shaking hands.

“Marianne is better.”

It was enough. Her mother took the words as the thirsty take water, with all her natural warmth and a force that undid her. In the same instant she was as overwhelmed by happiness as she had been by fear. Between Elinor and their friend she was supported into the drawing-room, shedding tears of joy and still unable to speak, embracing Elinor again and again; and each time she turned, she pressed Colonel Brandon’s hand with a look that said gratitude and recognition—recognition that he too had suffered, that his heart had been bound to theirs in this danger.

He returned that look with silence, deeper even than hers. The room itself seemed hushed in deference to it; Elinor noticed, as if for the first time, how the house sounded when a strong feeling ruled it—how even the crackle of the fire and the faint stir of curtains seemed to soften.

As soon as Mrs. Dashwood could command herself, her first desire was to see Marianne; and in two minutes she was beside her child. The meeting was wordless at first—kiss, touch, that desperate laying of a palm against a forehead as if warmth could be measured and life confirmed by the skin. Marianne, made dearer by absence, unhappiness, and danger, looked up with a weak, grateful calm that stabbed Elinor with tenderness. Elinor’s joy, in witnessing what each felt, was checked only by the fear that such emotion might steal Marianne’s strength and rob her of further rest.

But Mrs. Dashwood could be prudent when a child’s life depended upon it. She learned quiet with the same passion she had spent on despair. Marianne, satisfied merely to know her mother was near, and conscious of being too weak for talk, submitted readily to the silence and stillness that every nurse around her prescribed.

Mrs. Dashwood would sit up all night. Elinor, urged by her mother’s entreaty and by reason itself, went at last to bed; but rest would not come. A night entirely sleepless, and many hours of wearing anxiety, ought to have made sleep a mercy easily accepted; instead, her spirits were irritated into wakefulness. The house at night offered a different music—wind against panes, the sigh of a settling board, a distant cough, then the soft tread of someone pacing; but none of it quieted her.

Willoughby—poor Willoughby, as she now allowed herself to name him—would not leave her thoughts. She could not but have heard his vindication, and now she blamed herself for judging him so harshly, and now acquitted herself again. Yet the promise she had given—of relating his explanation to Marianne—lay upon her like a weight. She dreaded it: dreaded the telling, dreaded the effect; doubted whether Marianne, once armed with such knowledge, could ever give her whole heart elsewhere. For a moment—only a moment—she wished him a widower; and the instant that wish formed, she felt ashamed, remembering Colonel Brandon: his constancy, his sufferings borne in quiet, his honour that asked nothing and yet gave everything. To him, far more than to his rival, any reward from Marianne’s future was due. Elinor wished anything rather than the death of Mrs. Willoughby.

Colonel Brandon’s errand to Barton had been softened, in its effect upon Mrs. Dashwood, by her own previous alarm. Her uneasiness for Marianne had already driven her to determine, even before his arrival, to set out for Cleveland that very day without waiting for further intelligence; she had gone so far as to make arrangements that the Careys were expected every moment to fetch Margaret away, her mother unwilling to take the child where there might be infection.

Marianne mended with each day. Strength returned, at first in barely perceptible degrees—the steadier colour on her cheek, the lessening of that harsh, tight cough, the ability to take nourishment without exhaustion. The household’s sounds changed with her improvement: fewer hurried whispers, fewer doors closed in haste, more ordinary movements permitted. Mrs. Dashwood’s face grew brilliant with cheerfulness; her spirits rose so high that she repeatedly declared herself one of the happiest women in the world.

Elinor heard these declarations and watched their proofs with a strange inward question. Did her mother ever recollect Edward? But Mrs. Dashwood, trusting to the temperate account of Elinor’s own disappointment that had been sent to her, was carried away by the exuberance of her present joy. Marianne restored to her from danger—danger to which she could not help feeling her own mistaken judgement had contributed, by encouraging that unfortunate attachment—was blessing enough. Yet in Marianne’s recovery there was another source of happiness, one that Elinor had not anticipated.

It came out the first time they were alone together, when Marianne slept and the house, for once, seemed to hold its breath for them.

“At last we are alone,” Mrs. Dashwood said, taking Elinor’s hands with a fervour that would not be denied. “My Elinor—you do not yet know all my happiness. Colonel Brandon loves Marianne. He has told me so himself.”

Elinor, moved by turns to pleasure and pain, surprised and yet not surprised, listened in silence.

“You are never like me, dear Elinor, or I should wonder at your composure now. Had I sat down to wish for any possible good to my family, I should have fixed on Colonel Brandon’s marrying one of you as the object most desirable. And I believe Marianne will be the most happy with him of the two.”

Elinor was half inclined to ask why—because she was certain no reason, founded on an impartial comparison of age, character, and feelings, could be given—but her mother’s imagination always ran ahead on any subject that interested her. Elinor passed it off with a smile.

“He opened his whole heart to me yesterday as we travelled,” Mrs. Dashwood continued. “It came out quite unawares, quite undesignedly. I— you may well believe—could talk of nothing but my child. He could not conceal his distress. I saw it equalled my own, and he perhaps—thinking that mere friendship, as the world now goes, would not justify so warm a sympathy—or rather not thinking at all, I suppose—giving way to irresistible feelings, made me acquainted with his earnest, tender, constant affection for Marianne. He has loved her, my Elinor, ever since the first moment of seeing her.”

Elinor could hear, beneath the statement, the shaping hand of her mother’s fancy—not in the Colonel’s language, perhaps, but in the way Mrs. Dashwood embroidered it, as if to make it more delightful to taste.

“His regard for her,” Mrs. Dashwood went on, “infinitely surpassing anything that Willoughby ever felt—or feigned—as much more warm as more sincere or constant, whichever we are to call it, has subsisted through all the knowledge of dear Marianne’s unhappy prepossession for that worthless young man! And without selfishness—without encouraging a hope! Could he have seen her happy with another—such a noble mind! such openness, such sincerity! no one can be deceived in him.”

“Colonel Brandon’s character,” said Elinor, steady and sincere, “as an excellent man, is well established.”

“I know it is,” her mother replied more seriously, “or after such a warning, I should be the last to encourage such affection, or even to be pleased by it. But his coming for me as he did—with such active, such ready friendship—is enough to prove him one of the worthiest of men.”

“His character,” Elinor answered, “does not rest on one act of kindness, to which his affection for Marianne—were humanity out of the case—would have prompted him. To Mrs. Jennings, to the Middletons, he has been long and intimately known; they equally love and respect him; and even my own knowledge of him, though lately acquired, is very considerable; and so highly do I value and esteem him, that if Marianne can be happy with him, I shall be as ready as yourself to think our connection the greatest blessing to us in the world. What answer did you give him? Did you allow him to hope?”

“Oh, my love, I could not then talk of hope to him—or to myself. Marianne might at that moment be dying. But he did not ask for hope or encouragement. His was an involuntary confidence, an irrepressible effusion to a soothing friend, not an application to a parent. Yet after a time I did say—for at first I was quite overcome—that if she lived, as I trusted she might, my greatest happiness would lie in promoting their marriage; and since our arrival, since our delightful security, I have repeated it to him more fully, have given him every encouragement in my power. Time—a very little time, I tell him—will do everything. Marianne’s heart is not to be wasted for ever on such a man as Willoughby. His own merits must soon secure it.”

“To judge from the Colonel’s spirits, however,” Elinor observed, “you have not yet made him equally sanguine.”

“No. He thinks Marianne’s affection too deeply rooted for any change in it under a great length of time; and even supposing her heart again free, he is too diffident of himself to believe that, with such a difference of age and disposition, he could ever attach her. There, however, he is quite mistaken. His age is only so much beyond hers as to be an advantage—to make his character and principles fixed; and his disposition, I am well convinced, is exactly the very one to make your sister happy. And his person—his manners too—are all in his favour. My partiality does not blind me. He certainly is not so handsome as Willoughby; but at the same time, there is something much more pleasing in his countenance. There was always a something—if you remember—in Willoughby’s eyes at times, which I did not like.”

Elinor could not remember it; but her mother, without pausing for assent, continued:

“And his manners, the Colonel’s manners are not only more pleasing to me than Willoughby’s ever were, but they are of a kind I well know to be more solidly attaching to Marianne—their gentleness, their genuine attention to other people, and their manly unstudied simplicity. That is much more accordant with her real disposition than the liveliness—often artificial, and often ill-timed—of the other. I am very sure myself, that had Willoughby turned out as really amiable as he has proved himself the contrary, Marianne would yet never have been so happy with him as she will be with Colonel Brandon.”

She paused only long enough to let the picture settle.

“At Delaford, she will be within an easy distance of me,” Mrs. Dashwood added, the practical dream already laying out rooms and roads. “Even if I remain at Barton; and in all probability—for I hear it is a large village—indeed there certainly must be some small house or cottage close by, that would suit us quite as well as our present situation.”

Poor Elinor—there was the familiar current beneath the words, a new scheme carrying them all toward Delaford. But her spirit, quiet as it was, remained stubborn.

“His fortune too!” Mrs. Dashwood continued, as if tasting yet another agreeable possibility. “For at my time of life you know, everybody cares about that; and though I neither know nor desire to know what it really is, I am sure it must be a good one.”

At that moment they were interrupted by the entrance of a third person. Elinor withdrew, glad of solitude, to turn everything over in private: to wish success with her whole heart to a man she esteemed—and yet, even in wishing it, to feel an involuntary pang for Willoughby.

Marianne’s illness, though it had wasted her in a way that frightened even those accustomed to see the young recover, had not held long enough to make convalescence a laborious season. Youth, a constitution naturally sound, and—more potent than either—the steady nearness of her mother, drew her back by degrees that were almost daily measurable. Within four days of Mrs. Dashwood’s arrival, Marianne could be moved from the sick-room into Mrs. Palmer’s dressing-room, where the air was kinder, the light less cruel, and the household’s small, ordinary sounds—water poured, linen shaken, a footfall softened at the threshold—could re-enter her life without assaulting it.

From that refuge she sent, by her own earnest request, for Colonel Brandon. She was impatient, she said, to thank him for fetching her mother; and to Marianne, gratitude was not a coin to be paid at leisure, but a dish best served hot, while the heart still tasted the need that had called it forth.

When he came in, the room altered with him. He had the look of a man who had braced himself for a sight and yet had not been braced enough. At the first view of her changed face—the hollowness beneath the eye, the thinness over cheekbone, the helpless angle of her reclined posture—his composure wavered like a flame in a draught. Marianne, pale but resolved, held out her hand at once, as if to spare him ceremony and herself delay. He took it, and for a moment seemed unable to trust his voice.

Elinor, watching with that quiet exactness which missed neither word nor silence, believed his emotion rose from more than the affection he had long felt and the consciousness that others guessed it. There was a deeper tide in him, pulled by an old memory. That resemblance between Marianne and the unhappy Eliza—once acknowledged, often feared—was now sharpened by sickness into a cruel fidelity. The very signs of weakness that made Marianne pitiable to a stranger made her dangerous to his recollection: the sickly skin, the shadowed eyes, the languor that forced her to receive attentions instead of offering them. His melancholy look and shifting colour spoke of scenes revived against his will.

Mrs. Dashwood saw differently. Her mind, governed by feeling rather than inference, read nothing in the Colonel’s behaviour but the simplest tenderness and alarm; and in Marianne’s softened manner—those earnest thanks, that gentle steadiness—she fancied she could already glimpse something dawning beyond obligation.

A day or two more, and Marianne gathered strength as if each twelve hours had brought its own small harvest. With that improvement, Mrs. Dashwood—pressed at once by necessity and longing—began to speak of returning to Barton. On her decision hung the movements of their friends. Mrs. Jennings, generous to the point of being restless when she could not be useful, would not leave Cleveland while the Dashwoods remained; and Colonel Brandon, drawn into their circle by care as much as by affection, was soon urged—by both mother and benefactress—to consider his own staying as fixed, if not as indispensable.

Then came those exchanges of kindness that, like household cookery, depend upon the graceful give-and-take of offering and accepting. At Mrs. Jennings’s and the Colonel’s united request, Mrs. Dashwood was at last persuaded to take his carriage for the journey, that Marianne might travel with less jar and fatigue. And in return—pressed again by invitation, and pleased to be included as more than a provider of conveniences—Colonel Brandon promised to redeem that service by visiting them at the cottage within a few weeks. Mrs. Jennings, whose hospitable zeal seemed capable of feeding not only her own appetites but those of everyone around her, engaged him with a warmth that made refusal impossible.

The day of separation arrived, and with it the peculiar sorrow of leaving a place that had been, in one breath, a theatre of danger and a scene of salvation. Marianne’s leave-taking of Mrs. Jennings was long and particular: grateful, respectful, tender to the edge of self-reproach, as if she tasted now—with a sharpened palate for kindness—how much she had once overlooked. Her farewell to Colonel Brandon held the cordiality of a friend, and yet Elinor could not help noticing that the Colonel, in assisting her, seemed anxious she should occupy at least half the carriage, as though space itself could be offered in protection.

Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor followed; and then Mrs. Jennings and the Colonel were left together to talk of the travellers and feel the dullness that followed usefulness. Soon Mrs. Jennings was called away to her chaise, to find comfort—of a kind suited to her—among the gossip of her maid; and Colonel Brandon went, solitary, toward Delaford, with the road before him sounding, perhaps, louder than it ought in an empty day.

The Dashwoods were two days upon the road. Marianne bore both without essential fatigue. Everything that love and vigilance could contrive was done for her: cushions arranged, stops shortened, a shawl tucked closer at the first change in wind. Even the very sound within the carriage was managed for her comfort; her mother’s voice kept low and even, Elinor’s movements measured, as though quiet were a broth simmered patiently to restore a weakened body. In those small attentions each watcher found reward—in Marianne’s bodily ease, and, more precious still to Elinor, in her calmness of spirit.

For Elinor, who had witnessed week after week the same suffering—anguish that had neither courage to speak nor fortitude to conceal—this new composure was like seeing a storm-damaged garden begin, at last, to put out clean shoots. If it sprang from serious reflection, as Elinor hoped, it must lead in time to content and cheerfulness.

As they drew near Barton, however, and the familiar fields began to appear—each hedge and tree carrying its own private sting—Marianne grew silent. She turned her face from their notice and sat gazing out, intent as if listening not only with her eyes but with her whole skin to what the landscape remembered. When Elinor helped her down at the cottage door, she saw that Marianne had been crying; yet the tears had fallen without display, and that unobtrusiveness made them seem not less sorrowful but more to be respected.

Within, Marianne’s manner showed a mind forcing itself into new discipline. No sooner had they entered their common sitting-room than she looked around it with a resolute firmness, as if determined to accustom herself at once to every object that might summon Willoughby. She spoke little; but each sentence aimed at cheerfulness, and if a sigh escaped, it did not pass without the atonement of a smile—like someone tasting a bitter infusion and insisting, by sheer will, on finding the warmth it still carried.

After dinner she declared she would try her pianoforte. She went to it; but the music that lay uppermost was an opera Willoughby had procured for her, containing favourite duets, and upon the outer leaf her own name written in his hand. That would not do. She shook her head, put it aside, and ran her fingers over the keys for a minute. The notes—so quick to fill a room, so impossible to call back once struck—seemed to startle her more than silence could. She complained of feebleness in her hands and closed the instrument; yet even in withdrawing she declared, firmly, that she would practise much in future.

The next morning brought no abatement of these signs. Strengthened by rest, mind and body together, she spoke with more genuine spirit. She anticipated Margaret’s return and talked of the dear family party that would then be restored—of mutual pursuits and cheerful society—as the only happiness worth wishing.

“When the weather is settled, and I have recovered my strength,” she said, “we will take long walks every day. We will walk to the farm at the edge of the down, and see how the children go on; we will walk to Sir John’s new plantations at Barton Cross, and the Abbeyland; and we will go often to the old ruins of the Priory, and try to trace the foundations as far as we are told they once reached. I know we shall be happy. I know the summer will pass happily away. I mean never to be later in rising than six, and from that time till dinner I shall divide every moment between music and reading. I have formed my plan, and am determined to enter on a course of serious study. Our own library is too well known to me to be resorted to for anything beyond mere amusement. But there are many works worth reading at the Park; and there are others of more modern production which I know I can borrow of Colonel Brandon. By reading only six hours a day, I shall gain, in the course of a twelve-month, a great deal of instruction which I now feel myself to want.”

Elinor honoured her for a plan so nobly rooted; yet she could not help a slight smile at seeing the same eager fancy which had lately driven Marianne toward languid indolence and selfish repining now at work in threatening excess in a scheme of rational employment and virtuous control. The smile faded into a sigh when she remembered her promise to Willoughby—still unfulfilled—and feared that what she must communicate might unsettle Marianne again and overturn this fair prospect of busy tranquillity. She resolved, therefore, to wait until Marianne’s health was more secure.

It was a resolution made only to be broken.

Marianne had been two or three days at home before the weather turned fine enough for an invalid to venture out; but at last there came a morning soft and genial, with air that felt newly washed, and a quietness in the lane as if the world itself were trying to tread lightly. It tempted Marianne’s longing and justified her mother’s confidence. Leaning on Elinor’s arm, she was permitted to walk as long as she could without fatigue in the lane before the house.

They moved slowly, for Marianne’s weakness made the very act of walking—so natural once, so taken for granted—an experiment. They had not gone far beyond the cottage, only far enough to command a full view of the hill behind, when Marianne paused. Her eyes turned toward it, and she spoke with a calm that was more affecting than any passion.

“There—exactly there,” she said, pointing to the projecting mound, “there I fell; and there I first saw Willoughby.”

Her voice sank on his name; then, as if she refused to let that sinking be the last motion of her spirit, she added, “I am thankful to find that I can look with so little pain on the spot. Shall we ever talk on that subject, Elinor?” It was said with hesitation. “Or will it be wrong? I can talk of it now, I hope, as I ought to do.”

Elinor, tender and steady, invited her to speak openly.

“As for regret,” Marianne continued, “I have done with that, as far as he is concerned. I do not mean to talk to you of what my feelings have been for him, but what they are now. At present, if I could be satisfied on one point—if I could be allowed to think that he was not always acting a part, not always deceiving me; but above all, if I could be assured that he never was so very wicked as my fears have sometimes fancied him, since the story of that unfortunate girl—”

She stopped. Elinor stored the words, grateful for what they promised.

“If you could be assured of that,” Elinor said, “you think you should be easy.”

“Yes. My peace of mind is doubly involved in it; for not only is it horrible to suspect a person, who has been what he has been to me, of such designs—but what must it make me appear to myself? What, in a situation like mine, but a most shamefully unguarded affection could expose me to—”

“How then,” Elinor asked, “would you account for his behaviour?”

“I would suppose him—Oh, how gladly would I suppose him—only fickle, very, very fickle.”

Elinor said no more. Within herself she debated whether to begin her narration at once or postpone it until Marianne was stronger. They went on a few minutes in silence, their steps small upon the lane, the hedgerows rustling faintly with that restrained sound of rural life which heals precisely because it does not insist.

“I am not wishing him too much good,” Marianne said at last, with a sigh, “when I wish his secret reflections may be no more unpleasant than my own. He will suffer enough in them.”

“Do you compare your conduct with his?” Elinor asked.

“No. I compare it with what it ought to have been; I compare it with yours.”

“Our situations have borne little resemblance.”

“They have borne more than our conduct. Do not, my dearest Elinor, let your kindness defend what I know your judgment must censure. My illness has made me think. It has given me leisure and calmness for serious recollection. Long before I was recovered enough to talk, I was perfectly able to reflect.

“I considered the past: I saw in my own behaviour, since the beginning of our acquaintance with him last autumn, nothing but a series of imprudence towards myself, and want of kindness to others. I saw that my own feelings had prepared my sufferings, and that my want of fortitude under them had almost led me to the grave.
