“My illness, I well knew, had been entirely brought on by myself—by such negligence of my own health as I had felt even at the time to be wrong. Had I died, it would have been self-destruction. I did not know my danger till the danger was removed; but with such feelings as these reflections gave me, I wonder at my recovery—wonder that the very eagerness of my desire to live, to have time for atonement to my God, and to you all, did not kill me at once.

“Had I died, in what peculiar misery should I have left you—my nurse, my friend, my sister! You, who had seen all the fretful selfishness of my latter days; who had known all the murmurings of my heart! How should I have lived in your remembrance! My mother too! How could you have consoled her! I cannot express my own abhorrence of myself. Whenever I looked towards the past, I saw some duty neglected, or some failing indulged. Everybody seemed injured by me.

“The kindness—the unceasing kindness—of Mrs. Jennings I had repaid with ungrateful contempt. To the Middletons, to the Palmers, the Steeles—to every common acquaintance even—I had been insolent and unjust; with a heart hardened against their merits, and a temper irritated by their very attention. To John, to Fanny—yes, even to them, little as they deserve—I had given less than their due.

“But you—you above all, above my mother—had been wronged by me. I, and only I, knew your heart and its sorrows; yet to what did it influence me? Not to any compassion that could benefit you or myself. Your example was before me; but to what avail? Was I more considerate of you and your comfort? Did I imitate your forbearance, or lessen your restraints by taking any part in those offices of general complaisance or particular gratitude which you had hitherto been left to discharge alone? No; not less when I knew you to be unhappy, than when I believed you at ease, did I turn away from every exertion of duty or friendship—scarcely allowing sorrow to exist but with me, regretting only that heart which had deserted and wronged me, and leaving you, for whom I professed an unbounded affection, to be miserable for my sake.”

The rapid current of her self-reproach ceased. Elinor, longing to soothe yet too honest for flattery, gave her at once the praise her frankness and contrition deserved, and the support she needed to bear them.

Marianne pressed her hand. “You are very good,” she said. “The future must be my proof. I have laid down my plan; and if I am capable of adhering to it, my feelings shall be governed and my temper improved. They shall no longer worry others, nor torture myself.

“I shall now live solely for my family. You, my mother, and Margaret must henceforth be all the world to me; you will share my affections entirely between you. From you—from my home—I shall never again have the smallest incitement to move; and if I do mix in other society, it will be only to show that my spirit is humbled, my heart amended, and that I can practise the civilities, the lesser duties of life, with gentleness and forbearance.

“As for Willoughby—to say that I shall soon or that I shall ever forget him would be idle. His remembrance can be overcome by no change of circumstances or opinions. But it shall be regulated; it shall be checked by religion, by reason, by constant employment.”

She paused, and then, in a voice lowered as if she feared even the air might repeat it back too loudly, added, “If I could but know his heart, everything would become easy.”

Elinor had been turning the question of propriety over and over, like a careful cook uncertain when to add the sharp ingredient that will either perfect a dish or spoil it. Reflection did not bring her nearer to decision; and seeing that it would never do so, she understood that resolution must act where deliberation failed. Marianne’s last words decided her. She led, gently but steadily, to the facts.

As she related—simply and honestly—the chief points of Willoughby’s apology, she prepared Marianne with caution; she did justice to his repentance, and softened only those protestations of present regard which might have been more dangerous than consoling. Marianne did not interrupt with a single word. She trembled; her eyes fixed on the ground; her lips turned whiter than sickness had left them. Questions rose in her like breath held too long, yet she did not dare to shape one. She caught every syllable with a panting eagerness, her hand—without her knowing it—closing hard upon Elinor’s, while tears slid down her cheeks.

Elinor, fearing fatigue, drew her quietly toward home. Until they reached the cottage door she spoke of nothing but Willoughby and the conversation she had had with him, and, guessing the curiosity that Marianne would not voice, she was minute in every particular of speech and look where minuteness could be safely indulged—those small turns of expression, those pauses that carry meaning no dominant vocabulary can fully command.

Once inside, Marianne kissed her with a gratitude that shook through her whole frame. Only two words came, just articulate through tears: “Tell mama.” Then she withdrew from Elinor and walked slowly upstairs, seeking a solitude that was not flight but effort—a private chamber in which to attempt to translate what she had heard into something her heart could bear.

Elinor did not follow. She would not disturb a retreat so reasonable. With her mind anxiously arranging what might come of this disclosure, and with a firm intent to revive the subject again should Marianne fail to do so, she turned into the parlour to fulfil her sister’s request.

Mrs. Dashwood listened to Elinor’s account of their former favourite with a heart that could not help but soften at the clearing away of part of his disgrace. She was glad that some portion of the dark was proved not so black as rumour had painted it; she was sorry for him; she even wished him a quieter life than he had earned. Yet no warmth of present pity could re-kindle what the past had burned down. Nothing could give him back to Marianne with faith unbroken, with honour whole. Nothing could sweeten the knowledge of what Marianne had endured through him, nor rinse away the stain of his conduct to Eliza. Whatever compassion might be spared for him could not, for that reason, lessen Colonel Brandon’s claims.

Had Mrs. Dashwood, like Marianne, heard the story from Willoughby’s own lips—had she been met by the tremor of his voice, the forced composure, the expressive misery of a face that can plead when words fail—her compassion might have risen higher. But Elinor neither possessed the power, nor the inclination, to rekindle in another the very agitation she had herself felt on first hearing it. Reflection had cooled her; it had not excused him, only arranged him in his true proportions. She told her mother the plain substance—facts without ornament, tenderness without seduction—because she would not have imagination season the tale into indulgence.

That evening, when the small household sat together, the cottage settled into its familiar sounds: the tick of the clock that measured their uneasy quiet; the low scrape of a chair; the wind worrying the thatch and worrying, too, the mind; and, beneath it, the domestic murmur that always returned—Margaret’s half-whispered questions, the distant clink of a dish being set away, the hearth’s soft crackle. In such ordinary music, which should have soothed them, Marianne, after long sitting with that restless, unquiet thoughtfulness that made stillness itself oppressive, spoke of him again of her own accord.

It was not without effort. Colour rose and fell in her cheek, as if the very name brought heat and then shame; her voice, when she began, wavered like a flame in a draught.

“I wish to assure you both,” said she, “that I see every thing—as you can desire me to do.”

Mrs. Dashwood would have stopped her at once with those tender interruptions by which she tried to wrap pain in gentleness; but Elinor—who truly wished to hear Marianne’s unborrowed judgment, and to know it steadied—made an eager sign that held their mother silent.

Marianne went on slowly. “It is a great relief to me—what Elinor told me this morning—I have now heard exactly what I wished to hear.” For a few moments her voice failed her altogether; only the breath came, uneven, as though the words had lodged in her throat. Then, gathering herself again with more calmness than before, she added, “I am now perfectly satisfied. I wish for no change. I never could have been happy with him, after knowing, as sooner or later I must have known, all this. I should have had no confidence, no esteem. Nothing could have done it away to my feelings.”

“I know it—I know it,” cried Mrs. Dashwood, at last unable to keep back her ardour. “Happy with a man of libertine practices!—With one who so injured the peace of the dearest of our friends, and the best of men!—No—my Marianne has not a heart to be made happy with such a man!—Her conscience, her sensitive conscience, would have felt all that the conscience of her husband ought to have felt.”

Marianne sighed, as if even agreement cost her, and repeated only, “I wish for no change.”

“You consider the matter,” said Elinor, “exactly as a good mind and a sound understanding must consider it; and I dare say you perceive, as well as myself, not only in this, but in many other circumstances, reason enough to be convinced that your marriage must have involved you in many certain troubles and disappointments, in which you would have been poorly supported by an affection, on his side, much less certain.

“Had you married, you must have been always poor. His expensiveness is acknowledged even by himself, and his whole conduct declares that self-denial is a word hardly understood by him. His demands and your inexperience together, on a small—very small income, must have brought on distresses which would not be the less grievous to you, from having been entirely unknown and unthought of before. Your sense of honour and honesty would have led you, I know, when aware of your situation, to attempt all the economy that would appear to you possible: and, perhaps, as long as your frugality retrenched only on your own comfort, you might have been suffered to practice it—but beyond that—and how little could the utmost of your single management do to stop the ruin which had begun before your marriage? Beyond that, had you endeavoured, however reasonably, to abridge his enjoyments, is it not to be feared, that instead of prevailing on feelings so selfish to consent to it, you would have lessened your own influence on his heart, and made him regret the connection which had involved him in such difficulties?”

Marianne’s lips quivered; it was not the picture of poverty that most shook her, but the blunt word Elinor had used, like a knife laid cleanly on a truth she still did not want to feel. She repeated it, as though tasting it and finding it bitter.

“Selfish?” The tone implied, do you really think him that?

“The whole of his behaviour,” replied Elinor, “from the beginning to the end of the affair, has been grounded on selfishness. It was selfishness which first made him sport with your affections; which afterwards, when his own were engaged, made him delay the confession of it, and which finally carried him from Barton. His own enjoyment, or his own ease, was, in every particular, his ruling principle.”

“It is very true,” Marianne said, and the admission seemed to hollow her voice. “My happiness never was his object.”

“At present,” continued Elinor, “he regrets what he has done. And why does he regret it?—Because he finds it has not answered towards himself. It has not made him happy. His circumstances are now unembarrassed—he suffers from no evil of that kind; and he thinks only that he has married a woman of a less amiable temper than yourself. But does it follow that had he married you, he would have been happy? The inconveniences would have been different. He would then have suffered under the pecuniary distresses which, because they are removed, he now reckons as nothing. He would have had a wife of whose temper he could make no complaint, but he would have been always necessitous—always poor; and probably would soon have learned to rank the innumerable comforts of a clear estate and good income as of far more importance, even to domestic happiness, than the mere temper of a wife.”

“I have not a doubt of it,” said Marianne; “and I have nothing to regret—nothing but my own folly.”

“Rather say your mother’s imprudence, my child,” cried Mrs. Dashwood; “she must be answerable.”

Marianne would not let her proceed; and Elinor, satisfied that each felt her own error, and unwilling to send them turning their minds over old griefs till they bruised themselves anew, resumed the first subject.

“One observation may, I think, be fairly drawn from the whole of the story—that all Willoughby’s difficulties have arisen from the first offence against virtue, in his behaviour to Eliza Williams. That crime has been the origin of every lesser one, and of all his present discontents.”

Marianne assented with a feeling that did not spend itself in words; and Mrs. Dashwood, taking fire from the thought, began to enumerate Colonel Brandon’s injuries and merits with all the warmth that friendship and design could dictate together. Yet Marianne did not look as if much of it reached her. Her attention seemed to drift, caught by the same inward current that had so long carried her away from whatever was said beside her.

In the two or three days that followed, Elinor saw, as she had expected, that Marianne did not go on gaining strength as she had done. Her resolution remained; her effort to appear easy and cheerful continued; and that was enough, for Elinor could trust time to do what argument could not. The cottage life settled itself again into a routine—quietly taken up, quietly laid down. Margaret returned, and the family were restored to their usual completeness, like a table reset after disturbance: everything in its place again, though the appetite was changed. They were not pursuing their studies with quite the vigour of their first weeks at Barton, but they planned a vigorous prosecution of them in future, as people do who mean to believe tomorrow will be steadier than today.

Elinor, however, grew impatient for tidings of Edward. Since leaving London she had heard nothing—nothing new of his plans, nothing certain even of his present abode. Letters had passed between her and her brother on Marianne’s illness; and in the first of John’s there had been this sentence: “We know nothing of our unfortunate Edward, and can make no enquiries on so prohibited a subject, but conclude him to be still at Oxford;” which was all the intelligence Edward’s own name afforded her, for in the later letters it was not even mentioned.

She was not, however, doomed to long ignorance of his measures.

One morning, their man-servant had been sent to Exeter on business; and when, waiting at table, he had answered his mistress’s questions about the errand, he added, with the careless certainty of one who thinks himself the bearer of ordinary news:

“I suppose you know, ma’am, that Mr. Ferrars is married.”

The words, dropped like a stone into still water, struck every face at once. Marianne started violently, her eyes fastened on Elinor; she saw Elinor turn pale; and then Marianne fell back in her chair in hysterics.

Mrs. Dashwood, who had looked the same way at the same instant, was shocked by what she read in Elinor’s countenance; and then, in the very next moment, equally distressed by Marianne’s condition, she scarcely knew on which child she ought to fix her care. The servant, seeing only that Miss Marianne was taken ill, had sense enough to call one of the maids; and with Mrs. Dashwood’s help they supported Marianne into the other room. The passageway carried their hurried footsteps sharply, the house’s usual hush cut through by the sudden rustle of skirts and the quick, anxious instructions—sound itself turning, for a moment, into a kind of pain.

By the time Marianne was rather better, Mrs. Dashwood left her to Margaret and the maid and returned to Elinor. Elinor, though still much disordered, had recovered enough command of herself to be beginning, in a voice that scarcely sounded like her own, to question Thomas about his intelligence. Mrs. Dashwood immediately took the trouble on herself, sparing Elinor the effort of asking what she dreaded to hear answered.

“Who told you that Mr. Ferrars was married, Thomas?”

“I see Mr. Ferrars myself, ma’am, this morning in Exeter, and his lady too—Miss Steele as was. They was stopping in a chaise at the door of the New London Inn, as I went there with a message from Sally at the Park to her brother, who is one of the post-boys. I happened to look up as I went by the chaise, and so I see directly it was the youngest Miss Steele; so I took off my hat, and she knew me and called to me, and inquired after you, ma’am, and the young ladies, especially Miss Marianne, and bid me I should give her compliments and Mr. Ferrars’s, their best compliments and service, and how sorry they was they had not time to come on and see you, but they was in a great hurry to go forwards, for they was going further down for a little while; but howsever, when they come back, they’d make sure to come and see you.”

“But did she tell you she was married, Thomas?” Mrs. Dashwood pressed, as if there were still some small hope of mistake, some misheard word to undo what had been said.

“Yes, ma’am. She smiled, and said how she had changed her name since she was in these parts. She was always a very affable and free-spoken young lady, and very civil behaved. So I made free to wish her joy.”

“Was Mr. Ferrars in the carriage with her?”

“Yes, ma’am, I just see him leaning back in it, but he did not look up;—he never was a gentleman much for talking.”

Elinor’s heart could easily account for his not putting himself forward; and Mrs. Dashwood, by the sudden fixedness of her daughter’s eyes, probably found the same explanation without being told.

“Was there no one else in the carriage?”

“No, ma’am, only they two.”

“Do you know where they came from?”

“They come straight from town, as Miss Lucy—Mrs. Ferrars told me.”

“And are they going farther westward?”

“Yes, ma’am—but not to bide long. They will soon be back again, and then they’d be sure and call here.”

Mrs. Dashwood looked at Elinor; but Elinor knew better than to expect them. She recognized the whole of Lucy in the message—the airy civility, the careful display, the triumph dressed up as kindness—and was very confident Edward would never come near them. In a low voice, meant only for her mother, she observed that they were probably going down to Mr. Pratt’s near Plymouth.

Thomas’s intelligence seemed over, yet Elinor’s face, as if compelled to drink what harmed her, still looked as though it wished to hear more.

“Did you see them off, before you came away?” asked Mrs. Dashwood.

“No, ma’am—the horses were just coming out, but I could not bide any longer; I was afraid of being late.”

“Did Mrs. Ferrars look well?”

“Yes, ma’am, she said how she was very well; and to my mind she was always a very handsome young lady—and she seemed vastly contented.”

Mrs. Dashwood could think of no other question. Thomas and the tablecloth, now alike needless, were dismissed.

Marianne sent word that she should eat nothing more. Mrs. Dashwood’s and Elinor’s appetites were equally gone—as if the very notion of food, of comfort taken in at the mouth, had become ridiculous while grief sat heavy in the chest. Margaret, with the straightforward hunger of youth and the baffled resilience of one who cannot live on sorrow, might think herself fortunate that, with all the uneasiness her sisters had lately suffered and all the reason they had had to neglect their meals, she had never yet been obliged to go without her dinner before.

When dessert and wine were arranged and then left untouched, and Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor were alone together, they remained long in a sameness of thoughtfulness and silence. The house’s ordinary sounds—clock, fire, the faint creak of settling boards—seemed suddenly too loud, as if every small domestic noise insisted on the world continuing, when all feeling demanded it stop. Mrs. Dashwood feared to hazard any remark; she ventured not to offer consolation. She now found that she had erred in relying on Elinor’s representation of herself, and concluded—justly—that everything had been softened before, to spare her mother additional unhappiness while she suffered for Marianne. She saw she had been misled by Elinor’s careful, considerate attention into thinking the attachment—which she had once understood so well—much slighter than she had been wont to believe, or than it was now proved to be. She feared that, under this persuasion, she had been unjust, inattentive—nay, almost unkind—to her Elinor; that Marianne’s affliction, because more acknowledged and more immediately before her, had too much engrossed her tenderness, and led her away to forget that in Elinor she might have a daughter suffering almost as much—certainly with less self-provocation, and greater fortitude.

Elinor discovered, at last, the cruel distinction between bracing oneself for an unhappiness and having it laid, warm and weighty, into one’s hands. She had schooled her mind for months to call Edward’s marriage to Lucy a settled certainty; yet some obstinate portion of her heart—unbidden, unreasoned—had kept a small, living hope like a covered ember: that something would intervene. A resolution of his own; a friend’s mediation; an opening elsewhere for Lucy that would draw her away as surely as a new market draws the most calculating buyer. While he remained unmarried, that ember could be tended in secret. Now, with the report of his marriage, she reproached herself for ever having allowed its heat to exist, because it made the pain sharper, more immediate, as if the news had scorched her.

It startled her, too, that it should have happened so soon—so hurriedly—before he could, as she had imagined, be in orders, and therefore before any living could be his. But the momentary surprise dissolved into something colder and clearer: Lucy’s haste was exactly of a piece with Lucy’s prudence. In securing what she wanted, she would overlook every consideration except the peril of delay. Married in town; already travelling down, as the message said, to her uncle’s. Elinor could not prevent her mind from setting the scene with the exactness of a cook who sees a kitchen she has never entered and yet knows where every pot will be hung: the turn of their future rooms at Delaford; the parsonage hearth; Lucy brisk and busy, contriving at once smart appearance and strict saving—paring, stretching, substituting—ashamed only of being suspected of it, and yet never ashamed of its intention. She saw her courting favour with Colonel Brandon, with Mrs. Jennings, with every person whose purse might be useful—sweetening each connection as one sweetens a tart fruit, for ease of swallowing.

Of Edward in that picture she could not form any steady image. Whatever she tried to see—his contentment, his regret—nothing would stay in place. Every sketch displeased her, and she turned her thoughts away as if from a dish that, once beloved, had been spoiled past appetite.

Days went by without letter or detail. She had expected some hand in London to send particulars, to confirm, to explain; but the post brought nothing that would settle her mind. Though she could not, with justice, fix blame, she found herself resenting the silence of every absent friend, as if their indolence were a personal affront. Her impatience needed action, however small, the way an anxious housekeeper needs the sound of something being done—water set to boil, knives set to work—only to bear the waiting.

“When do you write to Colonel Brandon, ma’am?” she asked one morning, with a restlessness she could not disguise.

“I wrote to him, my love, last week,” Mrs. Dashwood replied, “and rather expect to see him, than to hear from him again. I pressed his coming earnestly, and should not be surprised to see him walk in today or tomorrow, or any day.”

Something to look forward to. Something approaching along the road, like the first faint hoofbeats that reach a quiet house before the rider is seen. Colonel Brandon must know more; Colonel Brandon must, by the very steadiness of his character, bring a steadier account.

She had scarcely settled into that expectation when her eye, turning to the window, caught the figure of a man on horseback at their gate. The animal’s stamp on the hard ground, the brief jingle of tack, the crunch of gravel under the descending boot—those little sounds, so ordinary, fell on her mind with unnatural force. He stopped. For an instant she was sure it was the Colonel. Then her heart corrected her with a shock: the air, the height—no. Again she looked. He had dismounted; the horse was being managed with familiar hands; the very movement of his shoulders was known.

It was Edward.

She moved away and sat down, as if the room had shifted and she must secure herself against falling. The thought came with a desperate command, like an order given in a kitchen when a crisis threatens to ruin the whole service: He comes from Mr. Pratt’s purposely to see us. I will be calm. I will be mistress of myself.

In the same instant she perceived that the others had made the same first mistake and the same correction. She saw her mother and Marianne change colour; saw them glance at her, whisper quickly together. Elinor would have given anything—anything—to speak then, to make them understand that she wished no coolness, no reproach, no awkwardness in their welcome. Whatever he had done or not done, whatever pain he brought with him, she could not bear that he should be met with a studied slight. But her throat was locked; her tongue would not obey. She could only sit and leave their behaviour to their own discretion.

Not a syllable was spoken aloud. They listened. His footsteps came along the gravel path—distinct, unhurried, each step sounding through the quiet like a spoon against a cup in a silent room. He was in the passage; then, almost immediately, before them.

His face, as he entered, held no joy that could have eased Elinor even had she been prepared to see it. He was pale with agitation; his manner had the guarded fear of a man uncertain of his reception and conscious that he did not deserve a kind one. Mrs. Dashwood, in the warmth of her heart resolving to be guided by Elinor’s supposed wishes, went forward with forced composure, gave him her hand, and wished him joy.

He coloured and stammered a reply that could scarcely be understood. Elinor felt her own lips move with her mother’s words, as if they would join them; and once the moment passed, she wished intensely that she too had held out her hand. But it was too late; to do it now would have been pointed, unnatural. With an expression meant to be open, she sat again and spoke of the weather—of the dryness of the season—as if the state of the fields were all that occupied her.

Marianne had retreated as far as she could, half hidden, to conceal distress that would not be governed. Margaret, catching fragments of meaning without comprehending the whole, believed dignity required distance and silence; she placed herself as far from Edward as possible and sat immovably still.

When Elinor had exhausted the subject of the season’s dryness, a pause fell—heavy, awful, filling the room with the kind of quiet in which every breath is heard. Mrs. Dashwood, feeling herself obliged to proceed, asked, with an effort at steadiness, whether he had left Mrs. Ferrars very well.

In a hurried manner he answered yes.

Another pause—longer, more oppressive.

Elinor forced herself to speak again, though she feared the sound of her own voice. “Is Mrs. Ferrars at Longstaple?”

“At Longstaple!” He looked up with real surprise. “No—my mother is in town.”

“I meant,” said Elinor, taking up some work from the table to give her hands occupation, “to enquire for Mrs. Edward Ferrars.”

She did not dare lift her eyes. She felt, rather than saw, the movement beside her; she knew her mother and Marianne were both looking directly at him.

Edward coloured again. He seemed perplexed—hesitating as if he doubted what he had heard and what he was expected to say. At last, with an uncertainty that made the words stumble out, he answered, “Perhaps you mean—my brother—you mean Mrs.—Mrs. Robert Ferrars.”

“Mrs. Robert Ferrars!” Marianne repeated, and her mother with her, in an accent of astonishment so complete it almost seemed to change the air of the room. Even Elinor, who could not utter a sound, fixed her eyes on him with the same impatient wonder.

He rose abruptly and walked to the window, as if he did not know where to place himself. There, with a restless hand, he took up a pair of scissors that lay near, and while—without thought—he cut and mangled the sheath as he spoke, spoiling both in his agitation, he said in a hurried voice, “Perhaps you do not know. You may not have heard that my brother is lately married to—to the youngest—to Miss Lucy Steele.”

The words were echoed—brokenly, incredulously—by all but Elinor. She remained bent over her work, yet the room swam; the meaning struck her with such sudden force that for a moment she scarcely knew where she was.

“Yes,” Edward added, “they were married last week, and are now at Dawlish.”

Elinor could endure the restraint no longer. She rose so quickly that her chair scraped the floor, and almost ran from the room. The moment the door shut behind her, the flood she had held back broke loose. She wept—not with the aching, hopeless tears of loss, but with a joy so sharp and relieving it seemed as if it might never end, as if her whole heart, long compressed, had been given space to breathe again.

Behind her, in the parlour, Edward—who until then had looked anywhere rather than at her—saw her sudden flight, and perhaps he saw, perhaps he even heard, what it meant. After that, he fell into a silence so deep that no remark, no question, no affectionate address from Mrs. Dashwood could reach him. At last, without speaking at all, he left the room and walked out towards the village, leaving Mrs. Dashwood, Marianne, and Margaret in a bewilderment that had no remedy but conjecture—astonished and perplexed by a change in his situation so extraordinary and so swift that it made every former certainty seem like a story misremembered.

However inexplicable the manner of it might seem to every mind in Barton Cottage, the fact sat plain as a loaf on the board: Edward was free. Free—after four years of bondage to a promise made in the dim, idle season of youth, when a heart, like dough left too long without a hand to work it, will rise into shapes it never meant to take. And what use, Elinor’s family decided, could possibly be made of such freedom, in a man who had once bound himself without his mother’s blessing? Why, to bind himself again—immediately, and this time where every honest wish in the cottage had long tended.

His errand, indeed, was as simple as a supper of good broth and bread: only to ask Elinor to marry him. Yet if he had ever rehearsed any question in his life, it was that one; and still he came to it as if the words must be coaxed into warmth like butter before it will spread. He wanted air. He wanted courage. He wanted—though he would not have named it so—the right hush of a kitchen at work, when the kettle has not yet found its boil and the knives are laid down, and even the birds outside seem to listen.

How soon he got himself into the resolution, how quickly fortune gave him the moment, what he said and how he was answered—those particulars slipped, for everyone in that house, into a blur of breath and beating hearts. It is enough that when they sat down to dinner at four o’clock, scarcely three hours from his arrival, Edward had secured his lady, gained Mrs. Dashwood’s consent, and was—by profession, by truth, by every visible alteration in voice and countenance—one of the happiest men alive.

His happiness held more than the common triumph of accepted love. He had the strange, sudden lift of a prisoner who finds the door unbarred without shame. He was loosed, without reproach to himself, from a snare that had long been misery—from a woman he had long ceased to love—and in the very same breath raised into a safety with another which, once he dared to desire it, must have seemed nearly beyond hope. He had not come from suspense to peace, but from wretchedness to joy; and the change ran through him in a grateful, unforced cheerfulness, as if some long-stifled spring had at last been opened and allowed to sing.

His heart, too, lay open to Elinor: weaknesses confessed, errors owned, that first attachment spoken of at the clear-eyed dignity of four-and-twenty. He even traced it to the hunger of an unoccupied life.

“It was a foolish, idle inclination on my side,” he said—“the consequence of knowing so little of the world, and having nothing to do. Had my mother given me some active profession when I was taken, at eighteen, from Mr. Pratt’s care, I am sure it would never have happened. I left Longstaple, thinking my preference unconquerable; but had I then had any pursuit—anything to engage my time and keep me away for a few months—I should soon have outgrown it. Instead, I returned home to be completely idle. For a twelvemonth I had not even the nominal employment of belonging to the university, for I was not entered at Oxford till I was nineteen. So I had nothing in the world to do but fancy myself in love. And as my mother did not make home comfortable, and I had no friend in my brother and disliked new acquaintance, it was natural I should be much at Longstaple, where I always felt welcome. From eighteen to nineteen I was there most of the time: Lucy appeared everything amiable and obliging. She was pretty too—at least I thought so then; and I had seen so little of other women that I had no comparisons. Considering all this, foolish as our engagement was, and foolish as it has since proved, it was not at the time wholly unnatural—nor, I hope, quite inexcusable.”

A few hours had worked so great a change in the Dashwoods’ minds and happiness as promised them all a night without sleep. Mrs. Dashwood, too happy to be comfortable, could not find a way to praise Elinor enough without embarrassing Edward, or to love Edward enough without overflowing into exclamations; nor could she decide whether it was kinder to leave them to themselves or keep them near her sight, like cherished, newly rescued things.

Marianne could speak her joy only in tears. Joy brought comparisons, and comparisons regrets; she loved her sister too truly not to feel happiness for her, but it was a happiness that did not lift her spirits, nor find her language.

Elinor—what could describe the successive turns of her composure? From the instant she learned Lucy was married to another and Edward was free, to the instant Edward justified the hope that sprang up with the news, she was everything by turns except tranquil. And when doubt and solicitude were at last swept away—when she compared what she had feared so lately with what now stood before her—saw him honourably released, and saw him immediately using that release to offer her an affection as tender and constant as she had ever believed—it was too much. She was oppressed by felicity; and though the human mind grows used to good fortune with surprising readiness, it required hours before her spirits settled into any sedateness, her heart into any quiet.

Edward was fixed at the cottage at least for a week. Whatever other claims might wait upon him, it was impossible that less than a week could be given to Elinor’s company, or suffice to say half that needed saying of past, present, and future. For though a few hours of hard, incessant talking may dispatch more subjects than can be common between any two rational creatures, lovers are not rational in that particular: with them nothing is finished, no account complete, until it has been told again and again, with each repetition tasting different—like yesterday’s stew warmed slowly, deepened, and somehow more nourishing for the time.

Lucy’s marriage, the unceasing wonder of them all, became early matter between the lovers; and Elinor’s knowledge of both parties made it appear, from every angle, one of the most extraordinary circumstances she had ever encountered. How Lucy and Robert could be thrown together—by what attraction Robert could be drawn to marry a girl of whose beauty he had spoken without admiration—a girl already engaged to his brother, and the very cause for which that brother had been cast off—was beyond her. To her heart it was delightful; to her imagination, ridiculous; to her judgment, a complete puzzle.

Edward could only grope toward an explanation: perhaps a first accidental meeting, vanity flattered on one side and artful flattery on the other, drawing them on by degrees.

Elinor remembered what Robert had once told her in Harley Street—his opinion of what his own mediation might have done, had it been applied for in time—and repeated it. Edward laughed with the familiarity of a brother.

“That is exactly like Robert,” said he; and after a moment added, “and that might have been in his head when the acquaintance first began. Lucy might at first have thought only of securing his good offices for me. Other designs could come afterward.”

How long it had been carrying on he could not guess, for at Oxford—where he had stayed by choice after leaving London—he had no means of hearing of Lucy but from herself; and her letters, to the very last, had been neither less frequent nor less affectionate than usual. No suspicion had prepared him. When at last the truth burst on him in a letter from Lucy herself, he believed he had been half-stupefied between wonder, horror, and joy.

He put the letter into Elinor’s hands.

“DEAR SIR,

“Being very sure I have long lost your affections, I have thought myself at liberty to bestow my own on another, and have no doubt of being as happy with him as I once used to think I might be with you; but I scorn to accept a hand while the heart was another’s. Sincerely wish you happy in your choice, and it shall not be my fault if we are not always good friends, as our near relationship now makes proper. I can safely say I owe you no ill-will, and am sure you will be too generous to do us any ill offices. Your brother has gained my affections entirely, and as we could not live without one another, we are just returned from the altar, and are now on our way to Dawlish for a few weeks, which place your dear brother has great curiosity to see, but thought I would first trouble you with these few lines, and shall always remain,

“Your sincere well-wisher, friend, and sister,

“LUCY FERRARS.

“I have burnt all your letters, and will return your picture the first opportunity. Please to destroy my scrawls—but the ring with my hair you are very welcome to keep.”

Elinor read it, and returned it without comment. The cottage was quiet for a beat, except for the small living sounds that always persist in a country house: a clock’s patient tick, the faint creak of settling wood, a sparrow’s brisk dispute in the eaves. Such sounds—unbought, unadvertised—seemed suddenly precious in contrast with the manufactured bustle of town, where even feelings are often paraded like goods.

“I will not ask your opinion of it as a composition,” Edward said at last. “For worlds would I not have had a letter of hers seen by you in former days. In a sister it is bad enough, but in a wife!—how I have blushed over her pages! And I believe I may say that, since the first half year of our—foolish business—this is the only letter I ever received from her, where the substance made me any amends for the defect of the style.”

“However it came about,” Elinor said after a pause, “they are certainly married. And your mother has brought on herself a most appropriate punishment. The independence she settled on Robert, in resentment against you, has put it in his power to choose for himself; and she has actually been bribing one son with a thousand a year to do the very thing she disinherited the other for intending. She can hardly be less hurt by Robert’s marrying Lucy than she would have been by your marrying her.”

“She will be more hurt,” Edward replied, “because Robert always was her favourite. She will be more hurt—and on the same principle will forgive him much sooner.”

In what state the affair stood between him and his family he could not tell; no communication had yet been attempted. He had left Oxford within twenty-four hours of receiving Lucy’s letter and set off with only one clear object—the nearest road to Barton. He could make no plan of conduct that did not begin and end in Elinor. He had to know his fate with Miss Dashwood first; and by the rapidity with which he came to learn it, it might be supposed that—despite an old jealousy of Colonel Brandon, despite modest thoughts of his own deserts, and despite the politeness with which he spoke of doubts—he did not truly expect a cruel reception. Yet it was his business to say that he did, and he said it prettily enough.

Lucy’s marriage led naturally to Lucy herself; and Elinor, without softness, understood that Lucy had intended to wound—meant, perhaps, to depart with a flourish of malice, as she had done once before by her message through Thomas. Edward, now fully enlightened to her character, had no difficulty believing her capable of wanton ill-nature. Yet he confessed how long he had resisted seeing her plainly.

“I had long been aware,” he said, “even before I knew you, of her ignorance—of a want of liberality in some of her opinions; but I always imputed it to her want of education. Until her last letter, I believed her well-disposed and good-hearted, and thoroughly attached to me. Nothing but such a belief could have kept me bound to an engagement which had become, long before it was discovered, a continual source of regret.

“I thought it my duty—independent of my feelings—to give her the option of continuing the engagement or not, when I was renounced by my mother and seemed without a friend in the world. In such a situation, where there was nothing to tempt vanity or avarice, how could I suppose—when she insisted so earnestly on sharing my fate—that anything but disinterested affection induced her? And even now I cannot comprehend what advantage she fancied. Why fetter herself to a man for whom she had no regard, and who had only two thousand pounds? She could not foresee Colonel Brandon’s living.”

“No,” Elinor answered, “but she might suppose something would occur in your favour; that your family might relent. And at any rate she lost nothing by continuing the engagement, since she has proved it fettered neither her inclination nor her actions. The connection was respectable and likely gained her consequence among her friends; and if nothing better offered, it would be better to marry you than remain single.”

Edward was, of course, immediately convinced that nothing could be more natural than Lucy’s conduct, nor more self-evident than her motives.

Elinor, in a tone that tried for severity and could not help tasting of relief, scolded him for his time at Norland—harshly as women often scold a fault that has flattered them.

“Your behaviour was certainly very wrong,” said she, “because—to say nothing of my own conviction—our relations were led away by it to expect what, as you were then situated, could never be.”

He could only plead ignorance of his own heart, and mistaken confidence in the strength of his engagement.

“I was simple enough to think,” he said, “that because my faith was plighted elsewhere, there could be no danger in being with you; that the consciousness of my engagement would keep my heart as safe and sacred as my honour. I felt I admired you, but I told myself it was only friendship; and until I began making comparisons between you and Lucy, I did not know how far I was gone. After that, I suppose I was wrong to remain so much in Sussex; and the arguments by which I reconciled myself to it were no better than these—the danger is my own; I injure nobody but myself.”

Elinor smiled, and shook her head.

Edward heard with pleasure that Colonel Brandon was expected at the cottage; he wanted not only a closer acquaintance, but a chance to convince him he no longer resented Delaford.

“At present,” Edward said, “after thanks so ungraciously delivered as mine were, he must think I have never forgiven him for offering.”

Now he wondered himself he had never yet been to Delaford; but he had taken so little interest in the matter that all he knew of it—the house, the garden, the glebe; the size of the parish, the condition of the land, the rate of the tithes—he owed to Elinor, who had listened to Colonel Brandon’s accounts with such attention as made her entirely mistress of the subject. There was something in that—something like an old domestic art preserved by careful hearing: the way knowledge, passed in plain speech from one person to another, can outlast louder, more fashionable talk that fills rooms and leaves nothing behind.

Only one question remained between them, one difficulty to overcome. They were united by mutual affection, with the warmest approbation of their true friends; their knowledge of each other promised happiness; and yet they wanted something to live upon. Edward had two thousand pounds; Elinor one; with the living of Delaford that was all they could call their own. Mrs. Dashwood could advance nothing. And neither Edward nor Elinor was quite enough in love to think that three hundred and fifty pounds a year would provide all the comforts of life.

Edward was not wholly without hopes of a favourable change in his mother, and on that he rested for what might remain deficient in their income. Elinor, knowing the temper of Mrs. Ferrars too well, could not depend on it. Since Edward would still be unable to marry Miss Morton, and since Mrs. Ferrars had spoken of his choosing Elinor as only a lesser evil than his choosing Lucy, Elinor feared that Robert’s offence would serve no purpose but to enrich Fanny.

About four days after Edward’s arrival Colonel Brandon appeared, completing Mrs. Dashwood’s satisfaction and giving her, for the first time since she had lived at Barton, the importance of having more company than her house could easily hold. Edward retained the privilege of first comer; Colonel Brandon therefore walked each evening to his old quarters at the Park, and returned in the morning early enough to interrupt the lovers’ first tête-à-tête before breakfast—before the tea had properly drawn, before the bread was fully aired from its cloth, before the cottage had quite settled into its day’s soft rural sound.

A three weeks’ residence at Delaford—where, in his evening hours at least, he had little to do but calculate the disproportion between thirty-six and seventeen—brought him to Barton with a mind that needed all the improvement in Marianne’s looks, all the kindness of her welcome, and all the encouragement of her mother’s language, to make it cheerful. Among such friends, however, and such flattery, he revived.

No rumour of Lucy’s marriage had yet reached him. He knew nothing of what had passed; his first hours were spent in hearing and wondering. Mrs. Dashwood explained everything; and he found fresh reason to rejoice in what he had done for Mr. Ferrars, since in the end it promoted Elinor’s interest.

It would be needless to say the gentlemen advanced in each other’s good opinion as they advanced in acquaintance; they could not do otherwise. Their resemblance in principles and sense, in disposition and manner of thinking, would likely have united them in friendship without any other attraction; but their being in love with two sisters, and those sisters fond of each other, made their mutual regard inevitable and immediate—quick as fire taking to dry kindling, but steadier, once lit, than mere sparks of vanity and show.

The letters from town—which a few days earlier would have made every nerve in Elinor thrill with transport—now arrived to be read with less emotion than mirth.

Mrs. Jennings wrote the wonderful tale, vented honest indignation against the jilting girl, and poured forth compassion for poor Mr. Edward, who, she was sure, had doted upon the worthless hussy and was now, by all accounts, almost broken-hearted at Oxford.

“I do think,” she continued, “nothing was ever carried on so sly; for it was but two days before Lucy called and sat a couple of hours with me. Not a soul suspected it, not even Nancy, who, poor soul! came crying to me the day after in a great fright for fear of Mrs. Ferrars, as well as not knowing how to get to Plymouth; for Lucy, it seems, borrowed all her money before she went off to be married, on purpose, we suppose, to make a show with, and poor Nancy had not seven shillings in the world; so I was very glad to give her five guineas to take her down to Exeter, where she thinks of staying three or four weeks with Mrs. Burgess, in hopes, as I tell her, to fall in with the Doctor again. And I must say Lucy’s crossness not to take them along in the chaise is worse than all. Poor Mr. Edward! I cannot get him out of my head, but you must send for him to Barton, and Miss Marianne must try to comfort him.”

Mr. Dashwood’s strains were more solemn. Mrs. Ferrars was the most unfortunate of women; poor Fanny had suffered agonies of sensibility; and he considered the continued existence of each under such a blow with grateful wonder. Robert’s offence was unpardonable, but Lucy’s infinitely worse. Neither were ever again to be mentioned to Mrs. Ferrars; and even if she could be induced to forgive her son, his wife should never be acknowledged as a daughter nor permitted to appear in her presence. The secrecy of the business rationally heightened the crime, because had any suspicion occurred, proper measures would have been taken to prevent the marriage. And he called on Elinor to join him in regretting that Lucy’s engagement with Edward had not rather been fulfilled than that she should thus be the means of spreading misery further in the family.

He proceeded:

“Mrs. Ferrars has never yet mentioned Edward’s name, which does not surprise us; but to our great astonishment, not a line has been received from him on the occasion. Perhaps he is kept silent by fear of offending, and I shall therefore give him a hint by a line to Oxford, that his sister and I both think a letter of proper submission from him, addressed perhaps to Fanny, and by her shown to her mother, might not be taken amiss; for we all know the tenderness of Mrs. Ferrars’s heart, and that she wishes for nothing so much as to be on good terms with her children.”

This paragraph mattered to Edward’s prospects and conduct. It determined him to attempt reconciliation—though not in the manner John suggested.

“A letter of proper submission!” he repeated. “Would they have me beg my mother’s pardon for Robert’s ingratitude to her, and breach of honour to me? I can make no submission. I am grown neither humble nor penitent by what has passed. I am grown very happy; but that would not interest. I know of no submission that is proper for me to make.”

“You may certainly ask to be forgiven,” Elinor said, “because you have offended; and I should think you might now venture so far as to profess some concern for having ever formed the engagement which drew on you your mother’s anger.”

He agreed that he might.

“And when she has forgiven you,” Elinor added, “perhaps a little humility may be convenient while acknowledging a second engagement—almost as imprudent, in her eyes, as the first.”

He had nothing to urge against it, yet still resisted the idea of a written submission; and therefore, to ease him—since he declared himself much more willing to make mean concessions by word of mouth than on paper—it was resolved that, instead of writing to Fanny, he should go to London and personally entreat her good offices.

“And if they really do interest themselves,” Marianne said, with the candour of her newer, chastened self, “in bringing about a reconciliation, I shall think even John and Fanny are not entirely without merit.”

After Colonel Brandon’s visit of only three or four days, the two gentlemen left Barton together. They were to go immediately to Delaford, that Edward might gain personal knowledge of his future home and assist his patron and friend in deciding what improvements were needed; and from thence, after a couple of nights, Edward was to proceed to town. The cottage, when their footsteps had died away down the lane, seemed to listen to its own restored quiet—the wind in the hedgerow, the distant lowing from pasture, the soft domestic clink of life continuing—while Elinor, with love at last permitted to be spoken aloud, began the slow, astonishing work of learning to believe her happiness was not a thing to be taken away.

Mrs. Ferrars resisted first, as if resistance were a duty laid upon her by birth and habit—violent enough to satisfy her own idea of dignity, steady enough to keep her safe from the suspicion of tenderness. Only after that proper battle was fought did she admit Edward to her presence, restore him—by a sentence rather than an embrace—to the rank of son.

Her household, in truth, had lately been as changeable as a kitchen ruled by caprice instead of season. For years she had possessed two sons, as securely as two well-fed hounds under the table. A few weeks ago Edward had been struck out of her life as if he had spoiled the broth beyond redemption; then Robert, by a similar act of “annihilation,” had left her, for a short and mortifying fortnight, without any heir at all. And now—by the odd resuscitation of Edward—she had one again, brought back as if the dish she had thrown away had returned to the platter, still warm.

Edward, though permitted once more to exist, did not feel the continuance of that existence entirely safe until he had served up the truth of his present engagement. It lay on his mind like an untasted sauce whose very name might offend the palate of the house; and he feared that, once announced, it might curdle his mother’s restored affection as abruptly as it had curdled before. He revealed it, therefore, with a caution so anxious it might have been mistaken for guilt; and yet Mrs. Ferrars listened with an unexpected stillness—the kind of calm that belongs to those who mean to disapprove at leisure.

At first she endeavoured, with what she considered reasonable solicitude, to dissuade him from marrying Miss Dashwood. She set before him Miss Morton—higher in rank, heavier in fortune—as if placing a richer joint on the board and expecting him to forget his chosen fare. Miss Morton, she said, was the daughter of a nobleman and had thirty thousand pounds; Miss Dashwood was only the daughter of a private gentleman with no more than three. Edward admitted every figure without flinching; he did not contest the weight of the purse, only refused to let money dictate the heart. When Mrs. Ferrars perceived that truth itself, marshalled into argument, could not move him an inch, she judged it wisest—taught by recent defeats—to submit. And so, after an ungracious delay that she owed to her pride, and which served to keep all suspicion of good-will safely at bay, she pronounced her consent.

What she would do to increase their income came next; and here it was plain that, though Edward was now her only son, he was by no means treated as her eldest. Robert, however inevitably endowed with a thousand a year, encountered no opposition; Edward, to gain at most two hundred and fifty, must take orders—and even then nothing was promised beyond the ten thousand already given with Fanny. It was as much, nevertheless, as Edward and Elinor desired, and more than they had expected; and Mrs. Ferrars herself, by the shuffling air of her excuses, seemed the only person astonished at her own restraint.

With an income sufficient to their wants thus secured, they had nothing to wait for, once Edward was in possession of the living, but the readiness of the house—Colonel Brandon, eager for Elinor’s comfort, improving it with an attention as steady as good husbandry. Yet improvements, like bread, must be allowed their time; and workmen, like careless scullions, have a talent for delay that no watching can cure. There were disappointments enough—boards not come, plaster not dry, a promise made in the morning and forgotten before noon—until Elinor, who had resolved not to marry until everything was ready, did what she so often did when resolution became unreasonable: she broke through it. The ceremony took place in Barton church early in the autumn, when the air had begun to smell of damp earth and late apples, and the lanes carried, in their hedgerows, the soft rustle of leaves turning.

The first month of their marriage they spent at the Mansion-house, that they might superintend the progress of the parsonage and direct every choice on the spot—papers for the walls, shrubberies for the grounds, even the invention of a sweep, as if laying out the courses of a meal and the order in which each comfort should arrive. Mrs. Jennings’s prophecies, though jumbled as her imagination always made them, were chiefly fulfilled; for by Michaelmas she visited them at the parsonage and found—so she firmly believed—one of the happiest couples in the world. In truth they had little to wish for but the marriage of Colonel Brandon and Marianne, and rather better pasturage for their cows.

Nearly all their relations and friends came to see them upon their first settling. Mrs. Ferrars arrived to inspect a happiness she was almost ashamed to have authorised, and the Dashwoods were even at the expense of a journey from Sussex to do them honour. John Dashwood, walking one morning with Elinor before the gates of Delaford House, could not keep his own calculations from seasoning his compliments.

“I will not say I am disappointed, my dear sister,” said he, “that would be saying too much; for certainly you have been one of the most fortunate young women in the world as it is. But I confess it would give me great pleasure to call Colonel Brandon brother. His property here—his place, his house—everything is in such respectable and excellent condition! And his woods—I have not seen such timber anywhere in Dorsetshire as there is now standing in Delaford Hanger! And though perhaps Marianne may not seem exactly the person to attract him, yet I think it would altogether be advisable for you to have them now frequently staying with you; for as Colonel Brandon seems a great deal at home, nobody can tell what may happen when people are much thrown together and see little of anybody else. And it will always be in your power to set her off to advantage, and so forth. In short, you may as well give her a chance: you understand me.”

But though Mrs. Ferrars did come, and always treated Edward and Elinor with a make-believe of decent affection, they were never insulted by her real favour. That was reserved—like the choice cut kept back for the diner she preferred—for the folly of Robert and the cunning of his wife; and it was earned by them before many months had passed away.

Lucy’s selfish sagacity, which had first drawn Robert into his scrape, became the principal instrument of his deliverance. Respectful humility, assiduous attentions, endless flatteries—she brought them out as a practiced cook brings out preserves and cordials when she has found the exact weakness of a guest. As soon as the smallest opening appeared, she slipped herself through it and began to sweeten Mrs. Ferrars’s temper by degrees. The whole of Lucy’s behaviour—and the prosperity that crowned it—may stand as a most encouraging instance of what unceasing attention to self-interest will do in securing every advantage of fortune, with no other sacrifice than time and conscience.

When Robert first sought her acquaintance and privately visited her in Bartlett’s Buildings, he meant only what Edward had imputed to him: to persuade her to give up the engagement. He expected affection—hers and Edward’s—to be the only obstacle; and presumed one or two interviews would settle the matter. In that point, however, and that only, he erred. Lucy soon gave him hopes that his eloquence would convince her in time—yet time always required another visit, another conversation. Some doubt lingered whenever they parted, which could only be removed by another half-hour of discourse. Thus his attendance was secured; and the rest followed in course.

Instead of talking of Edward, they came gradually to talk only of Robert—a subject on which he had always more to say than on any other, and in which she soon betrayed an interest equal to his own. It became speedily evident that he had supplanted his brother entirely. He was proud of the conquest, proud of tricking Edward, and very proud of marrying privately without his mother’s consent. What immediately followed is already known. They passed some months in great happiness at Dawlish; she had many relations and old acquaintances to cut, and he drew several plans for magnificent cottages. From thence returning to town, they procured the forgiveness of Mrs. Ferrars by the simple expedient of asking it—an expedient adopted at Lucy’s instigation.

The forgiveness at first, as was reasonable, comprehended only Robert; Lucy, owing his mother no duty and therefore able (in Mrs. Ferrars’s logic) to have transgressed none, remained some weeks longer unpardoned. But perseverance—humility in conduct and messages, self-condemnation for Robert’s offence, gratitude for the unkindness she received—procured her in time that haughty notice which overcame her by its graciousness, and led soon afterwards, by rapid degrees, to the highest state of affection and influence. Lucy became as necessary to Mrs. Ferrars as either Robert or Fanny. Edward was never cordially forgiven for having once intended to marry her; and Elinor, though superior to Lucy in birth and fortune, was spoken of as an intruder. Lucy, in everything, was considered and openly acknowledged as a favourite child.

They settled in town; they received very liberal assistance from Mrs. Ferrars; they were on the best terms imaginable with the Dashwoods. And setting aside the jealousies and ill-will continually subsisting between Fanny and Lucy—in which their husbands, of course, took a part—as well as the frequent domestic disagreements between Robert and Lucy themselves, nothing could exceed the harmony in which they all lived together.

What Edward had done to forfeit the right of eldest son might have puzzled many to find out; and what Robert had done to succeed to it might have puzzled them still more. The arrangement, however, was justified in its effects if not in its cause; for nothing in Robert’s style of living or talking ever hinted that he regretted the extent of his income as leaving his brother too little or bringing himself too much. And if Edward was judged by the ready discharge of his duties in every particular, by an increasing attachment to his wife and his home, and by the regular cheerfulness of his spirits, he might be supposed no less contented with his lot, no less free from any wish of exchange.

Elinor’s marriage divided her from her family as little as could be contrived without rendering Barton Cottage entirely useless; her mother and sisters spent much more than half their time with her. Mrs. Dashwood was acting from policy as well as pleasure in the frequency of her visits at Delaford; for her wish to bring Marianne and Colonel Brandon together was hardly less earnest—though rather more liberal—than John’s had expressed. It was now her darling object. Precious as her daughter’s company was to her, she desired nothing so much as to give up its constant enjoyment to her valued friend; and to see Marianne settled at the Mansion-house was equally the wish of Edward and Elinor. They each felt Brandon’s sorrows and their own obligations; and Marianne, by general consent, was to be the reward of all.

With such a confederacy against her—with a knowledge so intimate of Brandon’s goodness—with a conviction of his fond attachment to herself which, at last, though long after it had been visible to everyone else, burst upon her—what could she do?

Marianne Dashwood was born to an extraordinary fate: to discover the falsehood of her own opinions, and to counteract by her conduct her favourite maxims. She was born to overcome an affection formed so late as at seventeen, and with no sentiment superior to strong esteem and lively friendship voluntarily to give her hand to another—and that other a man who had suffered no less than herself under the event of a former attachment; whom, two years before, she had considered too old to marry; and who still sought the constitutional safeguard of a flannel waistcoat.

But so it was. Instead of falling a sacrifice to an irresistible passion, as once she had fondly expected—rather like a heroine in a song whose refrain repeats itself until it becomes law—instead of remaining forever with her mother and finding her only pleasures in retirement and study, as afterwards, in calmer judgment, she had determined on, she found herself at nineteen submitting to new attachments, entering on new duties, placed in a new home: a wife, the mistress of a family, and the patroness of a village.

Colonel Brandon was now as happy as all those who best loved him believed he deserved to be. In Marianne he was consoled for every past affliction; her regard and her society restored his mind to animation and his spirits to cheerfulness. That Marianne found her own happiness in forming his was equally the persuasion and delight of every observing friend. Marianne could never love by halves; and her whole heart became, in time, as devoted to her husband as it had once been to Willoughby.

Willoughby could not hear of her marriage without a pang; and his punishment was soon afterwards made complete by the voluntary forgiveness of Mrs. Smith. By stating his marriage with a woman of character as the source of her clemency, she gave him reason to believe that, had he behaved with honour towards Marianne, he might at once have been happy and rich. That his repentance, which thus brought its own punishment, was sincere need not be doubted; nor that he long thought of Colonel Brandon with envy and Marianne with regret. But that he was forever inconsolable—fled from society, contracted habitual gloom, or died of a broken heart—must not be depended on; for he did neither. He lived to exert, and frequently to enjoy himself. His wife was not always out of humour, nor his home always uncomfortable; and in his breed of horses and dogs, and in sporting of every kind, he found no inconsiderable degree of domestic felicity. For Marianne, however, in spite of his incivility in surviving her loss, he always retained a decided regard that interested him in everything that befell her, made her his secret standard of perfection in woman; and many a rising beauty would be slighted by him in after-days as bearing no comparison with Mrs. Brandon.

Mrs. Dashwood was prudent enough to remain at the cottage, without attempting removal to Delaford; and fortunately for Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, when Marianne was taken from them, Margaret had reached an age highly suitable for dancing and not very ineligible for being supposed to have a lover. Between Barton and Delaford there was that constant communication which strong family affection naturally dictates—the easy coming and going, the familiar sounds of carriage-wheels on the same road, voices recognized at the door before the knock is finished, and the quiet domestic music of two households answering one another across short distances, not drowned out by any harsher din.

And among the merits and happiness of Elinor and Marianne, let it not be ranked as the least considerable that, though sisters and living almost within sight of each other, they could live without disagreement between themselves, or producing coolness between their husbands.
